Adderall is a central nervous system stimulant.
Adderall is used to treat attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD).
Adderall is used to treat a sleep disorder called narcolepsy.
Adderall contains mixed amphetamine and dextroamphetamine salts.
Adderall contains a combination of amphetamine and dextroamphetamine.
These substances affect chemicals in the brain and nerves.
These chemicals contribute to hyperactivity and impulse control.
For ADHD, Adderall is used in children 3 to 17 years of age and in adults.
Adderall may help increase attention and decrease impulsiveness and hyperactivity.
For narcolepsy, Adderall is used in people 6 years and older.
It is not known if Adderall is safe and effective for ADHD in children under 3 years of age.
It is not known if Adderall is safe and effective for narcolepsy in children under 6 years of age.
Adderall is a federally controlled substance (CII).
Adderall contains amphetamine.
Amphetamine can be a target for people who abuse prescription medicines or street drugs.
Keep Adderall in a safe place to protect Adderall from theft.
Never give your Adderall to anyone else, because Adderall may cause death or harm them.
Selling or giving away Adderall is against the law.
Adderall may also be used for purposes not listed in this medication guide.
Adderall (mixed amphetamine and dextroamphetamine salts) was FDA-approved on January 19, 1960.
Extended-release formulations of Adderall (Adderall XR) are only used to treat ADHD.
Adderall has a Boxed Warning for abuse, misuse, and addiction.
Abuse, misuse, and addiction:
Adderall has a high chance of abuse and misuse.
It may lead to substance use problems, including addiction.
Misuse and abuse of Adderall, other amphetamine-containing medicines, and methylphenidate-containing medicines can lead to overdose and death.
The risk of overdose and death is increased with higher doses.
The risk of overdose and death is increased when the medicine is used in ways that are not approved.
Examples of unapproved use include snorting or injection.
Adderall may lead to physical dependence after prolonged use, even if taken as directed.
Your healthcare provider should check you or your child for the risk of abuse, misuse, and addiction before starting treatment.
Your healthcare provider will monitor you or your child during treatment.
Tell your healthcare provider if you or your child has ever abused or been dependent on alcohol, prescription medicines, or street drugs.
Heart-related risks:
Stimulants have caused stroke, heart attack, and sudden death in people with high blood pressure, heart disease, or a heart defect.
Your healthcare provider should check you or your child carefully for heart problems before starting Adderall.
Your healthcare provider should check blood pressure and heart rate regularly during treatment.
Call your healthcare provider right away or go to the nearest hospital emergency room if you or your child have any signs of heart problems.
These signs include chest pain, shortness of breath, or fainting during treatment.
Mental (psychiatric) problems:
Adderall may cause new or worse behavior and thought problems.
Adderall may cause new or worse bipolar illness.
Adderall may cause new psychotic symptoms, such as hearing voices, or seeing or believing things that are not real.
Adderall may cause new manic symptoms.
This is more likely if you have a history of depression, mental illness, or bipolar disorder.
Tell your healthcare provider about any mental problems you or your child have, or about a family history of suicide, bipolar illness, or depression.
Call your healthcare provider right away if you or your child have any new or worsening mental symptoms during treatment.
This is especially important if you experience hearing voices.
It is also important if you see or believe things that are not real.
It is also important if you have new manic symptoms.
Circulation problems:
You may have blood circulation problems that can cause numbness, pain, or discoloration in your fingers or toes.
Call your doctor right away if you have unexplained wounds appearing on your fingers or toes during treatment.
Other warnings:
Do not use Adderall if you have used an MAO inhibitor in the past 14 days.
Examples of MAO inhibitors include isocarboxazid, linezolid, phenelzine, rasagiline, selegiline, and tranylcypromine.
Do not use Adderall if you have received a methylene blue injection.
A dangerous drug interaction could occur.
You may not be able to use Adderall if you have glaucoma.
You may not be able to use Adderall if you have an overactive thyroid.
You may not be able to use Adderall if you have severe anxiety or agitation.
You may not be able to use Adderall if you have moderate to severe high blood pressure.
You may not be able to use Adderall if you have heart disease or coronary artery disease.
You may not be able to use Adderall if you have vascular disease.
You may not be able to use Adderall if you have a history of drug or alcohol addiction.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to Adderall.
These signs include hives.
These signs include difficult breathing.
These signs include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Serious side effects:
Adderall may cause serious side effects.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
signs of heart problems, such as chest pain, trouble breathing, or feeling like you might pass out
signs of psychosis, such as hallucinations, new behavior problems, aggression, hostility, or paranoia
signs of circulation problems, such as numbness, pain, a cold feeling, unexplained wounds, or skin color changes (pale, red, or blue appearance) in your fingers or toes
a seizure
muscle twitches (tics)
changes in your vision.
See warnings above.
Slowing of growth (height and weight) in children:
Children should have their height and weight checked often during treatment.
Your healthcare provider may stop treatment if your child is not growing or gaining weight as expected.
Serotonin syndrome:
This problem may happen when Adderall is taken with certain other medicines.
This problem may be life-threatening.
Stop taking Adderall.
Get medical help right away if you develop agitation, hallucinations, fever, sweating, shivering, fast heart rate, muscle stiffness, twitching, loss of coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea.
Common side effects:
Common side effects of Adderall may include stomach pain.
Common side effects of Adderall may include loss of appetite.
Common side effects of Adderall may include weight loss.
Common side effects of Adderall may include mood changes.
Common side effects of Adderall may include feeling nervous.
Common side effects of Adderall may include fast heart rate.
Common side effects of Adderall may include headache.
Common side effects of Adderall may include dizziness.
Common side effects of Adderall may include sleep problems (insomnia).
Common side effects of Adderall may include dry mouth.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Do not take Adderall if you or your child:
are allergic to amphetamine products or any of the ingredients in Adderall (see ingredients below)
are taking, or have taken within the past 14 days, an MAO inhibitor, including the antibiotic linezolid or the intravenous medicine methylene blue.
have a rare adrenal gland tumor called pheochromocytoma.
Before taking Adderall, tell your healthcare provider about all your medical conditions including if you or anyone in your family has ever had:
heart problems, heart disease, heart defects, or high blood pressure, or a family history of sudden death or heart problems
mental problems including psychosis, mania, bipolar illness, or depression, or a family history of suicide, bipolar illness, or depression
kidney or liver problems
seizures or an abnormal brain wave test (EEG)
circulation problems in the fingers or toes
repeated movements or sounds (tics) or Tourette's syndrome, or a family history of tics or Tourette's syndrome.
Also tell your doctor if you are pregnant, intending to become pregnant, or breastfeeding.
Taking Adderall during pregnancy can cause premature birth, low birth weight, or withdrawal symptoms in the newborn baby.
Tell your healthcare provider if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant.
There is a pregnancy registry that collects information about the health of females exposed to Adderall and females's babies.
Adderall passes into breast milk and may harm a nursing baby.
You should not breastfeed during treatment with Adderall.
Talk to your healthcare provider about the best way to feed the baby during treatment.
Take Adderall exactly as prescribed by your healthcare provider.
Follow all directions on your prescription label.
Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.
Do not take Adderall in larger or smaller amounts or for longer than recommended.
The first dose of the day is usually taken when you first wake up.
Taking Adderall in the afternoon may cause trouble sleeping.
Adderall can be taken with or without food.
Do not crush or chew an extended-release capsule.
Swallow it whole.
To make swallowing easier, you may open the capsule.
You may sprinkle Adderall into a spoonful of applesauce.
Then swallow all of the mixture right away.
Do not chew the mixture.
Do not divide Adderall in the capsule.
Do not save the mixture for later use.
Adderall may be habit-forming.
Never share Adderall with another person, especially someone with a history of drug abuse or addiction.
Keep Adderall where others cannot get to it.
While using Adderall, your doctor will need to check your progress at regular visits.
Adderall can cause unusual results with certain medical tests.
Tell any doctor who treats you that you are using Adderall.
Your healthcare provider may tell you to stop taking Adderall for a short time before you get certain imaging tests.
Usual adult dose for ADHD:
Immediate-release (IR):
Initial dose: 5 mg orally 1 or 2 times a day.
Maintenance dose: the daily dose may be raised in 5 mg increments at weekly intervals until an optimal response is obtained.
Maximum dose: only in rare cases will Maximum dose be necessary to exceed 40 mg per day.
The first dose should be given upon awakening.
One to two additional doses should be given at intervals of 4 to 6 hours.
Extended-release (XR), for patients starting treatment for the first time or switching from another medication:
Initial dose: 20 mg orally once a day.
Usual adult dose for narcolepsy:
Immediate-release (IR):
Initial dose: 10 mg orally per day in divided doses.
Maintenance dose: the daily dose may be raised in 10 mg increments at weekly intervals until an optimal response is obtained.
The usual dose is 5 to 60 mg per day in divided doses.
This depends on the individual patient response.
The first dose should be given on awakening.
One to two additional doses should be given at intervals of 4 to 6 hours.
Usual pediatric dose for ADHD:
Immediate-release (IR), ages 3 to 5 years:
Initial dose: 2.5 mg orally per day.
Maintenance dose: the daily dose may be raised in 2.5 mg increments at weekly intervals until an optimal response is obtained.
Immediate-release (IR), ages 6 to 17 years:
Initial dose: 5 mg orally 1 or 2 times a day.
Maintenance dose: the daily dose may be raised in 5 mg increments at weekly intervals until an optimal response is obtained.
Maximum dose: only in rare cases will Maximum dose be necessary to exceed 40 mg per day.
Extended-release (XR), ages 6 to 12 years:
Initial dose: 5 or 10 mg orally once a day in the morning.
Maintenance dose: the daily dose may be raised in 5 to 10 mg increments at weekly intervals.
Maximum dose: 30 mg per day.
Extended-release (XR), ages 13 to 17 years:
Initial dose: 10 mg orally once a day.
Maintenance dose: may be increased to 20 mg per day after one week if symptoms are not adequately controlled.
Maximum dose: 30 mg per day.
Usual pediatric dose for narcolepsy:
Immediate-release (IR), ages 6 to 11 years:
Initial dose: 5 mg orally per day in divided doses.
Maintenance dose: the daily dose may be raised in 5 mg increments at weekly intervals until an optimal response is obtained.
Immediate-release (IR), ages 12 years and older:
Initial dose: 10 mg orally per day in divided doses.
Maintenance dose: the daily dose may be raised in 10 mg increments at weekly intervals until an optimal response is obtained.
Narcolepsy rarely occurs in children under 12 years of age.
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember, but not late in the day.
Skip the missed dose if it is almost evening.
Do not take extra medicine to make up for the missed dose.
If you or your child takes too much Adderall, call your healthcare provider or the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
If you or your child takes too much Adderall, go to the nearest hospital emergency room right away.
An overdose of Adderall could be fatal.
Overdose symptoms may include restlessness.
Overdose symptoms may include tremor.
Overdose symptoms may include muscle twitches.
Overdose symptoms may include rapid breathing.
Overdose symptoms may include confusion.
Overdose symptoms may include hallucinations.
Overdose symptoms may include panic.
Overdose symptoms may include aggressiveness.
Overdose symptoms may include muscle pain or weakness.
Overdose symptoms may include dark colored urine.
These symptoms may be followed by depression and tiredness.
Other symptoms include nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, stomach pain, uneven heartbeats, feeling light-headed, fainting, seizure, or coma.
Adderall may impair your thinking or reactions.
Be careful if you drive or do anything that requires you to be alert.
Avoid drinking fruit juices or taking vitamin C at the same time you take Adderall.
These can make your body absorb less of the medicine.
Ask your doctor before using a stomach acid medicine (including Alka-Seltzer or sodium bicarbonate).
Some of stomach acid medicine can change the way your body absorbs Adderall.
It may increase side effects.
Some medicines can interact with amphetamine and dextroamphetamine and cause a serious condition called serotonin syndrome.
Tell your doctor about all your current medicines and any you start or stop using, especially:
selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) and serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs)
tricyclic antidepressants
medicines used to treat migraine headaches, called triptans
lithium, buspirone, tramadol, fentanyl, or tryptophan
St. John's Wort
blood pressure medicine
heartburn medicine
a blood thinner such as warfarin
cold or allergy medicine that contains a decongestant
opioid (narcotic) medicine
seizure medicine.
This list is not complete.
Many other drugs can interact with Adderall.
These include prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Give a list of all your medicines to any healthcare provider who treats you.
Do not start any new medicine during treatment with Adderall without talking to your healthcare provider first.
Store Adderall at room temperature between 68°F and 77°F (20°C and 25°C).
Protect Adderall from light.
Store Adderall in a safe place, such as a locked cabinet, and keep Adderall out of the reach of children.
Dispose of remaining, unused, or expired Adderall through a medicine take-back program at a US Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) authorized collection site.
If no take-back program or DEA-authorized collector is available, mix Adderall with an undesirable, nontoxic substance.
The substance should be such as dirt, cat litter, or used coffee grounds.
This makes it less appealing to children and pets.
Place the mixture in a sealed container such as a plastic bag and throw mixture away in the household trash.
Visit www.fda.gov/drugdisposal for more information on disposing of unused medicines.
Active ingredients: dextroamphetamine saccharate, amphetamine aspartate, dextroamphetamine sulfate, and amphetamine sulfate.
Inactive ingredients: colloidal silicon dioxide, compressible sugar (sucrose and maltodextrin), corn starch, magnesium stearate, microcrystalline cellulose, and saccharin sodium.
The 5 mg tablet is white to off-white and contains no color additives.
The 7.5 mg and 10 mg tablets also contain FD&C Blue #1 Aluminum Lake.
The 12.5 mg, 15 mg, 20 mg, and 30 mg tablets also contain FD&C Yellow #6 Aluminum Lake.
Adderall is manufactured for Teva Pharmaceuticals, Parsippany, NJ 07054.
For more information about Adderall, contact Teva at 1-888-838-2872.
Q: How long does Adderall stay in your system (urine drug test)?
A: Adderall would take just over 2 days for a dose to be out of your system.
You should allow for longer (7 days) if you have taken Adderall frequently and at high doses.
The primary difference between Adderall and Vyvanse lies in their composition.
Adderall contains a mixture of four different amphetamine salts.
Vyvanse contains only one type of amphetamine salt.
That one type of amphetamine salt is called lisdexamfetamine.
Lisdexamfetamine (Vyvanse) is a prodrug that converts into dexamphetamine once absorbed by the body.
Adderall's four amphetamine salts include dextroamphetamine saccharate.
They include amphetamine aspartate.
They include dextroamphetamine sulfate.
They include amphetamine sulfate.
Q: How should Adderall make you feel, with or without ADHD?
People taking Adderall for ADHD typically notice improved focus and attention.
People taking Adderall for ADHD typically notice reduced impulsivity.
In people without ADHD, Adderall can enhance focus.
In people without ADHD, Adderall can enhance alertness.
In people without ADHD, Adderall can enhance energy levels.
Q: How long does Adderall last in your system?
A: When taken as prescribed, the effects of Adderall last for about 4 to 6 hours.
The effects of Adderall XR last for up to 12 hours.
This is because this brand of Adderall is designed for slow and sustained release.
Q: Modafinil (Provigil) vs Adderall: What's the difference?
Modafinil and Adderall are both classified as CNS stimulants.
They have different chemical structures.
This means there are differences in their addiction potential and risk of side effects.
Modafinil and Adderall both have stimulant-type effects.
Modafinil is a much milder stimulant than Adderall.
Q: Focalin vs Adderall: What's the difference?
Focalin contains dexmethylphenidate.
Adderall contains a mixture of amphetamine salts.
Both Adderall and Focalin have a high potential for abuse and dependence.
The risk of abuse and dependence may be perceived as higher with Adderall.
Adderall is more popular than Focalin.
This perception does not mean Focalin is less likely to cause tolerance or dependence.
Q: How long does Adderall withdrawal last?
It is difficult to say exactly how long Adderall withdrawal lasts.
Adderall withdrawal depends on the person.
The process is different for everyone.
Some people's withdrawal symptoms may resolve in as little as 5 days.
Others may still be experiencing symptoms 4 weeks later or even longer.
People who have taken Adderall for a long time at a high dose should expect withdrawal symptoms.
The withdrawal symptoms may last longer.
Sometimes the symptoms can last several months.
Q: How do you get rid of Adderall tongue?
Adderall tongue is a common side effect of Adderall.
Adderall tongue can affect a person's quality of life.
The effect can be detrimental and serious.
To help relieve Adderall tongue stay well-hydrated.
Adderall and other ADHD medications can cause dehydration.
You need to drink at least 6 to 8 glasses of fluid per day.
Take frequent sips of water rather than drinking a whole glass of water at once.
Use artificial saliva products (such as sprays or lozenges) – artificial saliva products can help moisten the tissue and last longer than water.
There are many different brands of these available over the counter at drug stores.
Artificial saliva products can be used multiple times a day.
Chew sugar-free gum or suck on sugar-free candy – this contains xylitol which increases salivary flow and can help prevent dental decay.
Avoid sucking candy that contains sugar because sucking candy that contains sugar will increase your risk of dental decay
Q: Does Adderall help with anxiety and depression?
Adderall is only FDA-approved for the treatment of attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) and narcolepsy.
Adderall has been used rarely and off-label to treat depression.
This depression has not responded to other treatments.
However, this is controversial.
There is a lack of good quality evidence to support Adderall's use.
There is no evidence that Adderall helps with anxiety.
In many people with pre-existing mood disorders, Adderall can make depression and/or anxiety worse.
Albuterol is a bronchodilator.
Albuterol relaxes muscles in the airways.
Albuterol increases air flow to the lungs.
Albuterol inhalation is used to treat or prevent bronchospasm.
Bronchospasm is the narrowing of the airways in the lungs.
Albuterol inhalation is used in people with asthma.
Albuterol inhalation is used in people with certain types of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD).
Albuterol is also used to prevent exercise-induced bronchospasm.
Albuterol inhalation is for use in adults and children who are at least 2 years old.

Q: Is Salbutamol the same as Albuterol?
Salbutamol and albuterol are the same active drug.
Salbutamol is the international generic name.
Albuterol is the name used mainly in the United States.
Both names refer to the same short-acting beta-2 agonist bronchodilator.
Salbutamol and albuterol share the same chemical structure, mechanism of action, and clinical uses.
Most differences patients notice relate to branding.
Most differences patients notice relate to country naming conventions.
Most differences patients notice relate to inhaler devices.
Most differences patients notice do not relate to the medicine itself.
Q: Can you use an expired albuterol inhaler?
An albuterol inhaler should be discarded.
This should happen after an expired albuterol inhaler passes its expiration date.
An expired albuterol inhaler should be thrown out.
It should be thrown out even sooner if it has been 13 months since it was removed from the foil packaging.
The inhaler was removed from the foil packaging.
Allopurinol belongs to a class of medications called xanthine oxidase inhibitors.
Allopurinol works by reducing the production of uric acid in the body.
High levels of uric acid may cause gout attacks or kidney stones.
Allopurinol is used to treat gout.
Gout is a type of arthritis.
In gout, uric acid builds up in the joints.
Uric acid is a naturally occurring substance in the body.
The buildup of uric acid causes sudden attacks of redness, swelling, pain, and heat.
These attacks occur in one or more joints.
Allopurinol is used to prevent gout attacks.
It is not used to treat gout attacks once they occur.
Allopurinol does not need to be stopped during an acute flare.
Allopurinol is also used to treat high levels of uric acid.
The high levels of uric acid build up in the blood.
This buildup occurs as tumors break down.
This happens in people with certain types of cancer.
These people are being treated with chemotherapy medications.
Allopurinol is used to treat kidney stones.
The kidney stones have come back.
This treatment is for people with high levels of uric acid in their urine.
You should not use Allopurinol if you have ever had an allergic reaction to allopurinol.
Allopurinol can lower blood cells that help your body fight infections.
This can make it easier for you to bleed from an injury or get sick from being around others who are ill.
Your blood may need to be tested often.
Visit your doctor regularly.
In rare cases, allopurinol may cause a severe allergic reaction.
Stop taking Allopurinol and call your doctor right away if you have painful urination.
Stop taking Allopurinol and call your doctor right away if you have blood in your urine.
Stop taking Allopurinol and call your doctor right away if you have burning in your eyes.
Stop taking Allopurinol and call your doctor right away if you have swelling in your face or throat.
Stop taking Allopurinol and call your doctor right away if you have skin redness.
Stop taking Allopurinol and call your doctor right away if you have a rash that spreads and causes blistering and peeling.
Avoid drinking alcohol.
Allopurinol can make your condition worse.
Allopurinol may impair your thinking or reactions.
Be careful if you drive or do anything that requires you to be alert.
You should not use allopurinol if you are allergic to allopurinol.
To make sure this medicine is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:
diabetes;
congestive heart failure;
high blood pressure;
if you are receiving chemotherapy;
if you have a gene variation called HLA-B*58:01 allele (your doctor can test you for this);
liver disease; or
kidney disease.
Allopurinol may harm an unborn baby.
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant.
Do not breastfeed while using this medicine, and for at least 1 week after your last dose.
Take allopurinol exactly as prescribed by your doctor.
Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets.
Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.
Take the tablets with a full glass of water.
To reduce your risk of kidney stones, drink 8 to 10 full glasses of fluid every day.
Do this unless your doctor tells you otherwise.
Take allopurinol tablets after meals to help prevent stomach upset.
Allopurinol injection is injected into a vein by a healthcare provider.
The injection is usually only used if you are unable to take medicine by mouth.
You may need frequent medical tests while using Allopurinol.
You may need these tests even if you have no symptoms.
Your kidney and liver function may also need to be tested.
Your doctor may change your dose if your kidney function changes.
You may have gout attacks when you first start taking allopurinol tablets.
Your doctor may recommend another gout medication to take with allopurinol.
Keep taking Allopurinol as directed.
Your symptoms may not improve for up to 2 to 6 weeks.
Call your doctor if your symptoms do not improve after 6 weeks.
You may need to follow a special diet to help prevent kidney stones.
Follow all instructions of your doctor or dietitian.
Learn about the foods you should eat or avoid.
Store at room temperature away from moisture and heat.
Take allopurinol as soon as you can.
Skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose.
Do not take two doses at one time.
Call your doctor for instructions if you miss an appointment for your allopurinol injection.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Avoid driving or hazardous activity until you know how allopurinol will affect you.
Your reactions could be impaired.
Avoid drinking alcohol.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to allopurinol.
Signs of an allergic reaction to allopurinol include hives, difficult breathing, and swelling in your face or throat.
Get emergency medical help if you have a severe skin reaction.
Signs of a severe skin reaction include fever, sore throat, burning eyes, skin pain, and a red or purple skin rash with blistering and peeling.
Seek medical treatment if you have a serious drug reaction that can affect many parts of your body.
Symptoms may include skin rash, fever, swollen glands, muscle aches, severe weakness, unusual bruising, or yellowing of your skin or eyes.
Allopurinol may cause serious side effects.
Stop using Allopurinol and call your doctor at once if you have:
any skin rash, no matter how mild;
painful urination, blood in the urine;
inflammation of your blood vessels - numbness or tingling, skin rash, fever, headache, body aches, night sweats, weight loss, feeling or weak or tired;
signs of infection - fever, chills, sore throat, body aches, unusual tiredness, loss of appetite, bruising or bleeding; or
liver problems - loss of appetite, weight loss, stomach pain (upper right side), itching, dark urine, clay-colored stools, jaundice (yellowing of the skin or eyes).
Common allopurinol side effects may include:
an increase in gout attacks when you first start taking Allopurinol;
rash;
drowsiness;
abnormal liver function tests;
nausea, vomiting, diarrhea; or
kidney problems - swelling, urinating less, feeling tired or short of breath.
This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Tell your doctor about all your other medicines, especially:
azathioprine or mercaptopurine;
chlorpropamide;
cyclosporine;
cancer medicine;
other gout medications;
an antibiotic such as ampicillin or amoxicillin;
a blood thinner such as warfarin, Coumadin, Jantoven; or
a diuretic or "water pill".
This list is not complete.
Other drugs may interact with allopurinol.
These include prescription and over-the-counter medicines.
These also include vitamins and herbal products.
Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.

Amlodipine besylate is a prescription medicine.
It is used to treat high blood pressure (hypertension).
It is used to prevent certain types of angina (chest pain).
Amlodipine besylate belongs to a class of medications called calcium channel blockers.
Amlodipine besylate works by relaxing and widening the blood vessels.
This causes blood to flow more easily.
This improves blood pressure.
Amlodipine besylate controls angina chest pain by increasing the supply of blood to the heart.
If taken regularly, Amlodipine besylate controls chest pain.
Amlodipine besylate does not stop chest pain once Amlodipine besylate starts.
Your doctor may prescribe a different medication to take when you have chest pain.
Amlodipine is also used alone or in combination with other medicines to treat high blood pressure (hypertension) in adults and children at least 6 years old.
Lowering blood pressure may lower your risk of a stroke or heart attack.
Amlodipine besylate is available as:
Amlodipine 5 mg tablets (Norvasc)
Amlodipine 10 mg tablets (Norvasc)
Amlodipine 2.5 mg tablets (Norvasc)
Amlodipine 1 mg/mL solution (Norliqva)
Amlodipine 1 mg/mL oral suspension (Katerzia)
Amlodipine single-dose powder for oral solution (Sdamlo).
Common amlodipine side effects:
The most common amlodipine side may include:
dizziness, drowsiness;
swelling of your legs or ankles;
irregular heartbeat;
pounding heartbeats or fluttering in your chest;
muscle stiffness;
uncontrolled muscle movements;
feeling tired;
stomach pain, nausea; or
flushing (sudden warmth, redness, or tingly feeling).
Amlodipine besylate may cause serious side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to amlodipine.
Signs of an allergic reaction to amlodipine include hives.
Signs of an allergic reaction to amlodipine include difficulty breathing.
Signs of an allergic reaction to amlodipine include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
In rare cases, your chest pain may get worse when you first start taking this medicine.
In rare cases, you could have a heart attack when you first start taking this medicine.
This medicine is being taken for the first time.
Seek emergency medical attention or call your doctor right away if you have symptoms such as chest pain or pressure.
Seek emergency medical attention or call your doctor right away if you have symptoms such as pain spreading to your jaw or shoulder.
Seek emergency medical attention or call your doctor right away if you have symptoms such as nausea.
Seek emergency medical attention or call your doctor right away if you have symptoms such as sweating.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
worsening chest pain; or
a light-headed feeling, like you might pass out.
This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Before taking amlodipine besylate, tell your doctor if you have congestive heart failure or liver disease.
Drinking alcohol can further lower your blood pressure.
Drinking alcohol may increase certain side effects of amlodipine.
If you are being treated for high blood pressure, keep using amlodipine even if you feel well.
High blood pressure often has no symptoms.
You may need to use blood pressure medication for the rest of your life.
Amlodipine is only part of a complete program of treatment.
The program may also include diet, exercise, weight control, and other medications.
Follow your diet, medication, and exercise routines very closely.
Tell your doctor about all other heart or blood pressure medications you are taking.
Your chest pain may become worse when you first start taking amlodipine or when your dose is increased.
Call your doctor if your chest pain is severe or ongoing.
You should not take amlodipine if you are allergic to Amlodipine.
To make sure Amlodipine is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:
severe narrowing of a certain valve in your heart (aortic stenosis), or other heart problems;
coronary artery disease (clogged arteries); or
liver disease.
It is not known if amlodipine will harm an unborn baby.
However, having high blood pressure during pregnancy may cause complications.
It may also cause medical problems in both mother and baby.
The benefit of treating hypertension may outweigh any risks to the baby.
Do not breastfeed while taking amlodipine.
You can stop breastfeeding or take a different medicine.
Take amlodipine exactly as prescribed by your doctor.
Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets.
Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.
Amlodipine is usually taken once per day.
Take Amlodipine at the same time each day, with or without food.
Shake the oral suspension (liquid).
Measure a dose with the supplied measuring device (not a kitchen spoon).
Amlodipine for oral solution (Sdamlo) comes as a powder.
You must mix the powder with water.
This makes a liquid (solution) before taking or giving it.
Throw away Sdamlo if you do not take or give Sdamlo within 60 minutes of mixing and get a new bottle.
Do not store mixed Sdamlo.
Your blood pressure will need to be checked often.
If you have high blood pressure, keep using amlodipine even if you feel well.
High blood pressure often has no symptoms.
Do not change your doses or stop taking any of your medications without your doctor's advice.
Your treatment may also include diet, exercise, weight control, and blood tests.
Store amlodipine tablets and oral solution (liquid) at room temperature away from moisture, heat, and light.
Store amlodipine oral suspension in the refrigerator; do not freeze.
Usual Amlodipine Adult Dose for Hypertension:
Initial dose: 5 mg orally once a day.
Maintenance dose: 5 to 10 mg orally once a day.
Maximum dose: 10 mg/day.
Patients who are small or fragile may be started on 2.5 mg orally once a day.
Usual Adult Dose for Angina Pectoris:
Maintenance dose: 5 to 10 mg orally once a day Maximum dose: 10 mg/day
Usual Adult Dose for Coronary Artery Disease:
Maintenance dose: 5 to 10 mg orally once a day Maximum dose: 10 mg/day
Usual Geriatric Dose for Hypertension:
The initial dose is 2.5 mg orally once a day.
The maintenance dose is 2.5 to 10 mg orally once a day.
The maximum dose is 10 mg/day.
Usual Geriatric Dose for Angina Pectoris:
Initial dose: 5 mg orally once a day Maintenance dose: 5 to 10 mg orally once a day Maximum dose: 10 mg/day..
The drug is used alone or in combination with other antianginal agents for the symptomatic treatment of chronic stable angina.
The drug is used alone or in combination with other antianginal agents for the treatment of confirmed or suspected vasospastic angina.
The drug is used to reduce the risk of hospitalization for angina.
The drug is used to reduce the risk of a coronary revascularization procedure.
This use applies to patients with recently documented coronary artery disease by angiography.
This use applies to patients without heart failure.
This use applies to patients without an ejection fraction less than 40%.
Usual Pediatric Dose for Hypertension:
The maintenance dose for patients aged 6 to 17 years is 2.5 to 5 mg orally once a day.
The maximum dose is 5 mg per day.
Doses higher than 5 mg have not been studied in pediatric patients.
The dosage should be adjusted according to patient response.
In general, titration should proceed over 7 to 14 days.
Titration may proceed more rapidly if clinically warranted.
This is provided the patient is assessed frequently.
Use: Alone or in combination with other antihypertensive agents to treat hypertension
Take Amlodipine as soon as you can.
Skip the missed dose if you are more than 12 hours late for the dose.
Do not take two doses at one time.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Overdose symptoms may include rapid heartbeats, redness or warmth in your arms or legs, or fainting.
Avoid getting up too fast from a sitting or lying position, or you may feel dizzy.
Sometimes it is not safe to use certain medicines at the same time.
Some drugs can affect your blood levels of other drugs you use.
This may increase side effects.
This may make the medicines less effective.
Other drugs may interact with amlodipine.
These include prescription and over-the-counter medicines.
These also include vitamins and herbal products.
Tell your doctor about all other medicines you use.
Tell your doctor if you take simvastatin.
You should not take more than 20 mg of simvastatin daily while taking amlodipine.
Q: What is the best time of day to take blood pressure medication?
Research has suggested that taking your blood pressure medication at night instead of in the morning can provide better blood pressure control at night.
This approach does not compromise daytime blood pressure control.
It can reduce your overall risk of dying because of cardiovascular disease by 45%.
Q: How long does it take for amlodipine to work?
A: Amlodipine decreases blood pressure over 4 to 8 hours after a single dose.
The decrease in blood pressure is gradual.
A patient's heart rate is not affected.
Physiological reflexes are not activated.
These reflexes include an increase in heart rate.
Q: How do I reduce swelling from amlodipine?
Swelling from amlodipine is a common side effect of amlodipine.
Swelling from amlodipine is often troublesome.
5% to 9% of people stop amlodipine because of swelling.
If you develop swelling with amlodipine, tell your doctor right away.
The doctor may be able to change your blood pressure medications slightly.
This change may help reduce the swelling.
Swelling caused by amlodipine does not usually respond to treatment with diuretics.
Diuretics include drugs such as furosemide.
Swelling is caused by changes in capillary pressure.
These changes result in leakage into the spaces between capillaries.
These spaces are called the interstitial area.
This leakage occurs rather than water retention.
Q: Best time to take amlodipine - day or night?
A: You can take amlodipine in the morning or at night, whichever works best for your schedule.
The key is to take amlodipine at the same time each day.
This maintains consistent blood levels.
This supports amlodipine's effectiveness.
Q: How long does amlodipine stay in your system?
A: Amlodipine can stay in your system for up to 11.5 days after your last dose.
This is because amlodipine has a long elimination half-life.
The elimination half-life of amlodipine is 30 to 50 hours.
This means amlodipine takes that long for your body to reduce the drug level by half.
Q: Does amlodipine cause weight gain?
Some people taking amlodipine have reported weight gain.
Weight gain is not a common side effect of amlodipine.
Some people taking amlodipine have reported weight loss.
Weight gain with amlodipine may also be a symptom of edema.
Edema is when you retain fluid.
This fluid retention may be particularly noticeable around your ankles or lower legs.
The risk of edema is greater in women compared with men.
The risk of edema is greater in those people taking a higher dosage of amlodipine.
Edema is usually noticed as weight gain.
Amoxicillin is a penicillin antibiotic.
Amoxicillin is used to treat bacterial infections.
The infections include tonsillitis, bronchitis, sinusitis, pneumonia, and infections of the ear, nose, throat, skin, or urinary tract.
Amoxicillin clavulanate is a combination of amoxicillin and clavulanate potassium.
The amoxicillin fights bacteria.
The clavulanate potassium helps prevent certain bacteria from becoming resistant to amoxicillin.
Amoxicillin is sometimes used together with another antibiotic called clarithromycin (Biaxin).
This combination is used to treat stomach ulcers.
The ulcers are caused by Helicobacter pylori infection.
This combination is sometimes used with a stomach acid reducer called lansoprazole (Prevacid).
Amoxicillin 500mg and 250mg are available as tablets or capsules and as an oral suspension.
Many brands and forms of amoxicillin are available.
Not all brands are listed on this leaflet.
Common amoxicillin side effects:
Common amoxicillin side effects may include nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, or rash.
These side effects affected more than 1% of patients in clinical trials.
Serious amoxicillin side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to amoxicillin.
Signs of an allergic reaction to amoxicillin include hives, difficult breathing, and swelling in your face or throat.
Get emergency medical help if you have a severe skin reaction.
Signs of a severe skin reaction include fever, sore throat, burning eyes, skin pain, and a red or purple skin rash with blistering and peeling.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
severe stomach pain or vomiting (which may be signs of a serious intestinal condition); or
diarrhea that is watery or bloody (even if diarrhea occurs months after your last dose).
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may also report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Do not use Amoxicillin if you are allergic to amoxicillin.

Other penicillin antibiotics include brands such as Omnipen, Principen, Dycill, Dynapen, Bactocill, Beepen-VK, Ledercillin VK, Pen-V, Pen-Vee K, Pfizerpen, V-Cillin K, and Veetids.

There are other cephalosporins as well.

Tell your doctor if you have liver disease.
Tell your doctor if you have kidney disease.
Tell your doctor if you have a bleeding or blood-clotting disorder.

Tell your doctor if you have any type of allergy.
Amoxicillin can make birth control pills less effective.
Ask your doctor about using a non-hormone method of birth control (such as a condom, diaphragm, or spermicide) to prevent pregnancy while taking Amoxicillin.
Take Amoxicillin for the full prescribed length of time.
Your symptoms may improve before the infection is completely cleared.
Amoxicillin will not treat a viral infection such as the common cold or flu.
Do not share Amoxicillin with another person, even if another person have the same symptoms you have.
Antibiotic medicines can cause diarrhea.
Antibiotic medicines may happen while you are taking amoxicillin.
Antibiotic medicines may happen within a few months after you stop taking amoxicillin.
Diarrhea may be a sign of a new infection.
If you have diarrhea that is watery or bloody, stop taking Amoxicillin and call your doctor.
Do not use anti-diarrhea medicine unless your doctor tells you to.
You should not use amoxicillin if you are allergic to any penicillin antibiotic.
Examples of penicillin antibiotics include ampicillin, dicloxacillin, oxacillin, penicillin, and ticarcillin.

Tell your doctor if you have phenylketonuria (PKU).

Use all of your medications as directed.

Q: What are the best antibiotics for a tooth infection?
A: There are several antibiotics that kill the common mouth bacteria that cause tooth infections.
The best (first-line) antibiotics for tooth infection include: Amoxicillin, Penicillin, Cephalexin, Clindamycin, Azithromycin.
Amoxicillin is often the first choice.
Amoxicillin is widely effective.
Amoxicillin has the fewest gastrointestinal side effects.
Q: Can you drink alcohol with amoxicillin?
A: Yes, you can drink alcohol while taking the antibiotic amoxicillin.
The alcohol will not stop amoxicillin from working.
However, many health professionals will recommend you avoid alcohol.
This is to give your body the best chance possible.
The goal is to fight the infection.
Moderation is key.
Q: Does amoxicillin expire?
Is amoxicillin safe to take after expiration?
Amoxicillin capsules and tablets have an expiry of around 2 years.
Amoxicillin capsules and tablets should be stored as recommended.
Amoxicillin capsules and tablets should be kept in the original packaging.
If stored as recommended and in the original packaging, there will be a small margin of safety if used beyond expiry.
Amoxicillin suspension is different.
It has a very short shelf life of about 7-10 days once prepared.
Q: What are the best antibiotics for pneumonia?
A: The best antibiotic depends on your infection type, health, and local resistance patterns.
Common first choices include azithromycin, clarithromycin, or doxycycline.
More serious infections may require levofloxacin or amoxicillin/clavulanate.
Q: Is amoxicillin safe for dogs?
Amoxicillin is a prescription antibiotic that veterinarians commonly use to treat bacterial infections in dogs.
Amoxicillin is generally considered safe when given at the correct dose under veterinary supervision.
However, amoxicillin can cause side effects.
It can cause allergic reactions.
It can cause serious problems if the dose is wrong.
It can cause serious problems if the drug is not needed.
It can cause serious problems if human products are used without a veterinarian's guidance.
Q: Does amoxicillin help with tooth infection and pain?
A: Yes, amoxicillin will help your tooth infection.
Amoxicillin is one of the first antibiotics recommended for the treatment of a tooth infection.
Amoxicillin has shown to be widely effective.
It has fewer gastrointestinal side effects compared to other options.
Q: What is the Best Antibiotic for Strep Throat?
A: Penicillin and amoxicillin are the first-choice antibiotics for strep throat.
According to the CDC, Group A Streptococcus has never shown resistance to penicillin.
Group A Streptococcus is the bacteria that cause Strep throat.
This lack of resistance makes Penicillin the gold standard treatment.
Q: What's the difference between amoxicillin and penicillin?
The main difference between amoxicillin and penicillin is that amoxicillin is effective against a wider spectrum of bacteria.
This is compared with penicillin.
Both amoxicillin and penicillin belong to the class of antibiotics called penicillins.
Q: Amoxicillin rash: When should I be concerned?
From 5% to 10% of children will develop a rash from amoxicillin.
This occurs after taking Amoxicillin.
In most cases, this is considered a side effect of amoxicillin.
It is not an allergic rash.
In a small number of cases, the rash will be a sign of an allergic reaction.
This means the amoxicillin will need to be stopped right away.
Apixaban is used to lower the risk of stroke and blood clots.
It is used in adults with a heart rhythm disorder called atrial fibrillation.
The atrial fibrillation is not caused by a heart valve problem.
Apixaban is used in adults to treat blood clots in the legs (DVT) or lungs (PE).
Apixaban is used to lower the risk of blood clots happening again.
This risk reduction occurs after you have received treatment for blood clots.
Apixaban is used in adults after hip or knee replacement surgery.
It is used to prevent clots that can occur in the legs or in the lungs.
Clots in the legs are known as deep vein thrombosis (DVT).
Clots in the lungs are known as pulmonary embolism (PE).
Apixaban is used in children from birth to less than 18 years of age.
It is used to treat blood clots in the legs (DVT) or lungs (PE).
It is used to lower the risk of clots happening again.
This is done after receiving treatment for at least 5 days.
Apixaban is not approved for use in children who weigh less than 5.7 pounds (2.6 kg).
Apixaban may also be used for purposes not listed in this medication guide.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction: hives, difficult breathing, swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Also seek emergency medical attention if you have symptoms of a spinal blood clot.
Symptoms of a spinal blood clot include back pain, numbness, tingling, muscle weakness in your lower body, or loss of bladder or bowel control.
Apixaban can cause you to bleed more easily.
Call your doctor at once if you have signs of bleeding such as:
bruising or bleeding that will not stop, unusual bleeding (nosebleeds, bleeding gums, vaginal bleeding);
unexpected pain, joint pain or swelling;
headache, dizziness, weakness, feeling like you might pass out;
urine that looks red, pink, or brown; or
bloody or tarry stools, coughing up blood or vomit that looks like coffee grounds.
Common side effects of apixaban may include:
bleeding, including heavy menstrual bleeding; or
headache, vomiting.
This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Do not stop taking apixaban unless your doctor tells you to.
Stopping suddenly can increase your risk of blood clot or stroke.
Some drugs can increase your risk of bleeding when used with apixaban.
Tell your doctor about all other medicines you use.
Call your doctor at once if you have signs of bleeding.
Easy bruising is a sign of bleeding.
Unusual bleeding is a sign of bleeding.
Unexpected pain is a sign of bleeding.
Unexpected swelling is a sign of bleeding.
Feeling very weak is a sign of bleeding.
Feeling dizzy is a sign of bleeding.
Bleeding gums are a sign of bleeding.
Nosebleeds are a sign of bleeding.
Heavy menstrual bleeding is a sign of bleeding.
Blood in your urine is a sign of bleeding.
Blood in your stools is a sign of bleeding.
Coughing up blood is a sign of bleeding.
Vomit that looks like coffee grounds is a sign of bleeding.
Any bleeding that will not stop is a sign of bleeding.
Apixaban can cause a very serious blood clot around your spinal cord.
This can happen if you undergo a spinal tap.
This can also happen if you receive spinal anesthesia (epidural).
Tell any doctor who treats you that you are taking apixaban.
You should not take apixaban if you are allergic to apixaban or if you have active bleeding from any cause.
Apixaban can cause a serious blood clot.
This can happen if you undergo a procedure.
The procedure could be a spinal tap.
The procedure could be receiving spinal anesthesia.
Spinal anesthesia is also known as epidural.
This type of blood clot could cause permanent or long-term paralysis.
Apixaban can make it easier for you to bleed, even from a minor injury.
Call your doctor if you have any bleeding that will not stop.
Tell your doctor if you have or have ever had:
artificial heart valve;
a history of problems with your spine, a spinal surgery, or difficult or repeated spinal taps;
bleeding problems;
plan for surgery, or a medical or a dental procedure;
a thin tube (catheter) placed in your back to give you certain medicine;
antiphospholipid syndrome, an immune system disorder that increases the risk of blood clots; or
liver or kidney disease.
It is not known if apixaban will harm an unborn baby.
Taking apixaban during pregnancy may cause bleeding in the mother or the unborn baby.

Q: Xarelto vs Eliquis: How do Xarelto vs Eliquis compare?
Xarelto (rivaroxaban) and Eliquis (apixaban) are novel anticoagulants.
These anticoagulants make blood less likely to clot.
This process is also called thinning the blood.
Both belong to the class of medicines known as factor Xa (FXa) inhibitors.
They are two of the most popular blood thinners on the market.
Either one may be used to reduce the risk of stroke.
Either one may be used to treat and prevent blood clots in conditions such as DVT.
Q: What medications are known to cause hair loss?
A: Medication-induced hair loss represents a significant concern for patients across multiple therapeutic categories.
Chemotherapy drugs are most commonly associated with severe hair loss.
Many commonly prescribed medications can also cause this distressing side effect.
These medications include antibiotics, psychiatric drugs, cardiovascular agents, and hormonal therapies.
Q: How do you reverse bleeding with Xarelto?
A: There is no longer an FDA-approved antidote for Xarelto (rivaroxaban) in the United States.
Andexxa was pulled from the U.S. market on December 22, 2025.
Andexxa is the only approved reversal drug.
The FDA decided Andexxa's risks outweighed Andexxa's benefits.
For serious bleeding today, hospitals stop Andexxa, control the bleeding directly.
Atorvastatin is a prescription medicine.
Atorvastatin is used to treat high cholesterol.
Atorvastatin is used to reduce the risk of heart attack.
Atorvastatin is used to reduce the risk of stroke.
Atorvastatin is used to reduce the risk of angina.
Atorvastatin is used to decrease the chance that heart surgery will be needed.
Atorvastatin is used in people who have heart disease.
Atorvastatin is used in people who are at risk of developing heart disease.
Atorvastatin helps lower LDL cholesterol ('bad cholesterol') and triglyceride levels.
It increases HDL cholesterol ('good cholesterol') levels.
These actions improve your overall lipid profile.
Atorvastatin calcium belongs to a class of medications called HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors, commonly referred to as statins.
Atrovastatin tablets are taken once daily with or without food.
Atorvastatin works by selectively blocking HMG-CoA reductase.
HMG-CoA reductase is the key enzyme the liver uses to produce cholesterol.
Atorvastatin reduces cholesterol production in the liver.
Atorvastatin increases the number of LDL receptors on liver cells.
This helps remove more LDL ("bad") cholesterol from the bloodstream.
Atorvastatin works by slowing this process.
As a result, Atorvastatin lowers overall LDL levels.
It decreases the number of LDL particles.
It improves the body's ability to clear cholesterol.
Atorvastatin FDA-approved uses:
To reduce the risk of cardiovascular events in adults with:
Multiple risk factors for coronary heart disease (CHD), but without established CHD.
Use: to reduce the risk of myocardial infarction (MI), stroke, revascularization procedures, and angina.
Type 2 diabetes and multiple risk factors for CHD, without established CHD Use: to help reduce the risk of MI and stroke.
Adults with clinically evident CHD should use this to reduce the risk of non-fatal MI.
It is used to reduce the risk of fatal and non-fatal stroke.
It is used to reduce the risk of revascularization procedures.
It is used to reduce the risk of hospitalization for congestive heart failure.
It is used to reduce the risk of angina.
As an adjunct to diet to lower LDL-C:
Adults with primary hyperlipidemia.
Adults and pediatric patients (10 years and older) with heterozygous familial hypercholesterolemia (HeFH).
As an adjunct to other LDL-lowering therapies:
For adults and pediatric patients (10 years and older) with homozygous familial hypercholesterolemia.
As an adjunct to diet for additional lipid disorders in adults:
Primary dysbetalipoproteinemia.
Hypertriglyceridemia.
When cholesterol and fats build up along your artery walls.
This process is called atherosclerosis.
Cholesterol and fats narrow the blood vessels.
This reduces blood and oxygen flow to your heart, brain, and other parts of your body.
Atorvastatin helps prevent this buildup.
This is done by slowing cholesterol production.
This keeps blood flowing more freely to vital organs.
Lowering your blood level of cholesterol and fats with atorvastatin may help prevent heart disease.
It may also help prevent angina (chest pain).
It may also help prevent strokes.
It may also help prevent heart attacks.

LDL-C: Low-density lipoprotein (“bad” cholesterol)
HDL-C: High-density lipoprotein (“good” cholesterol)
TG: Triglycerides (a type of blood fat)
Total-C: Total cholesterol

Q: Best time to take atorvastatin: Morning or Night?
Atorvastatin can generally be taken either in the morning or at night.
This is because Atorvastatin is a long-acting statin.
It stays active in the body around the clock.
The most important factor is taking Atorvastatin at the same time every day.
This ensures your cholesterol levels remain stable over time.
This also ensures your cardiovascular protection remains stable over time.
Q: Rosuvastatin or Atorvastatin: What’s the better choice for you?
Rosuvastatin (Crestor) and atorvastatin (Lipitor) are both statins.
These statins lower "bad" LDL cholesterol.
They reduce the risk of heart attack and stroke.
This applies to people at increased cardiovascular risk.
The better choice for you depends on factors such as how much LDL reduction you need.
It depends on your medical history.
It depends on cost and insurance coverage.
Q: Do I need to eat food when taking atorvastatin?
A: Atorvastatin (also known as Lipitor) can be taken any time of the day, with or without food.
An oral dose of atorvastatin will be absorbed rapidly.
Maximum plasma concentrations occur within 1 to 2 hours after you take atorvastatin.
You may or may not eat food when taking atorvastatin.
There are certain foods you should avoid when taking the drug.
The drug is atorvastatin.
Q: How long does atorvastatin stay in the system after stopping atorvastatin?
A: Atorvastatin takes about 77 hours (3 days) for atorvastatin to be out of your system.
The elimination half life of atorvastatin is approximately 14 hours.
This is the time atorvastatin takes for your body to reduce plasma drug levels by half.
Atorvastatin takes approximately 5.5 x elimination half lives for a medicine to be out of ones system.
Q: Does atorvastatin cause weight gain?
Atorvastatin does not consistently cause weight gain in clinical trials.
Some studies suggest a small association between statin use and modest weight gain.
Some studies suggest a small association between statin use and higher diabetes risk over time.
Atorvastatin is a type of statin.
For most people, the heart-protective benefits of atorvastatin outweigh these potential metabolic effects.
This is especially true when combined with lifestyle measures.
These lifestyle measures support a healthy weight.
Q: Can you use limes or lime juice while taking statin drugs (i.e.
atorvastatin)?
Limes, pomegranates, pomelos, and Seville oranges can interact with some medications.
Some of these medications include statins.
An example of a statin is atorvastatin.
There just hasn't been as much research published regarding how other types of citrus (apart from grapefruit) interact with statins.
Large quantities of lime or lime peel should be avoided.
Small amounts (such as a twist of lime in a drink) are unlikely to cause a significant interaction.

These symptoms may occur with or without using medication such as bupropion.
Smoking cessation may also cause new or worsening mental health problems, such as depression.
Usual Adult Bupropion Dose for Depression:
Immediate-release tablets:
The initial dose is 100 mg orally twice a day.
If necessary, increase the dose after 3 days to 100 mg orally three times a day.
Maintenance dose: 100 mg orally three times a day
The maximum dose is 450 mg/day in up to 4 divided doses.
Single doses should not exceed 150 mg.
Immediate-release tablets should be given at least 6 hours apart.
Dose increases of the immediate-release tablet should not exceed 100 mg per day in a 3-day period.
Sustained-release tablets:
The initial dose is 150 mg orally once a day in the morning.
If necessary, increase the dose after 3 days to 150 mg orally twice a day.
Maintenance dose: 150 mg orally twice a day
Maximum dose: 400 mg/day; maximum single dose should not exceed 200 mg
Sustained-release oral tablets should be given at least 8 hours apart
Extended-release tablets:
The initial dose is 150 mg orally once a day in the morning.
If necessary, increase the dose to 300 mg orally once a day after 4 days.
Maintenance dose: 300 mg orally once a day
Maximum dose: 450 mg/day
Extended-release oral tablets should be given at least 24 hours apart
Comments:
A dose of 100 mg orally four times a day may be administered.
This dose uses the 100 mg tablet.
This is done so as not to exceed the maximum single dose of 150 mg for immediate-release tablets.
Patients may be switched to the extended-release 450 mg dose.
This applies to patients taking 300 mg/day for at least 2 weeks.
This also applies to patients currently taking 450 mg/day.
These patients may be taking any formulation.
Bupropion hydrobromide 174 mg oral tablet is equivalent to bupropion hydrochloride 150 mg.
Patients should be periodically reviewed.
The review should determine the need for maintenance treatment.
The review should determine the appropriate dose for such treatment.
Usual Adult Bupropion Dose for Seasonal Affective Disorder:
Extended-release tablets:
Initial dose: 150 mg orally once a day in the morning, increased if necessary after 7 days to 300 mg orally once a day
Maintenance dose: 150 to 300 mg orally once a day
Maximum dose: 300 mg/day
Comments:
Treatment should be initiated in the autumn prior to the onset of depressive symptoms.
Treatment should be continued through the winter season.
Treatment should be discontinued in early spring.
If bupropion hydrochloride 300 mg orally per day is not tolerated.
Bupropion hydrochloride 300 mg orally per day should be reduced to 150 mg once a day.
This reduction should occur before discontinuing treatment.
Patients taking bupropion hydrochloride 300 mg orally per day during the autumn-winter season should have their dose tapered.
The dose should be tapered to 150 mg once a day.
This tapering should occur for 2 weeks prior to discontinuation.
The timing of initiation and treatment duration for seasonal affective disorder should be individualized.
The individualization should be according to the patient's historical pattern.
The pattern refers to seasonal major depressive episodes.
Usual Adult Dose for Smoking Cessation:
Sustained-release tablets:
Initial dose: 150 mg orally once a day for 3 days, increased to 150 mg orally twice a day
Maintenance dose: 150 mg orally twice a day
Maximum dose: 300 mg/day; maximum single dose should not exceed 150 mg
Duration of therapy: 7 to 12 weeks
Begin dosing one week before quit day.
The target stop date should be set within the first 2 weeks of treatment.
Preferably, the target stop date should be set in the second week.
There should be an interval of at least 8 hours between doses.
May be used with a nicotine transdermal system.
If there is no effect after 12 weeks, treatment should be discontinued.
Longer treatment should be guided by the relative benefits and risks for the individual patient.
Skip the missed dose and take your next dose at the regular time.
Do not take an extra dose to make up for the missed dose.
Taking too much bupropion can increase your chance of having a seizure.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
An overdose of bupropion can be fatal.
Overdose symptoms may include muscle stiffness, hallucinations, fast or uneven heartbeat, shallow breathing, or fainting.
Drinking alcohol with bupropion may increase your risk of seizures.
If you drink alcohol regularly, talk with your doctor before changing the amount you drink.
Bupropion can also cause seizures in people who drink a lot of alcohol and then suddenly quit drinking.
This risk occurs when people start using the medication.
Bupropion may impair your thinking or reactions.
Be careful if you drive or do anything that requires you to be alert.
You may have a higher risk of seizures if you use certain other medicines while taking bupropion.
Many drugs can interact with bupropion.
Tell your doctor about all medicines you use, and those you start or stop using during your treatment.
This includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Some examples of drugs that interact with bupropion are:
Digoxin: May decrease plasma digoxin levels.
Monitor digoxin levels
MAOIs: Increased risk of hypertensive reactions can occur when used concomitantly with Bupropion.
Drugs that lower seizure threshold: Dose bupropion with caution.
Medicines that are CYP2B6 inducers may require a dose increase if coadministered with other CYP2B6 inducers.
Examples of CYP2B6 inducers include ritonavir, lopinavir, efavirenz, carbamazepine, phenobarbital, and phenytoin.
Dose increases should be based on clinical response.
Dose increases should not exceed the maximum recommended dose.
Medicines that are CYP2B6 inhibitors may require dose adjustment if coadministered with CYP2B6 inhibitors.
Examples of CYP2B6 inhibitors include ticlopidine or clopidogrel.
Bupropion inhibits CYP2D6.
Bupropion can increase concentrations of antidepressants.
Examples of antidepressants include venlafaxine, nortriptyline, imipramine, desipramine, paroxetine, fluoxetine, and sertraline.
Bupropion can increase concentrations of antipsychotics.
Examples of antipsychotics include haloperidol, risperidone, and thioridazine.
Bupropion can increase concentrations of beta-blockers.
An example of a beta-blocker is metoprolol.
Bupropion can increase concentrations of Type 1C antiarrhythmics.
Examples of Type 1C antiarrhythmics include propafenone and flecainide.
These medicines are metabolized by CYP2D6.
A dose reduction of antidepressants may be considered when used with bupropion.
Dopaminergic drugs (levodopa and amantadine): CNS toxicity can occur when used concomitantly with Bupropion.
Not all possible interactions are listed in this medication guide.
Store at room temperature, 20° to 25°C (68° to 77°F).
Keep Bupropion dry and out of the light.
Q: How long does it take Wellbutrin XL/SR (bupropion) to work?
You may notice an improvement in your sleep, energy, and appetite in the first week or two of taking Wellbutrin.
Improvement in sleep, energy, and appetite can be an important sign that Wellbutrin is working.
But, like other antidepressants, improvements in your mood or motivation may take 6 to 8 weeks to develop.
Q: Does Auvelity work better than Wellbutrin?
Auvelity worked significantly better than Wellbutrin (bupropion) at relieving symptoms of depression.
This finding was based on a small randomized double-blind, parallel trial.
The trial lasted for 6 weeks.
Auvelity had a faster onset of effect.
Improvement in the Montgomery-Åsberg Depression Rating Scale (MADRS) score was observed as early as week 1.
Differences were statistically significant by week 2.
Note that the brand name Wellbutrin has been discontinued.
Generic bupropion is still available.
Q: What medications are known to cause hair loss?
A: Medication-induced hair loss represents a significant concern for patients across multiple therapeutic categories.
Chemotherapy drugs are most commonly associated with severe hair loss.
Many commonly prescribed medications can also cause this distressing side effect.
These medications include antibiotics, psychiatric drugs, cardiovascular agents, and hormonal therapies.
Q: How does Zurzuvae differ from traditional antidepressants?
Zurzuvae (zuranolone) differs from traditional antidepressants because it's the first oral treatment approved for postpartum depression (PPD).
It is not approved for major depressive disorder.
It is faster-acting than most traditional antidepressants.
It has a unique mechanism of action.
It is only taken for 14 days.
Symptom improvement starts as early as the third day.
Buspirone is an anti-anxiety medicine.
Buspirone affects chemicals in the brain.
These chemicals may be unbalanced in people with anxiety.
Buspirone is used to treat symptoms of anxiety.
These symptoms include fear, tension, and irritability.
These symptoms also include dizziness and pounding heartbeat.
Other physical symptoms are also treated.
Buspirone is not an anti-psychotic medication.
It should not be used in place of medication prescribed by your doctor for psychotic disorders.

You should not use buspirone if you are allergic to buspirone.

This medicine has been used in children and adolescents under close medical supervision and at the appropriate dosage.

You should not stop the other medication suddenly.

Q: How long does it take for buspirone to work?
Buspirone is not a fast-acting medication.
Buspirone is commonly prescribed for anxiety.
It typically takes 2 to 4 weeks of regular use before most people experience noticeable improvement in symptoms.
Q: What drugs cause pinpoint pupils?
Pinpoint pupils refer to abnormally small, constricted pupils.
Pinpoint pupils are medically termed miosis.
These pupils do not dilate appropriately in low light.
This symptom can be caused by opioids, clonidine, buspirone, metoclopramide, and other medications.
Carvedilol is a beta-blocker.
Beta-blockers affect the heart and circulation (blood flow through arteries and veins).
Carvedilol is used to treat heart failure and hypertension (high blood pressure).
Carvedilol is also used after a heart attack that has caused your heart not to pump as well.
Carvedilol may also be used for purposes not listed in this medication guide.
Carvedilol is not approved for use by anyone younger than 18 years of age.
You should not take carvedilol if you have asthma, bronchitis, or emphysema.
You should not take carvedilol if you have severe liver disease.

You may take carvedilol if you have a serious heart condition such as heart block, "sick sinus syndrome," or slow heart rate and have a pacemaker.

Alcohol may cause the carvedilol in Coreg CR to be released too quickly into the body.
If you are being treated for high blood pressure, keep using carvedilol even if you feel well.
High blood pressure often has no symptoms.
You may need to use blood pressure medication for the rest of your life.

Cetirizine is a second generation antihistamine that reduces the natural chemical histamine in the body.
Histamine can produce symptoms of sneezing, itching, watery eyes, and runny nose.
Cetirizine is used to treat cold or allergy symptoms such as sneezing, itching, watery eyes, or runny nose.
Cetirizine is used to treat an allergic reaction.
Cetirizine treats itching caused by chronic urticaria.
Cetirizine treats swelling caused by chronic urticaria.
Cetirizine minimizes the symptoms of perennial allergic rhinitis.
Cetirizine eliminates the symptoms of perennial allergic rhinitis.
Cetirizine minimizes the symptoms of seasonal allergic rhinitis.
Cetirizine eliminates the symptoms of seasonal allergic rhinitis.
Cetirizine minimizes the symptoms of chronic idiopathic urticaria.
Cetirizine eliminates the symptoms of chronic idiopathic urticaria.
Cetirizine minimizes the symptoms of allergic asthma.
Cetirizine eliminates the symptoms of allergic asthma.
Cetirizine minimizes the symptoms of physical urticaria.
Cetirizine eliminates the symptoms of physical urticaria.
Cetirizine minimizes the symptoms of atopic dermatitis.
Cetirizine eliminates the symptoms of atopic dermatitis.
Cetirizine can cause side effects that may impair your thinking or reactions.
Be careful if you drive or do anything that requires you to be awake and alert.
Avoid drinking alcohol.
Cetirizine can increase some of the side effects of cetirizine.
Tell your doctor if you regularly use other medicines that make you sleepy.
Examples of such medicines include other cold or allergy medicine, narcotic pain medicine, sleeping pills, muscle relaxers, and medicine for seizures, depression, or anxiety.
Other medicines can add to sleepiness caused by cetirizine.
Call your doctor if your symptoms do not improve.
Call your doctor if symptoms get worse.
Call your doctor if you also have a fever.
If you are taking cetirizine for hives, get emergency medical help immediately if you have trouble swallowing.
Get emergency medical help immediately if you have dizziness.
Get emergency medical help immediately if you have swelling of the tongue.
Get emergency medical help immediately if you have swelling around the mouth.
Get emergency medical help immediately if you have trouble speaking.
Get emergency medical help immediately if you have drooling.
Get emergency medical help immediately if you have wheezing.
Cetirizine is not a substitute for an epinephrine injector.
Do not use cetirizine to prevent hives from known causes such as foods, insect stings, or medicines.
Call your doctor if your hives do not improve after 3 days.
Call your doctor if hives last more than 6 weeks.
Call your doctor if hives are an unusual color.
Call your doctor if hives look bruised.
Call your doctor if hives are blistered.
Call your doctor if hives do not itch.
Rarely, some people have reported severe itching when stopping cetirizine.
This occurred after daily, long-term use of at least a few months or several years.
If you experience widespread, severe itching on stopping this medication, contact your healthcare provider.
You should not use Cetirizine if you are allergic to cetirizine, levocetirizine, hydroxyzine, or any of the ingredients in the product.

Cetirizine is not recommended if you are breast-feeding.

Older adults may need to take a lower than normal dose.

The chewable tablet must be chewed before you swallow chewable tablet.

Call your doctor if your symptoms do not improve.
Call your doctor if symptoms get worse.
Call your doctor if you also have a fever.

Examples of such medicines include other cold or allergy medicine, narcotic pain medicine, sleeping pills, muscle relaxers, and medicine for seizures, depression, or anxiety.
Other medicines can add to sleepiness caused by cetirizine.
Cetirizine can cause side effects that may impair your thinking or reactions.
Be careful if you drive or do anything that requires you to be awake and alert.
Avoid drinking alcohol.

These signs include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.

Q: Can you take Benadryl and cetirizine together?
Benadryl should not be taken at the same time as cetirizine.
Cetirizine can increase side effects.
These side effects include dizziness, drowsiness, and difficulty concentrating.
Some people may find their thinking, judgement, and coordination impaired.
The elderly may find their thinking, judgement, and coordination impaired.
Children may find their thinking, judgement, and coordination impaired.
You should not drink alcohol while taking Benadryl.
You should not drink alcohol while taking either of the medications.
Drinking alcohol can make the side effects worse.
Q: Can cetirizine raise blood pressure?
Cetirizine does not increase blood pressure.
Blood pressure is not listed as a side effect in the product information.
But cetirizine can be found in combination with other medications.
Such medications include pseudoephedrine in products such as Zyrtec-D.
Pseudoephedrine does increase blood pressure.
Q: Is cetirizine OK to take antihistamines every day?
Yes, most people can take antihistamines every day.
This is possible if people have daily symptoms of allergies.
You may take a daily antihistamine.
This applies if you have chronic allergies with daily symptoms.
Q: Should cetirizine be taken at bedtime or upon awakening?
A: Cetirizine can be taken at any time of day.
Most people do not feel drowsy when taking cetirizine.
Therefore, they often take cetirizine in the morning.
However, a small number of people do experience sedation.
If cetirizine makes you feel drowsy, it is best to take cetirizine in the evening.
Q: Which antihistamines make you drowsy?
First-generation antihistamines often cause sedation.
Benadryl (diphenhydramine) is a first-generation antihistamine.
Chlor-Trimeton (chlorpheniramine) is a first-generation antihistamine.
First-generation antihistamines can include drowsiness, fatigue, and reduced alertness or concentration.
Q: Can you take antihistamines when pregnant?
According to the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG), several over-the-counter (OTC) antihistamines may be taken during pregnancy.
Older, first-generation antihistamines can be taken.
Antihistamines may cause drowsiness.
Newer, second-generation antihistamines can also be taken.
They have less risk of drowsiness.
Q: How long does it take for cetirizine to work?
A: Cetirizine is an allergy medicine that usually starts to work within about 20 to 60 minutes after you take it.
Many people notice less sneezing and itching within the first hour.
This effect is especially noticeable if they take cetirizine on an empty stomach.
One dose of cetirizine generally provides relief for up to 24 hours.
Most people only need to take cetirizine once a day.
Q: Does cetirizine make you drowsy?
Cetirizine is considered a non-drowsy (second-generation) antihistamine.
Cetirizine can still cause drowsiness in some people.
In clinical studies, the chance of feeling sleepy increased with higher doses.
Q: How long after taking cetirizine can I drink alcohol?
It is best to wait until cetirizine completely leaves your body before you drink alcohol.
Cetirizine may take up to 40 hours to leave your body.
This is almost 2 days.
Clopidogrel is used to lower your risk of having a stroke, blood clot, or serious heart problem.
This is done after you've had a heart attack, severe chest pain (angina), or circulation problems.
Clopidogrel may also be used for purposes not listed in this medication guide.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction: hives; difficult breathing; swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Clopidogrel increases your risk of bleeding, which can be severe or life-threatening.
Call your doctor or seek emergency medical attention if you have bleeding that will not stop.
Call your doctor or seek emergency medical attention if you have blood in your urine.
Call your doctor or seek emergency medical attention if you have black or bloody stools.
Call your doctor or seek emergency medical attention if you cough up blood or vomit that looks like coffee grounds.
Also call your doctor at once if you have:
nosebleeds, pale skin, easy bruising, purple spots under your skin or in your mouth;
jaundice (yellowing of your skin or eyes);
fast heartbeats, shortness of breath;
headache, fever, weakness, feeling tired;
little or no urination;
a seizure;
low blood sugar --headache, hunger, sweating, irritability, dizziness, fast heart rate, and feeling anxious or shaky; or
signs of a blood clot --sudden numbness or weakness, confusion, problems with vision or speech.

bleeding.

Usual Adult Dose for Acute Coronary Syndrome:
Unstable Angina (UA)/Non-ST-Elevation Myocardial Infarction (NSTEMI) has a loading dose of 300 mg orally once.
The maintenance dose is 75 mg orally once a day.
The duration of therapy is optimal unknown.
ST-Elevation Myocardial Infarction (STEMI) has an optional loading dose of 300 mg orally once.
The maintenance dose is 75 mg orally once a day, with or without thrombolytics.
The optimal duration of therapy is unknown.
Comments: -Administer Clopidogrel in combination with aspirin 75 mg to 325 mg orally once a day.
The benefit of Clopidogrel is unknown.
This applies to patients who undergo primary percutaneous coronary intervention.
