This includes patients with UA.
This includes patients with NSTEMI.
This includes those who are to be managed medically.
This includes those who are to be managed with coronary revascularization.
This also applies to patients with STEMI.

Cyclobenzaprine is a prescription muscle relaxant.
It is used short-term to relieve muscle spasms.
The spasms are caused by sprains, strains, and injuries.
These include low back and neck pain.
Cyclobenzaprine is used together with rest and physical therapy.
This combination helps relieve pain, tenderness, and limited movement.
These symptoms are caused by muscle spasm.
1,2
Cyclobenzaprine is also used to treat fibromyalgia in adults.
Cyclobenzaprine can help reduce pain.
For fibromyalgia, Cyclobenzaprine is taken as a sublingual (under the tongue) tablet at bedtime under the brand name Tonmya.
In clinical trials, Tonmya significantly reduced fibromyalgia pain compared with placebo over 14 weeks.
Close to half of treated patients achieved at least a 30% improvement in pain.
3
The Flexeril brand has been discontinued.
Cyclobenzaprine is available as generic tablets.
Cyclobenzaprine is also available as the brand-name products Amrix (extended-release capsules) and Fexmid.
2,4
Cyclobenzaprine works by reducing the nerve signals in the brain.
These signals drive muscle tone and spasm.
This reduction allows tight, overactive muscles to relax.
Cyclobenzaprine acts within the central nervous system.
It acts primarily at the level of the brain stem.
It does not act on the muscles themselves.
It does not act at the junction between nerves and muscle.
Cyclobenzaprine works through the nervous system in this way.
Cyclobenzaprine will not relieve muscle spasm caused by central nervous system disease.
Examples of central nervous system disease include cerebral palsy or spinal cord disease.
1,2
The way cyclobenzaprine works in fibromyalgia is not known.
Laboratory studies show Cyclobenzaprine blocks several receptor types.
These receptor types include serotonin (5-HT2A), histamine, and muscarinic receptors.
3
Cyclobenzaprine is chemically related to tricyclic antidepressants such as amitriptyline.
This relationship explains several of Cyclobenzaprine's side effects.
These side effects include drowsiness and dry mouth.
The relationship also explains some of Cyclobenzaprine's most important drug interactions.
1,2
No.
Cyclobenzaprine is not a controlled substance.
It is not a narcotic, opioid, or benzodiazepine.
Cyclobenzaprine is a skeletal muscle relaxant that is chemically related to tricyclic antidepressants.
Cyclobenzaprine's FDA labeling states directly that cyclobenzaprine is not a controlled substance.
3
Cyclobenzaprine is not considered addictive in the way opioids or benzodiazepines are.
Stopping suddenly after prolonged use may rarely cause nausea, headache, and a general unwell feeling.
These effects are not a sign of addiction.
1,3
Do not take cyclobenzaprine if you have an overactive thyroid (hyperthyroidism).
Do not take cyclobenzaprine if you have congestive heart failure.
Do not take cyclobenzaprine if you have a heart rhythm or conduction problem including heart block.
Do not take cyclobenzaprine if you have recently had a heart attack.
Do not take cyclobenzaprine within 14 days of taking an MAO inhibitor.
1,2,3
MAO inhibitors.
Do not take MAO inhibitors with cyclobenzaprine.
MAO inhibitors include isocarboxazid, linezolid, phenelzine, rasagiline, selegiline, and tranylcypromine.
Dangerously high fever, seizures, and deaths have occurred when cyclobenzaprine or similar drugs were combined with these medicines.
1,2,3
Serotonin syndrome.
Cyclobenzaprine can cause a serious condition called serotonin syndrome.
This condition occurs when cyclobenzaprine is taken with certain other medicines.
These medicines include antidepressants (SSRIs, SNRIs, tricyclics), tramadol, bupropion, meperidine, and verapamil.
Get medical help right away if you have agitation.
Get medical help right away if you have hallucinations.
Get medical help right away if you have fever.
Get medical help right away if you have sweating.
Get medical help right away if you have shivering.
Get medical help right away if you have a fast heart rate.
Get medical help right away if you have muscle stiffness or twitching.
Get medical help right away if you have loss of coordination.
Get medical help right away if you have nausea.
Get medical help right away if you have vomiting.
Get medical help right away if you have diarrhea.
1,2,3
Drowsiness and alcohol.
Cyclobenzaprine can impair your thinking and reactions.
This effect is stronger with alcohol or other medicines that cause drowsiness.
Do not drive or do anything that needs alertness until you know how it affects you.
1,2,3
Pregnancy: If you use Tonmya for fibromyalgia.
Tonmya may increase the risk of neural tube defects if used in early pregnancy.
Neural tube defects are a type of birth defect affecting the spine.
This risk is based on animal studies.
Do not use Tonmya in the two weeks before conception or during the first trimester.
See the Pregnancy section below.
3
You should not take cyclobenzaprine if you are allergic to cyclobenzaprine, or if you have:
hyperthyroidism (overactive thyroid);
heart block, a heart rhythm disorder, or congestive heart failure; or
had a recent heart attack.
1,2,3
To make sure cyclobenzaprine is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:
liver disease; 1,2,3
glaucoma or increased pressure in the eye; 1,2,3
a seizure disorder (Tonmya); 3
an enlarged prostate or problems with urination.
1,2,3
The safety and effectiveness of cyclobenzaprine tablets have not been established in children under 15 years of age.
The safety and effectiveness of Tonmya and Amrix have not been established in children of any age.
1,2,3
Older adults:
Cyclobenzaprine builds up to higher levels in older adults.
It is more likely to cause side effects such as confusion, hallucinations, heart problems, and falls.
In people 65 and older:
Immediate-release tablets should be used only if clearly needed.
The dosage should start at 5 mg.
The dosage should increase slowly under medical supervision.
1
Amrix extended-release capsules are not recommended.
Blood levels run about 40% higher.
Amrix stays in the body much longer.
The half-life is 50 hours versus 32 hours in younger adults.
2
Tonmya is limited to the lower 2.8 mg dose once daily at bedtime.
3
Pregnancy:
Pregnancy guidance differs by product.
Check which cyclobenzaprine product you use.
Tonmya is associated with fibromyalgia.
Animal data suggests Tonmya may increase the risk of neural tube defects.
This risk occurs when Tonmya is used two weeks before conception and through the first trimester.
Neural tube development happens early.
This often occurs before pregnancy is recognized.
Avoid Tonmya during this window.
Your healthcare provider should do a pregnancy test before you start Tonmya.
You should use effective contraception during treatment and for two weeks after the final dose.
3
Immediate-release tablets and Amrix (muscle spasm) are available.
Data have not identified a risk of major birth defects.
Data have not identified a risk of miscarriage.
Animal studies at usual doses did not show harm to the fetus.
Data are limited.
Cyclobenzaprine should be used during pregnancy only if clearly needed.
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant.
1,2
Breastfeeding:
Small published reports suggest cyclobenzaprine passes into breast milk in low amounts.
There is no information on effects on a breastfed baby or on milk supply.
Talk with your healthcare provider about the benefits of breastfeeding alongside your need for Cyclobenzaprine.
2,3
Cyclobenzaprine dose for muscle spasm:
Cyclobenzaprine is usually taken for up to 2 or 3 weeks for muscle spasm.
Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets.
Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.
Immediate-release tablets are taken three times a day.
Extended-release capsules are taken once daily, at about the same time each day.
Swallow the capsules whole.
You can open the capsule if you have difficulty swallowing them.
Sprinkle the contents onto a tablespoon of applesauce.
Swallow the mixture right away.
Do not chew the mixture.
Follow your doctor's dosing instructions very carefully.
Cyclobenzaprine for fibromyalgia:
Tonmya is taken once daily at bedtime, after brushing your teeth.
Drink a few sips of water first so your mouth is moist.
Place the tablet(s) under your tongue until dissolved.
Do not swallow whole, cut, crush, or chew.
Avoid eating or drinking for at least 15 minutes afterward.
Avoid hot, cold, or acidic drinks until morning.
Avoid talking for about 5 minutes after dosing.
3
The usual cyclobenzaprine dose for muscle spasm is 5 mg three times a day.
This dose may be increased to 10 mg three times a day if needed.
The maximum dose is 30 mg per day.
Cyclobenzaprine is taken for a maximum of 2 to 3 weeks.
1
Cyclobenzaprine dosage chart:
Immediate-release tablets: the usual starting dose is 5 mg three times a day, increased to 10 mg three times a day based on your response.
In adults 65 and older and in people with mild liver problems, treatment should start at 5 mg.
It should be increased slowly, if at all.
The tablets are not recommended with moderate to severe liver impairment.
1
The recommended dose of Amrix extended-release capsules is 15 mg once daily for most adults.
Some people may need 30 mg once daily.
This can be achieved with one 30 mg capsule or two 15 mg capsules.
Amrix is not recommended in adults 65 and older, or in anyone with liver impairment of any severity.
2
Tonmya is a sublingual tablet used for fibromyalgia.
The recommended starting dose is 2.8 mg (1 sublingual tablet) once daily at bedtime.
This starting dose is for the first 14 days.
The dose then increases to the target dose of 5.6 mg (2 sublingual tablets).
The target dose is taken once daily at bedtime from day 15 onward.
The maximum is 5.6 mg once daily.
In adults 65 and older and in people with mild liver impairment, the maximum is 2.8 mg once daily at bedtime.
Tonmya is not recommended with moderate or severe liver impairment.

Can I take 2 cyclobenzaprine 10 mg at the same time?
:
No.
Do not take two cyclobenzaprine 10 mg tablets at once.
The maximum recommended single dose of the immediate-release tablet is 10 mg.
It is taken up to three times a day.
Taking 20 mg of cyclobenzaprine at one time increases the risk of side effects.
Taking 20 mg at one time can cause severe drowsiness.
Taking 20 mg at one time can cause a fast heart rate.
In an overdose, cyclobenzaprine can cause dangerous heart rhythm problems.
Taking 20 mg at one time does not provide better muscle spasm relief.
If your dose is not controlling your symptoms, talk with your doctor rather than doubling up.
1
How often can you take cyclobenzaprine?
:
Immediate-release tablets are taken three times a day.
Amrix extended-release capsules and Tonmya sublingual tablets are taken once a day.
Do not take extra doses between scheduled doses.
1,2,3
How long does cyclobenzaprine take to work?
How long does cyclobenzaprine last?
:
In clinical trials of the immediate-release tablets, the muscle relaxant effect was measurable within the first several days.
There was significant improvement in muscle spasm and pain by day 3 or 4 of treatment.
1
With Amrix extended-release capsules, Cyclobenzaprine is first detectable in the blood about 1.5 hours after a dose.
Peak levels occur at 7 to 8 hours.
One daily dose provides day-long coverage.
2
How long does cyclobenzaprine stay in your system?
:
Cyclobenzaprine stays in your system for about 3 to 7 days after your last dose.
Cyclobenzaprine has a half-life of around 18 hours for the immediate-release tablets.
A drug takes 4 to 5 half-lives to clear from the body.
1
The once-daily forms last longer.
The half-life is about 32 hours for Amrix extended-release capsules.
The half-life is about 36 hours for Tonmya sublingual tablets.
Amrix extended-release capsules can take a week or more to fully clear.
Cyclobenzaprine also stays in the body longer in older adults.
It stays in the body longer in people with liver problems.
In people with liver problems, blood levels run higher.
1,2,3
Does cyclobenzaprine show up on a drug test?
:
Cyclobenzaprine does not usually show up on a drug test.
Cyclobenzaprine is not a controlled substance.
Cyclobenzaprine is not one of the five substances required on standard federal workplace drug tests.
The five substances required on standard federal workplace drug tests are amphetamines, cannabinoids, cocaine, opiates, and phencyclidine.
3,5
However, cyclobenzaprine has a similar structure to tricyclic antidepressants.
Cyclobenzaprine is a recognized cause of false positive results on tricyclic antidepressant immunoassays.
This includes urine immunoassays.
Tests for tricyclic antidepressants are not part of routine drugs-of-abuse screening.
5
If you take cyclobenzaprine and need a drug test, tell the testing provider what medicines you take.
A screening result is only presumptive.
A confirmatory laboratory test such as gas chromatography-mass spectrometry can tell cyclobenzaprine apart from a tricyclic antidepressant.
5
Immediate-release tablets and Amrix: take Cyclobenzaprine as soon as you can.
Skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose.
Do not take two doses at one time.
Tonmya: if you miss your bedtime dose, take your next dose at the regular time the following evening.
Do not take a missed dose during the day.
Do not take two doses to make up for a missed dose.
3
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
An overdose of cyclobenzaprine can be fatal.
Deliberate overdoses often involve other drugs or alcohol.
This raises the danger.
1,2
Overdose symptoms may include severe drowsiness.
They may include fast heartbeats.
They may include tremors.
They may include agitation.
They may include slurred speech.
They may include confusion.
They may include dizziness.
They may include nausea.
They may include vomiting.
They may include hallucinations.
Rare but critical effects include seizures, dangerously low blood pressure, heart rhythm disturbances, and cardiac arrest.
1,2
Avoid driving or hazardous activity until you know how cyclobenzaprine will affect you.
Cyclobenzaprine can impair your thinking and reactions.
This effect is stronger with alcohol or other medicines that cause drowsiness.
Avoid drinking alcohol while taking cyclobenzaprine.
1,2,3
The most common cyclobenzaprine side effects are drowsiness, dry mouth, dizziness, fatigue, and headache.
Most side effects are mild and settle as your body adjusts.
Cyclobenzaprine can occasionally cause serious heart rhythm effects and serotonin syndrome.
1
Common cyclobenzaprine side effects:
Common cyclobenzaprine side effects may include: 1
drowsiness and tiredness;
dry mouth;
dizziness;
fatigue;
headache;
nausea, indigestion, or constipation;
blurred vision;
unpleasant taste;
nervousness or confusion.
In placebo-controlled trials of the immediate-release tablets, side effects were dose-related.
The side effects occurred more often with 10 mg than with 5 mg.
Side effect | Cyclobenzaprine 5 mg | Cyclobenzaprine 10 mg | Placebo
Drowsiness | 29% | 38% | 10%
Dry mouth | 21% | 32% | 7%
Fatigue | 6% | 6% | 3%
Headache | 5% | 5% | 8%
With Amrix, the most common side effects are dry mouth, dizziness, fatigue, constipation, nausea, indigestion, and drowsiness.
Dry mouth occurs in up to 14% of patients at 30 mg.
2
With Tonmya, the most common side effects involve the mouth.
Tonmya dissolves under the tongue.
The side effects include reduced feeling or numbness in the mouth.
The side effects include mouth discomfort.
The side effects include abnormal taste.
The side effects include tingling.
The side effects include mouth pain.
The side effects include canker sores.
The side effects include sleepiness.
The side effects include fatigue.
The side effects include dry mouth.
Mouth-related effects usually begin within minutes of a dose.
Most resolve within an hour.
Moistening your mouth with sips of water before dosing reduces the risk of numbness.
Tell your healthcare provider if mouth effects are severe.
3
Serious cyclobenzaprine side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to cyclobenzaprine.
These signs include hives.
These signs include difficult breathing.
These signs include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
These signs include itching.
2,3
Stop taking cyclobenzaprine and call your doctor at once if you have: 1,2,3
fast, pounding, or irregular heartbeats;
chest pain or pressure, or pain spreading to your jaw or shoulder;
sudden numbness or weakness, slurred speech, or balance problems; or
symptoms of serotonin syndrome: agitation, hallucinations, fever, sweating, shivering, fast heart rate, muscle stiffness or twitching, loss of coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea.
Serious side effects, including confusion, hallucinations, and heart problems leading to falls, are more likely in older adults.
1
Does cyclobenzaprine make you sleepy?
:
Yes, drowsiness is the most common side effect of the tablets.
This side effect affects up to 38% of people taking the 10 mg dose.
These figures are from clinical trials.
This is one reason 5 mg is the usual starting dose.
5 mg causes less drowsiness.
5 mg has comparable effectiveness.
Analysis of trial data shows cyclobenzaprine relieves muscle spasm whether or not sedation occurs.
Drowsiness is not required for cyclobenzaprine to work.
1
Does cyclobenzaprine cause constipation?
:
Constipation is a known side effect.
It is reported in 1% to 3% of people taking the immediate-release tablets.
It is reported in up to 3% of people taking Amrix 30 mg in clinical trials.
Constipation results from the drug's anticholinergic (drying) effects.
The same mechanism is behind dry mouth and blurred vision.
1,2
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
The most serious cyclobenzaprine (Flexeril) drug interactions are with MAO inhibitors.
MAO inhibitors must never be combined with cyclobenzaprine.
The most serious cyclobenzaprine (Flexeril) drug interactions are also with antidepressants and other serotonergic medicines.
These medicines can cause serotonin syndrome.
Cyclobenzaprine interacts with alcohol and other medicines that cause drowsiness.
This interaction makes sedation worse.
1,2,3
Other medicines and substances that interact with cyclobenzaprine include: 1,2,3
MAO inhibitors such as isocarboxazid, linezolid, phenelzine, rasagiline, selegiline, and tranylcypromine (contraindicated; do not use within 14 days);
antidepressants of any kind, including SSRIs, SNRIs, and tricyclics (risk of serotonin syndrome);
bupropion, tramadol, or meperidine;
verapamil;
alcohol;
opioid medicines, sleeping pills, other muscle relaxers, and medicines for anxiety or seizures;
barbiturates and other CNS depressants;
anticholinergic medicines;
guanethidine or similar blood pressure medicines;
cold or allergy medicines containing an antihistamine.
Cyclobenzaprine may increase the risk of seizures in people taking tramadol.
It may block the blood pressure-lowering effect of guanethidine and similar drugs.
1,2,3
Tell your doctor about all your other medicines.
This list is not complete.
Other drugs may interact with cyclobenzaprine.
These include prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Can you take ibuprofen with cyclobenzaprine?
:
There is no listed drug interaction between cyclobenzaprine and ibuprofen.
Cyclobenzaprine and ibuprofen are commonly prescribed together for painful muscle injuries.
Cyclobenzaprine and ibuprofen work in different ways.
In studies, taking cyclobenzaprine with aspirin did not change blood levels of either drug.
Combining cyclobenzaprine with naproxen was well tolerated.
The combination of cyclobenzaprine and naproxen caused more drowsiness than naproxen alone.
Cyclobenzaprine and naproxen together caused more drowsiness than naproxen by itself.
Check with your doctor or pharmacist before combining medicines, especially if you take other drugs as well.
1
Can you take Tylenol with cyclobenzaprine?
:
There is no listed interaction between cyclobenzaprine and acetaminophen (Tylenol).
Cyclobenzaprine and acetaminophen may be used together when recommended by your doctor.
Be careful with combination cold or nighttime products, which may contain antihistamines or other sedating ingredients that do interact with cyclobenzaprine.
1
Flexeril was the original brand name for cyclobenzaprine.
It remains the name many people know Flexeril by.
The Flexeril brand has been discontinued.
The Flexeril brand is no longer on the market.
Flexeril was a commercial decision.
Flexeril was not a safety withdrawal.
Generic cyclobenzaprine tablets contain the same active ingredient.
Generic cyclobenzaprine tablets remain widely available.
Generic cyclobenzaprine tablets are inexpensive.

Current brand names include Fexmid (7.5 mg tablets).
Current brand names include Tonmya (2.8 mg sublingual tablets for fibromyalgia, approved August 2025).

Q: Does cyclobenzaprine make you sleepy?
Yes, cyclobenzaprine can make you very sleepy.
This side effect may get worse with higher doses.
Drowsiness is the most commonly reported side effect in studies, occurring in up to 38% (38 out of 100) of people.
Fatigue and dizziness may also occur.
Avoid or limit the use of alcohol or other sedating medicines while being treated with cyclobenzaprine.
Also avoid driving, operating machinery, or other hazardous activities until you know how cyclobenzaprine affects you.
Q: How long does it take for cyclobenzaprine to work?
A: Immediate-release cyclobenzaprine tablets work in about 30 to 60 minutes to start relieving your muscle spasm pain.
Its duration of action is 4 to 6 hours.
Cyclobenzaprine is typically given 3 times per day.
Cyclobenzaprine may take up to 7 days for you to see the full therapeutical effect.
The extended-release cyclobenzaprine capsules (brand name: Amrix) are usually given once per day.
They may take longer to start working.
Cyclobenzaprine use for periods longer than 2 or 3 weeks is not recommended.
Q: How much cyclobenzaprine can I take?
Immediate-release cyclobenzaprine is taken as 5 mg three times daily for most adults.
The dosage may be increased to 10 mg three times daily.
Extended-release cyclobenzaprine (Amrix) is usually 15 mg once daily.
It may be increased to 30 mg.
Tonmya for fibromyalgia has a maximum recommended dose of 5.6 mg nightly.
It has a dose of 2.8 mg daily for older adults or people with liver disease.
Q: How long does it take for Flexeril to work?
Flexeril (cyclobenzaprine) is a fast-acting medicine.
You should start to feel some muscle relaxant effects within 30 minutes to one hour.
The full effect can take up to 7 days.
The drowsy side effect will set in quickly.
You should be sure you are not driving or doing other hazardous activities.
Q: Is cyclobenzaprine a controlled substance or addictive?
A: No.
Cyclobenzaprine is not a controlled substance and is not considered addictive.
Rarely, stopping Cyclobenzaprine suddenly after prolonged use may cause nausea, headache, or malaise.
These symptoms are not signs of addiction.
Cyclobenzaprine can cause drowsiness.
It should not be combined with alcohol or other CNS depressants without medical advice.
Q: How long does Flexeril (cyclobenzaprine) stay in your system?
A: As an estimate, after taking a dose of Flexeril Flexeril should be out of your system within 5.5 days to 16.5 days.
Q: Can I take cyclobenzaprine and Tylenol together?
A: Yes, you can take cyclobenzaprine and Tylenol (acetaminophen) together.
There are no known interactions between these medicines.
However, this doesn't necessarily mean that no interaction exists.
Duloxetine is a selective serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor antidepressant (SSNRI).
Duloxetine affects chemicals in the brain that may be unbalanced in people with depression.
Duloxetine is used to treat major depressive disorder in adults.
Duloxetine is also used to treat general anxiety disorder in adults and children who are at least 7 years old.
Duloxetine is also used in adults to treat nerve pain caused by diabetes (diabetic neuropathy).
It is also used to treat chronic muscle or joint pain (such as low back pain and osteoarthritis pain).
Some brands of duloxetine are also used to treat fibromyalgia (a chronic pain disorder).
Drizalma is for treating fibromyalgia only in adults.
Cymbalta may be used to treat fibromyalgia in adults and children at least 13 years old.

Examples of MAO inhibitors include isocarboxazid, linezolid, methylene blue injection, phenelzine, tranylcypromine, and others.
A dangerous drug interaction could occur.
People with depression or mental illness may have thoughts about suicide.
Some young people may have increased suicidal thoughts when first starting a medicine to treat depression.
Tell your doctor right away if you have any sudden changes in mood or behavior, or thoughts about suicide.
Do not stop using duloxetine without first talking to your doctor.
You should not use duloxetine if you are allergic to duloxetine.

Q: What helps with Cymbalta withdrawal symptoms?
The best way to help with Cymbalta (duloxetine) withdrawal is to stop the medicine slowly.
The medicine should be stopped over 2 to 4 weeks or longer.
This process should be done with your prescriber's help.
A slow, step-down plan (called a taper) gives your brain time to adjust.
It lowers the chance of symptoms like dizziness, nausea, and headache.
While you taper, a few simple steps can ease symptoms.
Never stop Cymbalta suddenly without talking to your doctor first.
Q: Does Cymbalta cause weight gain?
Cymbalta is more likely to cause weight loss early in the first 8 to 9 weeks of treatment.
It results in a modest weight gain after 8 months of treatment.
In general, these weight changes are minimal.
These weight changes may be due to temporary side effects.
The temporary side effects include nausea or loss of appetite.
Nausea or loss of appetite are some of the most common side effects of Cymbalta treatment.
Q: How to sleep while taking Cymbalta?
A: Not being able to sleep (also called insomnia) is one of the most common side effects reported by people taking Cymbalta.
In some people, this side effect improves after a couple of weeks of taking Cymbalta.
In others, difficulty falling asleep or staying asleep can persist.
Here are some ways you can improve sleep while taking Cymbalta: Take your dose of Cymbalta in the morning.
Cymbalta may be given once a day.
Cymbalta may be given twice a day.
For those people experiencing insomnia, Cymbalta should be taken just once a day.
Cymbalta should be taken in the morning for those people experiencing insomnia.
Swallow Cymbalta capsules whole.
Do not crush Cymbalta capsules.
Do not chew Cymbalta capsules.
Do not open up Cymbalta capsules.
Do not sprinkle Cymbalta capsules on food.
Sprinkling Cymbalta capsules on food may cause Cymbalta to be absorbed more quickly than intended.
Increased absorption of Cymbalta may increase the risk of side effects such as insomnia.
Q: How long does it take for Cymbalta to work?
Cymbalta may take up to 6 to 8 weeks for the full effects to be seen.
Some improvement in symptoms may show in 1-2 weeks.
Improvement in sleep may show in 1-2 weeks.
Improvement in energy may show in 1-2 weeks.
Improvement in appetite may show in 1-2 weeks.
Symptoms such as depressed mood and lack of interest in activities may take the full 6-8 weeks to resolve.
When Cymbalta is used for pain, Cymbalta generally improves within two weeks.
Q: What do Cymbalta brain zaps feel like?
People have reported feeling electric shock-like sensations or "brain zaps".
These sensations occur when starting Cymbalta (duloxetine) treatment.
These sensations also occur during treatment.
These sensations also occur when discontinuing the treatment.
Most report feeling these in the brain although some report them in other parts of the body.
They have been described as feeling like a short, low voltage, electric shock.
Q: How do you manage neuropathy from chemo?
Chemotherapy-induced peripheral neuropathy (CIPN) is a frequent and often distressing side effect of cancer treatment.
CIPN affects the nerves.
Common symptoms of CIPN are numbness, tingling, burning pain, and muscle weakness.
These symptoms often occur in the hands and feet.

Q: How does Jardiance help with heart failure?
Jardiance (empagliflozin) helps with heart failure.
It does this by making your kidneys remove extra sugar, salt, and water.

This eases fluid buildup.
It lowers the pressure your heart pumps against.

This includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Not all possible interactions are listed here.
Q: Why does Lexapro (escitalopram) cause weight gain?
Complete Patient Guide A discusses Lexapro weight gain.
Lexapro weight gain affects up to 25% of patients who take this antidepressant medication.
Lexapro weight gain affects up to 41% of patients who take this antidepressant medication.
Although weight gain tends to be minimal, with an average gain of approximately 1% to 3.7%.
The weight gain associated with Lexapro occurs primarily due to the drug's mechanism of action.
The mechanism of action involves serotonin levels in the brain.
Lexapro blocks the serotonin transporter.
Lexapro increases serotonin availability.
Increased serotonin availability helps treat depression and anxiety.
Lexapro's action can trigger unwanted changes in appetite and metabolism.
Q: When's the best time to take Lexapro (escitalopram) - Morning or Night?
A: Lexapro (escitalopram) is usually taken once a day and can be taken either in the morning or at night.
Q: How long does it take for Lexapro (escitalopram) to work?
Lexapro may take several weeks to start working.
Lexapro increases serotonin levels within the brain.
Lexapro takes only a few hours to increase serotonin levels within the brain.
Signs that Lexapro has started working include an improvement in your sleep, energy, or appetite.
In some, this may happen in as little as 1 to 2 weeks.
Relief from symptoms may take up to 6 to 8 weeks to fully improve.
The symptoms include a depressed mood.
The symptoms include a lack of interest in activities you used to enjoy.
Q: Does Lexapro (escitalopram) cause night sweats?
Lexapro can cause night sweats.
Lexapro can cause increased sweating overall.
Increased sweating occurs both at night and during the day.
Increased sweating was one of the most common side effects reported with Lexapro.
Between 3% and 8% of people reported increased sweating as a side effect.
The percentage of people reporting increased sweating depends on the dose.
Night sweats can be worrisome.
Night sweats usually diminish with time.
Your body and brain adjust to the medication.
Famotidine is a histamine-2 blocker that works by decreasing the amount of acid the stomach produces.
Famotidine is used to treat and prevent ulcers in the stomach and intestines.
Famotidine also treats conditions in which the stomach produces too much acid, such as Zollinger-Ellison syndrome.
Famotidine treats gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD).
Famotidine treats other conditions in which acid backs up from the stomach into the esophagus.
This acid backup causes heartburn.
The Zantac 360 brand of Famotidine does not contain ranitidine.

Ranitidine was withdrawn from market in the United States.

Heartburn is often confused with the first symptoms of a heart attack.

You should not use Famotidine if you are allergic to famotidine.

cancer stomach; or

Famotidine injection is given as an infusion into a vein.

Q: How long does it take for famotidine to work?
A: Famotidine starts to block stomach acid within 15 minutes to 1 hour of taking the medicine.
The maximum effect usually occurs within 1 to 3 hours.
It lasts for 10 to 12 hours after a single dose.
Famotidine will also work to prevent heartburn if taken 10 to 60 minutes before a meal.
Do not take more than 2 tablets in 24 hours.
Q: Is famotidine better to take famotidine in the morning or at night?
You can take over-the-counter (OTC) famotidine at any time of the day or night.
Famotidine is also known as Pepcid AC or Zantac 360.
Taking famotidine helps treat heartburn symptoms.
Studies have shown it is best to take OTC famotidine 1 hour before an evening meal.
This helps prevent heartburn and indigestion in the evening and during bedtime.
These symptoms may interfere with your sleep.
Talk to your healthcare provider if your symptoms are severe or ongoing after 14 days.
Q: How much famotidine can I take in one day?
The maximum daily dose of over-the-counter (OTC) famotidine in adults and children over 12 years of age is 40 mg per day.
This applies to Pepcid AC and Zantac 360.
The dose may be different if your doctor prescribes famotidine otherwise.
If your symptoms persist more than 2 weeks, contact your doctor.
Higher doses may be used for other conditions such as erosive esophagitis or Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome.
Q: Is famotidine safer than omeprazole for heartburn?
For most people with mild, occasional heartburn, famotidine is usually the safer first choice.
Famotidine works quickly.
Famotidine can be taken as needed.
Famotidine has fewer long-term safety concerns than omeprazole.
Omeprazole is a stronger acid reducer.
Omeprazole may be a better option if you have frequent heartburn two or more days per week.
Omeprazole may be a better option if you have confirmed GERD.
Omeprazole is linked with more potential risks when used for many months.
Omeprazole is linked with more potential risks when used at high doses.
Q: Can you take famotidine long term?
A: Famotidine (brand name: Pepcid AC, Zantac 360) should not be used for longer than 14 days if you are treating heartburn symptoms.
Famotidine may be used for a longer period under the direction of your healthcare provider.
It is used for the treatment of intestinal or stomach ulcers.
It is used for the treatment of gastroesophageal reflux (GERD).
It is used for the treatment of erosive esophagitis.
It is used for the treatment of other acid-related conditions.
Q: Can I take omeprazole in the morning and famotidine at night?
You could take omeprazole in the morning and famotidine at night.
There are not many studies investigating this approach.
Preliminary results suggest that taking your medication this way may provide superior control of gastric acid secretion at night.
This method does not compromise the effectiveness of omeprazole in the morning.
More research is needed.

Q: What are the risks of using Flonase long-term?
Using Flonase (fluticasone) longer than the label recommends can raise your risk of nosebleeds.
It can also raise your risk of nose sores.
It can also raise your risk of a yeast infection in your nose or throat.
Rarer, body-wide risks include glaucoma or cataracts.
Rarer, body-wide risks include bone loss.
Rarer, body-wide risks include adrenal gland problems.
Rarer, body-wide risks include slower growth in children.
Most people who use Flonase as directed have no serious problems.
Adults should ask a doctor before going past 6 months of daily use.
Children ages 4 to 11 should not use Flonase more than 2 months a year
Q: How often can you use Flonase nasal spray?
Flonase (fluticasone propionate) nasal spray is used once per day in both adults and children.
It is used to provide 24-hour relief from allergy symptoms.
These symptoms include sneezing, runny nose, nasal congestion, and itchy, watery eyes.
Nasal steroids like Flonase are most effective when used on a regular basis.
Flonase may take 3 to 4 days of regular use for the full effect during allergy season.
Q: Which is stronger, Flonase OTC or prescription?
Over-the-counter (OTC) Flonase Allergy Relief is exactly the same strength, dose, and medicine as prescription Flonase.
Both contain fluticasone propionate 50 micrograms (mcg) per spray, so neither one is “stronger.”
The brand-name prescription Flonase has been discontinued in the U.S.
OTC Flonase, generic fluticasone, and store brands are still sold.
Q: How long does it take for Flonase to work?
Many people feel effects after the first dose.
Flonase may take 3 to 4 days of regular use for the full effect.
The full effect is on allergy symptoms.
Nasal steroids like Flonase are most effective when used on a regular basis.
They are used for long-term relief.
This is for this reason.
Q: Xhance vs Flonase Allergy Relief - What’s the difference?
A: Xhance is a prescription nasal spray used to treat nasal polyps in adults with or without chronic rhinosinusistis.
Flonase Allergy Relief is an over-the-counter (OTC) nasal spray for treatment of allergy symptoms in adults and children 4 years and older.
Q: Is Flonase Sensimist the same as Veramyst?
Flonase Sensimist is the over-the-counter version of Veramyst.

Fluoxetine is a prescription antidepressant used to treat major depressive disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), bulimia nervosa, and panic disorder.
Fluoxetine is used to treat depressive episodes associated with bipolar I disorder.
Fluoxetine is used to treat treatment-resistant depression.
These uses occur when Fluoxetine is used together with olanzapine (Zyprexa).
Fluoxetine belongs to a class of antidepressants called selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs).
The original brand name of fluoxetine is Prozac.
It is now available as a generic.
Fluoxetine is FDA-approved to treat the following conditions:
Major depressive disorder (MDD) for acute and maintenance treatment in adults and children aged 8 years and older
Obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) for acute and maintenance treatment in adults and children aged 7 years and older
Bulimia nervosa for acute and maintenance treatment of binge-eating and purging in adults with moderate to severe bulimia
Panic disorder for the treatment of panic attacks in adults
When used in combination with olanzapine, fluoxetine is also FDA-approved for:
Acute treatment of depressive episodes associated with Bipolar I disorder (depressive episodes) in adults and children aged 10 years and older
Treatment-resistant depression for adults who have not responded to at least two adequate antidepressant trials
Fluoxetine alone is not approved for bipolar I depressive episodes or treatment-resistant depression.
Olanzapine must be used in combination for these indications.
Fluoxetine works by blocking the reabsorption of serotonin in the brain.
This process leaves more serotonin active between nerve cells.
The reabsorption of serotonin is also known as reuptake.
Serotonin is a neurotransmitter that helps regulate mood, emotions, and sleep.
Fluoxetine can relieve symptoms of depression, anxiety, and OCD.
Fluoxetine relieves these symptoms by increasing the availability of serotonin.
Fluoxetine belongs to a class of antidepressants called selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs).
SSRIs are one of the most commonly prescribed antidepressant classes.
SSRIs are generally well-tolerated.
SSRIs are effective across a range of conditions.
Most people need to take fluoxetine for 4 to 6 weeks before feeling the full effects.
Like all medications, Prozac can cause side effects.
Not everyone experiences side effects.
Many improve after the first few weeks as your body adjusts to the medication.
Tell your doctor if any side effects bother you or do not go away.
Common Fluoxetine Side Effects:
The most commonly reported side effects of fluoxetine (Prozac) include:
Nausea
Trouble sleeping (insomnia)
Drowsiness or sleepiness
Anxiety or feeling nervous or on edge
Trembling or shaking (tremor)
Excessive sweating
Loss of appetite
Diarrhea
Dry mouth
Indigestion or upset stomach
Weakness or fatigue
Sore throat
Sinus congestion or infection
Flu-like symptoms
Skin rash
Flushing or feeling of warmth
Yawning
Unusual or vivid dreams
Decreased sex drive
Difficulty getting or keeping an erection
Delayed or abnormal ejaculation
Serious side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to Fluoxetine, such as hives, difficult breathing, or swelling in your face or throat.
Get emergency medical help if you have a severe skin reaction, such as fever, sore throat, burning eyes, skin pain, or a red or purple skin rash with blistering and peeling.
Report any new or worsening symptoms to your doctor.
Report mood changes to your doctor.
Report behavior changes to your doctor.
Report anxiety to your doctor.
Report panic attacks to your doctor.
Report trouble sleeping to your doctor.
Report feelings of impulsivity to your doctor.
Report feelings of irritability to your doctor.
Report feelings of agitation to your doctor.
Report feelings of hostility to your doctor.
Report feelings of aggression to your doctor.
Report feelings of restlessness to your doctor.
Report feelings of hyperactivity (mentally or physically) to your doctor.
Report increased depression to your doctor.
Report thoughts about suicide to your doctor.
Report thoughts about hurting yourself to your doctor.
Fluoxetine may cause serious side effects.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
blurred vision, tunnel vision, eye pain or swelling, or seeing halos around lights;
fast or pounding heartbeats, fluttering in your chest, shortness of breath, and sudden dizziness (like you might pass out);
low levels of sodium in the body - headache, confusion, slurred speech, severe weakness, vomiting, loss of coordination, feeling unsteady; or
severe nervous system reaction - very stiff (rigid) muscles, high fever, sweating, confusion, fast or uneven heartbeats, tremors, feeling like you might pass out.
Seek medical attention right away if you have symptoms of serotonin syndrome.
Symptoms of serotonin syndrome include agitation, hallucinations, fever, sweating, shivering, fast heart rate, muscle stiffness, twitching, loss of coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Do not take fluoxetine if any of the following apply to you.
Tell your doctor or pharmacist about all medications you currently take or have recently stopped taking.
Do not take fluoxetine if you:
Are allergic to fluoxetine or any of its ingredients
Currently take a monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI), including the antibiotic linezolid.
If you are unsure whether your medication is an MAOI, ask your doctor or pharmacist
Stopped taking an MAOI in the last 2 weeks
Currently take pimozide (Orap) as this combination can cause a serious, potentially life-threatening irregular heart rhythm
Currently taking thioridazine (Mellaril) as this combination can cause a dangerous irregular heart rhythm
Important timing rules with MAOIs and thioridazine:
Wait at least 2 weeks after stopping an MAOI before starting fluoxetine
Wait at least 5 weeks after stopping fluoxetine before starting an MAOI or thioridazine
Like all medications, Prozac carries some important warnings you should be aware of before and during treatment.
Contact your doctor right away if you experience any of the following.
Boxed warning:
Fluoxetine carries a boxed warning, the FDA's most serious warning, regarding suicidal thoughts and behavior.
Antidepressants may increase the risk of suicidal thoughts in children, adolescents, and young adults.
Fluoxetine is an antidepressant.
The risk is particularly high in the first few months of treatment.
The risk is also high when the dose is changed.
This risk was not seen in adults over 24.
Adults 65 and older actually showed a reduced risk.
Anyone starting Fluoxetine should be closely monitored for worsening mood or new or worsening suicidal thoughts.
Families and caregivers should stay in regular contact with the prescriber.
Other warnings:
Serotonin syndrome: A potentially life-threatening condition that can occur when serotonin builds up to dangerous levels.
This can occur when taken alone.
It is more likely when taken with other serotonergic medications.
Seek emergency care immediately if you experience agitation, hallucinations, rapid heartbeat, high fever, muscle rigidity, tremor, or seizures.
Severe allergic reactions require stopping Fluoxetine and seeking emergency help.
Stop taking Fluoxetine if you develop trouble breathing.
Stop taking Fluoxetine if you develop facial swelling.
Stop taking Fluoxetine if you develop hives.
Stop taking Fluoxetine if you develop a blistering rash.
Abnormal bleeding may occur with fluoxetine.
Fluoxetine may increase the risk of bleeding or bruising.
This risk is particularly high if fluoxetine is taken with blood thinners.
Examples of blood thinners include warfarin.
The risk is also increased if taken with NSAIDs.
Examples of NSAIDs include ibuprofen or naproxen.
The risk is further increased if taken with aspirin.
Eye problems include angle-closure glaucoma.
Fluoxetine can cause angle-closure glaucoma.
Symptoms of angle-closure glaucoma include eye pain, vision changes, or swelling around the eye.
These symptoms occur in some people.
An eye exam before starting treatment may be recommended for some people to assess their risk.
Tell your doctor if you have a history of seizures before starting this medicine.
Tell your doctor if you have conditions that potentially increase your risk of seizures.
Manic episodes may be triggered by Fluoxetine.
These episodes include racing thoughts.
They also include reckless behavior.
They include a drastically reduced need for sleep.
They include unusual bursts of energy or irritability.
Changes in appetite or weight can occur.
Weight and appetite changes can occur.
Some patients have significant weight loss.
Children and adolescents should have their height and weight monitored regularly during treatment.
Low sodium (hyponatremia): Low blood sodium levels can occur, particularly in older adults.
Symptoms include headache, weakness, confusion, and difficulty concentrating.
Heart rhythm changes include QT prolongation.
Fluoxetine can affect the heart's electrical activity.
This effect can potentially cause a serious and life-threatening irregular heartbeat.
Seek medical attention if you experience a fast, slow, or irregular heartbeat, shortness of breath, or fainting.
Sexual dysfunction: SSRIs, including fluoxetine, commonly affect sexual function.
In men, Sexual dysfunction may include delayed or absent ejaculation or erectile problems.
In women, Sexual dysfunction may include decreased libido or difficulty reaching orgasm.
Talk to your doctor if this occurs, as treatment options are available.
Drowsiness and impaired concentration: Fluoxetine may affect your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or react quickly.
Use caution when driving or operating machinery until you know how Fluoxetine affects you.
Anxiety and Insomnia: May occur in some patients while taking Fluoxetine.
People with diabetes may experience low blood sugar while taking Fluoxetine.
They may experience high blood sugar when Fluoxetine is stopped.
Your doctor may need to adjust your diabetes medications.

Using fluoxetine later in pregnancy may increase the risk of complications in the newborn.
Some complications require a longer hospital stay.
Some complications require breathing support.
Some complications require tube feeding.
A serious complication is a lung condition called persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (PPHN).
Untreated depression carries risks in pregnancy.

Major Depressive Disorder (MDD):
Adults: Treatment typically starts at 20 mg once daily in the morning.
Your doctor may increase the dose after several weeks if needed.
The maximum dose is 80 mg/day.
Children and adolescents (aged 8 and older): Treatment starts at 10 mg or 20 mg per day.
In lower-weight children, the starting dose and target dose may remain at 10 mg/day.
With all patients, the full effect of fluoxetine for depression may take 4 weeks or longer.
Obsessive Compulsive Disorder (OCD):
Adult dose: Treatment starts at 20 mg once daily in the morning.
It may be increased after several weeks if there is a lack of clinical improvement.
The recommended dose range is 20 mg to 60 mg per day.
Full therapeutic effect may take 5 weeks or longer.
Adolescents and higher-weight children (aged 7 and older): Treatment starts at 10 mg/day, increasing to 20 mg/day after 2 weeks.
Additional dose increases may be considered after several more weeks if insufficient clinical improvement is observed.
The recommended dose range is 20 mg to 60 mg per day.
Lower-weight children: Treatment starts at 10 mg/day with dose increases after several more weeks if there is a lack of clinical improvement.
The recommended dose range is 20 mg to 30 mg per day.
Experience with daily doses greater than 20 mg is very minimal.
Bulimia Nervosa:
Adult dose: The recommended dose is 60 mg once daily in the morning.
Your doctor may gradually increase your dose to this level over several days.
Clinical trials showed that the 60 mg dose was effective.
The 60 mg dose was effective in reducing binge-eating and purging behaviors.
Panic Disorder:
Adult dose: Treatment starts at 10 mg/day, increasing to 20 mg/day after one week.
Your doctor may adjust the dose after several weeks if needed.
Clinical trials used a dose range of 10 mg/day to 60 mg/day.
The most commonly used dose in clinical trials was 20 mg/day.

Is 40mg of fluoxetine a high dose?
40mg of fluoxetine is considered a moderate maintenance dose in adults for major depressive disorder (MDD) and OCD.
The standard starting dose for most conditions is 20mg per day.
The starting dose for panic disorder is 10mg.
If symptoms have not improved after several weeks, the dose may be increased.
The dose may be increased up to 40mg for MDD and OCD.
The dose may be increased up to 20mg for panic disorder.
The recommended dose for bulimia is 60mg per day.
Your doctor will determine the right dose for you based on your condition and response to treatment.
Does fluoxetine cause weight gain?
:
Fluoxetine is less likely to cause weight gain than many other antidepressants.
In the short term, Fluoxetine can cause mild weight loss.
This weight loss is due to reduced appetite.
Reduced appetite is one of Fluoxetine's common side effects.
However, some people do experience weight changes over longer-term use.
If you notice significant weight changes while taking Fluoxetine, speak to your doctor.
Dose adjustments or alternative treatments may be available.
How long does fluoxetine take to work?
:
Most people need to take fluoxetine for 4 to 6 weeks before feeling the full effects.
Some improvement in sleep, appetite, or energy levels may be noticeable within the first 1 to 2 weeks.
Mood and motivation typically take longer to improve.
For obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), the full benefit may take 5 weeks or longer to see.
Obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) is the condition being discussed.
It is important to keep taking fluoxetine as prescribed.
You should continue even if you do not feel immediate improvement.
Do not stop without speaking to your doctor first.
Is fluoxetine a controlled substance?
:
No, fluoxetine is not a controlled substance.
It is not classified under the DEA Controlled Substances Act.
This means fluoxetine does not have a recognized potential for dependence or abuse.
Drugs like opioids or benzodiazepines do have this potential.
Fluoxetine is, however, a prescription-only medicine.
Fluoxetine still requires a valid prescription from a licensed healthcare provider.
Is fluoxetine an SSRI?
:
Yes, fluoxetine is a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor (SSRI).
SSRIs work by blocking the reabsorption of serotonin in the brain.
This process increases the amount of serotonin available between nerve cells.
Serotonin is a neurotransmitter that helps regulate mood, emotions, and sleep.
Prozac was one of the first SSRIs approved.
It remains one of the most widely prescribed antidepressants worldwide.
What are the side effects of fluoxetine in females?
:
Females may experience all of the common fluoxetine side effects.
Some side effects are particularly relevant to women.
Sexual side effects are common, including decreased sex drive (libido) and difficulty reaching orgasm.
Sexual side effects can affect quality of life.
They should be discussed with your doctor.
Treatment options are available.
Fluoxetine may cause mild weight loss initially.
Longer-term weight changes can occur.
Some women report changes to their menstrual cycle.
Menstrual changes is not among the most commonly reported side effects.
Pregnancy - taking Fluoxetine in late pregnancy may increase the risk of certain complications in newborns.
Women who are pregnant, planning to become pregnant, or breastfeeding should discuss the risks and benefits with their doctor.
These women should do this before continuing or starting this medicine.
Does fluoxetine (Prozac) help with anxiety?
:
Fluoxetine is not FDA-approved specifically for generalized anxiety disorder.
Fluoxetine is FDA-approved to treat panic disorder.
Panic disorder is a type of anxiety disorder.
Fluoxetine is also approved for obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD).
OCD has a significant anxiety component.
Fluoxetine is sometimes prescribed off-label by doctors for anxiety symptoms.
Fluoxetine is not FDA-approved for generalized anxiety disorder.
SSRIs are a well-established treatment class for anxiety conditions.
Off-label means the drug is being used in a way not specifically approved by the FDA.
It is supported by clinical evidence.
You may experience anxiety alongside depression.
Fluoxetine may help both anxiety and depression.
Fluoxetine is FDA-approved for major depressive disorder.
Depression and anxiety commonly occur together.
Talk to your doctor about whether fluoxetine is appropriate for your anxiety symptoms.
How long does fluoxetine stay in your system?
:
Fluoxetine stays in your system longer than most other antidepressants.
After stopping this medicine, Fluoxetine takes approximately 1 to 4 days for Fluoxetine itself to be reduced by half.
Fluoxetine converts in the body to an active metabolite called norfluoxetine.
Norfluoxetine has a much longer half-life of 4 to 16 days.
This means Fluoxetine can remain detectable in your system for up to 5 weeks after your last dose.
Fluoxetine's active metabolite can also remain detectable in your system for up to 5 weeks after your last dose.
This is clinically important for two reasons:
Switching medications requires a washout period.
This applies if you are switching from fluoxetine to certain other antidepressants.
These other antidepressants include MAOIs.
Your doctor will require a washout period of at least 5 weeks.
This period allows fluoxetine to fully clear your system.
This avoids dangerous drug interactions.
Stopping fluoxetine.
The long half-life means withdrawal symptoms are generally milder with Fluoxetine than with shorter-acting antidepressants.
Fluoxetine tapers itself out of your system gradually.
Always talk to your doctor before stopping fluoxetine or switching to another medication.
Q: What does Prozac feel like when Prozac starts working?
A: Most people feel lower levels of anxiety, restlessness and tiredness when Prozac first starts to work.
Your sleep, energy and appetite may improve over the first month.
You may have a better focus on daily tasks.
However, a depressed mood can take 6 to 8 weeks to fully respond to treatment.
Q: What drugs can cause serotonin syndrome?
Common drug classes that can cause serotonin syndrome include selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs), and opioids like tramadol.
Prozac and Zoloft represent two of the most widely prescribed antidepressant medications.
These medications are in the United States.
Both medications belong to a class of drugs known as selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs).
SSRIs have revolutionized depression treatment since their introduction in the late 1980s and early 1990s.
Q: Is Prozac (fluoxetine) safe for dogs?
Studies have shown fluoxetine is usually a safe and effective medicine for dogs.
Fluoxetine is used for the treatment of behavioral disorders like separation anxiety.
Fluoxetine is safe and effective when prescribed by your veterinarian.
Fluoxetine is most effective when used in conjunction with a behavior modification plan for your pet.
Do not give your dog Prozac or fluoxetine unless Prozac has been prescribed by your veterinarian.
Furosemide is a loop diuretic.
Furosemide is used to treat fluid retention (edema).
It is used in people with congestive heart failure.
It is used in people with liver disease.
It is used in people with a chronic kidney disorder such as nephrotic syndrome.
Furosemide is also used to treat high blood pressure (hypertension).
Furosemide works by increasing the amount of urine the body makes.
This helps reduce swelling and symptoms of fluid retention.
It also helps lower high blood pressure.
Furosemide tablets are sometimes called water pills as they increase how much you urinate.
Furosemide is also known as frusemide in some countries.
Furosemide has been available in the United States since the 1960s.
Furosemide comes as tablets.
Furosemide comes as an oral liquid.
Furosemide comes as an intravenous solution.
Furosemide comes as subcutaneous injections and infusions.
Subcutaneous injections and infusions can be given at home.
Subcutaneous injections and infusions are available under the brand names Furoscix and Lasix ONYU.
Common side effects:
Common furosemide side effects may include:
diarrhea, constipation, loss of appetite
numbness or tingling
headache, dizziness
blurred vision
redness, bruising, swelling, or pain where a subcutaneous dose is given.
Serious furosemide side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to furosemide, such as hives, difficult breathing, or swelling in your face or throat.
Get emergency medical help if you have a severe skin reaction, such as fever, sore throat, burning eyes, skin pain, or a red or purple skin rash with blistering and peeling.
Furosemide may cause other serious side effects.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
a light-headed feeling, like you might pass out
ringing in your ears, hearing loss
muscle spasms or contractions
pale skin, easy bruising, unusual bleeding
high blood sugar, with increased thirst, increased urination, dry mouth, or fruity breath odor
kidney problems, with swelling, urinating less, feeling tired or short of breath
signs of liver or pancreas problems, such as loss of appetite, upper stomach pain that may spread to your back, nausea or vomiting, dark urine, or jaundice (yellowing of the skin or eyes)
signs of an electrolyte imbalance, such as increased thirst or urination, constipation, muscle weakness, leg cramps, numbness or tingling, feeling jittery, or fluttering in your chest.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Subcutaneous furosemide given at home:
Two brands of furosemide are designed to be given under the skin at home rather than in hospital.
Furoscix is used to treat edema in adults with chronic heart failure or chronic kidney disease.
This includes the nephrotic syndrome.
It is also used in children weighing 43 kg (94.7 lb) or more.
Furoscix comes in two forms:
the Furoscix ReadyFlow, a prefilled autoinjector that delivers 80 mg of furosemide in about 10 seconds.
Furoscix can be used in adults and in children weighing 43 kg or more.
the Furoscix On-Body Infusor, a wearable device that delivers 30 mg of furosemide over the first hour and then 12.5 mg per hour for the next 4 hours, giving 80 mg over about 5 hours.
Furoscix can be used in adults and in children weighing 43 kg or more.
Do not use the Furoscix On-Body Infusor if you are allergic to medical adhesives.
The Furoscix ReadyFlow contains benzyl alcohol as a preservative.
Lasix ONYU is a pre-programmed infusor.
The infusor contains furosemide.
The infusor is FDA-approved for the treatment of edema in adults with chronic heart failure.
Subcutaneous furosemide is not for use in an emergency.
Subcutaneous furosemide is not for long-term use.
Subcutaneous furosemide should be replaced with oral diuretics as soon as practical.

Furoscix ReadyFlow autoinjector
Choose a site at least two inches from your belly button, avoiding skin that is broken or irritated and any scars or stretch marks.
Clean it and let it dry.
Pull the blue cap straight off.
Press the device firmly against your skin.
Hold the device there for about 10 seconds.
Continue holding until the yellow plunger rod stops moving and fills the viewing window.

Do not use a second device unless you are told to.
Furoscix On-Body Infusor and Lasix ONYU Infusor
The infusion takes about 5 hours.
So, plan to stay somewhere you can relax and reach a bathroom.
Apply the device to a flat, clean, hairless area of the abdomen below the rib cage and above the belt line.
Press the device firmly into place.
Do not remove and reapply it.
Limit your activity while wearing device and avoid bending as much as you can.
Do not exercise or do anything that might knock device off.
Do not get the infusor wet.
Do not bathe, shower, or swim while wearing device.
Do not apply lotions or creams to the area where device is placed.
Traveling by car or airplane while wearing the infusor is not recommended.
The Furoscix On-Body Infusor cannot be used in an MRI setting.
Do not use the infusor within 12 inches of mobile phones, tablets, computers, or wireless accessories such as a remote control or Bluetooth device.
Your healthcare provider will show you what the indicator light and alarms mean.
You must use the infusor in a setting where you can detect and respond to these alarms.
You must use the infusor in a setting where you can detect and respond to these alarms to ensure you receive the full dose.
If the device falls off, call your healthcare provider.
If the light blinks red, call your healthcare provider.
Do not reuse the device.

Do not put autoinjectors, infusors, and cartridges in household trash.
Do not recycle them.
General furosemide dosing information:
Do not use more than your recommended dose.
High doses of furosemide may cause irreversible hearing loss.
Doses are based on weight in children and teenagers.
Your child's dose may change if the child gains or loses weight.
This medicine will make you urinate more often.
You may get dehydrated easily.

You may need other medical tests.
If you have high blood pressure, keep using furosemide even if you feel well.
High blood pressure often has no symptoms.

Usual adult dose for edema associated with congestive heart failure, cirrhosis of the liver, and chronic renal disease (including nephrotic syndrome):
Oral: initial dose 20 to 80 mg orally once.
The initial dose may be repeated.
The repeated dose may be the same or increased by 20 or 40 mg.
This repetition occurs no sooner than 6 to 8 hours after the previous dose.
The process continues until the desired diuretic effect has been obtained.
Maintenance dose: administer the dose that provided the desired diuretic effect once or twice a day (for example, at 8 am and 2 pm).
Comments: edema may be most efficiently and safely mobilized by giving this drug on 2 to 4 consecutive days each week.
When doses greater than 80 mg/day are given for prolonged periods.
Careful clinical observation and laboratory monitoring are particularly advisable.
Usual Furoscix adult and pediatric 43 kg and above dose (ReadyFlow autoinjector):
80 mg of furosemide given subcutaneously in about 10 seconds.
Usual Furoscix adult and pediatric 43 kg and above dose (On-Body Infusor):
30 mg of furosemide over the first hour, then 12.5 mg per hour for the subsequent 4 hours.
This dose is delivered automatically by the On-Body Infusor.
The device must be applied by a healthcare provider or adult caregiver.
This applies to children weighing at least 43 kg.
Furoscix is not for chronic use.
It should be replaced with oral diuretics as soon as practical.
People with significantly reduced kidney function may need additional diuretic therapy.
Both Furoscix devices deliver a fixed 80 mg dose.
The therapy is needed because both Furoscix devices deliver a fixed 80 mg dose.
Usual adult furosemide dose for pulmonary edema:
IV: 40 mg IV slowly over 1 to 2 minutes.
If a satisfactory response does not occur within one hour.
A satisfactory response may be increased to 80 mg.
The response is administered IV slowly over 1 to 2 minutes.
Use: adjunctive therapy in acute pulmonary edema.
IV administration is indicated when a rapid onset of diuresis is desired.
Usual adult furosemide dose for hypertension:
Oral: initial dose 80 mg/day, usually divided into 40 mg orally twice a day.
Use: treatment of hypertension alone or in combination with other antihypertensive agents.
Usual pediatric furosemide oral dose for edema:
Initial dose: 2 mg/kg orally once.
If the diuretic response to the initial dose is not satisfactory, the diuretic response may be increased by 1 or 2 mg/kg.
This increase should be given no sooner than 6 to 8 hours after the previous dose.
Doses greater than 6 mg/kg body weight are not recommended.
Maintenance dose: adjust to the minimum effective dose.
Use: treatment of edema associated with congestive heart failure, cirrhosis of the liver and renal disease, including the nephrotic syndrome, especially when an agent with greater diuretic potential is desired.

Gabapentin is an anticonvulsant and nerve pain medication from the drug class gabapentinoids
Gabapentin brand names are Neurontin, Gralise, Horizant, and Relgaabi.
These brand names may not be interchangeable.
Gabapentin is not a federally controlled substance.
Some states classify Gabapentin as Schedule V.
Other states require gabapentin prescriptions to be reported to their prescription drug monitoring program.
This program is known as the PDMP.
Rules on refills and quantity limits vary by state.
The most common side effects of gabapentin are dizziness.
They include drowsiness (somnolence).
They also include swelling in the hands or feet (peripheral edema).
Side effects often improve as your body adjusts.
Do not drive until you know how gabapentin affects you.
Gabapentin is FDA-approved to treat partial onset seizures in patients 3 years and older.
It is used with other seizure medicines.
Gabapentin is FDA-approved to treat nerve pain from shingles (postherpetic neuralgia) in adults.
A related product, gabapentin enacarbil (Horizant), is approved for moderate to severe restless legs syndrome in adults.
The approved uses depend on the specific product.
No single gabapentin product is approved for all of these conditions.
See the table below for a breakdown by brand.
Doctors also prescribe gabapentin off-label for other types of nerve pain.
This includes diabetic nerve pain (diabetic neuropathy).
Doctors also prescribe it for anxiety.
They prescribe it for fibromyalgia.
They prescribe it for hot flashes.
They prescribe it for alcohol withdrawal.
Off-label means that the condition Gabapentin is being prescribed for is not an FDA-approved indication.
Different brands of gabapentin are not interchangeable.
Different brands of gabapentin are FDA-approved for different conditions.
Use only the brand and form of gabapentin your doctor has prescribed.
Check your medicine each time you get a refill to make sure you receive the correct form.
Gralise and Horizant are not substitutes for Neurontin or generic gabapentin.
This is true even at the same milligram dose.
The release profile differs.
Switching without a prescriber's instruction can significantly change blood levels.
Brand (form) | Partial onset seizures (add-on) | Postherpetic neuralgia | Restless legs syndrome
Neurontin (immediate-release capsule, tablet, oral solution) | Adults and children 3 years and older | Adults | Not approved
Generic gabapentin (immediate-release) | Adults and children 3 years and older | Adults | Not approved

Sources: FDA prescribing information for Neurontin, Gralise, and Horizant; Relgaabi prescribing information (DailyMed)
Gabapentin works by calming overactive electrical signals in the brain and nervous system.
Gabapentin does this by binding to the alpha-2-delta subunit of calcium channels in nerve cells.
This subunit is thought to normalize chemical signals nerves send to each other.
This results in a calmer, less excitable nerve circuit.
That same effect helps in different ways depending on what gabapentin is treating:
Epilepsy - dampens the runaway electrical firing that causes seizures.
Postherpetic neuralgia (shingles nerve pain) - reduces the abnormal pain signals from nerves damaged by shingles.
Restless legs syndrome (gabapentin enacarbil / Horizant) has an exact mechanism that is less clear.
The same calming effect on overactive nerves is thought to ease the urge-to-move sensation.
Gabapentin is not an opioid, sedative, or anti-inflammatory painkiller.
Gabapentin works directly on the nerves.
Gabapentin helps nerve-related pain.
Gabapentin isn't useful for everyday pain like headaches or sprains.
Most people need 1 to 2 weeks of regular dosing for the full effect to build up.
Gabapentin can start working for pain after 1 week in some people.
It usually takes several weeks to reach Gabapentin's full effect.
Gabapentin works differently than other pain medicines.
You need to take Gabapentin regularly over several weeks for the full pain-relieving effect to develop.
Respiratory depression.
Gabapentin can cause life-threatening breathing problems.
These breathing problems are called respiratory depression.
Respiratory depression is especially likely if you already have a breathing disorder.
COPD is an example of a breathing disorder.
Respiratory depression is especially likely if you are elderly.
Respiratory depression is especially likely if you use other medicines that can make you drowsy or slow your breathing.
Opioids are examples of medicines that can make you drowsy or slow your breathing.
CNS depressants are examples of medicines that can make you drowsy or slow your breathing.
Seek emergency medical attention if you have very slow breathing.
Suicidal thoughts or behaviors.
Some people have thoughts about suicide while taking and after discontinuing Gabapentin.
Stay alert to changes in your mood or symptoms.
Tell your doctor right away if you have any sudden changes in mood or behavior, or thoughts about suicide.
Abrupt or rapid discontinuation.
Seizures may occur if you stop using gabapentin suddenly.
Ask your doctor before stopping Gabapentin.
Stopping abruptly can cause withdrawal symptoms such as anxiety, trouble sleeping, nausea, pain, and sweating.
Sleepiness, sedation, and dizziness.
Avoid driving or participating in hazardous activities until you know how gabapentin will affect you.
Dizziness or drowsiness can cause falls, accidents, or severe injuries.
Brand substitution.
Brands of gabapentin may not be interchangeable.
The most common side effects of gabapentin are dizziness.
They include drowsiness (somnolence).
They include swelling in the hands or feet (peripheral edema).
They include tiredness.
Other common gabapentin side effects may include:
unsteadiness, poor coordination, or jerky movements
nausea or vomiting
headache
trouble speaking
blurred or double vision
tremor
signs of infection such as fever, chills, or sore throat
Serious side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to gabapentin.
These signs include hives.
These signs include difficult breathing.
These signs include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Seek medical treatment if you have a serious drug reaction that can affect many parts of your body.
Symptoms may include skin rash, fever, swollen glands, muscle aches, severe weakness, unusual bruising, or yellowing of your skin or eyes.
Tell your doctor right away if you have new or sudden changes in mood or behavior.
New or sudden changes in mood or behavior include new or worse depression or anxiety.
New or sudden changes in mood or behavior include panic attacks.
New or sudden changes in mood or behavior include trouble sleeping.
New or sudden changes in mood or behavior include feeling impulsive, irritable, agitated, hostile, aggressive, restless, more active, or talkative.
New or sudden changes in mood or behavior include thoughts about suicide or hurting yourself.
These changes may occur while taking Gabapentin.
These changes may occur after stopping Gabapentin.
Gabapentin can slow or stop your breathing, especially if you have recently used an opioid medicine or alcohol.
A person caring for you should seek emergency medical attention.
You should seek attention if you have slow breathing with long pauses.
You should seek attention if you have blue-colored lips.
You should seek attention if you are hard to wake up.
Some side effects are more likely in children taking gabapentin.
Call your doctor if the child has behavior changes, memory problems, trouble concentrating, or is restless, hostile, or aggressive.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
drowsiness, dizziness, or weakness
problems with balance or muscle movement
increased seizures
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Gabapentin can cause weight gain.
Gabapentin is not common.
In clinical trials, a small number of people (roughly 2 in 100) gained weight while taking Gabapentin.
Gabapentin can also cause swelling in the legs, feet, or hands.
This swelling is known as peripheral edema.
The swelling may add to weight changes.
The swelling becomes more common with age.
You should not take gabapentin if you are allergic to Gabapentin.
To make sure Gabapentin is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:
breathing problems
diabetes
depression, a mood disorder, or suicidal thoughts or actions
a history of alcohol use or drug addiction
a seizure
(for patients with restless legs syndrome) if you are a day sleeper or work a night shift
kidney disease, or if you are on dialysis
Some people have thoughts about suicide while taking seizure medicine.
Children taking gabapentin may have behavior changes.
Stay alert to changes in your mood or symptoms.
Your family or caregivers should also watch for sudden changes in your behavior.
Pregnancy:
It is not known if gabapentin will harm an unborn baby.
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant.
Do not start or stop seizure medication during pregnancy without your doctor's advice.
Having a seizure during pregnancy could harm both mother and baby.
If you are pregnant, your name may be listed on a pregnancy registry.
The registry is used to track the effects of gabapentin on the baby.
Breastfeeding:
Ask a doctor if it is safe to breastfeed while using gabapentin.
Take gabapentin exactly as prescribed by your doctor.
Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets.
Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.
Never take gabapentin in larger amounts, or for longer, than prescribed.
Your dose needs may change if you switch to a different brand, strength, or form of gabapentin.
Avoid medication errors by using only the medicine your doctor prescribes.
Neurontin can be taken with or without food.
Gralise should be taken with food, with the evening meal.
Swallow Gralise and Horizant tablets whole.
Do not crush, break, chew, or dissolve them.
If you break a scored Neurontin tablet and take only half, take the other half at your next dose.
Use Neurontin tablet within 28 days.
Measure liquid medicine with the supplied measuring device, not a kitchen spoon.
Doses are based on weight in children.
Your child's dose may change if the child gains or loses weight.
Do not stop taking gabapentin suddenly.
Stopping suddenly may cause increased seizures or withdrawal symptoms.
Follow your doctor's instructions about tapering your dose, usually gradually over at least a week.
This medicine can affect the results of certain medical tests.
Tell any doctor who treats you that you are using gabapentin.
Your kidney function may need to be checked.
Your dose may change based on the results.
Store tablets and capsules at room temperature away from moisture and heat.
Store the liquid medicine in the refrigerator; do not freeze.
Follow all storage instructions provided.
Your pharmacist can provide more information about how to store this medicine.
