Usual Gabapentin Dose for Epilepsy (immediate-release):
Patients 12 years and older Dose: starting dose gabapentin 300 mg 3 times a day.
This dose may be titrated up to a maximum dose of 600 mg 3 times a day if needed.
Patients 3 to 11 years of age Dose: the starting dose range is 10 to 15 mg/kg/day, given in 3 divided doses.
The recommended dose in patients 3 to 4 years of age is 40 mg/kg/day.
In patients 5 to 11 years of age, the dose is 25 to 35 mg/kg/day.
This dose is given in 3 divided doses.
The recommended maintenance dose is reached by upward titration over approximately 3 days.
Less than 3 years: Not recommended
Note:
Leave no more than 12 hours between doses
Dose should be adjusted in patients with reduced kidney function.
Your doctor will determine your dose based on your kidney function.
Use: Adjunctive therapy in the treatment of partial onset seizures, with and without secondary generalization, in patients 3 years of age and older.
Usual Adult Dose for Postherpetic Neuralgia (immediate-release):
The initial dose is gabapentin 300 mg orally on day one.
On day two, the dose is 300 mg orally 2 times a day.
On day three, the dose is 300 mg orally 3 times a day.
The dose is then titrated up as needed for pain relief.
Maximum Gabapentin dose in 24 hours: 1800 mg per day (600 mg orally 3 times a day) Notes:
Doses above 1,800 mg a day did not add benefit in studies.
Dose should be modified in renal impairment.
Usual Adult Dose for Postherpetic Neuralgia (extended release - Gralise):
Maintenance dose: Gralise should be titrated to 1800 mg orally once daily with the evening meal.
Recommended titration schedule:
Day 1: 300 mg orally with the evening meal
Day 2: 600 mg orally with the evening meal
Days 3 through 6: 900 mg orally with the evening meal
Days 7 through 10: 1200 mg orally with the evening meal
Days 11 through 14: 1500 mg orally with the evening meal
Day 15: 1800 mg orally with the evening meal
Notes
Gralise is not interchangeable with other gabapentin products.
This is because of differing pharmacokinetic profiles.
These profiles affect the frequency of administration.
Dose should be modified in renal impairment.
Usual Adult Dose of Gabapentin enacarbil (Horizant) for Postherpetic Neuralgia:
Initial dose: 600 mg orally once each morning on days 1 to 3 Maintenance dose: 600 mg orally 2 times a day (1200 mg/day total) from day 4 onward
Notes:
Doses above 1200 mg a day did not add benefit and caused more side effects
Take with food and swallow tablets whole; do not crush or chew
Horizant is not interchangeable with other gabapentin products
Dose should be modified in renal impairment
Usual Adult Dose of Gabapentin enacarbil (Horizant) for Restless Legs Syndrome:
Gabapentin enacarbil (Horizant) dose: 600 mg orally once daily with food at about 5 PM.
Use: For the treatment of moderate-to-severe primary Restless Legs Syndrome (RLS) in adults.
Notes:
A 1200 mg dose gave no added benefit and caused more side effects
Dose should be modified in renal impairment.

Some medicines change the amount of gabapentin in your body:
Antacids that contain aluminum or magnesium (such as Maalox) can reduce gabapentin absorption.
Take gabapentin at least 2 hours after an antacid.

This may increase drowsiness.

Gabapentin does not usually interact with other seizure medicines.
Examples of these medicines include phenytoin, carbamazepine, valproic acid, or phenobarbital.
This is because Gabapentin is not broken down by the liver.

Chronic pain in dogs includes arthritis, back pain, or nerve pain.

Q: Lyrica vs Gabapentin: What's the difference?
Lyrica (pregabalin) is an anticonvulsant and gabapentinoid.
Gabapentin (Neurontin) is an anticonvulsant and gabapentinoid.
Lyrica (pregabalin) and gabapentin (Neurontin) are used to treat nerve pain.
Lyrica (pregabalin) and gabapentin (Neurontin) are used to treat seizures.
Lyrica (pregabalin) and gabapentin (Neurontin) differ significantly in potency.
Lyrica (pregabalin) and gabapentin (Neurontin) differ significantly in absorption speed.
Lyrica (pregabalin) and gabapentin (Neurontin) differ significantly in regulatory status.
Lyrica is generally more potent.
Lyrica is faster-acting.
Lyrica peaks in approximately 1 hour.
Lyrica is a Schedule V controlled substance.
Gabapentin peaks in 3 to 4 hours.
Gabapentin is less regulated in most states.
Gabapentin is usually cheaper.
Q: How long does gabapentin withdrawal last?
Case reports have shown that gabapentin withdrawal often lasts for 5 to 10 days.
Some people have taken as long as 18 weeks to completely taper off gabapentin.
These people managed withdrawal symptoms during the tapering process.
The symptoms depend on how much of the drug you are taking.
The symptoms depend on how long you've been taking it.
How long the symptoms last depends on how much of the drug you are taking.
How long the symptoms last depends on how long you've been taking it.
Q: Is gabapentin considered a painkiller?
Gabapentin is commonly used to treat some types of nerve pain.
Gabapentin is classified as an anticonvulsant medicine.
Gabapentin is not classified as an opioid or narcotic painkiller.
Gabapentin is used to treat nerve pain.
Nerve pain can occur due to an outbreak of shingles.
Nerve pain can occur due to restless legs syndrome (RLS).
Nerve pain can occur due to some forms of epilepsy.
Q: Is gabapentin safe for cats?
A: Yes, Gabapentin is safe for cats when prescribed by a veterinarian.
This medication is commonly used to treat cat pain.
This medication is commonly used to treat anxiety.
This medication is commonly used to treat feline hyperesthesia syndrome.
The medication has minimal side effects.
Mild sedation and drowsiness are the most common reactions.
These reactions typically improve with continued use.
Q: Does gabapentin help treat nerve pain?
Gabapentin can help relieve nerve pain in some people.
It helps people with postherpetic neuralgia (shingles nerve pain).
It helps people with diabetic peripheral neuropathy (diabetic foot pain).
Q: How long does it take gabapentin to work?
It can take several weeks for gabapentin to reach its full effect.
This depends on the condition being treated and your individual response to the drug.
Q: Does gabapentin cause weight gain?
Gabapentin may cause weight gain.
It is an uncommon side effect.
Studies have shown that a small number of people taking gabapentin weight gain.
People who do gain weight may gain about 5 pounds after 6 weeks of use.
Q: Does gabapentin help you sleep?
A: Gabapentin is a prescription medication that may help you sleep.
That may be why Gabapentin has been prescribed for people with insomnia.
Gabapentin is not approved for that use.
Hydrochlorothiazide is a thiazide diuretic.
It is often called a water pill.
It is used to treat high blood pressure.
It is used to reduce a build-up of fluid in the body.
This build-up of fluid is called edema.
Hydrochlorothiazide works by making the kidneys pass more salt and water out of the body.
You urinate more.
Hydrochlorothiazide is often shortened to HCTZ on prescriptions and medical records.
Hydrochlorothiazide is available as a tablet, capsule, and liquid (suspension) taken by mouth.
It is usually taken once or twice a day.
Hydrochlorothiazide is available as:
oral tablet (12.5 mg, 25 mg, and 50 mg)
oral capsule (12.5 mg)
powder for oral suspension (10 mg/mL)
Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) brand names included HydroDIURIL tablets (discontinued).
Esidrix (discontinued) was a brand name for Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ).
Microzide capsules (discontinued) were a brand name for Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ).
Now generic tablets and capsules are available.
The brand name of hydrochlorothiazide oral suspension is Inzirqo.
Hydrochlorothiazide is also an ingredient in many combination blood pressure medicines.
These medicines include combinations with losartan, lisinopril, valsartan, and more.
Hydrochlorothiazide is approved to treat high blood pressure (hypertension) and fluid retention (edema) in certain patients.
High blood pressure:
HCTZ is used to manage high blood pressure (hypertension).
HCTZ is used on its own.
HCTZ is used alongside other blood pressure medicines.
Lowering blood pressure reduces the risk of fatal and non-fatal cardiovascular events, mainly strokes and heart attacks.
All three forms of hydrochlorothiazide, tablets, capsules and the oral suspension, are approved for high blood pressure in adults.
The oral suspension is also approved for children.
Fluid retention:
Hydrochlorothiazide tablets are used to treat a build-up of fluid (edema) associated with:
congestive heart failure
cirrhosis of the liver
kidney conditions, including nephrotic syndrome, acute glomerulonephritis and chronic kidney failure
treatment with corticosteroid or estrogen medicines
The HCTZ oral suspension is approved for fluid retention.
The fluid retention is associated with congestive heart failure, cirrhosis of the liver, and kidney disease.
Kidney disease includes nephrotic syndrome.
The approval applies to both adults and children.
The capsules are approved for high blood pressure only, not for fluid retention.
Not all forms of HCTZ are approved for all indications and age groups.
Approved HCTZ uses by age groups and dosage form:

Hydrochlorothiazide works by inhibiting the kidneys from reabsorbing sodium and chloride.
More salt and water leave the body in the urine.
This helps reduce excess fluid buildup (edema).
Exactly how thiazides lower blood pressure is not fully understood.
The early effect is thought to come from a drop in blood volume.
With longer-term use, blood volume returns towards normal.
The blood vessels themselves offer less resistance to blood flow.
Hydrochlorothiazide does not usually change blood pressure that is already normal.
Hydrochlorothiazide is a thiazide diuretic.
Do not take hydrochlorothiazide if you are unable to pass urine (anuria).
Do not take hydrochlorothiazide if you are allergic to hydrochlorothiazide or to other sulfonamide-derived medicines (sometimes called sulfa drugs).
Before you start HCTZ, tell your doctor if you have or have ever had:
kidney disease or reduced kidney function
liver disease, including cirrhosis
diabetes
gout or high uric acid levels
high cholesterol or high triglycerides
high calcium levels or a parathyroid gland disorder
low potassium or low sodium levels
lupus (systemic lupus erythematosus)
glaucoma
an allergy to sulfa medicines or to penicillin
Also tell your doctor if you are pregnant, planning to become pregnant, or breastfeeding.
Hydrochlorothiazide may cause jaundice or bleeding problems in a newborn.
Hydrochlorothiazide can pass into breast milk.
Hydrochlorothiazide may cause serious side effects in a nursing baby.
You and your doctor should decide whether to take Hydrochlorothiazide or breastfeed.
Fluid and salt imbalance:
Hydrochlorothiazide can lower the levels of potassium, sodium, magnesium and chloride in your blood.
Low potassium (hypokalemia) is the most common of these.
It is more likely at higher doses.
It is more likely during rapid fluid loss.
It is more likely if you have severe cirrhosis.
It is more likely after long-term use.
Your doctor will check your blood before you start and at intervals during treatment.
Contact your doctor if you have any of the following, which can be signs of a fluid or salt imbalance:
dry mouth, thirst, or unusual weakness or tiredness
drowsiness, restlessness or confusion
muscle pain, cramps or muscle fatigue
fast heartbeat
passing much less urine than usual
nausea or vomiting
seizures
Kidney and liver problems:
HCTZ reduces the fluid volume in your body.
This reduction can lead to sudden kidney injury.
The risk is higher if you have chronic kidney disease.
The risk is higher if you have heart failure.
The risk is higher if you are already low on fluid.
If you have liver disease, small changes in fluid and salt balance can trigger a serious complication.
The complication is called hepatic coma.
Your doctor will monitor your kidney function and watch for changes in alertness.
Sudden vision changes need urgent care:
Rarely, hydrochlorothiazide causes sudden short-sightedness (acute myopia).
It also causes a sharp rise in pressure inside the eye (acute angle-closure glaucoma).
These side effects usually occur within hours to weeks of starting the medicine.
Stop taking Hydrochlorothiazide and get medical help immediately if your vision drops suddenly.
Stop taking Hydrochlorothiazide and get medical help immediately if your eye hurts.
Left untreated, acute angle-closure glaucoma can cause permanent loss of vision.
A history of allergy to sulfa medicines or penicillin may increase the risk.
Blood sugar, cholesterol, uric acid and calcium:
Hydrochlorothiazide can raise blood sugar, cholesterol, triglycerides, uric acid and calcium.
Your diabetes medicine or insulin dose may need adjusting.
Diabetes that was previously unnoticed can become apparent during treatment.
The rise in uric acid is related to the dose.
It can cause gout.
It can make existing gout worse.
Hydrochlorothiazide is stopped before parathyroid function tests.
This is because the medicine also affects calcium.
Tell any doctor arranging tests that you take it.
Your doctor will check these levels from time to time.
Skin cancer risk:
Long-term use of hydrochlorothiazide is linked to a small increase in the risk of non-melanoma skin cancer.
The risk is mainly due to squamous cell carcinoma.
In a large safety study, the increase was about 1 extra case per 16,000 people per year.
This figure rose to about 1 extra case per 6,700 people per year among white patients who had taken a total of 50,000 mg or more.
Protect your skin from the sun.
Wear protective clothing and use sunscreen.
Avoid sunlamps and tanning beds.
Have regular skin checks.
Tell your doctor about any new or changing skin growth, or a sore that does not heal.
Lupus and allergic reactions:
Thiazide diuretics have been reported to worsen lupus or bring it on.
Sensitivity reactions can happen whether or not you have a history of allergies or asthma.
The most noticeable effect of hydrochlorothiazide is passing urine more often.
This effect is particularly noticeable in the first few weeks.
This is how the medicine works rather than a side effect.
A side effect usually settles as your body adjusts.
Serious side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction, such as hives, difficulty breathing, or swelling of your face, lips, tongue or throat.
Get emergency medical help if you have a severe skin reaction, such as a spreading red or purple rash with blistering and peeling, or skin that is painful to touch.
Stop taking hydrochlorothiazide if you have a sudden change in your vision.
Get medical help immediately if you have pain in your eye.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
severe pain in your upper stomach that spreads to your back, with nausea and vomiting, which can be a sign of inflammation of the pancreas (pancreatitis)
yellowing of your skin or the whites of your eyes (jaundice)
fever, chills, sore throat, mouth sores or other signs of infection
unusual bruising or bleeding, or pale skin and unusual tiredness, which can be signs of a blood problem
shortness of breath, wheezing, or a cough with frothy mucus
passing much less urine than usual, or swelling in your legs and ankles
light-headedness or fainting, especially when standing up
signs of dehydration or a salt imbalance, such as extreme thirst, dry mouth, confusion, muscle cramps, irregular heartbeat or unusual weakness
Other side effects:
Other hydrochlorothiazide side effects include:
dizziness, a spinning sensation (vertigo), headache, restlessness, or tingling and numbness (paresthesia)
weakness
nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, constipation, stomach cramping, stomach irritation, or loss of appetite
muscle spasm
increased sensitivity to sunlight, causing sunburn more easily
hair loss or thinning
difficulty getting or keeping an erection
temporary blurred vision, or objects looking yellow-tinged (xanthopsia)
swelling and tenderness of the salivary glands
raised blood sugar, sugar in the urine, or raised uric acid
If a side effect is moderate or severe, your doctor may lower your dose.
Your doctor may stop Hydrochlorothiazide.
Do not stop taking HCTZ on your own without speaking to your doctor first.
This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Your doctor will choose the smallest dose that controls your condition.
Your doctor may adjust the dose over time.
Follow your prescription label and do not change your dose yourself.
HCTZ dose for high blood pressure (adults):
Tablets: The starting dose is hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg once a day.
The dose may be increased to 50 mg a day.
The dose is taken as a single dose or split into two doses.
Doses above 50 mg a day are often associated with a marked drop in blood potassium.
Most people do not need more than 50 mg a day when hydrochlorothiazide is taken alongside other blood pressure medicines
The starting dose is a hydrochlorothiazide 12.5 mg capsule once a day.
It can be taken on its own or with other blood pressure medicines.
Total daily doses above 50 mg are not recommended.
Capsules are approved for high blood pressure only, not for fluid retention.
HCTZ dose for fluid retention (adults):
Tablets: The usual dose is hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg to 100 mg a day, taken as a single dose or split into two doses.
Many people with fluid retention respond to intermittent treatment.
Intermittent treatment includes taking treatment every other day.
Intermittent treatment also includes taking treatment on 3 to 5 days each week.
Excessive response and undesirable electrolyte imbalance are less likely to occur with intermittent dosing.
HCTZ dose for children for high blood pressure and fluid retention:
The usual HCTZ dose in children is 1 mg to 2 mg per kilogram of body weight (0.5 mg to 1 mg per pound) a day.
This dose is used for both high blood pressure and fluid retention.
The dose is given as a single dose or split into two doses.
Infants under 6 months may need doses of up to 3 mg per kilogram (1.5 mg per pound) a day.
These doses are given as two divided doses up to the same maximum 37.5 mg daily limit.
Infants and children under 2 years: do not exceed 37.5 mg a day
Children 2 to 12 years: do not exceed 100 mg a day
The dose is based on your child's weight and must be worked out by their doctor.
Taking the oral suspension:
If your child has been prescribed the liquid form.
The liquid form is an oral suspension.
The following applies.
Shake the bottle well before every dose.
Measure each dose with the oral dosing syringe.
Ask your pharmacist for a syringe and a bottle adapter if you do not have them.
Never measure a dose with a household teaspoon or tablespoon, because these do not give an accurate amount.
Store the made-up suspension at room temperature and do not freeze suspension.
Throw away any unused suspension 30 days after suspension was made up.
The pharmacist will write this date on the bottle.
The oral suspension can be taken with or without food.
What happens if I miss a dose?
:
Take the medicine as soon as you can.
Skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose.
Do not take two doses at one time.
What happens if I overdose?
:
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Overdose symptoms may include nausea, dizziness, dry mouth, thirst, and muscle pain or weakness.
Hydrochlorothiazide starts to work within 2 hours of a dose.
The peak effect occurs at about 4 hours.
The increase in urine output lasts for around 6 to 12 hours.
The medicine's overall action on the body can persist for up to 24 hours.
Lowering blood pressure takes longer than the fluid effect.
Your doctor will usually reassess your blood pressure after a few weeks rather than straight away.
Hydrochlorothiazide is well absorbed.
It is not broken down by the body.
It is cleared by the kidneys.
Most of a dose passes out unchanged in the urine.
Its half-life has been reported as 6 to 15 hours.
Its half-life means the time for blood levels to fall by half.
In people with kidney disease, levels in the blood are higher.
In people with kidney disease, Hydrochlorothiazide stays in the body longer.
Hydrochlorothiazide controls high blood pressure but does not cure it.
Keep taking it even if you feel well, because high blood pressure often causes no symptoms.
Tell your doctor and pharmacist about all the medicines you take, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Medicines that need particular care:
Lithium.
Lithium and diuretics generally should not be taken together.
Hydrochlorothiazide reduces how quickly the kidneys clear lithium.
This reduction carries a high risk of lithium toxicity.
Cholestyramine and colestipol.
These cholesterol-lowering resins bind hydrochlorothiazide in the gut.
They can reduce how much you absorb by up to 85 percent for cholestyramine and 43 percent for colestipol.
The doses need to be separated.
The label advises taking hydrochlorothiazide for the oral suspension.
The label advises taking hydrochlorothiazide at least 4 hours before the resin.
The label advises taking hydrochlorothiazide 4 to 6 hours after the resin.
NSAIDs such as ibuprofen (Advil, Motrin), naproxen (Aleve), diclofenac, meloxicam, indomethacin, celecoxib and aspirin.
NSAIDs can reduce the fluid-clearing and blood-pressure-lowering effects of hydrochlorothiazide.
Your doctor will check whether the medicine is still working as intended.
Insulin and oral diabetes medicines.
Your diabetes medicine dose may need adjusting.
Corticosteroids and ACTH.
These increase the loss of salts from the body.
The loss of salts is particularly potassium.
This effect occurs when taken with hydrochlorothiazide.
Digoxin and other digitalis medicines.
Low potassium caused by hydrochlorothiazide can make the heart more sensitive to the toxic effects of digitalis.
These toxic effects include irregular heart rhythms.
Other blood pressure medicines.
The effects add together.
This may be intended.
However, it can also lower blood pressure more than expected.
Alcohol, barbiturates and opioid pain medicines.
Alcohol, barbiturates and opioid pain medicines can make the drop in blood pressure on standing worse.
This can cause dizziness or fainting.
Muscle relaxants used during surgery (nondepolarizing muscle relaxants such as tubocurarine).
You may respond more strongly to Muscle relaxants.
Tell your surgeon and anesthetist that you take hydrochlorothiazide.
Medicines that raise blood pressure (pressor amines such as norepinephrine).
Your response to Medicines that raise blood pressure may be reduced.
This does not usually prevent Medicines that raise blood pressure's use.
This list is not complete.
Other medicines may affect hydrochlorothiazide.
Hydrochlorothiazide may affect other medicines.
Does hydrochlorothiazide cause weight loss?
:
Hydrochlorothiazide is not a weight loss medicine.
Some people see the number on the scales drop in the first days or weeks of treatment.
This is extra fluid leaving the body.
This is not any loss of body fat.
Fluid levels off.
Do not take hydrochlorothiazide to lose weight.
Can you drink alcohol while taking hydrochlorothiazide?
:
Alcohol can make the drop in blood pressure that happens when you stand up worse.
This drop in blood pressure may leave you feeling dizzy or faint.
The same applies to barbiturates and opioid pain medicines.
Tell your doctor how much you drink so doctor can advise you.
Does hydrochlorothiazide lower your potassium?
:
Yes.
Hydrochlorothiazide can lower blood potassium.
This condition is known as hypokalemia.
This effect is more likely at higher doses.
This effect is more likely when fluid is lost quickly.
This effect is more likely if you have severe cirrhosis.
This effect is more likely after taking the medicine for a long time.
Your doctor will check your potassium with blood tests.
Tell doctor if you have muscle cramps, unusual weakness or tiredness, or an irregular heartbeat.
Should you eat more potassium while taking hydrochlorothiazide?
:
Only if your doctor tells you to.
Low potassium can sometimes be managed with potassium-rich foods.
Low potassium can sometimes be managed with a potassium supplement.
Low potassium can sometimes be managed with a different type of diuretic.
This diuretic holds on to potassium.
Do not start any of these yourself.
Too much potassium can be harmful.
Some people on hydrochlorothiazide do not need extra potassium at all.
Is hydrochlorothiazide 12.5mg enough?
:
For many people Hydrochlorothiazide is.
In published trials comparing the two doses, 12.5 mg kept most of the blood pressure reduction seen with 25 mg.
Low potassium was consistently less common at 12.5 mg.
Starting at 12.5 mg is recommended for adults over 65.
Your doctor will decide the right dose for you.
Your doctor may adjust the dose over time.
Q: What is the best time of day to take blood pressure medication?
Research has suggested that taking your blood pressure medication at night instead of in the morning can provide better blood pressure control at night.
This approach does not compromise daytime blood pressure control.
It can reduce your overall risk of dying because of cardiovascular disease by 45%.
Hydrocodone and acetaminophen is a strong prescription combination pain medicine.
The medicine contains an opioid, hydrocodone.
The medicine also contains acetaminophen.
Acetaminophen is used to manage moderate to moderately severe pain.
The medicine is used when other pain treatments do not treat your pain well enough.
The medicine is also used when you cannot tolerate non-opioid pain medicines.
An opioid pain medicine that can put you at risk for overdose and death.
You are at risk for opioid addiction, abuse, and misuse that can lead to death.
This risk exists even if you take your dose correctly as prescribed.
Hydrocodone and acetaminophen is a Schedule II controlled substance.
All branded hydrocodone and acetaminophen (Norco, Vicodin, Lortab, Hycet, Lorcet, Verdrocet, Xodol, Zamicet) have been discontinued.
Generic hydrocodone and acetaminophen tablets and oral solution is still available.
Hydrocodone and acetaminophen first gained FDA approval on May 3, 1982.
Get emergency help right away if you take too many hydrocodone and acetaminophen tablets or oral solution (overdose).
Serious or life-threatening breathing problems that can lead to death may occur.
These problems may occur when you first start taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen.
These problems may occur when your dose is changed.
These problems may occur if you take too much (overdose).
Never give anyone else your hydrocodone and acetaminophen.
Hydrocodone and acetaminophen could die from taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen.
Store hydrocodone and acetaminophen away from children and in a safe place to prevent stealing or abuse.
Selling or giving away hydrocodone and acetaminophen products is against the law.
Common Side Effects:
The possible side effects of hydrocodone and acetaminophen are constipation, nausea, sleepiness, vomiting, tiredness, headache, dizziness, and abdominal pain.
Call your healthcare provider if you have any of these symptoms and symptoms are severe.

Trouble breathing
Shortness of breath
Fast heartbeat
Chest pain
Swelling of your face, tongue, or throat
Extreme drowsiness
Light-headedness when changing positions
Feeling faint
Agitation
High body temperature
Trouble walking
Stiff muscles
Mental changes such as confusion.

Severe asthma, trouble breathing, or other lung problems
A bowel blockage or a narrowing of the stomach or intestines
Signs of an allergy, such as a rash
Are allergic to any of its ingredients.

Head injury, seizures

Problems urinating
Pancreas or gallbladder problems
Abuse of street or prescription drugs, alcohol addiction, or mental health problems.
Tell your healthcare provider if you are:
Pregnant or planning to become pregnant.
Prolonged use of hydrocodone and acetaminophen during pregnancy can cause withdrawal symptoms in your newborn baby.
These symptoms could be life-threatening if not recognized and treated.
Breastfeeding.
Hydrocodone and acetaminophen pass into breast milk and may harm your baby.
Carefully observe infants for increased sleepiness (more than usual), breathing difficulties, or limpness.
Seek immediate medical care if you notice these signs.
Taking prescription or over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, or herbal supplements.
Taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen with certain other medicines can cause serious side effects that could lead to death.

Adult Dose of Hydrocodone And Acetaminophen for Moderate to Severe Pain:
Hydrocodone 2.5 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg per tablet, Hydrocodone 5 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg per tablet:
Initial dose: 1 to 2 tablets every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain
Maximum dose: 8 tablets/24 hours
Hydrocodone 7.5 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg, Hydrocodone 10 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg per tablet:
Initial dose: 1 tablet every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain
Maximum dose: 6 tablets/24 hours
Hydrocodone 7.5 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg per 15 mL oral solution:
Initial dose: 15 mL every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain
Maximum dose: 90 mL/24 hours
Hydrocodone 10 mg-acetaminophen 300 mg per 15 mL oral solution:
Initial dose: 11.25 mL every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain
Maximum dose: 67.5 mL/24 hours
Comments:
Doses should be individually titrated.
The titration should provide adequate analgesia.
The titration should minimize adverse reactions.
The lowest effective dose should be used.
The dose should be used for the shortest duration.
The duration should be consistent with individual patient treatment goals.
This is because of the risks of addiction, abuse, and misuse.
Monitor patients closely for respiratory depression within the first 24 to 72 hours of initiating therapy and following any increase in dose.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
An overdose of acetaminophen and hydrocodone can be fatal.
Acute overdosage with hydrocodone and acetaminophen can cause respiratory depression.
It can cause sleepiness progressing to near-unconsciousness or coma.
It can cause floppy muscles.
It can cause cold and clammy skin.
It can cause constricted pupils.
It can cause pulmonary edema in some cases.
It can cause a slow heartbeat.
It can cause low blood pressure.
It can cause partial or complete airway obstruction.
It can cause unusual snoring.
It can cause death.
If the patient who has overdosed becomes very low in oxygen.
Pupils may become very dilated instead of constricted.
Your doctor may recommend that you get naloxone.
Naloxone is a medicine to reverse an opioid overdose.
Your doctor may recommend that you keep naloxone with you at all times.
A person caring for you can give the naloxone if you stop breathing or don't wake up.
Your caregiver must still get emergency medical help.
Your caregiver may need to perform CPR (cardiopulmonary resuscitation) on you while waiting for help to arrive.
Anyone can buy naloxone from a pharmacy or local health department.
Make sure any person caring for you knows where you keep naloxone and how to use it.

The first signs of an acetaminophen overdose include nausea.
The first signs of an acetaminophen overdose include vomiting.
The first signs of an acetaminophen overdose include stomach pain.
The first signs of an acetaminophen overdose include sweating.
The first signs of an acetaminophen overdose include confusion or weakness.

Later symptoms may include dark urine.
Later symptoms may include yellowing of your skin or the whites of your eyes.

Q: What is the difference between Apadaz and Vicodin?
Vicodin contains a combination of acetaminophen and the opioid hydrocodone.
Apadaz is a combination of the prodrug, benzhydrocodone and acetaminophen.
When ingested, stomach enzymes cleave the benzhydrocodone prodrug.
This process releases the active parent drug hydrocodone.
Hydroxyzine is a prescription antihistamine.
It is used to treat anxiety and tension.
It is used to treat itching from allergic skin conditions.
It is used for sedation before and after general anesthesia.
Hydroxyzine comes in three oral forms:
Hydroxyzine hydrochloride (HCl) tablets in 10 mg, 25 mg, and 50 mg strengths
Hydroxyzine hydrochloride oral solution (a syrup) containing 10 mg in each 5 mL
Hydroxyzine pamoate capsules in 25 mg
There is also a hydroxyzine hydrochloride injection.
Hydroxyzine is given into a muscle by a healthcare professional.
It is never given into a vein or under the skin.
Hydroxyzine is not chemically related to benzodiazepines, phenothiazines, reserpine, or meprobamate.
Hydroxyzine taken by mouth is approved for three uses.
Three uses are the same for hydroxyzine HCl and hydroxyzine pamoate.
Anxiety and tension.
For symptomatic relief of anxiety and tension linked to psychoneurosis, and as an add-on treatment in physical illnesses where anxiety is present.
Itching from allergic skin conditions.
For managing itching (pruritus) caused by allergic conditions such as long-term hives (chronic urticaria), eczema-type rashes, and rashes caused by contact with an irritant.
Hydroxyzine is also used for itching caused by histamine release.
Sedation around surgery.
As a sedative given before a procedure (premedication) and after general anesthesia.
The injection is also approved for nausea and vomiting.
It is usually given in hospital or clinic settings.
Hydroxyzine should not be used for nausea and vomiting caused by pregnancy.
Hydroxyzine has not been studied for long-term use in anxiety.
Studies have not assessed whether Hydroxyzine still works as an anti-anxiety medicine.
The assessment period is beyond four months.
Your doctor should review from time to time whether you still need it.
Hydroxyzine hydrochloride (HCl) and hydroxyzine pamoate are two salt forms of the same active medicine.
FDA-approved labeling gives Hydroxyzine hydrochloride and hydroxyzine pamoate the same approved uses.
FDA-approved labeling gives them the same doses.
FDA-approved labeling gives them the same warnings.
FDA-approved labeling gives them the same side effects.
These drugs are taken by mouth.
The injection is the exception.
It is a hydroxyzine HCl product.
There is no pamoate injection.
It has its own approved uses, doses, and warnings.
The practical differences are:
Form.
Hydroxyzine hydrochloride comes as tablets and as an oral solution.
Hydroxyzine pamoate comes as capsules.
How Hydroxyzine hydrochloride and Hydroxyzine pamoate dissolve.
Hydroxyzine hydrochloride is very soluble in water.
Hydroxyzine pamoate is practically insoluble in water.
How the strength is measured.
Hydroxyzine pamoate capsules are labeled by the amount of hydroxyzine HCl Hydroxyzine pamoate capsules are equivalent to.
A 25 mg pamoate capsule delivers the same amount of active medicine as a hydroxyzine hydrochloride 25 mg tablet.
The two salts also had different brand names.
Vistaril was the brand name for hydroxyzine pamoate.
Atarax was the brand name for hydroxyzine hydrochloride.
Both brands have been discontinued in the United States.
Hydroxyzine is now available as a generic medicine.
Is hydroxyzine pamoate stronger or longer lasting than hydroxyzine hydrochloride?
:
No.
You may read that hydroxyzine pamoate works better for anxiety.
You may read that hydroxyzine pamoate lasts longer.
You may read that hydroxyzine pamoate reaches the brain more effectively than hydroxyzine hydrochloride.
FDA-approved labeling does not support this.
Labeling for both salt forms states that hydroxyzine's effects are usually noticed within 15 to 30 minutes.
The effects are noticed after a dose taken by mouth.
Both are approved for the same three uses.
Both carry the same doses.
The doses are 50 to 100 mg four times a day for anxiety in adults.
The doses are 25 mg three or four times a day for itching.
No FDA labeling describes a difference in how strongly or how long either form works.
The reason the doses match is that hydroxyzine pamoate capsules are labeled by their hydroxyzine hydrochloride equivalent.
Hydroxyzine pamoate capsules are labeled by their hydroxyzine hydrochloride equivalent.
A 25 mg pamoate capsule and a 25 mg hydrochloride tablet deliver the same amount of active medicine.
If you feel that one form suits you better than the other, tell your doctor.
People can respond differently to the same medicine.
Hydroxyzine is thought to work by quieting activity in certain deeper regions of the brain.
These regions are located below the outer layer known as the cortex.
Hydroxyzine is not a cortical depressant.
This means Hydroxyzine does not work by slowing down the outer layer of the brain.
Some other sedatives work by slowing down the outer layer of the brain.
Hydroxyzine also blocks histamine, a chemical your body releases during an allergic reaction.
Blocks histamine is what relieves itching.
Laboratory and clinical studies have also shown that hydroxyzine relaxes skeletal muscle.
Hydroxyzine widens the airways (bronchodilation).
Hydroxyzine reduces pain.
These are effects that have been observed rather than uses Hydroxyzine is approved for.
Hydroxyzine has also been shown to reduce nausea and vomiting.
Reduce nausea and vomiting is an approved use for the injection, but not for the forms taken by mouth.

The most common side effects of hydroxyzine are drowsiness, dry mouth, and headache.

A skin rash is different.
If you develop a rash while taking hydroxyzine, stop taking hydroxyzine and call your doctor.
A rash can be the first sign of a serious reaction.
Common side effects:
Common hydroxyzine side effects include dry mouth, headache, and drowsiness.
The drowsiness is usually temporary.

It may go away when the dose is lowered.
Serious side effects:
Stop taking hydroxyzine and call your doctor right away if you develop a skin rash.
Stop taking hydroxyzine and call your doctor right away if a skin condition you are treating gets worse.
Stop taking hydroxyzine and call your doctor right away if you have any other sign of an allergic reaction.
In rare cases, hydroxyzine can cause a serious skin reaction called acute generalized exanthematous pustulosis (AGEP).
Signs include fever together with many small, shallow, pus-filled spots appearing across large areas of swollen, red skin.
If you have had AGEP or another allergic reaction to hydroxyzine, you should also avoid cetirizine and levocetirizine.
This is because a similar reaction can happen with cetirizine and levocetirizine.
Hydroxyzine has also been linked to a heart rhythm problem called QT prolongation.
QT prolongation can lead to a serious irregular heartbeat known as Torsade de Pointes.
Other reported side effects include:
tremor or seizures (convulsions), which are rare and have usually happened at doses much higher than recommended
hallucinations (seeing or hearing things that are not there)
skin reactions including rash, hives, itching, and fixed drug eruptions, which are rashes that come back in the same spot each time the medicine is taken
allergic reaction
Serious breathing problems have not been reported at recommended doses.

In animal studies, hydroxyzine caused birth defects in rats and mice at doses well above those used in people.

a recent heart attack

other conditions that make heart rhythm problems more likely

Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
The most common sign of hydroxyzine overdose is heavy sedation.
Other reported signs include seizures (convulsions), stupor, nausea, and vomiting.
Overdose can also cause QT prolongation and Torsade de Pointes.
Heart monitoring with an ECG is recommended.
There is no specific antidote for hydroxyzine overdose.
Store hydroxyzine tablets, capsules, and oral solution at room temperature, between 68°F and 77°F (20°C to 25°C).
Keep tablets and capsules in a tightly closed, light-resistant container with a child-resistant cap.
Keep the oral solution in a tightly closed, light-resistant bottle with a child-resistant cap.
Protect oral solution from light and do not let oral solution freeze.
Keep this and all medicines out of the reach of children.
Q: Can you take hydroxyzine for anxiety and sleep?
Hydroxyzine can be prescribed short term for anxiety.
It can also be prescribed for anxiety-related trouble sleeping.
It is usually used as an "as-needed" or bridging option.
It is not typically used as a long-term daily medicine.
Evidence supports hydroxyzine's short‑term anxiolytic and sedating effects.
Hydroxyzine's effectiveness and safety for chronic use beyond several months are not well established.
Q: Can Hydroxyzine be Taken Long-term For Anxiety?
A: Hydroxyzine is intended for short-term anxiety treatment only, typically less than 4 months.
Long-term hydroxyzine use is considered off-label.
This is because no studies have examined the safety of extended use beyond this timeframe.
Some research suggests a link between long-term use of medications with anticholinergic properties and dementia.
The medications include hydroxyzine.
The link is particularly observed in older adults.
Brain fog has also been reported with long-term use.
Q: How long does hydroxyzine last?
Hydroxyzine has noticeable calming and sedating effects.
These effects typically last about 4–6 hours for most people.
Hydroxyzine itself stays in the body much longer.
This is due to an elimination half-life usually around 14–25 hours in adults.
The elimination half-life is about 7 hours in children.
Q: Does Benadryl help with or cause anxiety?
No, Benadryl does not treat anxiety.
In some people, Benadryl can cause anxiety.
Benadryl (diphenhydramine) is an allergy medicine that often makes people sleepy.
Benadryl may feel calming for a few hours.
But drowsiness is not the same as treating anxiety.
Benadryl is not FDA-approved for anxiety.
Q: Hydroxyzine Pamoate vs HCl: What’s the difference?
Hydroxyzine pamoate delivers the active drug hydroxyzine.
Hydroxyzine HCl delivers the active drug hydroxyzine.
Hydroxyzine pamoate and hydroxyzine HCl deliver the same active drug.
The clinical effects of hydroxyzine include sedation.
The clinical effects of hydroxyzine include itch relief.
The clinical effects of hydroxyzine include anxiety reduction.
The clinical effects of hydroxyzine pamoate and hydroxyzine HCl are broadly similar.
The main difference is the salt form.
The salt form is pamoate vs hydrochloride.
The salt form influences how the medicine is formulated.
Q: Is hydroxyzine a controlled substance or addictive?
Hydroxyzine is not classified as a controlled substance in the United States.
Current evidence does not show that hydroxyzine causes physical dependence.
Current evidence does not show that hydroxyzine causes a characteristic withdrawal syndrome when stopped.
Hydroxyzine is a prescription-only sedating antihistamine.
It should be taken only under medical supervision.
Q: Hydroxyzine vs Benadryl: What’s the difference?
Hydroxyzine and Benadryl (diphenhydramine) are both older, sedating H1 antihistamines.
Hydroxyzine and Benadryl are different drugs with different FDA‑approved uses.
Hydroxyzine is available only by prescription in the United States.
Diphenhydramine (Benadryl) is sold over the counter (OTC) in many allergy and sleep products.
Q: Can dogs take hydroxyzine?
A: Yes, dogs can take hydroxyzine.
Hydroxyzine is an antihistamine.
Hydroxyzine may help treat allergies in dogs.
Hydroxyzine may help treat anxiety in dogs.
Hydroxyzine may help treat motion sickness in dogs.
Q: Does hydroxyzine cause weight gain or loss?
Hydroxyzine is not strongly linked to either weight gain or weight loss.
Any changes in weight are usually mild and indirect rather than a direct effect of the drug.
In clinical studies, weight changes are uncommon.
Weight changes are generally not considered clinically significant.
Other side effects such as drowsiness are considered clinically significant.
Ibuprofen is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID).
Ibuprofen works by reducing hormones that cause inflammation and pain in the body.
Ibuprofen is used to reduce fever.
Ibuprofen is used to treat pain or inflammation.
The pain or inflammation is caused by many conditions.
These conditions include headache, toothache, back pain, arthritis, menstrual cramps, or minor injury.
Ibuprofen is used in adults and children.
Some formulations are also used in premature babies to close a certain heart defect.
Ibuprofen can increase your risk of fatal heart attack, heart failure, or stroke.
Do not use Ibuprofen just before or after heart bypass surgery (coronary artery bypass graft, or CABG).
Avoid taking ibuprofen after a recent heart attack, unless your doctor tells you to.
Ibuprofen may also cause stomach or intestinal bleeding.
This bleeding can be fatal.
These conditions can occur without warning while you are using Ibuprofen, especially in older adults.
Do not take more than your recommended dose.
An ibuprofen overdose can damage your stomach or intestines.
Use only the smallest amount of medication needed to get relief from your pain, swelling, or fever.
You should not use ibuprofen if you are allergic to ibuprofen.
You should not use ibuprofen if you have ever had an asthma attack or severe allergic reaction after taking aspirin or an NSAID.
Ask a doctor or pharmacist if this medicine is safe to use if you have ever had:
heart disease, high blood pressure, high cholesterol, diabetes, or if you smoke;
a heart attack, stroke, or blood clot;
stomach ulcers or bleeding;
liver or kidney disease;
asthma; or
if you take aspirin to prevent heart attack or stroke.
Ask a doctor before using this medicine if you are pregnant or breastfeeding.
If you are pregnant, you should not take ibuprofen unless your doctor tells you to.
Taking an NSAID at 20 weeks or later in pregnancy can cause serious heart or kidney problems in the unborn baby and possible complications with your pregnancy.
Do not take ibuprofen after 30 weeks of pregnancy.
Ibuprofen may cause a temporary delay in ovulation.
This delay can make it harder to get pregnant.
Tell your doctor if you are having trouble getting pregnant.
Do not give ibuprofen to a child younger than 6 months old without the advice of a doctor.
Use ibuprofen exactly as directed on the label, or as prescribed by your doctor.
Use the lowest dose that is effective in treating your condition.
An ibuprofen overdose can damage your stomach or intestines.
The maximum amount of ibuprofen for adults is 800 milligrams per dose or 3200 mg per day (4 maximum doses).
A child's dose is based on the age and weight of the child.
Carefully follow the dosing instructions provided with children's ibuprofen for the age and weight of your child.
Ask a doctor or pharmacist if you have questions.
Take ibuprofen with food or milk to lessen stomach upset.
Shake the oral suspension (liquid) before you measure a dose.
Use the dosing syringe provided, or use a medicine dose-measuring device (not a kitchen spoon).
You must chew the chewable tablet before you swallow chewable tablet.
Store at room temperature away from moisture and heat.
Do not allow the liquid medicine to freeze.
Ibuprofen is used when needed.
You may not be on a dosing schedule.
Skip any missed dose if it's almost time for your next dose.
Do not use two doses at one time.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Overdose symptoms may include nausea, vomiting, stomach pain, drowsiness, black or bloody stools, coughing up blood, shallow breathing, fainting, or coma.
Ask a doctor or pharmacist before using other medicines for pain, fever, swelling, or cold/flu symptoms.
Other medicines may contain ingredients similar to ibuprofen.
These ingredients include aspirin, ketoprofen, or naproxen.
Avoid taking aspirin unless your doctor tells you to.
If you also take aspirin to prevent stroke or heart attack.
Taking ibuprofen can make aspirin less effective.
Aspirin protects your heart and blood vessels.
If you take both medicines, take ibuprofen at least 8 hours before or 30 minutes after you take aspirin.
The aspirin is the non-enteric coated form.
Avoid drinking alcohol.
Alcohol may increase your risk of stomach bleeding.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to ibuprofen.
Signs of an allergic reaction to ibuprofen include hives, difficult breathing, and swelling in your face or throat.
Get emergency medical help if you have a severe skin reaction.
Signs of a severe skin reaction include fever, sore throat, burning eyes, skin pain, and a red or purple skin rash with blistering and peeling.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of a heart attack or stroke.
These signs include chest pain spreading to your jaw or shoulder.
These signs include sudden numbness or weakness on one side of the body.
These signs include slurred speech.
These signs include leg swelling.
These signs include feeling short of breath.
Stop using Ibuprofen and call your doctor at once if you have:
changes in your vision;
shortness of breath (even with mild exertion);
swelling or rapid weight gain;
a skin rash, no matter how mild;
signs of stomach bleeding - bloody or tarry stools, coughing up blood or vomit that looks like coffee grounds;
liver problems - nausea, upper stomach pain, itching, tired feeling, flu-like symptoms, loss of appetite, dark urine, clay-colored stools, jaundice (yellowing of the skin or eyes);
low red blood cells (anemia) - pale skin, feeling light-headed or short of breath, rapid heart rate, trouble concentrating; or
kidney problems - little or no urinating, painful or difficult urination, swelling in your feet or ankles, feeling tired or short of breath.
Common ibuprofen side effects may include:
nausea, vomiting, gas;
bleeding; or
dizziness, headache.
This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Ask your doctor before using ibuprofen if you take an antidepressant.
Taking certain antidepressants with an NSAID may cause you to bruise or bleed easily.
Ask a doctor or pharmacist before using ibuprofen with any other medications, especially:
cyclosporine;
lithium;
methotrexate;
a blood thinner (warfarin, Coumadin, Jantoven);
heart or blood pressure medication, including a diuretic or "water pill"; or
steroid medicine (such as prednisone)
digoxin.
This list is not complete.
Other drugs may interact with ibuprofen.
These drugs include prescription and over-the-counter medicines.
These drugs also include vitamins and herbal products.
Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.
Q: Meloxicam vs Ibuprofen: What's the difference?
Meloxicam is stronger, prescription-only, longer-acting, and used mainly for arthritis.
Ibuprofen is milder, OTC, shorter-acting, and treats various pains.
Q: Naproxen vs ibuprofen: What's the difference?
Ibuprofen is short acting.
Naproxen is long acting.
Naproxen is more likely to cause an upset stomach.
Naproxen also increases the risk of uncontrolled bleeding in people taking blood thinners by 4-fold.
Ibuprofen increases the risk 2-fold.
Naproxen and ibuprofen are both NSAIDs.
Naproxen and ibuprofen are similar in many ways.
There are important differences between Naproxen and ibuprofen.
Q: Can you take ibuprofen on an empty stomach?
There is a common belief that you must take ibuprofen with food.
You can safely take low-dose ibuprofen on an empty stomach.
The recommended dosage is up to 1200 mg daily in divided doses for 1 to 7 days.
Taking low-dose ibuprofen on an empty stomach provides faster pain relief.
Taking ibuprofen with food provides slower pain relief.
Food slows ibuprofen absorption time.
Food doesn't affect the total amount absorbed.
There is actually no scientific evidence that taking ibuprofen with food prevents stomach irritation.
Over-the-counter ibuprofen doses have a low incidence of gastric problems anyway.
Q: Can you take expired ibuprofen?
Taking expired ibuprofen is not recommended by the manufacturer.
The actual shelf-life of ibuprofen is likely to be longer than that indicated by the expiry date.
FDA testing shows many drugs are active for years beyond their expiry date.
Specific data on ibuprofen is not available.
An ibuprofen tablet that is a few months past its expiry date is probably safe to take.
This is true within reason.
Ibuprofen that is years past Ibuprofen's expiry date should not be taken.
Q: Can You Take Tramadol with Acetaminophen, Ibuprofen, or Aspirin?
Tramadol can be safely combined with acetaminophen.
Tramadol can be safely combined with ibuprofen.
Tramadol can be safely combined with aspirin.
Combining tramadol with these medicines provides better pain relief.
This relief is better than using either medicine alone.
However, there are important age restrictions and clinical considerations to be aware of.
Q: What's the difference between aspirin and ibuprofen?
Aspirin and ibuprofen are popular NSAIDs.
NSAIDs are nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs.
Aspirin and ibuprofen work by blocking prostaglandin production.
Blocking prostaglandin production reduces pain and inflammation.
However, these over-the-counter pain relievers have important differences.
The differences are in safety, uses, and side effects.
Q: Can you overdose on ibuprofen?
Ibuprofen has a wide safety margin.
It is possible to overdose on ibuprofen.
Death from ibuprofen overdose is rare.
Children are more susceptible to the effects of ibuprofen.
Children are likely to experience an overdosage of ibuprofen if high dosages are ingested.
High dosages are defined as more than 400mg/kg.
A dosage of 400mg/kg is equivalent to 8000mg for a 20kg child.
This amount is equivalent to 40 x 200mg ibuprofen tablets.
Studies have reported patients ingesting less than 99 mg/kg are unlikely to have symptoms.
Q: How much ibuprofen can I take and how often?
Adults can take ibuprofen for pain relief.
The dosage is 200 mg to 400 mg.
This is equivalent to one or two tablets.
The medication can be taken every 4-6 hours.
It is safe for adults to take.
The maximum dose in 24 hours for over-the-counter ibuprofen is 1200 mg (6 tablets of 200 mg each).
Ibuprofen is also available on a prescription from a doctor.
It may be prescribed at a higher strength (600 mg or 800 mg every 6 hours) for severe pain.
Insulin glargine is a long-acting insulin.
It is used to treat type 1 and type 2 diabetes in certain patients.
The treatment aims to improve and maintain blood glucose levels.
Insulin glargine is a man-made form of human insulin.
It is used once daily.
It provides a base level of insulin.
This insulin keeps working for 24 hours or longer.
Insulin glargine is available under the brand names Lantus, Basaglar, Toujeo, Semglee, and Rezvoglar.
Langlara is an interchangeable biosimilar to Lantus that was FDA-approved on May 4, 2026.
Rezvoglar, Lantus, Langlara, Semglee, and Toujeo are FDA-approved.
They are approved for use in adult and pediatric patients.
These patients have type 1 and type 2 diabetes.
Basasglar is FDA-approved to be used by adults for type 2 diabetes.
It is also approved for adults and pediatric patients with type 1 diabetes.
In patients with type 1 diabetes, insulin glargine must be used together with a short-acting insulin.
Common insulin glargine side effects may include low blood sugar.
Common insulin glargine side effects may include swelling.
Common insulin glargine side effects may include weight gain.
Common insulin glargine side effects may include allergic reaction.
Common insulin glargine side effects may include itching.
Common insulin glargine side effects may include rash.
Common insulin glargine side effects may include thickening or hollowing of the skin where you injected Insulin glargine (lipodystrophy).
Serious insulin glargine side effects
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of insulin allergy.
Signs of insulin allergy include redness or swelling where an injection was given.
Signs of insulin allergy include an itchy skin rash over the entire body.
Signs of insulin allergy include trouble breathing.
Signs of insulin allergy include fast heartbeats.
Signs of insulin allergy include feeling like you might pass out.
Signs of insulin allergy include swelling in your tongue or throat.
Insulin glargine may cause other serious side effects.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
rapid weight gain, swelling in your feet or ankles;
shortness of breath; or
low blood potassium--leg cramps, constipation, irregular heartbeats, fluttering in your chest, increased thirst or urination, numbness or tingling, muscle weakness or limp feeling.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Never share an injection pen, even if you changed the needle.
You should not use insulin glargine if you are allergic to insulin.
You should not use insulin glargine if you are having an episode of hypoglycemia.
You should not use insulin glargine if you are having an episode of diabetic ketoacidosis.
Call your doctor for treatment.
Different brands of insulin glargine are specifically approved for different age groups and different diabetes types.
The safety and effectiveness of insulin glargine in pediatric patients younger than 6 years of age with type 1 diabetes has not been established.
The safety and effectiveness of insulin glargine in pediatric patients with type 2 diabetes has not been established.
Tell your doctor if you have ever had:
liver or kidney disease; or
heart failure or other heart problems.

Pregnancy:
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant, as it is not known if insulin glargine will harm your unborn baby.
Follow your doctor's instructions about using Insulin glargine if you are pregnant or you become pregnant.
Controlling diabetes is very important during pregnancy.
Breastfeeding:
Tell your healthcare provider if you are breastfeeding or are planning to breastfeed.
It is not known if insulin glargine may harm your breastfeeding baby.

Prepare an injection only when you are ready to give it.

Insulin glargine is injected under the skin, using a syringe or insulin pen, once per day at the same time of day.
Insulin can be injected into your upper legs (thighs), upper arms, or stomach area (abdomen).
Do not inject into the same place two times in a row.

When treating type 1 diabetes, use your short-acting insulin before meals as directed by your doctor.
Insulin glargine must not be given with an insulin pump or mixed with other insulins.

Sharing these devices can pass infections from person to person.
Blood sugar can be affected by stress, illness, surgery, exercise, alcohol use, or skipping meals.
Low blood sugar (hypoglycemia) can make you feel very hungry, dizzy, irritable, or shaky.
To quickly treat hypoglycemia, eat or drink hard candy, crackers, raisins, fruit juice, or non-diet soda.
Your doctor may prescribe glucagon injections in case of severe hypoglycemia.
Tell your doctor if you have frequent symptoms of high blood sugar (hyperglycemia), such as increased thirst or urination.

Q: How and where should I inject insulin?
Injecting insulin is not difficult.
Injecting insulin does take a bit of practice.
There are three main sites where insulin can be injected.
One site is the stomach area.
The stomach area excludes a 2-inch circle around your navel.
Another site is the soft part of your waist.
The waist site excludes areas near your spine.
A third site is the top and outer part of your thighs.
The thigh site excludes your inner thighs.
The thigh site also excludes areas close to your knee.
The final site is the outer back of your upper arm.
This arm site must have a pocket of fatty tissue.
Q: When is the best time to take Toujeo?
Toujeo may be given at any time of the day.
Once you have chosen a time, it is best to inject Toujeo within three hours of that time every day.
If needed, you can change the time you administer Toujeo.
You should talk to your doctor about this.
Q: How long can Toujeo stay out of the refrigerator?
A: Toujeo can stay out of the refrigerator for up to 56 days (8 weeks) according to the prescribing information written on 03/2023.
If Toujeo if left out the refrigerator for more than 20 or 30 minutes, Toujeo should not be put back into the refrigerator.
Q: What are the different types of insulin?
A: Insulins are usually grouped as fast-acting, intermediate-acting, or long-acting.
Within these groups, Insulins can be further classified as human insulin and human insulin analogs.
An insulin analog is a human insulin.
The insulin analog has one or two amino acids changed.
The change affects how quickly the insulin analog is absorbed after injection.
The change affects how fast or slow the insulin analog acts.
Insulin analogs are usually given within 15 minutes of a meal or at the same time as food.
Q: Soliqua vs Lantus: What’s the difference between Soliqua?
Soliqua is a combination of insulin glargine plus lixisenatide.
Lantus only contains insulin glargine.
Both are once-daily injections.
Both are given under the skin.
Both are given subcutaneously.
Soliqua can only be used in adults with type 2 diabetes.
Lantus may be used to treat type 1 diabetes in adults and children over the age of six years.
Lantus may be used to treat type 2 diabetes in adults and children over the age of six years.
Both are made by Sanofi-Aventis.
Q: Can Toujeo be given twice a day?
Toujeo is usually given once a day.
Some people may experience better blood sugar control if the dose is split.
The split dose is given twice a day every 12 hours.
Q: How many pens are in a box of Toujeo?
A: There are three pens in a box of Toujeo Solostar.
There are two pens in a box of Toujeo Max Solostar.
Q: Is Toujeo a fast or long-acting insulin?
A: Toujeo is a long-acting insulin that is usually given once a day.
Toujeo lasts for up to 36 hours.
Q: What is the difference between Soliqua and Xultophy?
A: The main difference between Soliqua and Xultophy is what is inside each pen.
Both are once-a-day shots for type 2 diabetes.
The shots combine a long-acting (basal) insulin with a GLP-1 medicine.
Soliqua 100/33 pairs insulin glargine (Lantus) with lixisenatide (Adlyxin).
Xultophy 100/3.6 pairs insulin degludec (Tresiba) with liraglutide (Victoza).
Soliqua and Xultophy also differ in how and when you inject them.
They differ in their heart-health data.
They differ in their common side effects.
They differ in their cost.
Levothyroxine is a man-made form of thyroxine (T4), one of the hormones your thyroid gland normally produces.
Levothyroxine belongs to a class of medications called hormones.
Levothyroxine works by replacing thyroid hormone that your body is not making enough of.
Levothyroxine is used in adults and children, including newborn babies, to:
treat hypothyroidism (underactive thyroid - a condition where the thyroid gland does not produce enough thyroid hormone).
Levothyroxine replaces the missing hormone whether the condition was present from birth or developed later.
It replaces the missing hormone whether the problem starts in the thyroid gland itself or in the pituitary gland or hypothalamus.
lower the level of thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) in the blood in people being treated for a certain type of thyroid cancer, alongside surgery and radioactive iodine therapy.
Levothyroxine injection is used in adults to treat myxedema coma.
Myxedema coma is a rare and life-threatening complication.
Myxedema coma is a complication of a severely underactive thyroid.
Without thyroid hormone, your body cannot function properly.
This may result in poor growth.
It may result in slow speech.
It may result in a lack of energy.
It may result in excessive tiredness.
It may result in constipation.
It may result in weight gain.
It may result in hair loss.
It may result in dry, thick skin.
It may result in increased sensitivity to cold.
It may result in joint and muscle pain.
It may result in heavy or irregular menstrual periods.
It may result in depression.
When taken correctly, levothyroxine reverses these symptoms.
Levothyroxine may take several weeks before you notice a difference.
Levothyroxine is usually taken for life.
What levothyroxine is not used for:
Levothyroxine is not used to shrink benign thyroid nodules.
Levothyroxine is not used to shrink a nontoxic diffuse goiter.
This applies to people who get enough iodine in their diet.
Levothyroxine does not help in these situations.
Taking more thyroid hormone than your body needs can cause an overactive thyroid.
Levothyroxine is not used to treat hypothyroidism.
This applies during the recovery phase of subacute thyroiditis.
Subacute thyroiditis is inflammation of the thyroid gland.
Levothyroxine is not used to treat obesity or for weight loss.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to levothyroxine.
Signs of an allergic reaction to levothyroxine include hives, difficult breathing, and swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Allergic reactions are usually caused by the inactive ingredients in a product rather than by levothyroxine itself.
Your doctor may suggest trying a different brand or form.
Levothyroxine may cause serious side effects.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
sudden pain or trouble moving your hip, wrist, or back;
fast or irregular heartbeats, or a pounding heartbeat;
chest pain, pain spreading to your jaw or shoulder;
wheezing or shortness of breath;
swelling in your legs or ankles, or new or worsening breathlessness when lying down;
fever, swollen glands, itching, joint pain, or not feeling well;
nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea;
confusion or feeling disoriented;
a seizure; or
high blood sugar - increased thirst, increased urination, dry mouth, fruity breath odor.
Common levothyroxine side effects may include:
fever, hot flashes, increased sweating, feeling too hot;
tiredness;
skin rash, flushing, hair loss;
chest pain, fast or irregular heartbeats, shortness of breath;
headache, leg cramps, muscle cramps, muscle pain or weakness;
tremors, feeling nervous, anxious, irritable, or emotional, sleep problems (insomnia);
increased or change in appetite;
weight loss or weight gain;
changes in your menstrual periods, or difficulty getting pregnant; or
stomach cramps, vomiting, diarrhea.
Most of these side effects happen when the levothyroxine dose is higher than your body needs.
This situation produces symptoms of an overactive thyroid.
Tell your doctor if you have any of symptoms so your dose can be checked.
Side effects in children:
Children taking levothyroxine may develop increased pressure around the brain.
Increased pressure around the brain can cause headaches.
Increased pressure around the brain can cause vomiting.
Increased pressure around the brain can cause vision changes.
Children taking levothyroxine may develop a hip problem called slipped capital femoral epiphysis.
Slipped capital femoral epiphysis causes hip pain.
Slipped capital femoral epiphysis causes groin pain.
Slipped capital femoral epiphysis causes knee pain.
Slipped capital femoral epiphysis causes limping.
Call your child's doctor about any of these symptoms.
Too much levothyroxine in babies can cause the soft spots on the skull to close too early.
Too much levothyroxine in older children can cause the growth plates to close too early.
Closing of the growth plates may reduce final adult height.
Too little levothyroxine can affect a child's learning and growth.
This is why children need regular blood tests and check-ups.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Levothyroxine should not be used to treat obesity or weight problems.
Dangerous side effects or death can occur from the misuse of Levothyroxine.
This risk is especially high if you are taking other weight-loss medications.
This risk is especially high if you are taking appetite suppressants.
Taking more than your recommended dose will not make Levothyroxine more effective.
Taking more than your recommended dose may cause serious side effects.
Levothyroxine has a narrow therapeutic index.
This means the difference between too little and too much is small.
Taking too much or too little over time can affect your heart.
It can affect your bones.
It can affect your blood sugar.
It can affect your cholesterol.
It can affect your digestion.
It can affect your memory and concentration.
It can affect your fertility.
In children, it can affect growth and development.
Your doctor will start you on a suitable dose and adjust dose gradually based on blood tests.
You may not be able to use levothyroxine if you have certain medical conditions.
Tell your doctor if you have an untreated or uncontrolled adrenal gland disorder or any heart problems such as a recent heart attack.
You will need to start treatment with a steroid (glucocorticoid) medicine.
You will need to start levothyroxine.
You must start the steroid medicine before you start levothyroxine.
This is required if you have an adrenal gland problem.
Starting levothyroxine first can trigger a sudden, serious drop in adrenal hormones known as an adrenal crisis.
Older adults and people with heart disease are usually started on a lower dose.
This is because too much thyroid hormone can cause an irregular heartbeat, angina, or other heart problems.
The irregular heartbeat is most commonly atrial fibrillation.
Tell your doctor if you develop chest pain, breathlessness, or a fast or irregular heartbeat.
Taking more levothyroxine than your body needs over a long period can weaken your bones and lead to a higher risk of fractures.
This is more likely after menopause.
Your doctor will aim to give you the lowest dose that controls your thyroid levels.
Oral levothyroxine should not be used to treat myxedema coma.
Levothyroxine given into a vein is used instead.
You should not use levothyroxine if you have an untreated or uncontrolled adrenal gland disorder.
You should not use certain liquid or injectable forms of levothyroxine.
This applies if you are allergic to glycerin.
This also applies if you are allergic to edetate disodium.
To make sure Levothyroxine is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:
a thyroid nodule;
thyroiditis (inflammation of the thyroid gland);
heart problems such as a heart attack, stroke, angina, or an irregular heartbeat;
a blood clot or a blood clotting disorder;
diabetes (your diabetes medicine may need to be adjusted);
anemia (low red blood cells);
weak bones (osteoporosis), or low bone mineral density;
problems with your pituitary or adrenal gland;
an allergy to any food or drugs;
recently received radiation therapy with iodine (such as I-131); or
kidney disease.
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant.
Having hypothyroidism during pregnancy may increase the risk of miscarriage.
It may also increase the risk of high blood pressure in pregnancy.
It may increase the risk of pre-eclampsia.
It may increase the risk of stillbirth.
It may increase the risk of premature birth.
Untreated hypothyroidism can affect the baby's brain development.
The benefit of treating hypothyroidism outweighs these risks.
Levothyroxine should not be stopped during pregnancy.
Your dose needs are often higher during pregnancy.
Your doctor will check your thyroid blood tests as soon as your pregnancy is confirmed.
Your doctor will check your thyroid blood tests at least once in each trimester.
Your doctor will usually put you back on your pre-pregnancy dose straight after you give birth.
Your doctor will perform another blood test 4 to 8 weeks later.
Tell your doctor if you are breastfeeding.
Levothyroxine passes into breast milk.
No harmful effects on breastfed babies have been reported.
Treating hypothyroidism properly may help milk supply.
This may happen if Levothyroxine is low.
Your dose needs may be different while you are nursing.
You may be more likely to have a broken bone while using levothyroxine.
This is particularly true if your dose is higher than you need.
Talk with your doctor about ways to keep your bones healthy.
Take levothyroxine exactly as prescribed by your doctor.
Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets.
Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.
Some brands of levothyroxine have different dosage instructions.
Some brands of levothyroxine have different recommendations.
The recommendations concern how soon you need to take levothyroxine before eating food.
Always check the instructions on the label.
Oral levothyroxine:
Oral levothyroxine is taken by mouth.
It is available as tablets, capsules, and an oral solution.
Keep using Oral levothyroxine even if you feel well.
Oral levothyroxine may take 4 to 6 weeks to get the full benefit of a dose.
You will need frequent medical tests.
Your next dose may be delayed based on the results.
Oral levothyroxine can affect the results of certain medical tests.
Tell any doctor who treats you that you are using Oral levothyroxine.
Tell your doctor if you have a planned surgery or dental procedure.
Taking more than your recommended dose will not make Oral levothyroxine more effective.
Taking more than your recommended dose may cause serious side effects.
Tablets and capsules:
Take levothyroxine tablets and capsules on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast with a full glass of water.
A full glass of water is important.
It is important to avoid choking or gagging.
The tablet can start to break apart quickly.
Take Oral levothyroxine at the same time each day.
Swallow the capsule whole and do not crush, chew, break, or open it.
Tell your doctor if your child cannot swallow a capsule whole.
You cannot swallow the tablet whole.
Crush the tablet.
Mix the tablet with 1 or 2 teaspoons of water.
Give the mixture right away by spoon or dropper.
Make sure all of the mixture is taken.
Do not save mixture for later use.
Do not mix levothyroxine into soy-based infant formula or other foods that can stop levothyroxine being absorbed.
Keep each tablet or capsule in the blister pack until you are ready to take one.
Store at room temperature away from moisture, heat, and light.
Oral solution (liquid medicine):
Measure liquid medicine with the supplied measuring device (not a kitchen spoon).
Dosages are based on weight in children and teenagers.
Your child's dose may change if the child gains or loses weight.
Your dose needs may change if you switch to a different brand, strength, or form of levothyroxine.
Avoid medication errors by using only the medicine your doctor prescribes.
Tirosint-Sol oral solution comes in single-dose ampules and can be administered 15 minutes before eating breakfast.
Squeeze the liquid directly into your mouth, onto a spoon, or into a glass of water to drink right away.
Use all ampules within 3 months after opening the foil pouch.
Ermeza and Thyquidity liquids should be taken on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast.
Store Ermeza and Thyquidity liquids in Ermeza and Thyquidity liquids's original bottle.
Use Ermeza within 90 days and Thyquidity within 8 weeks of opening the bottle.
Store at room temperature away from moisture, heat, and light.
Intravenous levothyroxine:
Levothyroxine is given as an injection into a vein by a healthcare provider.
It should only be used to treat myxedema coma.
Blood tests, biotin, and monitoring:
Your doctor will use blood tests to check that your dose is right.
This is usually done 6 to 8 weeks after any dose change.
Then, blood tests are performed every 6 to 12 months once your dose is stable.
Children are tested more often.
They are tested 2 and 4 weeks after starting treatment.
They are also tested 2 weeks after any dose change.
Tell your doctor or pharmacist if you take biotin, or a multivitamin, hair, skin, or nail supplement that contains biotin (sometimes listed as vitamin B7).
Biotin can interfere with thyroid blood tests.
This interference can give a falsely high or falsely low result.
This could lead to the wrong dose change.
Stop biotin and any biotin-containing supplements at least 2 days before a thyroid blood test.
If your symptoms and blood tests still suggest an underactive thyroid even though you are on a dose that should be enough, an underactive thyroid may be because the medicine is not being absorbed properly.
An underactive thyroid may be because doses are being missed.
An underactive thyroid may be because another medicine is interfering.
Talk to your doctor before your dose is increased.
In a medical setting, you are not likely to miss a dose of levothyroxine injection.
Take the missed oral levothyroxine dose as soon as you remember.
Then go back to your regular schedule.
Do not use two doses at one time.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
An overdose can be fatal.
Overdose symptoms may include headache.
Overdose symptoms may include leg cramps.
Overdose symptoms may include tremors.
Overdose symptoms may include feeling nervous or irritable.
Overdose symptoms may include confusion.
Overdose symptoms may include chest pain.
Overdose symptoms may include shortness of breath.
Overdose symptoms may include fast or pounding heartbeats.
Overdose symptoms may include stroke.
Overdose symptoms may include seizures.
Overdose symptoms may include shock.
Overdose symptoms may include coma.
Symptoms may not appear straight away and can take several days to develop.
Get medical advice even if the person seems well.
Avoid the following food products within 1 hour of taking oral levothyroxine.
If you do not avoid these products, levothyroxine will not be as effective.
The food products to avoid are grapefruit juice, infant soy formula, soybean flour, cottonseed meal, walnuts, and high-fiber foods.
Grapefruit juice can both delay absorption and reduce the amount of levothyroxine that gets into your body.
If you eat or drink any of these regularly, tell your doctor.
Your dose may need adjusting.
Sometimes it is not safe to use certain medicines at the same time.
Some drugs can affect your thyroid hormone levels.
Some drugs can also make levothyroxine less effective.

If you have diabetes, levothyroxine can raise your blood sugar.
You may need more of your diabetes medicine or insulin.
Check your blood sugar more often when you start, change, or stop levothyroxine.
Tell your doctor about any changes.
If you take a blood thinner such as warfarin, levothyroxine can increase levothyroxine's effect.
You may need more frequent blood clotting tests and a lower blood thinner dose.

List includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
