Q: Does levothyroxine cause weight gain or loss?
A: In most cases, levothyroxine causes some weight loss.
According to the American Thyroid Association, when levothyroxine is started, you may lose up to 10% of your weight.
This weight is mainly water weight, since being hypothyroid makes you retain water.
Q: What can I eat for breakfast after taking levothyroxine?
Most foods are considered fine to eat for breakfast.
This is true as long as foods are eaten 30 to 60 minutes after taking levothyroxine.
Levothyroxine should be taken once a day on an empty stomach in the morning.
Q: Can I take other medications with levothyroxine?
Levothyroxine is known to interact with many other medications.
These interactions are called drug-drug interactions.
There are different kinds of drug-drug interactions.
Some interactions require that drugs not be taken at the same time.
Other interactions require more frequent laboratory testing or monitoring of your disease symptoms.
Q: How soon after taking levothyroxine can I take omeprazole?
There is a known drug interaction between levothyroxine and omeprazole.
The timing of when you take the two medicines is not relevant.
Omeprazole decreases the absorption of your thyroid medication.
If you need to be on a course of omeprazole while taking levothyroxine.
Your doctor may order more frequent laboratory testing.
The testing is to confirm you are absorbing enough levothyroxine.
Q: Does levothyroxine cause hair loss?
Levothyroxine may cause hair loss.
Levothyroxine is the main treatment for a sluggish thyroid gland.
Levothyroxine may cause other side effects in addition to hair loss.
You may experience partial hair loss in the first few months of treatment.
Partial hair loss often resolves and goes back to normal in a short time.
Q: How long after taking levothyroxine can you drink milk?
A: You should avoid drinking milk until at least 4 hours after taking levothyroxine.
Dairy products contain high calcium levels.
Dairy products include milk, cheese, and yogurt.
High calcium levels affect how levothyroxine is absorbed into your body.
Q: How long does levothyroxine stay in your system?
A: Levothyroxine will stay in your system for around 4 to 6 weeks.
Levothyroxine takes longer for people with hypothyroidism to eliminate than for those with normal thyroid function.
People with hypothyroidism take closer to 6 weeks to eliminate levothyroxine.
People with normal thyroid function eliminate levothyroxine faster.
Q: What is the difference between Levoxyl and Synthroid?
A: Levoxyl and Synthroid are brand names for the drug levothyroxine.
Both medications contain the same active ingredient, levothyroxine.
Levoxyl and Synthroid contain different inactive ingredients.
Q: Can you take thyroid supplements with levothyroxine?
A: Taking any other medication or supplement with levothyroxine may cause you to need a higher or lower dose of levothyroxine.
Even supplements like calcium or iron can affect absorption of levothyroxine.
Lisinopril is used alone or in combination with other medications to treat high blood pressure in adults and children 6 years of age and older.
Lisinopril is also used in adults to treat congestive heart failure.
It is used to improve survival after a heart attack.
Lisinopril belongs to a class of medications called angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitors.
Lisinopril decreases certain chemicals that tighten the blood vessels.
Blood flows more smoothly.
The heart can pump blood more efficiently.
High blood pressure is a common condition.
When not treated, it can cause damage to the brain, heart, blood vessels, kidneys, and other parts of the body.
Damage to these organs may cause heart disease.
It may cause a heart attack.
It may cause heart failure.
It may cause a stroke.
It may cause kidney failure.
It may cause loss of vision.
It may cause other problems.
Taking medication will help to control your blood pressure.
Making lifestyle changes will also help to control your blood pressure.
These changes include eating a diet that is low in fat and salt.
These changes include maintaining a healthy weight.
These changes include exercising at least 30 minutes most days.
These changes include not smoking.
These changes include using alcohol in moderation.
Do not use lisinopril if you are pregnant.
Lisinopril could harm the unborn baby.
Stop using Lisinopril and tell your doctor right away if you become pregnant.
You should not use Lisinopril if you have ever had angioedema.
Do not take lisinopril within 36 hours before or after taking medicine that contains sacubitril (such as Entresto).
If you have diabetes, do not use lisinopril together with any medication that contains aliskiren.
Examples of medications containing aliskiren include Amturnide, Tekturna, and Tekamlo.
You may also need to avoid taking lisinopril with aliskiren if you have kidney disease.
You should not use lisinopril if you are allergic to lisinopril.
You should not use lisinopril if you are allergic to any other ACE inhibitor.
Examples of other ACE inhibitors include captopril, fosinopril, enalapril, benazepril, moexipril, perindopril, quinapril, ramipril, or trandolapril.
Do not take lisinopril within 36 hours before or after taking medicine that contains sacubitril (such as Entresto).
If you have diabetes, do not take lisinopril with any medication that contains aliskiren (a blood pressure medicine).
Do not take lisinopril if you have a history of angioedema (severe allergic reaction).
To make sure this medicine is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:
heart disease, heart problems such as a recent heart attack;
low blood pressure;
low white blood cell count;
stomach pain;
if you are on a low-salt diet;
diabetes;
liver disease; or
kidney disease (or if you are on dialysis).
You may also need to avoid taking lisinopril with aliskiren if you have kidney disease.
Stop using this medicine and tell your doctor right away if you become pregnant.
Lisinopril can cause injury or death to the unborn baby if you use the medicine during your second or third trimester.
Do not breastfeed.

Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets.
Your doctor may occasionally change your dose..
You may take lisinopril with or without food.
Measure liquid medicine with the supplied measuring device (not a kitchen spoon).
Your blood pressure will need to be checked often.
You may need frequent blood tests.
Tell your doctor if you have a planned surgery.
Call your doctor if you have ongoing vomiting or diarrhea, or if you are sweating more than usual.
You can easily become dehydrated while taking lisinopril.
This can lead to very low blood pressure, an electrolyte imbalance, or kidney failure.
If you have high blood pressure, keep using lisinopril even if you feel well.
High blood pressure often has no symptoms.

Do not freeze.
Usual Adult Dose for Hypertension:
The initial dose is 10 mg orally once a day.
The maintenance dose is 20 to 40 mg orally once a day.
The maximum dose is 80 mg orally once a day.
The initial dose is 5 mg orally once a day in patients receiving a diuretic.
-The 80 mg dose is used but does not appear to give greater effect.
If blood pressure is not controlled with lisinopril alone, a low dose of a diuretic may be added.
Hydrochlorothiazide is an example of such a diuretic.
The dose of hydrochlorothiazide is 12.5 mg.
It may be possible to reduce the dose of lisinopril.
This is after the addition of a diuretic.
Usual Adult Dose for Congestive Heart Failure:
Initial dose: 2.5 to 5 mg orally once a day.
Maintenance dose: Dosage should be increased as tolerated.
Maximum dose: 40 mg orally once a day.
The diuretic dose may need to be adjusted.
This adjustment may help minimize hypovolemia.
Hypovolemia may contribute to hypotension.
The appearance of hypotension after the initial dose of lisinopril does not preclude subsequent careful dose titration.
The dose titration involves lisinopril.
Usual Adult Dose for Myocardial Infarction:
The initial dose is 5 mg orally within 24 hours of the onset of acute myocardial infarction.
Subsequent doses are 5 mg orally after 24 hours.
Then, the dose is 10 mg orally after 48 hours.
Maintenance dose: 10 mg orally once a day.
Dosing should continue for at least 6 weeks.
Therapy should be initiated at 2.5 mg.
This applies to patients with a low systolic blood pressure.
The low systolic blood pressure is defined as less than or equal to 120 mm Hg and greater than 100 mmHg.
Therapy should be initiated during the first 3 days after the infarct.
Therapy should be withdrawn.
This occurs if prolonged hypotension happens.
Prolonged hypotension is defined as a systolic blood pressure less than 90 mmHg for more than 1 hour.
Uses: Reduction of mortality in acute myocardial infarction
Usual Adult Dose for Diabetic Nephropathy:
The initial dose is 10 to 20 mg orally once a day.
The maintenance dose is 20 to 40 mg orally once a day.
The dosage may be titrated upward every 3 days.
This is not an approved indication.
Usual Geriatric Dose for Hypertension:
Initial dose: 2.5 to 5 mg orally once a day.
Maintenance dose: Dosages should be increased at 2.5 mg to 5 mg per day at 1 to 2 week intervals.
Maximum dose: 40 mg orally once a day
Usual Pediatric Dose for Hypertension:
Pediatric patients greater than or equal to 6 years of age were studied.
The initial dose was 0.07 mg/kg orally once a day.
The maximum initial dose is 5 mg once a day.
The maintenance dose should be adjusted according to blood pressure response.
Adjustments should occur at 1 to 2 week intervals.
Maximum dose information is provided.
Doses above 0.61 mg/kg have not been studied in pediatric patients.
Doses greater than 40 mg have not been studied in pediatric patients.
This drug is not recommended in pediatric patients less than 6 years old.
This drug is not recommended in pediatric patients with a glomerular filtration rate less than 30 mL/min.

a light-headed feeling, like you might pass out;
high blood potassium - nausea, weakness, tingly feeling, chest pain, irregular heartbeats, loss of movement;

kidney problems - swelling, urinating less, feeling tired or short of breath; or
liver problems - loss of appetite, stomach pain (upper right side), tiredness, itching, dark urine, clay-colored stools, jaundice (yellowing of the skin or eyes).

These medicines are for infections, cancer, or osteoporosis.

a diuretic or "water pill" that may increase blood potassium such as spironolactone, triamterene, or amiloride;
NSAIDs (nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) - aspirin, ibuprofen (Advil, Motrin), naproxen (Aleve), celecoxib, diclofenac, indomethacin, meloxicam, and others;

Q: Which blood pressure medications cause coughing?
Angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitors are a class of prescription blood pressure medications.
These medications commonly cause coughing as a side effect.
Available medications in this class include...
Q: Why does lisinopril cause a dry cough and how to fix cough?
A: A dry, tickly cough is one of the most common side effects of lisinopril and other ACE inhibitors.
Cough is usually not dangerous, but can be very annoying.
In most people, the cough improves or disappears after the medication is changed.
There are safe alternatives such as ARBs that your clinician can use instead.
Q: When is the best time to take lisinopril?
Lisinopril is usually taken once a day.
For most people, lisinopril can be taken in the morning or evening.
This is as long as lisinopril is taken at the same time every day.
Morning dosing is common.
This is because lisinopril fits daily routines.
It also aligns with how blood pressure naturally rises after waking.
Q: Does Green Tea interact with any drugs?
Drug interactions with green tea can include certain blood pressure medicines.
Drug interactions with green tea can include statin cholesterol-lowering treatments.
Drug interactions with green tea can include warfarin.
Warfarin is an anticoagulant used to help prevent blood clots.
Drug interactions with green tea can include medicines used to treat hot flashes and night sweats in women due to menopause.
Q: Can I just stop taking lisinopril?
Lisinopril is a commonly prescribed medication for high blood pressure and heart conditions.
Stopping Lisinopril suddenly without medical advice can be dangerous.
It is not recommended in most situations.
Q: Does lisinopril cause weight gain?
A: No, lisinopril does not cause weight gain.
Weight gain was not reported as a side effect in the clinical studies used to approve lisinopril.
Research reviews describe lisinopril as "weight neutral."
If you notice the scale going up after starting lisinopril, the cause is usually something else.
The cause is usually fluid buildup.
The cause is usually another medicine.
The cause is usually a health condition.

Usual Adult Dose for Diabetic Nephropathy:
The initial dose is 50 mg orally once a day.
The maximum dose is 100 mg orally once a day.
If hypertensive patients with left ventricular hypertrophy require titration from the initial dose, increase to 100 mg orally once a day.
If type 2 diabetic nephropathy patients require titration from the initial dose, increase to 100 mg orally once a day.
Antihypertension is substantially present within 1 week.
In some studies, the maximal effect occurred in 3 to 6 weeks.
Uses: -Treatment of hypertension.
-To reduce the risk of stroke in patients with hypertension and left ventricular hypertrophy.
-Treatment of diabetic nephropathy with an elevated serum creatinine and proteinuria (urinary albumin to creatinine ratio 300 mg/g or greater) in patients with type 2 diabetes and a history of hypertension.

Q: Does Losartan block COVID-19 Coronavirus Receptors?
Losartan (Cozaar) does not directly block SARS-CoV-2 coronavirus receptors.
Losartan is an angiotensin receptor blocker (ARB).
Losartan may influence COVID-19 outcomes.
Losartan influences COVID-19 outcomes through its effects on the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS).
Losartan works by blocking angiotensin II from binding to angiotensin II type 1 receptors (AT1).
These receptors are located in the heart, blood vessels, kidneys, and lungs.
This action reduces the activity of RAAS.
Reducing RAAS activity lowers blood pressure.
It may also modulate the inflammatory response.
Meloxicam is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID).
Meloxicam works by reducing hormones that cause pain, fever, and inflammation in the body.
Meloxicam is used to relieve pain, tenderness, swelling, and stiffness.
These symptoms are caused by osteoarthritis and rheumatoid arthritis.
Osteoarthritis is arthritis caused by a breakdown of the lining of the joints.
Rheumatoid arthritis is arthritis caused by swelling of the lining of the joints.
Meloxicam is used to relieve pain, tenderness, swelling, and stiffness.
These symptoms are caused by juvenile rheumatoid arthritis.

Meloxicam is used in children 2 years of age and older.

Usual Adult Dose of Meloxicam IV for Pain:
30 mg IV bolus (over 15 seconds) once a day.

Q: Meloxicam vs Ibuprofen: What's the difference?
Meloxicam is stronger, prescription-only, longer-acting, and used mainly for arthritis.
Ibuprofen is milder, OTC, shorter-acting, and treats various pains.
Q: Should I take meloxicam tablets at night or in the morning?
A: Meloxicam can be taken in the morning or at night.
Oral meloxicam is usually taken once daily, at about the same time each day.
You can take meloxicam with or without food.
Food may help with stomach upset.
Follow your prescribed dose and do not take more than recommended.
Q: How long does it take for meloxicam to work?
Meloxicam usually starts to ease pain within the first day.
The full benefit for chronic conditions like osteoarthritis can take several days to a couple of weeks.
How quickly you feel relief depends on the dose.
It depends on the formulation (oral tablet vs intravenous).
It depends on whether you are treating short‑term postoperative pain or long‑term arthritis.
Q: How much meloxicam can I give my dog?
A: The amount of meloxicam you can give your dog will depend on your dog’s weight and veterinarian’s instructions.
The typical dose is 0.09 mg/lb (0.2 mg/kg) on day 1.
It is given by mouth or mixed with food.
Then the dose is 0.045 mg/lb (0.1 mg/kg) once a day.
Q: How long do I wait after taking ibuprofen to take meloxicam?
Wait until the ibuprofen has worn off before taking your first dose of meloxicam.
This applies if you are switching from ibuprofen to meloxicam.
For most people, that’s about 6 hours after the last dose of ibuprofen.
Q: Is meloxicam very similar to Celebrex?
Both meloxicam and Celebrex are prescription NSAIDs that treat arthritis pain and swelling.
For most people, meloxicam works about the same.
Celebrex is a more "stomach‑friendly" COX‑2 selective drug.
Meloxicam usually costs less and lasts longer in the body.
Q: Can meloxicam cause drowsiness and headache?
A: Drowsiness isn’t a known side effect of meloxicam.
In fact, some people may have trouble sleeping instead.
Dizziness can occur.
Headache is a fairly common but usually mild side effect.
Q: Is meloxicam a narcotic?
A: No, meloxicam is not a narcotic.
Meloxicam is in a group of drugs called nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs).
Metformin is a first-line treatment used for type 2 diabetes.
It is used in adults and children 10 years and older.
It helps improve blood sugar levels.
Metformin is also used off-label to improve symptoms of polycystic ovarian syndrome (PCOS) and other conditions.
Metformin tablets should be used together with dietary changes and exercise for better results.
Metformin belongs to the drug class biguanides.
Metformin works by reducing glucose absorption from the intestines.
It works by lowering liver glucose production.
It works by improving insulin sensitivity.
Managing blood sugar levels with medications like metformin can prevent diabetes complications.
These complications include kidney damage, nerve issues, blindness, and amputations.
Managing blood sugar levels with medications like metformin can also lower the risk of cardiovascular disease.
Boxed warning for lactic acidosis:
Metformin has a boxed warning for lactic acidosis.
Lactic acidosis is a dangerous build-up of lactic acid in your blood.
Lactic acidosis can cause death, hypothermia, hypotension, and resistant bradyarrhythmias.
A boxed warning is the strongest safety-related warning issued by the FDA.
A boxed warning is an extremely rare side effect.
Certain things can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Kidney problems can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Taking certain other medicines can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Being over 65 can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Having an imaging test that uses contrast dye can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Having surgery or other medical procedures can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Conditions that lower oxygen levels in the body can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Drinking too much alcohol can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Liver problems can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Certain rare inherited metabolic disorders (mitochondrial diseases) can raise your risk of lactic acidosis.
Stop taking metformin and call your healthcare professional immediately if you have any symptoms of lactic acidosis, including if you feel:
very weak and tired
have unusual (not normal) muscle pain
have trouble breathing
have unusual sleepiness or sleep longer than usual
have unexplained stomach or intestinal problems with nausea and vomiting, or diarrhea
cold, especially in your arms and legs
dizzy or lightheaded
have a slow or irregular heartbeat
Other warnings:
Vitamin B12 deficiency: Metformin can lower your vitamin B12 levels over time.
Your doctor may check your blood counts every year.
Your doctor may check your B12 levels every 2 to 3 years.
This is done to catch any problems early.
Low blood sugar is a risk when taken with insulin or other diabetes medicines.
Taking metformin along with insulin or certain other diabetes drugs raises the risk of low blood sugar.
These certain other diabetes drugs are ones that boost insulin release.
If you're on one of these combinations.
Your doctor may lower the dose of the other medicine.
There have been no clinical studies that clearly show metformin reduces the risk of macrovascular disease.
Macrovascular disease includes diseases or conditions that affect the coronary arteries, the aorta, and large arteries in the brain and limbs.

Common side effects of metformin hydrochloride tablets include diarrhea, nausea, vomiting, wind (flatulence), indigestion, and headache.

The patients were in the US clinical trial.
The trial was for using metformin for type 2 diabetic patients.
In the same study, 6% of patients discontinued metformin due to diarrhea.
Less common side effects were abnormal stools, hypoglycemia, myalgia, lightheadedness, dyspnea, nail disorder, rash, increased sweating, taste disorder, chest discomfort, chills, flu syndrome, flushing, and palpitation.
These side effects occurred between 1% and less than 5% of patients.

Immediate-release tablets:
Initial dose: 500 mg orally twice a day.
Dose titration: Increase in 500 mg increments weekly as tolerated.
Daily dose should be taken in divided doses 2 to 3 times a day with meals.
Maximum dose: 2000 mg/day.
Extended-release oral suspension:
Initial dose: 500 mg orally once a day with evening meal.
Dose titration: Increase in 500 mg increments weekly based on glycemic control and tolerability.
Maximum dose: 2000 mg/day.
Renal Impairment:
Before starting treatment, assess renal function with estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR)
Do not use Metformin in patients with eGFR below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m 2
It is not recommended to start metformin tablets in patients with eGFR between 30 to 45 mL/minute/1.73 m 2
While taking Metformin, assess the risk/benefit of continuing if eGFR falls below 45 mL/minute/1.73 m 2
If eGFR falls below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m 2 discontinue Metformin
Metformin immediate release tablets are available as 500 mg, 850 mg, and 1,000 mg tablets.
Hepatic Impairment:
Avoid use in patients with hepatic impairment.

Zonisamide is used for seizures.

Dichlorphenamide is used for a muscle condition called periodic paralysis.
These medicines commonly lower a substance in your blood called bicarbonate.
Your body needs bicarbonate to keep its acid levels balanced.

Some medicines can cause metformin levels to increase.

They do this by blocking the same pathway your kidneys use to clear metformin out of your system.

Drinking alcohol in large amounts or regularly raises the risk of lactic acidosis.

Your doctor may have you restart metformin once your kidney function is confirmed to be back to normal.
Does metformin work for weight loss?
:
Yes.
Metformin can lead to a modest weight loss.
Results vary.
The weight loss from Metformin is generally smaller than what is seen with medications specifically approved for weight loss.
In the landmark Diabetes Prevention Program (DPP), participants taking metformin lost more weight than those on placebo.
The DPP was a randomized controlled trial in people at high risk for diabetes.
Metformin patients lost 2.7% of body weight after one year.
This was compared to 0.43% for placebo.
Metformin patients also lost 2.1% of body weight after two years.
This was compared to essentially no change (0.02%) for placebo.
29% of people on metformin lost at least 5% of their starting body weight after one year.
Just 13% of people on placebo lost at least 5% of their starting body weight after one year.
This gap held up at the two-year mark.
At the two-year mark, 26% of people on metformin lost at least 5% of their starting body weight.
At the two-year mark, 14% of people on placebo lost at least 5% of their starting body weight.
Metformin is not FDA-approved for weight loss.
Metformin is prescribed to manage polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS).
Metformin improves how the body responds to insulin.
PCOS affects about 1 in 10 women of reproductive age.
PCOS is often marked by insulin resistance.
In insulin resistance, the body doesn't respond well to insulin.
This lack of response prompts the pancreas to produce more insulin.
This excess insulin drives up androgen production.
Androgen is a male hormone.
This process worsens symptoms like irregular periods, infertility, acne, and excess hair growth.
Metformin helps by lowering insulin levels.
Metformin helps by decreasing the liver's glucose production.
Metformin helps by improving glucose uptake by muscles.
Metformin helps by reducing circulating androgen levels.
Reducing circulating androgen levels can support more regular ovulation.
Reducing circulating androgen levels can support more regular menstrual cycles.
Metformin is not FDA-approved to treat PCOS.
Metformin is widely prescribed as an off-label treatment.
Metformin is prescribed particularly for those with insulin resistance.
Metformin is prescribed particularly for those with fertility difficulties.
Metformin is backed by extensive research.
Metformin is backed by clinical guidelines.
Metformin helps regulate cycles.
Metformin supports ovulation.
Metformin may help reduce androgen-related symptoms.
Androgen-related symptoms include acne.
Androgen-related symptoms include excess hair growth.
Metformin can aid with weight management.
This weight management aid is for those affected by PCOS-related weight gain.
Q: When is the best time to take metformin?
Metformin should be taken after having a meal or eating food.
This is especially important when you are first starting treatment.
This can help reduce any stomach side effects.
Q: Can you take metformin and Ozempic together?
A: Yes, metformin and Ozempic (semaglutide) are often used together under a healthcare provider’s guidance to manage type 2 diabetes.
Metformin works through a specific mechanism to lower blood sugar.
Ozempic works through a different mechanism to lower blood sugar.
Metformin and Ozempic may also help with weight management.

Metformin has kidney function requirements.
Q: How long does metformin take to work?
Metformin will usually start lowering your blood sugar (glucose) levels in the first week of treatment.
Metformin may take 2 to 3 months to see Metformin's full effect.
Metformin is a preferred initial oral treatment for people with type 2 diabetes.
It is usually taken on a long-term basis.
Metformin may be combined with other blood glucose-lowering treatments, if needed.
Q: What is the safest diabetes medication?
A: Metformin is widely considered the safest first-line medication for type 2 diabetes.
Metformin remains the preferred initial treatment.
This preference is according to the American Diabetes Association (ADA).
Metformin has decades of clinical use.
Metformin has extensive safety data.
However, the safest medication for any individual depends on their specific health profile.
The safest medication also depends on their age.
The safest medication also depends on their kidney function.
The safest medication also depends on their treatment goals.
Q: Why does metformin cause diarrhea?
Metformin causes diarrhea.
Metformin changes gut bacteria.
Metformin affects bile acid absorption.
Metformin blocks water absorption in the intestines.
Metformin increases gut-stimulating hormones.
These combined effects make stools looser and more frequent.
Q: Can you take metformin without food?
If you take metformin without food, you may end up with an upset stomach.
This is especially true if you are just starting treatment.
Nausea and vomiting are some of the most common side effects with metformin.
They can occur in over 30% of patients.
Q: Does metformin lower A1C, if so how much?
Metformin lowers A1C by about 1 to 1.5 percentage points on average.
This occurs when metformin is used alone as a single agent.
This effect is seen at standard therapeutic doses.
A1C is a measure of longer-term blood sugar control.
In most patients, metformin is suggested as the initial treatment for type 2 diabetes.
Metformin's glucose-lowering effect may not be adequate for all patients if used alone.
Q: Does metformin cause weight loss?
Metformin is generally considered weight neutral or modestly weight reducing.
Average losses are around 1–3 kg (2–7 lb) over several months.
This applies to people with type 2 diabetes or obesity.
Many people lose little or no weight.
The effect varies widely between individuals.

Q: What is metilfenidato used for?
Metilfenidato is the Spanish, Italian, and Portuguese translation for methylphenidate.
Methylphenidate may be used to treat children or adults with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD).
It is used to help with hyperactivity and impulsive behavior.
It allows children or adults to concentrate better.
Metilfenidato may also be used to treat adults with narcolepsy.
Metilfenidato translates to methylphenidate.
Metoprolol is a beta-blocker medication.
Metoprolol is used to treat high blood pressure.
Metoprolol is used to treat chest pain (angina).
Metoprolol is used to treat heart failure.
Metoprolol is used to improve survival after a heart attack.
Metoprolol works mainly on the heart.
Metoprolol is called a cardioselective beta-blocker.
Metoprolol works by blocking the effect of adrenaline and noradrenaline on beta-1 receptors in your heart.
This slows your heart rate.
It reduces how hard your heart squeezes.
It lowers your blood pressure.
Because the heart is doing less work, Metoprolol needs less oxygen.
This is why metoprolol also helps with angina.
Exactly how metoprolol works is not fully understood.
It works for high blood pressure.
It works for heart failure.
It works after a heart attack.
At higher blood levels, metoprolol also blocks beta-2 receptors in the airways and blood vessels.
Metoprolol still needs care in people with breathing problems.
This is because metoprolol blocks beta-2 receptors in the airways and blood vessels at higher blood levels.
Metoprolol comes as two different salts, metoprolol tartrate and metoprolol succinate.
Metoprolol tartrate and metoprolol succinate are not interchangeable dose for dose:
Metoprolol tartrate, an immediate-release tablet usually taken once or twice a day (brand name Lopressor)
Metoprolol succinate, an extended-release tablet or capsule taken once a day (brand names Toprol-XL and Kapspargo Sprinkle)
Metoprolol tartrate is also given as an injection into a vein in a hospital during a heart attack.
Metoprolol is FDA-approved to treat high blood pressure, angina, heart failure, and heart attack.
The two forms are not approved for the same conditions.
Condition | Metoprolol tartrate (immediate release) | Metoprolol succinate (extended release)
High blood pressure (hypertension) in adults | Yes | Yes
High blood pressure in children 6 years and older | No | Yes
Angina (chest pain) | Yes | Yes
Heart failure | No | Yes
Heart attack (myocardial infarction) | Yes | No
High blood pressure (hypertension).
Lowering blood pressure reduces the risk of fatal and non-fatal heart and blood vessel events.
These events mainly include strokes and heart attacks.
Metoprolol can be used on its own or with other blood pressure medicines.
Many people need more than one medicine to reach their blood pressure goal.
Angina.
Metoprolol is used for long-term treatment of angina.
It is used to reduce how often attacks happen.
It is used to help you do more before chest pain starts.
Metoprolol is not used to stop an attack that has already begun.
Heart failure.
Only the extended-release form is approved for heart failure.
Metoprolol is used to reduce the risk of dying from heart and blood vessel causes.
It is used to reduce hospital admissions for heart failure.
Heart attack (myocardial infarction).
Metoprolol tartrate is used in people whose blood pressure and circulation are stable after a definite or suspected heart attack.
It is used to reduce the risk of dying from heart and blood vessel causes.
In hospital, Metoprolol may be started as an injection into a vein and then continued as tablets.
The two forms release the same medicine at different speeds.
The two forms are approved for different conditions.
Metoprolol tartrate is released all at once.
Metoprolol tartrate acts sooner and wears off sooner.
Metoprolol succinate is released slowly over the day.
One dose lasts about 24 hours.
Only the extended-release form is approved for heart failure and for children.
Only the immediate-release form is approved for use after a heart attack.

If you switch from immediate-release metoprolol to the extended-release form.
The total amount you take in a day stays the same.
Your prescriber makes this change, not you.
The most common side effects of metoprolol are tiredness, dizziness, low mood (depression), diarrhea, shortness of breath, a slow heartbeat (bradycardia), and itching or rash.
Most side effects reported in studies were mild and did not last.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to metoprolol.
These signs include hives, difficulty breathing, or swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Common side effects of metoprolol:
In studies of people taking metoprolol for high blood pressure or angina:
Tiredness and dizziness occurred in about 10% of people
Low mood or depression was reported in about 5 out of 100 people
Diarrhea occurred in about 5% of people
Itching or rash occurred in about 5% of people
Shortness of breath and a slow heartbeat occurred in about 3% of people
Cold hands and feet, palpitations, swelling of the ankles, and low blood pressure were each reported in about 1% of people
Nausea, dry mouth, stomach pain, constipation, gas, and heartburn were each reported in about 1% of people
Headache, nightmares, trouble sleeping, confusion, and short-term memory loss have also been reported.
In the heart failure study of extended-release metoprolol, dizziness or vertigo was reported by 1.8% of people taking metoprolol compared with 1.0% taking placebo.
A slow heartbeat was reported by 1.5% of people taking metoprolol compared with 0.4% taking placebo.
Other reported side effects:
Side effects have been reported since metoprolol came on the market.
Side effects's frequency is not known:
Hair thinning or hair loss, which reversed after stopping
Reduced sex drive and difficulty getting an erection
Dry eyes, blurred vision, and ringing in the ears
Sweating, sensitivity to sunlight, and worsening of psoriasis
Change in taste, joint and muscle pain, and fainting
Liver inflammation (hepatitis)
A severe drop in white blood cells (agranulocytosis), which can show up as fever, chills, or a sore throat
Serious side effects of metoprolol:
Some of the side effects are also listed as common side effects above.
These side effects include shortness of breath and a slow heartbeat.
Side effects need urgent attention when side effects are severe, new, or getting worse.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
A very slow heartbeat, pauses in your heartbeat, or a feeling that you might faint.
Sinus pause, heart block, and cardiac arrest have all been reported.
Signs that heart failure is getting worse, such as weight gain over a few days, more shortness of breath, or swelling of your feet or lower legs
Wheezing or trouble breathing
Chest pain that is worse or more frequent than usual, especially if you have recently stopped or missed doses
Cold, pale, or blue fingers and toes, or pain in your legs when you walk
Confusion, hallucinations, or memory problems
Yellowing of your skin or eyes, which can be a sign of liver inflammation
This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
The most important warning with metoprolol is not to stop Metoprolol suddenly.
Stopping a beta blocker abruptly can bring on worse chest pain.
In some cases, it can bring on a heart attack.
If your treatment needs to stop, your prescriber will lower the dose gradually over 1 to 2 weeks.
Your prescriber will watch how you respond.
This applies even if you only take metoprolol for blood pressure.
Heart disease is common.
Heart disease is not always known about.
Talk to your care team before starting metoprolol if any of these apply to you.
Asthma or other breathing problems.
Beta blockers can tighten the airways.
Metoprolol is used in people with these conditions only when other treatments are not suitable.
It is used at the lowest possible dose.
A reliever inhaler is kept on hand.
Heart failure.
Heart failure can get worse while the dose is being increased.
Tell your doctor about weight gain or increasing breathlessness.
A slow heart rate or a conduction problem in the heart.
This includes first-degree heart block, sinus node problems, and Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome.
Your heart rate should be monitored.
Diabetes or long gaps without food.
Metoprolol can hide the early warning signs of low blood sugar, such as a fast heartbeat.
Metoprolol can make low blood sugar more severe or last longer.
The risk is higher if you are fasting, not eating regularly, or vomiting.
An overactive thyroid.
Metoprolol can mask signs of an overactive thyroid.
Stopping it suddenly can trigger a thyroid crisis.
Circulation problems in your arms or legs.
Beta blockers can make symptoms of poor circulation worse.
A history of severe allergic reactions.
If you have anaphylaxis while taking a beta blocker.
The usual dose of injectable epinephrine may not work as well.
Pheochromocytoma (a tumor of the adrenal gland).
Metoprolol must only be started after an alpha blocker has been started.
Otherwise, blood pressure can rise sharply.
Liver disease.
Metoprolol is broken down by the liver, so levels can build up.
Treatment is usually started at a lower dose.
Surgery.
Tell your surgeon and dentist that you take metoprolol.
Long-term beta blocker treatment is not usually stopped before surgery.
Starting a high dose of extended-release metoprolol shortly before non-cardiac surgery is avoided.
This practice is avoided because in one large trial it was linked to adverse outcomes.
The adverse outcomes included more slow heartbeats, low blood pressure, strokes, and deaths.
You should not take metoprolol if you have a very slow heart rate.
You should not take metoprolol if you have heart block beyond the first degree.
You should not take metoprolol if you have sick sinus syndrome without a pacemaker.
You should not take metoprolol if you have cardiogenic shock.
You should not take metoprolol if you have heart failure that is not under control.
Metoprolol is not for you if you have:
Severe bradycardia (a very slow heart rate)
Second-degree or third-degree heart block, which is a fault in the heart's electrical signal
Sick sinus syndrome, unless you have a permanent pacemaker
Cardiogenic shock, when the heart cannot pump enough blood
Decompensated heart failure, meaning heart failure that is not stable
An allergy to metoprolol or to any of the other ingredients in your product
Immediate-release metoprolol tartrate tablets should not be used.
This applies if your top blood pressure reading is below 100 mmHg.
Tell your doctor or pharmacist if you:
Take any other medicine for your heart or blood pressure, especially digoxin, clonidine, diltiazem, or verapamil
Take fluoxetine, paroxetine, quinidine, or propafenone, which raise metoprolol levels in your blood
Are pregnant, planning a pregnancy, or breastfeeding
Have liver problems
Have ever had a severe allergic reaction to anything, including foods and insect stings
Kidney problems do not usually change your dose.
The amount of metoprolol in your body is not meaningfully different in people with kidney failure.
How long metoprolol lasts is not meaningfully different in people with kidney failure.
Take metoprolol at the same time each day, exactly as prescribed.
Do not change your dose or stop taking metoprolol without talking to your prescriber first.
Immediate-release metoprolol tartrate tablets should be taken with a meal or immediately after a meal.
Extended-release tablets and capsules are preferred to be taken with or just after a meal.
Food does not have a significant effect on how much metoprolol your body absorbs.
Keep taking metoprolol even if you feel well.
High blood pressure often causes no symptoms.
Extended-release tablets are scored and can be split in half if your prescriber tells you to.
Swallow the whole or half tablet without crushing or chewing it.
Extended-release capsules (Kapspargo Sprinkle) should be swallowed whole.
If you cannot swallow the capsule, you can open it.
You can sprinkle the contents over a teaspoon of soft food.
Examples of soft food include applesauce, pudding, or yogurt.
Swallow the mixture within 60 minutes and do not save any for later.
Tell your doctor if you are fasting or vomiting, because this raises your risk of low blood sugar.
Your blood pressure and heart rate will need checking regularly.
Store metoprolol at room temperature, between 68°F and 77°F (20°C to 25°C), away from moisture and heat.
Metoprolol injection is given into a vein by a healthcare professional.
This is done in a coronary care unit or similar setting.
In this setting, your blood pressure, heart rate, and heart tracing can be watched.
You are then switched to metoprolol tablets.
The usual starting dose is 25 mg to 100 mg once a day for extended-release metoprolol.
The usual starting dose is 100 mg a day for immediate-release metoprolol.
Your dose is then adjusted at intervals of a week or longer.
This continues until your blood pressure or symptoms are controlled.
Metoprolol tartrate (immediate-release tablets) dosage for adults:
Condition | Starting dose | Usual range
High blood pressure | 100 mg a day, as one dose or split into two | 100 mg to 450 mg a day.
Doses above 450 mg a day have not been studied.
Angina | 100 mg a day, split into two doses | 100 mg to 400 mg a day.
Doses above 400 mg a day have not been studied.
After a heart attack | 50 mg every 6 hours for 48 hours, starting 15 minutes after the last dose given into a vein.

25 mg is used.
| Maintenance is 100 mg twice a day, continued for at least 3 months.
Immediate-release tablets are used.
A once-daily dose may not keep blood pressure controlled for the full 24 hours.
This lack of control is especially true at a 100 mg dose.
Splitting the dose may be needed.
Taking a larger daily dose may be needed.
Metoprolol succinate (extended-release tablets and capsules) dosage for adults:
Condition | Starting dose | Usual range
High blood pressure | 25 mg to 100 mg once a day | Adjust weekly or less often.
Doses above 400 mg a day have not been studied.
Angina | 100 mg once a day | Increase weekly until symptoms are controlled or your heart rate slows noticeably.
Doses above 400 mg a day have not been studied.
Heart failure is treated with 25 mg once a day for two weeks, or 12.5 mg once a day in more severe heart failure.
The dose should be doubled every two weeks to the highest dose you tolerate, up to 200 mg once a day.
Kapspargo Sprinkle capsules are not suitable to start heart failure treatment if a dose below 25 mg is needed.
Metoprolol dosage for children:
Extended-release metoprolol is the only form approved for children.
It is approved only for high blood pressure in children aged 6 years and older.
The starting dose is 1 mg for each kilogram of body weight once a day.
This is up to a maximum first dose of 50 mg.
Doses above 2 mg/kg have not been studied.
Doses above 200 mg a day have not been studied.
Extended-release metoprolol has not been studied in children under 6 years.
Dose adjustments:
Liver problems.
Start at a lower dose than usual and increase slowly.
Kidney problems.
No dose reduction is needed.
Older adults.
A low starting dose is generally used.
This is because liver, kidney, and heart function are more often reduced.
Other medicines are more often involved.
Switching between forms.
When changing from immediate-release to extended-release metoprolol, the same total daily dose is used.
Skip the missed dose and take your next dose at the usual time.
Do not take two doses at once and do not double up to catch up.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Too much metoprolol can cause a dangerously slow heartbeat, very low blood pressure, and cardiogenic shock.
Metoprolol can also cause heart block.
It can cause heart failure.
It can cause wheezing.
It can cause low oxygen levels.
It can cause drowsiness or loss of consciousness.
It can cause nausea.
It can cause vomiting.
Overdose is treated in an intensive care setting.
Dialysis does not remove metoprolol effectively.
Avoid driving and other tasks that need alertness until you know how metoprolol affects you.
It can make you feel tired or less alert.
Get up slowly from sitting or lying down, because metoprolol can make you feel dizzy or lightheaded.
Avoid alcohol if you take Kapspargo Sprinkle capsules.
Alcohol makes the capsule release metoprolol much faster.
This raises the risk of side effects.
Do not stop taking metoprolol on your own, even if you feel fine.
The interactions that matter most are medicines that also slow the heart.
The interactions that matter most are medicines that block the liver enzyme CYP2D6.
This enzyme raises metoprolol levels in your blood.
Digoxin, clonidine, diltiazem, and verapamil.
These all slow the heart's electrical signal and heart rate.
Taking Digoxin, clonidine, diltiazem, and verapamil with metoprolol raises the risk of a very slow heartbeat.
Clonidine, when stopping either medicine.
You take both medications.
Metoprolol is stopped several days before clonidine is slowly withdrawn.
Beta blockers can worsen the blood pressure rebound that follows clonidine withdrawal.
Clonidine withdrawal causes a blood pressure rebound.
Quinidine, fluoxetine, paroxetine, and propafenone.
These strong CYP2D6 blockers double the amount of metoprolol in your blood.
Quinidine tripled levels of one form of metoprolol and doubled how long metoprolol lasted.
Weaker CYP2D6 blockers are likely to raise levels too.
Reserpine and MAO inhibitors.
These deplete adrenaline-type chemicals.
These can add to metoprolol's effect.
This can cause low blood pressure or a marked slowing of the heart.
Epinephrine.
If you need epinephrine for a severe allergic reaction, the usual dose may not work as well.
This reduced effectiveness occurs while you are on a beta blocker.
Other medicines that lower blood pressure or heart rate can add to metoprolol's effect.
Fingolimod is one of these medicines.
Fingolimod lowers blood pressure or heart rate.
Tell each of your healthcare providers about all medicines you take, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Not all possible interactions are listed here.
Metoprolol crosses the placenta and passes into breast milk in small amounts.
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant, planning a pregnancy, or breastfeeding.
Studies published to date have not shown a link between metoprolol taken in pregnancy and major birth defects or miscarriage.
Reports on growth restriction, early birth, and death around the time of birth have been inconsistent.
Those studies have limitations that make studies hard to interpret.
Untreated high blood pressure and untreated heart failure in pregnancy carry their own serious risks for both mother and baby.
Babies born to mothers taking metoprolol may have low blood pressure, low blood sugar, a slow heartbeat, or slow breathing.
Babies born to mothers taking metoprolol are watched after birth.
The amount of metoprolol a breastfed baby receives is estimated at 0.05 mg to under 1 mg a day.
This amount is about 0.5% to 2% of the mother's weight-adjusted dose.
No side effects in breastfed babies have been identified.
Babies are still monitored for a slow heartbeat and for signs such as unusual sleepiness.
Q: Does metoprolol cause weight gain?
A: Metoprolol is not likely to cause weight gain.
However, weight gain can be a warning sign that heart failure is getting worse.
If you are taking metoprolol for heart failure, tell your doctor right away if you gain weight suddenly.
This weight gain is due to extra fluid in the body.
The extra fluid may cause swelling in the feet, ankles, legs and belly.
The fluid builds up because the heart isn’t working as well as heart should.
Q: Does metoprolol lower blood pressure?
Metoprolol causes the blood vessels to relax.
This helps to lower blood pressure.
If you are prescribed metoprolol for high blood pressure, your doctor may ask you to check your blood pressure regularly at home.
You can use an automatic, cuff-style device to check your blood pressure.
The device goes around your upper arm.
Keeping track of your blood pressure at home is one way to help your doctor.
This helps the doctor know if the prescribed dosage of metoprolol is working for you.
The prescribed dosage of metoprolol is intended to work for you.
Q: Should I take metoprolol in the morning or at night?
A: When you take metoprolol depends on which salt form is prescribed.
Metoprolol tartrate is usually taken twice daily.
Metoprolol tartrate may be taken in the morning then at night.
Metoprolol succinate is taken once daily.
Metoprolol succinate may be taken either morning or night.
Both forms are usually taken with or immediately after a meal.
Follow your health care provider’s directions for when to take metoprolol.
Q: What is the best time of day to take blood pressure medication?
Research has suggested that taking your blood pressure medication at night instead of in the morning can provide better blood pressure control at night.
This approach does not compromise daytime blood pressure control.
It can reduce your overall risk of dying because of cardiovascular disease by 45%.
Q: Does metoprolol lower resting heart rate?
A: Yes, metoprolol can lower resting heart rate.
Metoprolol is a beta-blocking medication.
Metoprolol is used to lower blood pressure.
Metoprolol is used to relieve chest pain from angina pectoris.
Q: Metoprolol Succinate vs Tartrate: What's the difference between them?
A: Metoprolol is available as two different salts: metoprolol tartrate and metoprolol succinate.
The main difference between metoprolol tartrate and metoprolol succinate is that metoprolol tartrate is only available as an immediate-release tablet.
Metoprolol tartrate must be taken several times per day.
Metoprolol succinate is an extended-release tablet.
Metoprolol succinate can be taken once a day.
As a result, there are differences in the dosages and indications for both metoprolol tartrate and metoprolol succinate.
Metoprolol tartrate and metoprolol succinate are not considered interchangeable.
Q: What medications are known to cause hair loss?
A: Medication-induced hair loss represents a significant concern for patients across multiple therapeutic categories.
Chemotherapy drugs are most commonly associated with severe hair loss.
Many commonly prescribed medications can also cause this distressing side effect.
These medications include antibiotics, psychiatric drugs, cardiovascular agents, and hormonal therapies.
Q: Does metoprolol make you sleepy?
A: Yes, metoprolol can make you sleepy.
One reason may be because beta blockers slow down your heart rate and lower your blood pressure.
Q: How long does it take for metoprolol to work?
A: How long metoprolol takes metoprolol to work depends on what form is given and what condition is being treated.
Effects on heart rate can be seen within one hour after taking metoprolol tartrate tablets.
Effects on heart rate can be seen within 20 minutes after the intravenous form is given.
The onset of action of metoprolol succinate is similar to metoprolol tartrate.

Q: Why is montelukast usually taken at night?
Montelukast is usually taken in the evening.
Montelukast was studied in clinical trials.
Montelukast was proven to be effective in clinical trials.
Most clinical trials and prescribing information have traditionally used once-daily evening dosing for asthma.
This is why this schedule is commonly recommended.
Q: What is the mechanism of action of montelukast?
Montelukast works by blocking leukotriene D4 and related cysteinyl leukotrienes.
These substances bind to the CysLT1 receptor.
The CysLT1 receptor is located on airway and nasal tissues.
Montelukast reduces inflammation, swelling, and allergy or asthma symptoms.
Q: Is montelukast an antihistamine?
A: Montelukast is not an antihistamine; Montelukast belongs to a different drug class.
Montelukast is a leukotriene receptor antagonist (LTRA).
LTRA is a type of medicine.
This medicine blocks the effects of leukotrienes.
Leukotrienes are chemicals that contribute to inflammation in asthma and some allergy conditions.
Q: Is Montelukast used for allergies?
A: Montelukast is approved to treat allergy-related nasal symptoms in people with seasonal and perennial allergic rhinitis.
Major guidelines recommend reserving montelukast for specific patients.
These patients are those who do not respond to other standard allergy treatments.
These patients are also those who cannot tolerate other standard allergy treatments.
This recommendation exists because of a boxed warning.
The warning concerns potential neuropsychiatric side effects.

Omeprazole is used to treat excess stomach acid.
It is used in conditions such as stomach ulcers, heartburn, or gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD).
It is used in active duodenal ulcer, Zollinger-Ellison syndrome, and erosive esophagitis.
Omeprazole may be given together with antibiotics.

The ulcers are caused by infection with Helicobacter pylori (H. pylori).
The treatment is for adult patients.
Omeprazole is a proton pump inhibitor (PPI).
Omeprazole works by blocking the acid pump in your stomach.
This action reduces how much acid Omeprazole makes.
This gives irritated or damaged tissue a chance to heal.
Omeprazole brand names are Prilosec, Prilosec OTC.
Generic brands are available.

frequent heartburn that occurs 2 or more days a week in adults 18 years and older
Omeprazole is taken as a 14-day course, once a day for 14 days in a row.
It may take 1 to 4 days to reach its full effect.
Omeprazole is not used for immediate relief of symptoms.

Do not start another 14-day course within 4 months.
Do this unless your doctor tells you to.

active duodenal ulcer (a sore in the first part of the small intestine) in adults

active benign gastric ulcer (a non-cancerous sore in the stomach lining) in adults
symptoms of gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD), such as heartburn, in adults and children 1 year of age and older
erosive esophagitis (EE) caused by acid reflux in adults and children 1 month of age and older
keeping erosive esophagitis healed after treatment, in adults and children 1 year of age and older
pathological hypersecretory conditions (conditions in which the stomach makes too much acid) in adults, including Zollinger-Ellison syndrome

This is not the same as when your heartburn or acid reflux symptoms stop.
Omeprazole does not work immediately on symptoms you are already feeling.
It is a proton pump inhibitor (PPI).
Omeprazole reduces how much acid your stomach makes.
Omeprazole does not neutralize acid that is already there.
Take the medicine as soon as you can.
Skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose.
Do not take two doses at one time.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Omeprazole can cause diarrhea, which may be a sign of a new infection.
If you have diarrhea that is watery or bloody, call your doctor before using anti-diarrhea medicine.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to omeprazole.
Signs of an allergic reaction to omeprazole include hives.
Signs of an allergic reaction to omeprazole include difficulty breathing.
Signs of an allergic reaction to omeprazole include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Stop using Omeprazole and call your doctor at once if you have:
severe stomach pain, diarrhea that is watery or bloody;
new or unusual pain in your wrist, thigh, hip, or back;
seizure (convulsions);
kidney problems - fever, rash, nausea, loss of appetite, joint pain, urinating less than usual, blood in your urine, weight gain;
low magnesium - seizures, dizziness, irregular heartbeats, feeling jittery, muscle aches or weakness, muscle cramps, muscle spasms, or spasms of the hands, feet, or voice;
new or worsening symptoms of lupus - joint pain, and a skin rash on your cheeks or arms that worsens in sunlight.
severe skin reactions - skin rash with blistering, peeling, or bleeding, fever, chills, body aches, shortness of breath, or enlarged lymph nodes.
Taking omeprazole long-term may cause you to develop stomach growths called fundic gland polyps.
Talk with your doctor about this risk.
If you use this medicine for longer than 3 years, you could develop a vitamin B-12 deficiency.
Talk to your doctor if you develop symptoms.
These symptoms include shortness of breath, lightheadedness, irregular heartbeat, muscle weakness, pale skin, feeling tired, mood changes, and tingling or numbness in the arms and legs.
Common side effects of omeprazole may include:
cold symptoms such as stuffy nose, sneezing, sore throat (especially in children);
fever (especially in children);
stomach pain, gas;
nausea, vomiting, diarrhea; or
headache.
This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Sometimes it is not safe to use certain medications at the same time.
Some drugs can affect your blood levels of other drugs you take.
This may increase side effects.
This may make the medications less effective.
Tell your doctor about all your current medicines.
Many drugs can affect omeprazole, especially:
digoxin;
clopidogrel;
methotrexate;
St. John's wort;
a diuretic or "water pill"; or
an antibiotic - amoxicillin, clarithromycin, rifampin.
This list is not complete and many other drugs may interact with omeprazole.
List includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.
Q: What foods should I avoid when taking omeprazole?
There are no specific foods that interact with omeprazole.
Therefore, there are no foods that you need to avoid.
Q: Pantoprazole vs. omeprazole: What's the difference between them?
Pantoprazole and omeprazole are both proton pump inhibitors (PPIs).
These PPIs reduce stomach acid.
They show similar effectiveness for healing acid‑related conditions.
An example of such a condition is erosive esophagitis.
Pantoprazole and omeprazole's main differences relate to FDA‑approved uses.
The differences also relate to availability.
Omeprazole is OTC.
Pantoprazole is prescription.
The differences also relate to timing with food.
The differences also relate to the option for intravenous (IV) pantoprazole in the hospital setting.
Q: How soon after taking levothyroxine can I take omeprazole?
There is a known drug interaction between levothyroxine and omeprazole.
The timing of when you take the two medicines is not relevant.
Omeprazole decreases the absorption of your thyroid medication.
If you need to be on a course of omeprazole while taking levothyroxine.
Your doctor may order more frequent laboratory testing.
The testing is to confirm you are absorbing enough levothyroxine.
Q: Should I take omeprazole with or without food?
A: Omeprazole is usually best to take Omeprazole 1 hour before meals.
Omeprazole reduces the amount of omeprazole that reaches the bloodstream.
This occurs when omeprazole is taken with food.
Q: Can you take an antacid with omeprazole?
You can take an antacid with omeprazole.
You can do this if you are still getting symptoms of indigestion.
This is because omeprazole can take several days to start working.
Take omeprazole as directed.
It is usually taken once daily on an empty stomach.
Take antacids as needed.
This is done to relieve indigestion pain after eating.
But tell your doctor if several weeks have gone past and omeprazole does not seem to be working or you are still taking antacids.
Q: Is famotidine safer than omeprazole for heartburn?
For most people with mild, occasional heartburn, famotidine is usually the safer first choice.
Famotidine works quickly.
Famotidine can be taken as needed.
Famotidine has fewer long-term safety concerns than omeprazole.
Omeprazole is a stronger acid reducer.
Omeprazole may be a better option if you have frequent heartburn two or more days per week.
Omeprazole may be a better option if you have confirmed GERD.
Omeprazole is linked with more potential risks when used for many months.
Omeprazole is linked with more potential risks when used at high doses.
Q: Can I take omeprazole in the morning and famotidine at night?
You could take omeprazole in the morning and famotidine at night.
There are not many studies investigating this approach.
Preliminary results suggest that taking your medication this way may provide superior control of gastric acid secretion at night.
This method does not compromise the effectiveness of omeprazole in the morning.
More research is needed.
Q: Is omeprazole (Prilosec) bad for your kidneys?
Omeprazole (Prilosec) is a proton pump inhibitor (PPI) that is used to reduce the amount of acid in your stomach.
Omeprazole can be bad for your kidneys.
Omeprazole can cause acute kidney injury.
Omeprazole may also worsen the progression of chronic kidney disease (CKD).
Q: Does omeprazole cause cancer?
Long-term use of proton pump inhibitor (PPI) acid-suppressing agents has been associated with an increased risk of stomach cancer.
Stomach cancer is also called gastric cancer.
Omeprazole is an example of a proton pump inhibitor (PPI) acid-suppressing agent.
This is based on the results of several observational studies.
It is thought to be because PPIs such as omeprazole are potent gastric acid suppressants.
These suppressants may increase the risk of gastric cancer.
They may cause atrophy (thinning) of the stomach lining.
They may elevate levels of a hormone called gastrin.
They may cause an overgrowth of bacteria in the stomach.
Ondansetron blocks the actions of chemicals in the body that can trigger nausea and vomiting.
Ondansetron is used to prevent nausea and vomiting.
Nausea and vomiting may be caused by surgery, cancer chemotherapy, or radiation treatment.
Ondansetron may be used for purposes not listed in this medication guide.
You should not use ondansetron if you are also using apomorphine (Apokyn).
You should not use ondansetron if you are allergic to ondansetron.
You should not use ondansetron if you are allergic to similar medicines.
Similar medicines include dolasetron (Anzemet), granisetron (Kytril), or palonosetron (Aloxi).
Before taking ondansetron, tell your doctor if you have liver disease, or a personal or family history of Long QT syndrome.
Ondansetron orally disintegrating tablets may contain phenylalanine.
Tell your doctor if you have phenylketonuria (PKU).
Serious side effects of ondansetron include blurred vision or temporary vision loss.
The vision loss lasts from only a few minutes to several hours.
Serious side effects of ondansetron include slow heart rate.
Serious side effects of ondansetron include trouble breathing.
Serious side effects of ondansetron include anxiety.
Serious side effects of ondansetron include agitation.
Serious side effects of ondansetron include shivering.
Serious side effects of ondansetron include feeling like you might pass out.
Serious side effects of ondansetron include urinating less than usual or not at all.
Stop taking ondansetron.
Call your doctor at once if you have any of these side effects.
Ondansetron may impair your thinking or reactions.
Be careful if you drive or do anything that requires you to be alert.
You should not use ondansetron if:
you are also using apomorphine (Apokyn); or
you are allergic to ondansetron or similar medicines (dolasetron, granisetron, palonosetron).
To make sure ondansetron is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have:
liver disease;
an electrolyte imbalance (such as low levels of potassium or magnesium in your blood);
congestive heart failure, slow heartbeats;
a personal or family history of long QT syndrome; or
a blockage in your digestive tract (stomach or intestines).
Ondansetron is not expected to harm an unborn baby.
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant.
It is not known whether ondansetron passes into breast milk.
It is not known if ondansetron could harm a nursing baby.
Tell your doctor if you are breast-feeding a baby.
Ondansetron is not approved for use by anyone younger than 1 month old.
Ondansetron orally disintegrating tablets may contain phenylalanine.
Tell your doctor if you have phenylketonuria (PKU).
Take ondansetron exactly as prescribed by your doctor.
Follow all directions on your prescription label.
Do not take ondansetron in larger or smaller amounts or for longer than recommended.
Ondansetron can be taken with or without food.
The first dose is usually taken before the start of your surgery, chemotherapy, or radiation treatment.
Follow your doctor's dosing instructions very carefully.
Take the ondansetron regular tablet with a full glass of water.
To take the orally disintegrating tablet (Zofran ODT):
Keep the tablet in its blister pack until you are ready to take it.
Open the package and peel back the foil.
Do not push a tablet through the foil or you may damage the tablet.
Use dry hands to remove the tablet and place it in your mouth.
Do not swallow the tablet whole.
Allow it to dissolve in your mouth without chewing.
Swallow several times as the tablet dissolves.
To use ondansetron oral soluble film (strip) (Zuplenz):
Keep the strip in the foil pouch until you are ready to use the medicine.
Using dry hands, remove the strip and place strip on your tongue.
Strip will begin to dissolve right away.
Do not swallow the strip whole.
Allow strip to dissolve in your mouth without chewing.
Swallow several times after the strip dissolves.
If desired, you may drink liquid to help swallow the dissolved strip.
Wash your hands after using Zuplenz.
Measure liquid medicine with the dosing syringe provided, or with a special dose-measuring spoon or medicine cup.
If you do not have a dose-measuring device, ask your pharmacist for one.
Store at room temperature away from moisture, heat, and light.
Store liquid medicine in an upright position.
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember.
Skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next scheduled dose.
Do not take extra medicine to make up the missed dose.

These signs also include fever and chills.
These signs also include difficult breathing.
These signs also include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.

severe constipation, stomach pain, or bloating;

Usual Adult Dose of Ondansetron for Nausea/Vomiting -- Chemotherapy Induced:
Oral administration is recommended for Highly Emetogenic Cancer Chemotherapy (HEC).
The recommended oral dose for HEC is 24 mg.
This dose should be taken 30 minutes before the start of single-day HEC.
This recommendation includes cisplatin doses of 50 mg/m2 or greater.
Oral administration is recommended for Moderately Emetogenic Cancer Chemotherapy (MEC).
The recommended oral dose for MEC is 8 mg.
The first dose of 8 mg should be taken twice a day.
The first dose is administered 30 minutes before the start of chemotherapy.
The subsequent dose is administered 8 hours later.
After the initial doses, 8 mg is taken orally 2 times a day.
These subsequent doses are given every 12 hours.
This regimen continues for 1 to 2 days after the completion of chemotherapy.
Parenteral administration is recommended.
The recommended parenteral dose is 0.15 mg/kg IV.
The first dose is infused over 15 minutes.
The first dose is given 30 minutes before the start of emetogenic chemotherapy.
Subsequent doses are given 4 and 8 hours after the first dose.
The maximum dose is 16 mg per dose.
Multi-day, single-dose administration of 24 mg orally for HEC has not been studied.
-The injection formulation should be diluted prior to IV administration.
Uses: -Prevention of nausea and vomiting associated with HEC or MEC -Prevention of nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of emetogenic chemotherapy

Oral administration is recommended for Highly Emetogenic Cancer Chemotherapy (HEC).
The recommended oral dose for HEC is 24 mg.
This dose should be taken 30 minutes before the start of single-day HEC.
This recommendation includes cisplatin doses of 50 mg/m2 or greater.

The recommended oral dose for MEC is 8 mg.

The first dose is administered 30 minutes before the start of chemotherapy.
The subsequent dose is administered 8 hours later.

These subsequent doses are given every 12 hours.
This regimen continues for 1 to 2 days after the completion of chemotherapy.
