Usual Adult Dose of Ondansetron for Nausea/Vomiting -- Postoperative:
The recommended oral dose is 16 mg.
The oral dose should be taken 1 hour before the induction of anesthesia.
The recommended parenteral dose is 4 mg IV.
The IV dose should be undiluted.
The IV dose should be given immediately before induction of anesthesia.
The IV dose can also be given postoperatively.
Postoperative administration is for nausea and/or vomiting within 2 hours after surgery.
An alternative route is 4 mg IM.
The IM dose should be undiluted.
Administration of a second dose does not provide additional control of nausea and vomiting.
Use: -Prevention of postoperative nausea and vomiting
Usual Adult Dose for Nausea/Vomiting--Radiation Induced:
The recommended dose is 8 mg orally 3 times a day.
For total body irradiation, the dose is 8 mg orally 1 to 2 hours before each fraction of radiotherapy administered each day.
For single high-dose fraction radiotherapy to the abdomen, the dose is 8 mg orally 1 to 2 hours before radiotherapy.
For single high-dose fraction radiotherapy to the abdomen, subsequent doses are 8 mg orally every 8 hours after the first dose for 1 to 2 days after the completion of radiotherapy.
For daily fractionated radiotherapy to the abdomen, the dose is 8 mg orally 1 to 2 hours before radiotherapy.
For daily fractionated radiotherapy to the abdomen, subsequent doses are 8 mg orally every 8 hours after the first dose for each day radiotherapy is given.
The use of this medication is for the prevention of nausea and vomiting associated with radiotherapy.
This prevention applies to total body irradiation.
This prevention applies to single high-dose fraction radiotherapy.
This prevention applies to daily fractions to the abdomen.
Usual Pediatric Dose for Nausea/Vomiting -- Postoperative:
Parenteral administration is recommended for patients aged 1 month to 12 years weighing less than 40 kg.
The recommended dose for this group is 0.1 mg/kg IV over 2 to 5 minutes.
This dose is administered immediately prior to or following anesthesia induction.
This dose is also administered postoperatively if nausea and/or vomiting occur shortly after surgery.
Parenteral administration is recommended for patients weighing 40 kg and greater.
The recommended dose for this group is 4 mg IV over 2 to 5 minutes.
This dose is administered immediately prior to or following anesthesia induction.
This dose is also administered postoperatively if nausea and/or vomiting occur shortly after surgery.
The use of this medication is for the prevention of postoperative nausea and vomiting.
Usual Pediatric Dose for Nausea/Vomiting -- Chemotherapy Induced:
The recommended oral dose for children aged 4 to 11 years is 4 mg taken three times a day.
The first oral dose for this age group is administered 30 minutes before the start of chemotherapy.
Subsequent oral doses for this age group are given 4 and 8 hours after the first dose.
After the initial doses, the oral regimen continues as 4 mg three times a day every 8 hours.
This continued oral dosing lasts for 1 to 2 days after the completion of chemotherapy.
The recommended oral dose for individuals 12 years and older is 8 mg taken twice a day.
The first oral dose for this age group is administered 30 minutes before the start of chemotherapy.
The subsequent oral dose for this age group is given 8 hours later.
After the initial doses, the oral regimen continues as 8 mg twice a day every 12 hours.
This continued oral dosing lasts for 1 to 2 days after the completion of chemotherapy.
The recommended parenteral dose for children aged 6 months to 18 years is 0.15 mg/kg given intravenously.
The first parenteral dose is infused over 15 minutes.
The first parenteral dose is administered 30 minutes before the start of emetogenic chemotherapy.
Subsequent parenteral doses are given 4 and 8 hours after the first dose.
The maximum parenteral dose per administration is 16 mg.
The injection formulation should be diluted in 50 mL prior to intravenous administration.
-Ondansetron should be used to prevent nausea and vomiting associated with moderately to highly emetogenic chemotherapy.
Uses: -Prevention of nausea and vomiting associated with moderately emetogenic cancer chemotherapy -Prevention of nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of emetogenic chemotherapy
Ondansetron can cause a serious heart problem.
This risk is especially high if you use certain medicines at the same time.
These medicines include antibiotics, antidepressants, heart rhythm medicine, antipsychotic medicines, and medicines to treat cancer, malaria, HIV or AIDS.
Tell your doctor about all medicines you use, and those you start or stop using during your treatment with ondansetron.
Taking ondansetron while using certain other medicines can cause high levels of serotonin to build up in your body.
This condition is called "serotonin syndrome."
Serotonin syndrome can be fatal.
Tell your doctor if you also use:
medicine to treat depression;
medicine to treat a psychiatric disorder;
a narcotic (opioid) medication; or
medicine to prevent nausea and vomiting.
This list is not complete and many other drugs can interact with ondansetron.
List includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Give a list of all your medicines to any healthcare provider who treats you.
Q: How long before a meal should you take ondansetron?
You are taking ondansetron for nausea that occurs with meals.
The standard tablet should be taken half an hour to 1 hour before meals.
The orally disintegrating tablet can be taken 15 minutes before meals.
The oral soluble film can be taken 15 minutes before meals.
However, if you are taking ondansetron for constant, all day nausea.
Then ondansetron should be taken at regular intervals during the day as prescribed.
It should be taken with or without food.
Q: How often can you take ondansetron?
You can take ondansetron more frequently initially.
You can take ondansetron to prevent or treat nausea or vomiting from chemotherapy.
You can take 4mg of ondansetron.
You can take another 4mg dose at 4 hours after the first dose.
You can take another 4mg dose at 8 hours after the first dose.
The following day you should only take ondansetron every 8 hours (3 times a day).
You are at higher risk of side effects if you continue taking ondansetron more frequently than this.
The side effects include constipation, headache, or heart effects.
Q: Chemo side effects: What should I expect and how to cope?
A: Chemotherapy is a drug treatment used to kill fast-growing cancer cells in your body.
Chemotherapy is an effective treatment for many types of cancer.
Chemotherapy can also damage healthy cells that divide quickly.
Examples of these cells include those in your hair follicles, digestive tract, and bone marrow.
This widespread effect on healthy tissue causes the various side effects.
These side effects are associated with chemotherapy treatment.
Q: Can you take ondansetron while pregnant?
Ondansetron is not specifically approved by the FDA to treat nausea and vomiting during pregnancy.
The use of ondansetron to treat nausea and vomiting during pregnancy is common.
Approximately 25% of pregnant women are prescribed ondansetron to treat nausea and vomiting during pregnancy.
Overall, studies show the use of ondansetron appears to be associated with an additional 3 instances of oral cleft defects.
These defects include cleft lip or cleft palate.
This association occurs for every 10,000 women exposed to ondansetron during their first trimester.
There may also be a very slight increased risk of a type of heart defect called a ventricular septal defect.
There is no apparent increased risk of other birth defects.
There is no apparent increased risk of miscarriage.
There is no apparent increased risk of fetal death.
Q: How long does ondansetron take to work?
Oral ondansetron usually starts to work within 30 minutes.
Oral ondansetron may take up to two hours to reach full effect.
Typical timing is: 30 minutes before chemotherapy, 1 hour before surgery, 1 to 2 hours before radiation therapy.
Q: Does ondansetron make you sleepy?
A: Yes, ondansetron (Zofran) might make you feel sleepy or tired.
Ondansetron injection has been reported to cause drowsiness or sedation in 8% of patients.
Ondansetron injection is used for the treatment of post-operative nausea and vomiting.
7% of those using a placebo experienced drowsiness or sedation.
A placebo is an inactive treatment.
Malaise and fatigue have also been reported in 13% of patients.
Malaise is generally feeling unwell.
Fatigue is tiredness or lack of energy.
This occurred when Ondansetron was used orally for the prevention of chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting.
The rate was 2% for the placebo group.
Pantoprazole is a proton pump inhibitor that decreases the amount of acid produced in the stomach.
Pantoprazole is used to treat erosive esophagitis.
Erosive esophagitis is damage to the esophagus from stomach acid.
This damage is caused by gastroesophageal reflux disease, or GERD.
Pantoprazole is used in adults and children who are at least 5 years old.
Pantoprazole is usually given for up to 8 weeks at a time while your esophagus heals.
Pantoprazole is also used to treat Zollinger-Ellison syndrome and other conditions involving excess stomach acid.
Pantoprazole is not for the immediate relief of heartburn symptoms.
Heartburn is often confused with the first symptoms of a heart attack.
Seek emergency medical attention if you have chest pain or heavy feeling, pain spreading to the arm or shoulder, nausea, sweating, and a general ill feeling.
Long-term treatment with pantoprazole may make it harder for your body to absorb vitamin B-12.
This difficulty in absorption may result in a deficiency of vitamin B-12.
Talk with your doctor if you need long-term pantoprazole treatment and you have concerns about vitamin B-12 deficiency.
Pantoprazole can cause kidney problems.
Tell your doctor if you are urinating less than usual, or if you have blood in your urine.
Diarrhea may be a sign of a new infection.
Call your doctor if you have diarrhea that is watery or has blood in it.
Pantoprazole may cause new or worsening symptoms of lupus.
Tell your doctor if you have joint pain and a skin rash on your cheeks or arms that worsens in sunlight.
You may be more likely to have a broken bone while taking this medicine long term or more than once per day.
Heartburn can mimic early symptoms of a heart attack.
Get emergency medical help if you have chest pain that spreads to your jaw or shoulder and you feel anxious or light-headed.
You should not use Pantoprazole if:
you also take medicine that contains rilpivirine (Edurant, Complera, Juluca, Odefsey);
if you had breathing problems, kidney problems, or a severe allergic reaction after taking pantoprazole in the past; or
you are allergic to pantoprazole or similar medicines (lansoprazole, omeprazole, Nexium, Prevacid, Prilosec, and others).
Tell your doctor if you have ever had:
low levels of magnesium, calcium, or potassium in your blood;
lupus; or
osteoporosis or low bone mineral density.
You may be more likely to have a broken bone while using pantoprazole long-term or more than once per day.
Talk with your doctor about ways to keep your bones healthy, especially if you are an adult over 50.
This medicine may harm an unborn baby.
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant.
Pantoprazole can pass into breast milk.
Talk with your doctor about the best way to feed your baby if you take Pantoprazole.
Pantoprazole is not approved for use by anyone younger than 5 years old.
Take pantoprazole exactly as prescribed by your doctor.
Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets.
Use the medicine exactly as directed.
Use the lowest dose for the shortest amount of time needed to treat your condition.
Pantoprazole is taken by mouth (oral) or given as an infusion into a vein (injection).
A healthcare provider may teach you how to properly use the injection by yourself.
Pantoprazole tablets are taken by mouth, with or without food.
The oral granules should be taken 30 minutes before a meal.
Do not crush, chew, or break the tablet or oral granules.
Swallow the tablet whole.
The oral granules should be mixed with applesauce or apple juice when taken by mouth.
If giving through a nasogastric (NG) or gastrostomy tube, mix only with apple juice.
Read and carefully follow any Instructions for Use provided with your medicine.
Ask your doctor or pharmacist if you have any questions.
Use this medicine for the full prescribed length of time, even if your symptoms quickly improve.
Call your doctor if your symptoms do not improve or if symptoms get worse while you are using this medicine.
Pantoprazole can cause false results with certain medical tests.
Tell the doctor or laboratory staff that you are using this medicine.
Pantoprazole may also affect a drug-screening urine test.
You may have false results.
Tell the laboratory staff that you use this medicine.

Usual Adult Dose for Erosive Esophagitis:
Treatment of Erosive Esophagitis involves taking 40 mg orally once a day for up to 8 weeks.
However, an additional 8 weeks may be considered for patients who have not healed after the initial treatment.
Safety and efficacy beyond 16 weeks of therapy have not been established.
Maintenance of Healing of Erosive Esophagitis: 40 mg orally once a day.
Controlled studies have been limited to 12 months of pantoprazole therapy.
Usual Adult Dose for Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease:
Parenteral: 40 mg once a day for 7 to 10 days, administered via intravenous infusion over a period of 15 minutes.
Intravenous therapy should be discontinued as soon as the patient is able to resume oral therapy.
Oral: 40 mg orally once a day, for short-term administration (up to 8 weeks).
However, an additional 8 weeks may be considered for patients who have not healed after the initial treatment.
Safety and efficacy beyond 16 weeks of therapy have not been established.
Usual Adult Dose for Duodenal Ulcer:
Study (n=54) took 40 mg orally once a day.
The dose was increased every 12 weeks by 40 mg increments.
The maximum dose was 120 mg per day.
The study lasted for 28 weeks.
Data have revealed that monotherapy with daily doses of 40 mg have been associated with complete duodenal ulcer healing.
Healing was observed in up to 87% of patients after 4 weeks.
Healing was observed in up to 94% of patients after 8 weeks.
Usual Adult Dose for Gastric Ulcer:
40 mg orally once a day.
Data have revealed that monotherapy with daily doses of 40 mg have been associated with complete gastric ulcer healing.
This healing occurred in up to 87% of patients after 4 weeks.
This healing occurred in up to 97% of patients after 8 weeks.
Usual Adult Dose for Helicobacter pylori Infection:
A study with 242 participants was conducted.
Triple therapy was administered at 40 mg orally twice daily for 7 days.
This therapy was commonly used in conjunction with clarithromycin and either amoxicillin or metronidazole.
The goal was to eradicate Helicobacter pylori.
Pantoprazole was administered at 40 mg orally once daily until day 28.
Triple therapy has resulted in eradication rates of greater than 95%.
The QUADRATE Study (n=405) involves quadruple therapy.
This therapy consists of 40 mg of the primary drug taken orally twice daily for 7 days.
It is administered concomitantly with bismuth subcitrate, taken four times daily.
It is also administered concomitantly with tetracycline, taken four times daily.
It includes metronidazole 200 mg taken three times daily.
It includes metronidazole 400 mg taken at bedtime.
Helicobacter Pylori eradication was achieved in 82% of patients.
Usual Adult Dose for Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome:
Parenteral: 80 mg every 12 hours, administered by 15-minute infusion.
Daily doses higher than 240 mg administered in equally divided doses by 15-minute infusion have not been studied.
Doses administered for more than 6 days have not been studied.
Oral: 40 mg twice daily, to a maximum of 240 mg per day.
Some patients have received treatment with pantoprazole for more than 2 years.
Usual Adult Dose for Stress Ulcer Prophylaxis:
The study included 21 participants.
The study focused on stress ulcer bleeding prophylaxis in the critical care setting.
The prophylaxis was administered as 80 mg twice daily.
The dose was given as a bolus infusion over a period of 15 minutes.
The maximum daily dose was 240 mg.
The daily dose was divided into three equal doses.
A study with 20 participants investigated peptic ulcer rebleeding prophylaxis after hemostasis in the critical care setting.
The treatment involved an 80 mg IV bolus.
This was followed by a continuous infusion of 8 mg/hr for 3 days.
After this period, therapy may be continued with an oral PPI.
Usual Adult Dose for Peptic Ulcer:
The study included 21 participants.
The study focused on stress ulcer bleeding prophylaxis in the critical care setting.
The prophylaxis was administered as 80 mg twice daily.
The dose was given as a bolus infusion over a period of 15 minutes.
The maximum daily dose was 240 mg.
The daily dose was divided into three equal doses.
A study with 20 participants investigated peptic ulcer rebleeding prophylaxis after hemostasis in the critical care setting.
The treatment involved an 80 mg IV bolus.
This was followed by a continuous infusion of 8 mg/hr for 3 days.
After this period, therapy may be continued with an oral PPI.
Use the medicine as soon as you can.
Skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose.
Do not use two doses at one time.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Pantoprazole can cause diarrhea, which may be a sign of a new infection.
If you have diarrhea that is watery or bloody, call your doctor.
Do not use anti-diarrhea medicine unless your doctor tells you to.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to pantoprazole.
Signs of an allergic reaction to pantoprazole include hives.
Signs of an allergic reaction to pantoprazole include difficulty breathing.
Signs of an allergic reaction to pantoprazole include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
severe stomach pain, diarrhea that is watery or bloody;
sudden pain or trouble moving your hip, wrist, or back;
bruising or swelling where intravenous pantoprazole was injected;
kidney problems - fever, rash, nausea, loss of appetite, joint pain, urinating less than usual, blood in your urine, weight gain;
low magnesium - dizziness, fast or irregular heart rate, tremors (shaking) or jerking muscle movements, feeling jittery, muscle cramps, muscle spasms in your hands and feet, cough or choking feeling; or
new or worsening symptoms of lupus - joint pain, and a skin rash on your cheeks or arms that worsens in sunlight.
severe skin reactions - fever, chills, body aches, shortness of breath, enlarged lymph nodes, or a skin rash that blisters, peels, or bleeds;
Taking pantoprazole long-term may cause you to develop stomach growths called fundic gland polyps.
Talk with your doctor about this risk.
If you use pantoprazole for longer than 3 years, you could develop a vitamin B-12 deficiency.
Talk to your doctor about how to manage this condition if you develop it.
Common pantoprazole side effects may include:
headache, dizziness;
stomach pain, gas, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea;
joint pain; or
fever, rash, or cold symptoms (most common in children).

digoxin;
methotrexate; or
a diuretic or "water pill."

Q: How long does pantoprazole take to work?
Pantoprazole starts to work within 2.5 hours.
This is the time Pantoprazole takes to block the secretion of acid in the stomach.
Pantoprazole lasts for 24 hours.
Pantoprazole does not provide fast-acting relief from indigestion, acid reflux, or dyspepsia symptoms.
Antacids such as Tums or Maalox provide fast-acting relief from indigestion, acid reflux, or dyspepsia symptoms.
Pantoprazole will not treat current symptoms.
Pantoprazole may take up to 4 weeks to work properly.
You may still have some symptoms during this time.
Q: Pantoprazole vs. omeprazole: What's the difference between them?
Pantoprazole and omeprazole are both proton pump inhibitors (PPIs).
These PPIs reduce stomach acid.
They show similar effectiveness for healing acid‑related conditions.
An example of such a condition is erosive esophagitis.
Pantoprazole and omeprazole's main differences relate to FDA‑approved uses.
The differences also relate to availability.
Omeprazole is OTC.
Pantoprazole is prescription.
The differences also relate to timing with food.
The differences also relate to the option for intravenous (IV) pantoprazole in the hospital setting.
Q: Can you take pantoprazole 40 mg twice a day?
The usual dosage for pantoprazole 40mg is 40mg once a day.
This applies if you are buying pantoprazole without a prescription.
This also applies if you are buying pantoprazole without a physician's evaluation of your symptoms.
Pantoprazole 40mg twice a day is recommended only for conditions characterized by excessively high gastric acid secretion.
Zollinger Ellison syndrome is a condition characterized by excessively high gastric acid secretion.
If you have conditions characterized by excessively high gastric acid secretion, you should be under the care of a physician.
You should be under the care of a physician if you have Zollinger Ellison syndrome.
Studies have shown that for reflux, dyspepsia, or GERD, dosages higher than 40mg did not offer any further significant lowering of stomach acid.
The comparison was made against 40mg dosages.
Q: How long can I take pantoprazole?
You should only take pantoprazole for up to 8 weeks.
This applies if you are buying pantoprazole without a prescription.
This also applies if you are buying pantoprazole without a physician evaluation of your symptoms.
Certain stomach symptoms persist for more than 4 to 8 weeks.
These symptoms should be investigated by a doctor.
Stomach symptoms may be a sign of something more serious.
Something more serious may be cancer.
Pantoprazole is very good at relieving and hiding serious stomach symptoms.
These symptoms may make you think you don't need to see a physician.
Long-term use of pantoprazole has also been associated with vitamin B12 deficiency.
It has been associated with low magnesium, calcium, iron, or vitamin C levels.
It has been associated with an increased risk of fractures of the hip, wrist, or spine.
Elderly, the malnourished, or those on dialysis are more at risk.
Q: Does pantoprazole cause bloating?
A: Bloating has been reported as a side effect of regular pantoprazole use.
One study reported bloating in 43% of patients.
The patients were taking a similar proton pump inhibitor to pantoprazole.
The inhibitor was called esomeprazole.
The bloating occurred after 8 weeks.
By 6 months, over 50% reporting bloating.
Other similar side effects reported with pantoprazole include flatulence, abdominal pain, and diarrhea.
Bloating can also be a side effect of pantoprazole.
Symptoms of bloating include tightness, fullness, or swelling of your stomach area.
Q: When should you take pantoprazole?
A: Pantoprazole is usually taken once daily, 30 minutes before a meal.
Pantoprazole is most often taken before breakfast to control acid and relieve GERD or heartburn symptoms.
Pantoprazole may be taken with or without food.
Taking Pantoprazole on an empty stomach before eating helps Pantoprazole work more effectively.
Q: Can pantoprazole cause anxiety?
Pantoprazole is not known to commonly cause anxiety.
Most people take Pantoprazole without experiencing mood or anxiety symptoms.
However, anxiety can be a symptom of low vitamin B12 levels.
Low vitamin B12 levels are a long-term complication of pantoprazole treatment.

Q: Can you eat bananas when taking losartan?

Prednisone is a prescription corticosteroid.
Prednisone is used to reduce inflammation.
Prednisone is used to suppress an overactive immune system.
Prednisone is used to treat endocrine and metabolic disorders.
Prednisone is used for a wide range of conditions.
These conditions include severe allergies.
These conditions include asthma and COPD flare-ups.
These conditions include rheumatoid arthritis and other forms of arthritis.
These conditions include lupus.
These conditions include psoriasis and other skin conditions.
These conditions include ulcerative colitis.
These conditions include Crohn's disease.
These conditions include eye inflammation.
These conditions include some blood disorders.
These conditions include low adrenal hormone levels.
Prednisone works by mimicking the effects of glucocorticoid hormones secreted naturally by our adrenal glands.
Prednisone is a man-made (synthetic) version of these hormones.
Prednisone is different from steroids taken to build muscle (anabolic steroids).
Prednisone belongs to the drug class called glucocorticoids, which are a type of corticosteroid.
Prednisone tablets come in 1 mg, 2.5 mg, 5 mg, 10 mg, 20 mg, and 50 mg strengths.
Prednisone 20 mg and prednisone 10 mg tablets are the strengths most often used for short courses in adults.
Prednisone also comes as a delayed-release tablet (Rayos).
It also comes as an oral liquid.
It also comes as a concentrated oral solution (Prednisone Intensol) that is measured with a special dropper.
Many of the original brands are discontinued.
Generic prednisone is available.
Common prednisone side effects:
The most common prednisone side effects are trouble sleeping.
The most common prednisone side effects are mood changes.
The most common prednisone side effects are increased appetite and weight gain.
The most common prednisone side effects are fluid retention and swelling.
The most common prednisone side effects are increased sweating.
The most common prednisone side effects are acne.
The most common prednisone side effects are thinning skin that bruises easily.
The most common prednisone side effects are slow wound healing.
The most common prednisone side effects are higher blood sugar.
The most common prednisone side effects are indigestion and stomach upset.
Many of these effects are dose related.
These effects are more likely on higher doses and on longer courses.
Most of them settle after Prednisone is stopped.
Serious prednisone side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to prednisone.
Signs of an allergic reaction to prednisone include hives.
Signs of an allergic reaction to prednisone include difficult breathing.
Signs of an allergic reaction to prednisone include swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
signs of infection, such as fever, chills, sore throat, cough or a wound that is not healing, since prednisone can hide the usual warning signs;
blurred vision, eye pain or seeing halos around lights, which can be signs of glaucoma (raised pressure in the eye) or cataracts;
You have severe pain in your upper stomach.
The pain spreads to your back.
You experience nausea.
You experience vomiting.
These symptoms can be a sign of pancreatitis.
Pancreatitis is an inflamed pancreas.
bloody or black, tarry stools, or vomit that looks like coffee grounds, which can be signs of a bleeding stomach ulcer;
severe depression, unusual excitement, confusion, changes in personality or behavior, or a seizure;
swelling, rapid weight gain or shortness of breath, which can be signs of fluid retention or heart failure;
signs of low potassium, such as confusion, an uneven heartbeat, extreme thirst, passing more urine than usual, leg discomfort, or muscle weakness;
a very high blood pressure reading with severe headache, blurred vision, ringing in your ears, chest pain or shortness of breath;
sudden pain, swelling or bruising in a tendon, especially at the back of the ankle, since prednisone can weaken tendons and cause the Achilles tendon to rupture;
new bone pain, especially in a hip, shoulder or knee, which can be a sign of aseptic necrosis (bone tissue that has lost its blood supply); or
flat, painless spots that are red or purple on light skin, or bluish, brown or black on dark skin, which can be a sign of Kaposi's sarcoma.
Prednisone side effects in women:
Women taking prednisone may notice irregular or missed periods.
Women taking prednisone may notice unwanted facial hair growth.
Women taking prednisone may notice acne.
Women taking prednisone may notice changes in body fat.
These changes include a rounder face.
These changes include more fat around the neck and waist.
Long courses of prednisone also raise the risk of osteoporosis (thinning of the bones).
Postmenopausal women are already at higher risk.
Ask your doctor whether you need a bone density scan or a calcium and vitamin D supplement.
Long-term prednisone side effects:
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of osteoporosis.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of broken bones.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of muscle weakness.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of muscle loss.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of cataracts.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of glaucoma.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of diabetes.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of high blood pressure.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of Cushing's syndrome.
Cushing's syndrome is a set of changes caused by too much steroid.
Cushing's syndrome includes a rounded face.
Cushing's syndrome includes fat around the trunk.
Taking prednisone for months or years raises the risk of adrenal suppression.
Adrenal suppression is where your body stops making enough of its own steroid hormones.
Children who take prednisone for a long time may grow more slowly.
Your child's doctor should check Children's growth at regular visits.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Do not take prednisone if you have a systemic fungal infection, which is a fungal infection that has spread through your body.
Prednisone weakens your immune system.
This makes it easier to get an infection.
An existing infection may become worse.
An existing infection can reactivate an old infection, such as tuberculosis or hepatitis B.
It can also hide the usual signs of infection.
An infection may be advanced before you notice it.
Tell your doctor about any illness or infection you have had in the past few weeks.
Stay away from people who are sick.
Chickenpox and measles can be serious or even fatal in someone taking a steroid.
Call your doctor straight away if you are exposed to either one.
Do not have a live vaccine while you are taking prednisone unless your doctor says it is safe.
Do not stop taking prednisone suddenly after more than a few days of treatment.
Your body needs time to start making its own steroid hormones again.
Your doctor will lower your dose in steps.
Call your doctor at once if you have shortness of breath.
Call your doctor at once if you have severe pain in your upper stomach.
Call your doctor at once if you have bloody or black stools.
Call your doctor at once if you have severe depression.
Call your doctor at once if you have changes in personality or behavior.
Call your doctor at once if you have vision problems.
Call your doctor at once if you have eye pain.
You should not use prednisone if you are allergic to prednisone.
You should not use prednisone if you have a systemic fungal infection.
To make sure prednisone is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have or have ever had:
an infection of any kind, including tuberculosis, hepatitis B, herpes infection of the eye, or a fungal infection;
Diarrhea has no clear cause.
Time is spent in the tropics.
Prednisone can wake up a parasite infection.
The parasite infection is amebiasis.
The parasite infection is threadworm.
malaria, especially cerebral malaria (malaria affecting the brain);
liver disease, such as cirrhosis;

heart disease, heart failure, high blood pressure or low potassium levels;

glaucoma or cataracts;
stomach ulcers, ulcerative colitis, diverticulitis, or a history of bleeding in the stomach or bowel;

depression, mood swings or another mental health condition.
Long-term steroid use can cause bone loss.
Your risk is higher if you smoke.
Your risk is higher if you do not exercise.
Your risk is higher if you do not get enough calcium or vitamin D.
Your risk is higher if osteoporosis runs in your family.
Talk with your doctor about protecting your bones.
Pregnancy:
Prednisone crosses the placenta.
Taking Prednisone in the first trimester may raise the risk of birth defects.
Taking higher doses later in pregnancy may affect the baby's growth and baby's adrenal glands.
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant.
Some conditions still need steroid treatment during pregnancy.
This is a decision to make with your doctor rather than stopping on your own.
Breastfeeding:
Prednisone passes into breast milk in small amounts.
Tell your doctor if you are breastfeeding so you can talk through the dose and the timing of feeds.
Children:
Prednisone is used in children.
Long courses can slow growth and affect bone development.
The dose should not be higher than prescribed.
Your child should be monitored during treatment.
Older adults:
Older adults are more likely to have liver, kidney, or heart problems that affect how prednisone is handled.
Older adults are more likely to already have osteoporosis, diabetes, or high blood pressure.
A lower dose may be needed.
Take prednisone exactly as prescribed, with food or milk to protect your stomach.
Your doctor will often start you on a higher dose and then lower it.
Do not change your dose or stop prednisone without advice.
Take your dose in the morning if your doctor tells you to.
Your adrenal glands are most active between 2 am and 8 am.
Taking a steroid at that time interferes with adrenal glands the least.
A morning dose may also help you sleep better.
That is a separate benefit.
Swallow delayed-release tablets whole.
Do not crush, break, or chew them.

You may mix the concentrated solution with juice or a soft food such as applesauce.

The usual starting dose of prednisone for adults is 5 mg to 60 mg per day.
The dose depends on the condition being treated.
The dose also depends on how severe the condition is.
There is no single standard dose.
Your doctor will decide on a starting dose.
Then, the doctor will lower the dose in small steps.
The goal is to reach the smallest dose that still controls your condition.
Doses for children are based on the condition rather than strictly on age or weight.
They must be set by a doctor.
Short courses of prednisone for problems such as an asthma flare, gout, or a bad allergic reaction are often given as a short fixed-dose course for a few days.
A dose pack steps the dose down day by day over about six days.
Follow the instructions on your pack exactly, since the number of tablets changes each day.
Prednisone treatment takes one of two shapes.
Some people take Prednisone for a few days or weeks.
They take it for a problem that will settle.
Examples of such problems include an asthma flare or a severe allergic reaction.
Others take Prednisone every day for months or years for an ongoing condition such as rheumatoid arthritis or ulcerative colitis.
If you take prednisone long term, your doctor will settle you on a maintenance dose.
Maintenance dose is the lowest daily dose that still keeps your condition under control.
Maintenance dose is reached by lowering the dose in small steps rather than all at once.
Your maintenance dose is not fixed.
Maintenance dose may need to go up for a time if your condition flares.
It may need to go up for a time if you have surgery, a fever, an infection, or another serious illness.
Maintenance dose may come down again afterwards.
Do not adjust dose yourself.
How do you stop taking prednisone safely?
:
If you have taken prednisone for more than a few days, your doctor will taper the dose rather than stopping dose all at once.
While you are taking a steroid, your adrenal glands slow down their own hormone production.
Adrenal glands need time to start up again.
Stopping suddenly can cause extreme tiredness, weakness, body aches, upset stomach, weight loss, a craving for salt, and changes in skin color.
Call your doctor if you feel unwell while your dose is being lowered or after you finish.

The bigger risk comes from taking high doses over a long time.

This can cause acne.

Limit alcohol.
Both alcohol and prednisone irritate the stomach lining.
Together, they raise the risk of stomach bleeding.

Taking NSAIDs with prednisone raises the risk of stomach bleeding.

Prednisone makes your body hold onto fluid.

Others add to prednisone's side effects.
Tell your doctor about every medicine you take, including anything you start or stop during treatment.
Be especially careful with:
medicines that raise prednisone levels, such as ketoconazole, itraconazole, posaconazole, voriconazole, clarithromycin, and some HIV and hepatitis C medicines;
medicines that lower prednisone levels and can make prednisone work less well, such as rifampin, rifabutin, rifapentine, carbamazepine, phenytoin, phenobarbital, primidone and St. John's wort;
NSAIDs and aspirin, which raise the risk of stomach ulcers and bleeding;
blood thinners such as warfarin, since prednisone can change how your blood clots and you may need extra monitoring;
insulin and diabetes tablets, since prednisone raises blood sugar and your doses may need to go up;
diuretics (water pills) and amphotericin B, which can lower potassium further;
digoxin, which is more likely to cause problems if your potassium is low;
live vaccines, which should usually be avoided;
cyclosporine, which interacts in both directions and raises the risk of seizures; and
birth control pills and hormone therapy.
This list is not complete.
Many other prescription medicines, over-the-counter products, vitamins and herbal products can interact with prednisone.
Give a list of all your medicines to any healthcare provider who treats you.
Is prednisone a steroid?
:
Yes.
Prednisone is a corticosteroid, also called a glucocorticoid.
Corticosteroids are copies of hormones your adrenal glands make naturally.
They work by lowering inflammation and immune activity.
They are not the same as the anabolic steroids some athletes misuse to build muscle.
How long does it take for prednisone to work?
:
Prednisone starts to be absorbed within about an hour.
Many people notice a difference in symptoms such as swelling, pain, or wheezing within a few hours to a day.
Some conditions take several days or longer to respond.
Delayed-release tablets are designed to release the medicine about four hours after you take them.
Therefore, the timing of your dose matters.
How long does prednisone stay in your system?
:
Prednisone and prednisolone have a half-life of about 2 to 3 hours.
This means half of a dose leaves your blood in that time.
Most of a single dose is out of your bloodstream within about a day.
The effect lasts longer than the drug itself.
A single dose of prednisone quiets your own adrenal glands.
This effect lasts for about one and a quarter to one and a half days.
This is why prednisone is described as short-acting.
It is short-acting compared with steroids such as dexamethasone.
A course of prednisone can affect your blood sugar, your sleep, and your energy.
This effect can last longer than the drug is measurable in your blood.
Why has my doctor prescribed prednisone every other day?
:
Alternate day dosing means taking twice your usual daily dose every other morning instead of a dose every day.
Prednisone is used for people who need steroid treatment for a long time.
Giving your body a day off from the steroid lets your adrenal glands recover between doses.
This recovery reduces the risk of adrenal suppression, Cushing's syndrome, withdrawal symptoms, and slowed growth in children.
The trade-off is that your symptoms may flare a little late in the day.
This happens if you do not take it.
Tell your doctor if this happens.
Other treatment can often be added for that gap.
Do not switch yourself to an every other day schedule.
Do not double up on a day you were not meant to take a dose.
What is the difference between prednisone and prednisolone?
:
Prednisone has to be converted by your liver into prednisolone before it can work.
Prednisolone is already in the active form.
For most people the two work in a very similar way at equivalent doses.
Prednisolone is often chosen for people with significant liver disease.
It is also chosen for children because Prednisolone comes in liquid forms that are easier to dose.
Does prednisone cause weight gain?
:
Weight gain is a common side effect of prednisone.
Weight gain happens for two reasons.
Prednisone makes your body hold onto salt and water.
Holding onto salt and water adds weight quickly in the first days or weeks.
Prednisone increases appetite.
Increased appetite adds body fat over longer courses.
Prednisone also moves fat toward the face, neck, upper back and waist.
Weight from fluid usually goes once you stop.
Weight from extra eating takes longer to shift.
Can you drink alcohol while taking prednisone?
:
There is no direct interaction between alcohol and prednisone.
Drinking is best kept to a minimum.
Alcohol and prednisone both irritate the stomach lining.
Together, they raise the risk of stomach ulcers and bleeding.
Alcohol also raises blood sugar and weakens bones.
Prednisone does both of these too.
Ask your doctor what is reasonable for you, especially if you are on a long course.
Can you take ibuprofen with prednisone?
:
It is usually best to avoid ibuprofen while you are taking prednisone.
Ibuprofen is an NSAID.
Taking an NSAID with a steroid raises the risk of stomach ulcers and bleeding.
The same applies to naproxen, diclofenac, and regular doses of aspirin.
If you need a painkiller, ask your doctor or pharmacist first.
Can you take Tylenol with prednisone?
:
Acetaminophen does not interact with prednisone.
Acetaminophen does not irritate the stomach.
Prednisone is a medication.
Acetaminophen is generally the preferred choice for pain or fever while taking prednisone.
Stay within the recommended daily limit on the packet and check whether any other product you take already contains acetaminophen.
Why does prednisone keep me awake?
:
Prednisone acts like your body's stress hormone, which naturally peaks in the morning to help you wake up.
Taking prednisone, especially later in the day, can leave you feeling wired, restless or unable to sleep.
Taking your dose in the morning often helps.
Talk to your doctor before changing when you take prednisone.
Does prednisone raise blood pressure?
:
Prednisone can.
Prednisone makes your body hold onto salt and water.
This raises blood pressure in some people.
This effect is particularly seen at higher doses or on longer courses.
Your blood pressure should be checked during treatment.
Your doctor may ask you to reduce salt in your diet.
What is considered a high dose of prednisone?
:
There is no official cutoff.
Doctors generally treat doses under 7.5 mg a day as low.
Doctors generally treat doses from 7.5 mg to 40 mg a day as moderate.
Doctors generally treat doses over 40 mg a day as high.
A dose of 20 mg a day sits in the moderate range.
What matters as much as the number is how long you take prednisone.
Side effects build up over time.
Can prednisone make you tired?
:
Yes.
Some people feel energized on prednisone while others feel drained.
Poor sleep, mood changes and muscle weakness all contribute.
Tiredness can start as your dose is being lowered.
Tiredness can start after you stop taking the medication.
This tiredness can be a sign that your adrenal glands have not caught up yet.
Tell your doctor if tiredness is severe.
Tell your doctor if tiredness comes with dizziness.
Tell your doctor if tiredness comes with nausea.
Tell your doctor if tiredness comes with body aches.
Q: What is considered a high dose of prednisone?
A: The starting dose of prednisone may be between 5 mg to 60 mg per day.
A dose above 40 mg per day may be considered a high dose.
However, everybody responds differently to prednisone.
So what might be a high dose depends on the person and the condition.
Q: Can you take prednisone with Keytruda?
A: Use prednisone with Keytruda or Keytruda Qlex only if your healthcare provider prescribes prednisone.
Prednisone is a corticosteroid that lowers immune system activity.
Prednisone may reduce how well Keytruda works or increase your risk of infection.
However, your doctor may prescribe prednisone to treat certain immune-related side effects caused by Keytruda.
Q: What's a good prednisone taper schedule?
A prednisone taper depends on your dose.
A prednisone taper depends on how long you have taken prednisone.
A prednisone taper depends on the condition being treated.
If prednisone has been taken for less than 3 to 4 weeks, a taper is usually not needed.
This applies regardless of the dose.
The risk of adrenal insufficiency increases with longer treatment and higher doses.
Treatment for 3 to 4 weeks or longer at doses above about 4 to 6 mg of prednisone per day may suppress the body's normal cortisol production.
Such treatment may require a gradual taper.
Q: Is Prednisone OK to drink alcohol with Prednisone?
There is no direct drug interaction between prednisone and alcohol.
Mixing prednisone together is not a good idea.
Some of the effects of alcohol and the side effects of prednisone are similar.
Taking both prednisone and alcohol at the same time can increase the risk of these side effects occurring.
Q: How long can you take prednisone safely?
A: There is no set limit on how long you can safely take prednisone.
It depends on the dose of prednisone and the condition being treated.
It may be prescribed short term or long term.
The dosage will be adjusted or stopped based on your response or lack of response to the medication.
Q: How long does it take for prednisone to work?
Prednisone usually works very quickly.
It works within a few hours to days of taking the first dose.
The timing depends on the condition you are treating.
You may notice an effect within hours.
This effect occurs if the prescribed dose of prednisone is effective at reducing your inflammation.
Q: Prednisone: What are 12 Things You Should Know?
Prednisone has been on the market for more than 70 years.
It is still one of the most commonly prescribed anti-inflammatory or immunosuppressive medications.
Prednisone is used to treat dozens of different conditions such as skin disorders, breathing disorders, allergies, arthritis, and ulcerative colitis.
But did you know Prednisone also has some pretty serious side effects as well?
Q: Do I take all 6 prednisone pills at once on the first day?
A: It's best to take prednisone as a single dose once a day straight after breakfast.
For example, if your dose is 30mg daily, it is usual to take 6 tablets (6 x 5mg).
You should take them all at the same time after breakfast.
Q: How long does prednisone stay in your system?
A: In general, you could expect a dose or prednisone to stay in your system for 16.5 to 22 hours based on a half life of 3 to 4 hours.
It usually takes around 5.5 half lives for a drug to be completely eliminated from your system.
Rosuvastatin (Crestor) is a prescription medicine.
The medicine is used to treat high cholesterol.
The medicine is used to reduce the risk of major cardiovascular events.
Major cardiovascular events include heart attack and stroke.
Rosuvastatin belongs to a class of medicines called statins.
Rosuvastatin works by blocking HMG-CoA reductase.
HMG-CoA reductase is the rate-limiting enzyme the liver uses to make cholesterol.
With cholesterol synthesis reduced, the liver increases its LDL receptors.
The liver clears more LDL from the blood.
LDL is "bad" cholesterol.
LDL and total cholesterol levels fall.
The maximum reduction is usually reached by four weeks.
Rosuvastatin comes as a tablet.
Rosuvastatin was first approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) on August 12, 2003, under the brand name Crestor.
Many generic versions of rosuvastatin tablets have since been approved.
Rosuvastatin is used:
The goal is to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events.
These events include death from cardiovascular disease.
These events include heart attack.
These events include stroke.
These events include the need for procedures to improve blood flow to the heart.
These procedures are called arterial revascularization.
The target population is adults at increased risk for these events.
along with diet and exercise to lower the level of low-density lipoprotein (LDL-C) cholesterol or "bad" cholesterol: in adults with primary hypercholesterolemia.
and slow the buildup of fatty deposits (plaque) in the walls of blood vessels in adults.
in adults and children 8 years of age and older with high blood cholesterol due to heterozygous familial hypercholesterolemia (HeFH) (an inherited condition that causes high levels of LDL-C).
in adults and children 7 years of age and older with homozygous familial hypercholesterolemia (HoFH) (an inherited condition that causes high levels of LDL-C).
along with diet for the treatment of adults with: primary dysbetalipoproteinemia (an inherited condition that causes high levels of cholesterol and fat).
hypertriglyceridemia.
It is not known if rosuvastatin is safe and effective in children younger than 8 years of age with HeFH.
It is not known if rosuvastatin is safe and effective in children younger than 7 years of age with HoFH.
It is not known if rosuvastatin is safe and effective in children with other types of hypercholesterolemia.
These other types of hypercholesterolemia are other than HeFH or HoFH.
Common side effects:
The most common side effects may include headache, nausea, muscle aches and pains, weakness, and constipation.
Serious side effects:
Rosuvastatin may cause serious side effects, including:
Muscle pain, tenderness and weakness (myopathy).
Muscle problems, including muscle breakdown, can be serious in some people.
They rarely cause kidney damage that can lead to death.
Tell your doctor right away if you have unexplained muscle pain, tenderness, or weakness.
Tell your doctor right away if you have a fever.
Tell your doctor right away if you feel more tired than usual.
These symptoms may occur while you take this medicine.
you have muscle problems that do not go away even after your doctor has told you to stop taking this medicine.
Your doctor may do further tests to diagnose the cause of your muscle problems.
Your chances of getting muscle problems are higher if you are taking certain other medicines while you take rosuvastatin.
Your chances of getting muscle problems are higher if you are 65 years of age or older.
Your chances of getting muscle problems are higher if you are of Asian descent.
Your chances of getting muscle problems are higher if you have thyroid problems (hypothyroidism) that are not controlled.
Your chances of getting muscle problems are higher if you have kidney problems.
Your chances of getting muscle problems are higher if you are taking higher doses of rosuvastatin.
Liver problems.
Your doctor may do blood tests to check your liver before you start taking rosuvastatin.
Your doctor may do blood tests to check your liver if you have symptoms of liver problems while you are taking rosuvastatin.
Call your doctor right away if you have any of the following symptoms of liver problems.
The symptoms include feeling unusually tired or weak.
The symptoms include loss of appetite.
The symptoms include upper belly pain.
The symptoms include dark urine.
The symptoms include yellowing of your skin or the whites of your eyes.
Protein and blood in the urine.
Rosuvastatin may cause you to have protein and blood in your urine.
If you develop protein or blood in your urine, your doctor may decrease your dose.
Increase in blood sugar (glucose) levels.
Rosuvastatin may cause an increase in your blood sugar levels.
Additional side effects have been reported with rosuvastatin.
The reported side effects include abdominal pain, dizziness, memory loss, and confusion.
Tell your doctor if you have any side effect that bothers you or that does not go away.
These are not all the possible side effects.
For more information, ask your doctor or pharmacist.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Do not take rosuvastatin if you:
are allergic to rosuvastatin or any of the ingredients in your rosuvastatin tablets.
See below for a complete list of ingredients.
have liver problems.

Swallow the tablet whole.

Do not change your dose or stop rosuvastatin without talking to your doctor.
You should do this even if you are feeling well.
Your doctor may do blood tests to check your cholesterol levels before and during your treatment with rosuvastatin.
Your doctor may change your dose if needed.
Your doctor may start you on a cholesterol-lowering diet before giving you rosuvastatin.
Stay on this diet when you take rosuvastatin.
If you take an antacid that contains a combination of aluminum and magnesium hydroxide.
Take rosuvastatin at least 2 hours before you take the antacid.
If you miss a dose of rosuvastatin, take your next dose at your normal scheduled time.
Do not take an extra dose.
In case of an overdose, get medical help.

Rosuvastatin (Crestor) and atorvastatin (Lipitor) are both statins.
These statins lower "bad" LDL cholesterol.
They reduce the risk of heart attack and stroke.
This applies to people at increased cardiovascular risk.
The better choice for you depends on factors such as how much LDL reduction you need.

Semaglutide is used for weight loss and weight management.

What is Ozempic used for?
:

reducing the risk of kidney function decline, kidney failure, and cardiovascular death in patients with type 2 diabetes and chronic kidney disease (CKD)

weight loss and weight management in adults with obesity or adults who are overweight and have a weight-related medical problem (e.g., blood pressure, type 2 diabetes, or high cholesterol)

weight loss and weight management patients with obesity who are 12 years and older

Semaglutide works by:
Stimulates insulin production in the pancreas
Reduces sugar production in the liver
Slows down digestion, so food stays in the stomach longer
Decreases appetite and food intake
These combined actions help you feel full longer.
They help you eat less.
They help you maintain better control over blood sugar levels.
As a result, semaglutide supports weight loss.
It improves blood sugar management.
It helps reduce the risk of cardiovascular events.
Semaglutide's mechanism of action mimics a natural hormone in the body called GLP-1.
GLP-1 is released after eating.
GLP-1 helps regulate appetite and blood sugar levels.
The way semaglutide works for kidney-related risk reduction is not fully understood.
Semaglutide belongs to the drug class called GLP-1 receptor agonists.

Common semaglutide side effects:
Common semaglutide side effects may include upset stomach, heartburn, burping, gas, bloating, nausea, vomiting, stomach pain, loss of appetite, diarrhea, constipation, stomach flu symptoms, headache, dizziness, tiredness, hair loss, and changes in skin sensations.
Stomach or gastrointestinal side effects are common.
Stomach or gastrointestinal side effects tend to be mild.
Stomach or gastrointestinal side effects clear up in a few weeks.
Stomach or gastrointestinal side effects usually will not interfere with long-term treatment.
Stomach side effects can be more common with higher doses.
Tips for managing stomach side effects:
Following these tips may help you manage stomach semaglutide side effects:
Eat more slowly
Eat smaller meals
Choose more bland, low-fat foods (like crackers, toast, and rice)
Avoid eating greasy, fried foods or sugar treats
Eat foods that contain water (like soup or gelatin)
Do not lie down right after eating
Drink clear or ice-cold liquids
If possible, go outside for fresh air if you feel sick to your stomach
Ryebelsus is not recommended in patients with severe gastroparesis.
Tips for managing low blood sugar levels (hypoglycemia) side effects:
Test blood sugar levels regularly if you are also taking diabetic medicines, including insulin or sulphonylureas (e.g., glibenclamide/glyburide, glipizide, and gliclazide) and when you increase your semaglutide dose.
Your prescriber might reduce your dose of insulin or sulphonylureas if needed to reduce the risk of hypoglycemia
Symptoms of low blood sugar levels include:
Treatment for hypoglycemia
Severe hypoglycemia may require a glucagon injection.
Serious semaglutide side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction.
Signs of an allergic reaction include hives, itching, dizziness, fast heartbeats, difficulty breathing, and swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
vision changes;
unusual mood changes, thoughts about hurting yourself;
pounding heartbeats or fluttering in your chest;
a light-headed feeling, like you might pass out;
signs of a thyroid tumor - swelling or a lump in your neck, trouble swallowing, a hoarse voice, feeling short of breath;
symptoms of pancreatitis - severe pain in your upper stomach spreading to your back, nausea with or without vomiting, fast heart rate;
gallbladder problems - upper stomach pain, fever, clay-colored stools, jaundice (yellowing of the skin or eyes);
low blood sugar - headache, hunger, weakness, sweating, confusion, irritability, dizziness, fast heart rate, or feeling jittery;
kidney problems - swelling, urinating less, feeling tired or short of breath; or
Stomach flu symptoms include stomach cramps, vomiting, loss of appetite, and diarrhea.
The diarrhea may be watery or bloody.
Severe stomach problems include severe constipation, fecal impaction, or intestinal obstruction.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Semaglutide has a boxed warning for the risk of thyroid C-cell tumors.
A boxed warning is the strongest safety-related warning issued by the FDA.
Semaglutide may cause serious side effects, including possible thyroid tumors and thyroid cancer.
Call your doctor at once if you have signs of a thyroid tumor.
These signs include swelling or a lump in your neck.
They also include trouble swallowing.
A hoarse voice is another sign.
Shortness of breath is also a sign.
You should not use semaglutide if you have multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2.
Multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2 involves tumors in your glands.
You should not use semaglutide if you have a personal or family history of medullary thyroid cancer.
Semaglutide also has warnings for pancreatitis.
It has warnings for diabetic retinopathy complications.
It has warnings for hypoglycemia that can be severe.
It has warnings for acute kidney injury.
It has warnings for acute gallbladder disease.
It has warnings for hypersensitivity reactions.
It has warnings for pulmonary aspiration during general anesthesia or deep sedation.
For Wegovy, there are also warnings for a heart rate increase and suicidal behaviour or ideation.
For Rybelsus, there is also a warning for severe gastrointestinal side effects.
You should not use semaglutide if you are allergic to semaglutide, or if you have:
multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2 (tumors in your glands);
a personal or family history of medullary thyroid carcinoma (a type of thyroid cancer); or
diabetic ketoacidosis (call your doctor for treatment).
Tell your doctor if you have ever had:
a stomach or intestinal disorder;
pancreatitis;
are planning surgery or other procedure that uses anesthesia or deep sedation;
kidney disease; or
eye problems caused by diabetes (retinopathy).
In animal studies, semaglutide caused thyroid tumors or thyroid cancer.
It is not known whether these effects would occur in people.
Ask your doctor about your risk.
Pregnancy:
Men and women should stop using semaglutide at least 2 months before Men and women plan to get pregnant.
Ask your doctor for a safer medicine to use during this time.
Controlling diabetes is very important during pregnancy.
Gaining the right amount of weight is also very important during pregnancy.
Even if you are overweight, losing weight during pregnancy could harm the unborn baby.
There is a pregnancy exposure registry for women who use Wegovy during pregnancy.
The purpose of this registry is to collect information about the health of you and your baby.
Talk to your healthcare provider about how you can take part in this registry.
Contact Novo Nordisk at 1-877-390-2760 or www.wegovypregnancyregistry.com.
Breastfeeding:
Ask a doctor if it is safe to breastfeed while using Ozempic or Wegovy injections.
Breastfeeding is not recommended during treatment with Wegovy tablets.
You should not breastfeed while using Rybelsus.
Semaglutide injection directions:
Semaglutide injections (Ozempic and Wegovy) are injected under the skin, usually once per week at any time of the day, with or without food.
Use an injection on the same day each week.
Read and follow all instructions you have been given.
Ask your doctor or pharmacist if you need help.
Prepare an injection only when you are ready to give it.
Call your pharmacist if the medicine looks cloudy, has changed colors, or has particles in it.
Your healthcare provider will show you where to inject semaglutide.
Do not inject into the same place twice in a row.
If you are visually impaired, the Wegovy FlexTouch pen is not recommended for self-injection.
If you choose a different weekly injection day, start your new schedule after at least 2 days have passed since the last injection you gave.
Do not use different brands of semaglutide at the same time.
Semaglutide tablets directions:
Semaglutide oral tablets (Ozempic, Wegovy, Rybelsus) are taken once a day.
They are taken at least 30 minutes before the first food, beverage, or other oral medications of the day.
They are taken with no more than 4 ounces of plain water only.
After 30 minutes of taking the tablet, you may eat, drink, or take other oral medicines.
Swallow tablets whole.
Do not split, crush, or chew tablets.
General semaglutide use:
Blood sugar can be affected by stress, illness, surgery, exercise, alcohol use, or skipping meals.
Low blood sugar (hypoglycemia) can make you feel very hungry, dizzy, irritable, or shaky.
To quickly treat hypoglycemia, eat or drink hard candy, crackers, raisins, fruit juice, or non-diet soda.
Your doctor may prescribe glucagon injections in case of severe hypoglycemia.
Tell your doctor if you have frequent symptoms of high blood sugar (hyperglycemia), such as increased thirst or urination.
Ask your doctor before changing your dose or medication schedule.
Your treatment may also include diet, exercise, weight control, medical tests, and special medical care.
You may get dehydrated during prolonged illness.
Call your doctor if you are sick with vomiting or diarrhea, or if you eat or drink less than usual.
Never share an injection pen, even if you changed the needle.
Sharing an injection can pass infection or disease from person to person.
The Ozempic injection pen contains more than one dose.
After your first use, store the pen with the needle removed in a refrigerator or at room temperature.
Protect from heat and light.
Keep the cap on when not in use.
Throw the pen away 56 days after the first use or if less than 0.25 mg is shown on the dose counter.
Do not reuse a needle.
Place needle in a puncture-proof "sharps" container and dispose of it following state or local laws.
Keep out of the reach of children and pets.
Ozempic Dosing Information:
Ozempic Injection Recommended Dosage:
Start Ozempic with a 0.25 mg subcutaneous injection (injection under the skin) once weekly for 4 weeks.
The 0.25 mg dosage is intended for treatment initiation.
It is not effective for glycemic control.
After 4 weeks on the 0.25 mg dosage, increase the dosage to 0.5 mg once weekly.
If additional glycemic control is needed after at least 4 weeks on the 0.5 mg dosage.
The dosage may be increased to 1 mg once weekly.
If additional glycemic control is needed after at least 4 weeks on the 1 mg dosage.
The dosage may be increased to 2 mg once weekly.
The maximum recommended dosage is 2 mg once weekly.
Administer Ozempic once weekly, on the same day each week, at any time of the day, with or without meals.
The day of weekly administration can be changed if necessary.
The time between two doses must be at least 2 days (>48 hours).
If a dose is missed, administer Ozempic as soon as possible within 5 days after the missed dose.
If more than 5 days have passed, skip the missed dose.
Administer the next dose on the regularly scheduled day.
In each case, patients can resume their regular dosing schedule.
The dosing schedule is once-weekly.
Ozempic is available as single-patient-use injections.
There are multiple strength options.
0.25 mg or 0.5 mg dose pen (4 doses of 0.25 mg and 2 doses of 0.5 mg, or 4 doses of 0.5 mg)
1 mg dose pen (4 doses per pen)
2 mg dose pen (4 doses per pen)
Each Ozempic pen carton contains 4 NovoFine Plus needles with 1mg and 2mg pens.
It also contains 6 NovoFine Plus needles with the 0.25/0.5mg dose pen.
Each injection is pre-filled and intended for use by one patient only.

Starting Ozempic Dosage (Initiation phase):

Days 31 to 60: 4 mg orally once daily.

Wegovy Dosing Information:
Wegovy injection dosing information:
Initiate Wegovy injection with a dose of 0.25 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly.
Follow the dose escalation schedule below to minimize gastrointestinal adverse reactions.
Initial Wegovy Injection Dose Escalation Schedule (all indications):
Week 1 to week 4: 0.25mg once a week
Week 5 to week 8: 0.5mg once a week
Week 9 to week 12: 1mg once a week
Week 13 to week 16: 1.7mg once a week
Maintenance injection dose guidance:
Adult weight reduction maintenance dose:
1.7 mg or 2.4 mg (recommended) injected subcutaneously once weekly.
Dose may be increased to 7.2 mg subcutaneously once weekly.
This increase applies to patients who tolerate the 2.4 mg dosage for at least 4 weeks.
The increase is also required when additional weight reduction is clinically indicated.
Maximum dose 7.2 mg once weekly
Consider treatment response and tolerability when selecting the maintenance dosage
Pediatric patients' weight reduction maintenance dose (patients aged 12 years and older):
Either 2.4 mg (recommended) or 1.7 mg once weekly.
Consider treatment response and tolerability when selecting the maintenance dosage
Cardiovascular risk reduction maintenance dose:
Either 2.4 mg (recommended) or 1.7 mg once weekly, based on patient response and tolerance.
Non-cirrhotic metabolic dysfunction–associated steatohepatitis (MASH) with moderate to advanced liver fibrosis maintenance dose:
2.4 mg injected once weekly.
If a patient does not tolerate the 2.4 mg once-weekly maintenance dose, the dose may be reduced.
The dose may be reduced to 1.7 mg once weekly.
Re-escalation to 2.4 mg once weekly may be considered.
This is if the lower dose is tolerated.
In patients with type 2 diabetes, monitor blood glucose prior to starting Wegovy and during Wegovy treatment.

Rybelsus Dosing Information:
Take Rybelsus at least 30 minutes before the first food, beverage, or other oral medications of the day with no more than 4 ounces of plain water only.
After 30 minutes of taking Semaglutide, you may eat, drink, or take other oral medicines.

Rybelsus Recommended Dosage R1 formulation
Start Rybelsus with 3 mg once daily for 30 days.
The 3 mg dose is intended for treatment initiation.
It is not effective for glycemic control.
After 30 days on the 3 mg dose, increase the dose to 7 mg once daily.
Dose may be increased to 14 mg once daily.
This increase is needed if additional glycemic control is required.
This requirement applies after at least 30 days on the 7 mg dose.
Taking two 7 mg Rybelsus tablets to achieve a 14 mg dose is not recommended.
If a dose is missed, the missed dose should be skipped.
The next dose should be taken the following day.
Rybelsus tablets strengths are available in 3mg, 7mg, and 14mg
Rybelsus Recommended Dosage R2 formulation

It is not effective for glycemic control.
After 30 days on the 1.5 mg dose, increase the dose to 4 mg once daily.
Dose may be increased to 9 mg once daily.
This increase is needed if additional glycemic control is required.
The increase occurs after at least 30 days on the 4 mg dose.
If a dose is missed, the missed dose should be skipped.
The next dose should be taken the following day.
Rybelsus tablets strengths are available in 1.5 mg, 4 mg, and 9 mg
Switching Patients between Ozempic and Rybelsus
Patients treated with Rybelsus 14 mg daily can be transitioned to Ozempic subcutaneous injection 0.5 mg once weekly.
Patients can start Ozempic the day after their last dose of Rybelsus.
Patients treated with once-weekly Ozempic 0.5 mg subcutaneous injection can be transitioned to Rybelsus 7 mg or 14 mg.
Patients can start Rybelsus up to 7 days after their last injection of Ozempic.
There is no equivalent dose of Rybelsus for Ozempic 1 mg.
For Rybelsus: Skip the missed dose and use your next dose at the regular time.
For Ozempic injection: Use Ozempic as soon as you can and then go back to your regular schedule.
If you are more than 5 days late for Ozempic, skip the missed dose.
Return to your regular schedule.
For Wegovy injection: Use Wegovy as soon as you can and then go back to your regular schedule.
If your next dose is due in less than 2 days (48 hours), skip the missed dose.
Return to your regular schedule.
Call your doctor if you miss more than 2 doses in a row of Wegovy injection.
You may need to restart Wegovy at a lower dose to avoid stomach problems.
This instruction applies to Wegovy tablets.
If you miss a dose of Wegovy tablets, skip the missed dose.
Then go back to your regular schedule for the next dose.
Do not use two doses of semaglutide at one time.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Overdose may cause severe nausea, vomiting, or low blood sugar.
Semaglutide can slow your digestion.
Semaglutide may take longer for your body to absorb any medicines you take by mouth.
Tell your doctor about all your other medicines, especially insulin or other diabetes medicines, such as dulaglutide, exenatide, liraglutide, Byetta, Trulicity, Victoza, and others.
Other drugs may affect semaglutide.
These other drugs include prescription medicines.
These other drugs include over-the-counter medicines.
These other drugs include vitamins.
These other drugs include herbal products.

Prior to first use, Ozempic should be stored in a refrigerator between 36ºF to 46ºF (2ºC to 8ºC).
Do not store in the freezer or directly adjacent to the refrigerator cooling element.
Do not freeze Ozempic and do not use Ozempic if Ozempic has been frozen.
After the first use of the Ozempic pen, the pen can be stored for 56 days.
The pen can be stored at controlled room temperature.
The pen can be stored in a refrigerator.
The controlled room temperature is 59°F to 86°F (15°C to 30°C).
The refrigerator temperature is 36°F to 46°F (2°C to 8°C).
Do not freeze.
Keep the pen cap on when not in use.
Ozempic should be protected from excessive heat and sunlight.

Always use a new needle for each injection.

Wegovy, Ozempic, Rybelsus Tablets
Store Wegovy tablets at room temperature between 68°F and 77°F (20°C to 25°C) in their original closed bottle until you are ready to take one.
