Q: Which GLP-1 drug is best for weight loss?
The most effective GLP-1 drug for weight loss currently is tirzepatide.
Tirzepatide is marketed under the name Zepbound for weight loss.
Tirzepatide is marketed under the name Mounjaro for diabetes.
Clinical trials and recent meta-analyses consistently show tirzepatide delivers the most substantial weight reduction among GLP-1 medications.
The average weight loss is up to 22.5% of body weight.
Q: Wegovy vs Ozempic: Which is Right for You?
Both Wegovy and Ozempic contain the GLP-1 agonist semaglutide.
Wegovy is prescribed for long-term weight management.
Wegovy is prescribed for certain forms of liver disease.
Ozempic is used to control blood sugar in type 2 diabetes.
Ozempic is used to help protect the heart.
Ozempic is used to help protect the kidneys.
Wegovy and Ozempic both lower the risk for heart-related complications.
They differ in other approved uses and populations.
They differ in side effects.
They differ in dosage forms.
They differ in costs.
Q: How long does it take for Ozempic to work?
Your blood glucose (sugar) levels should start to decline.
This decline should occur within the first days to a week.
This happens after you start using Ozempic (semaglutide).
You will start with lower doses for the first 4 weeks of treatment to help reduce initial stomach side effects.
This is not an effective dose to lower blood sugar over the long-term.
The full effect can take 12 weeks or longer, depending upon how well you tolerate Ozempic.
Q: Tirzepatide vs semaglutide: How do Tirzepatide vs semaglutide compare?
A: Semaglutide and tirzepatide are prescription medicines used for type 2 diabetes and long-term weight management.
Semaglutide products include Ozempic, Rybelsus, Wegovy, and Wegovy HD.
Tirzepatide products include Mounjaro and Zepbound.
Semaglutide and tirzepatide differ in dosing, approved uses, side effects, and cost.
You should discuss options with your healthcare provider.
Q: How long until I'll lose weight with Wegovy?
Wegovy and Wegovy HD take time to work.
You may see some weight loss results in the first 4 weeks of treatment.
Weight loss for both the pill and injection may increase.
This increase may happen when you get closer to your full maintenance dose.
This usually happens after a few months.
In studies, the average long-term weight loss with Wegovy was 35 to 39 lbs (16 to 17.7 kg).
It was about 21% of body weight with Wegovy HD.
Q: How does Ozempic work for weight loss?
Ozempic (semaglutide) can help you lose weight.
It can help you lose weight by reducing hunger.
It can help you lose weight by increasing feelings of fullness.
It can help you lose weight by helping you eat fewer calories.
Ozempic works like the natural GLP-1 hormone to regulate appetite and food intake.
Ozempic also slows stomach emptying.
This does not fully explain Ozempic's effects on long-term weight loss.
Q: Can you get tirzepatide from a compounding pharmacy?
Tirzepatide cannot be legally compounded in a U.S. pharmacy at this time.
This is because Tirzepatide was removed from the FDA drug shortage list.
The removal occurred as of October 2, 2024.
When drugs are in short supply, the FDA can allow temporary compounding of some medicines.
This allows patients to access their treatments.
Tirzepatide is now widely available as a prescription medicine.
The medicine is made by Eli Lilly.
It is sold under the brand name Mounjaro for type 2 diabetes.
It is sold under the brand name Zepbound for weight loss and sleep apnea.
Q: Why am I not losing weight on Ozempic?
Ozempic is not FDA-approved for weight loss.
Over 86% of people lose at least 5% of their body weight in trials.
The trials studied semaglutide.
Semaglutide is the active ingredient of Ozempic.
But the same clinical trials also show that just under 14% don't lose weight.
Some may even gain weight.
Q: Where and how should Ozempic be injected?
A: Ozempic (semaglutide) comes as a prefilled pen given by self-injection once a week, on the same day each week, with or without meals.
Ozempic is injected under the skin (subcutaneously) in your stomach (abdomen), thigh, or upper arm area.
Ozempic also comes as an oral tablet taken once daily if you prefer.
Sertraline is a prescription medicine.
Sertraline is used to treat depression.
Sertraline is used to treat obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD).
Sertraline is used to treat panic disorders.
Sertraline is used to treat posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD).
Sertraline is used to treat social anxiety disorder.
Sertraline is used to treat premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD).
Sertraline is sold under the brand name Zoloft.
Generic sertraline is widely available.
Sertraline belongs to a group of medicines called selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, or SSRIs.
Sertraline increases serotonin activity in the brain.
It does this by blocking the reuptake of serotonin back into nerve cells.
Serotonin is a chemical messenger that nerve cells use to communicate.
Consequently, more serotonin stays available.
Sertraline has only very weak effects on the reuptake of norepinephrine and dopamine.
Sertraline does not block monoamine oxidase.
Sertraline is available as tablets (25 mg, 50 mg, and 100 mg).
It is available as an oral solution (20 mg/mL).
It is available as capsules (150 mg and 200 mg).
Sertraline is not a controlled substance.
Sertraline is FDA-approved to treat six conditions in adults:
Major depressive disorder (MDD), the clinical term for depression.
Obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), where unwanted thoughts drive repeated behaviors.
Panic disorder, which causes sudden attacks of intense fear along with physical symptoms.
Posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD), which can follow a frightening or traumatic event.
Social anxiety disorder (SAD), also called social phobia.
Premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD), a severe form of premenstrual symptoms.
In children and teenagers, sertraline is approved only for OCD, from age 6 upward.
Sertraline's safety and effectiveness have not been established in children under 6 years of age.
Sertraline's safety and effectiveness have not been established in children and teenagers for any condition other than OCD.
Not every sertraline product is approved for all six conditions:
Sertraline tablets and oral solution are approved for all of them.
Sertraline 150 mg and 200 mg capsules are approved only for depression in adults and for OCD in adults and children aged 6 and over.
Sertraline carries a boxed warning, the FDA's most serious type of warning.
Antidepressants increased the risk of suicidal thoughts and behavior in children, teenagers, and young adults in short-term studies.
Pooled analyses of placebo-controlled antidepressant trials were conducted.
The trials covered about 77,000 adults and more than 4,500 children and teenagers.
The risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviors in people aged 24 and under was higher with an antidepressant than with placebo.
The risk varied considerably between different antidepressants.
The risk also varied by condition.
The highest rate of risk was in people being treated for depression.
The difference between antidepressant and placebo, per 1,000 patients treated, was:
Age (years) | Difference compared with placebo
Under 18 | 14 more patients with suicidal thoughts or behaviors
18 to 24 | 5 more patients
25 to 64 | 1 fewer patient
65 and over | 6 fewer patients
It is not known whether this risk continues beyond about four months of treatment.
There is good evidence that antidepressants delay depression coming back in adults.
Depression itself raises the risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviors.
Anyone taking sertraline should be watched closely.
They should be watched for worsening symptoms.
They should be watched for new or sudden changes in mood, behavior, thoughts, or feelings.
This monitoring is especially important in the first few months of treatment.
This monitoring is also important whenever the dose is changed.
Call your healthcare provider right away, or call 911 in an emergency, if you notice:
thoughts about suicide or dying, or attempts to harm yourself
new or worse depression, anxiety, or panic attacks
feeling agitated, restless, angry, or irritable
acting aggressive or violent, or acting on dangerous impulses
trouble sleeping
a big increase in activity or talking more than is normal for you
any other unusual change in behavior or mood
Tell family members or caregivers which symptoms to watch for.
Family members or caregivers can call your provider if you are not able to.
Common sertraline side effects:
The most common sertraline side effects are nausea, diarrhea or loose stools.
They include trouble sleeping, dry mouth, tiredness, dizziness, and sleepiness.
Other common side effects are tremor, indigestion, agitation, reduced appetite, increased sweating, and sexual problems.
The table below shows how often each side effect was reported.
The data comes from pooled placebo-controlled trials.
The trials included 3,066 adults.
The trials included 2,293 people taking placebo.
Side effect | Sertraline | Placebo
Nausea | 26% | 12%
Diarrhea or loose stools | 20% | 10%
Insomnia (trouble sleeping) | 20% | 13%
Dry mouth | 14% | 9%
Fatigue (tiredness) | 12% | 8%
Dizziness | 12% | 8%
Somnolence (sleepiness) | 11% | 6%
Tremor (shaking) | 9% | 2%
Dyspepsia (indigestion) | 8% | 4%
Agitation | 8% | 5%
Decreased appetite | 7% | 2%
Hyperhidrosis (heavy sweating) | 7% | 3%
Constipation | 6% | 4%
Decreased libido (lower sex drive) | 6% | 2%
Palpitations | 4% | 2%
Visual impairment | 4% | 2%
Vomiting | 4% | 1%
Some side effects were more common with particular conditions.
Sleepiness was more common in depression trials.
Insomnia and agitation were more common in OCD trials.
Constipation and agitation were more common in panic disorder trials.
Tiredness was more common in PTSD trials.
Insomnia, dizziness, tiredness, dry mouth, and a general feeling of being unwell were more common in social anxiety disorder trials.
In PMDD trials, sleepiness, dry mouth, dizziness, tiredness, and stomach pain were more common.
In these trials, 12% of people taking sertraline stopped treatment.
They stopped treatment because of a side effect.
This was compared with 4% of people taking placebo.
The most common reason for stopping was nausea (3%).
Diarrhea (2%) was another common reason for stopping.
Agitation (2%) was also a common reason for stopping.
Insomnia (2%) was a common reason for stopping.
Sexual side effects of sertraline:
SSRIs including sertraline can cause sexual problems.
In men, sexual problems can mean delayed ejaculation or being unable to ejaculate.
Sexual problems can also mean lower sex drive.
Sexual problems can also mean difficulty getting or keeping an erection.
In women, sexual problems can mean lower sex drive.
Sexual problems can mean delayed orgasm.
Sexual problems can mean being unable to have an orgasm.
Sexual side effect | Sertraline | Placebo
Ejaculation failure (men) | 8% | 1%
Decreased libido (men) | 7% | 2%
Erectile dysfunction (men) | 4% | 1%
Ejaculation disorder (men) | 3% | 0%
Male sexual dysfunction | 2% | 0%
Decreased libido (women) | 4% | 2%
Changes in sex drive and sexual function can also be part of depression and anxiety themselves.
The timing of when symptoms started matters.
These numbers may underestimate how often sexual side effects happen.
People and their providers do not always raise the subject.
The underestimation occurs because people and their providers do not always raise the subject.
Tell your provider about any change in sexual function, since there may be ways to manage it.
Serious sertraline side effects:
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to sertraline.
These signs include a rash, hives, swelling of the face, lips, tongue, or throat, or difficulty breathing.
Call your doctor at once if you have:
signs of serotonin syndrome, which can be life threatening: agitation, hallucinations, confusion, coma or other changes in mental state, a racing heartbeat, high or low blood pressure, dizziness, heavy sweating, fever, muscle stiffness, twitching or overactive reflexes, loss of coordination, seizures, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea;
unusual bleeding or bruising, including nosebleeds, bleeding gums, blood in vomit or stools, or black tarry stools;
signs of a manic episode: greatly increased energy, severe trouble sleeping, racing thoughts, reckless behavior, unusually grand ideas, excessive happiness or irritability, or talking more or faster than usual;
seizures or convulsions;
eye pain, changes in vision, or swelling or redness in or around the eye, which can be signs of angle-closure glaucoma (a sudden rise in pressure inside the eye);
Signs of low sodium in the blood (hyponatremia) include headache, trouble concentrating or thinking, memory problems, confusion, weakness, or feeling unsteady.
These symptoms can lead to falls.
Severe cases have caused hallucinations, fainting, seizures, coma, and death;
fainting, chest discomfort, or an irregular heartbeat, which can be signs of a heart rhythm problem called QTc prolongation.
Sertraline side effects in children and teenagers:
The overall pattern of side effects in 281 children and teenagers treated with sertraline was generally similar to that seen in adults.
Side effects were reported in at least 2% of children and teenagers.
These side effects occurred at least twice as often as with placebo.
These side effects were not common in adults.
The reported side effects included fever.
The reported side effects included hyperkinesia.
Hyperkinesia is abnormally increased movement.
The reported side effects included urinary incontinence.
The reported side effects included aggression.
The reported side effects included nosebleeds.
The reported side effects included purpura.
Purpura are purple spots under the skin.
The reported side effects included joint pain.
The reported side effects included weight loss.
The reported side effects included muscle twitching.
The reported side effects included anxiety.
Reduced appetite and weight loss have been seen with SSRIs.
Therefore, height and weight should be monitored during treatment.

Less common side effects were reported during studies.
Tachycardia was reported.
Tachycardia is a fast heartbeat.

Tinnitus is ringing in the ears.

Priapism is a long-lasting erection.

Sertraline came onto the market.
Side effects were reported after sertraline came onto the market.
Increased clotting times were reported as a side effect.
AV block was reported as a side effect.
Slow heartbeat was reported as a side effect.
Atrial arrhythmias were reported as a side effect.

Pancreatitis was reported as a side effect.

Vasculitis was reported as a side effect.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Do not take sertraline with a monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI).
Do not take sertraline within 14 days of stopping one.
This is because of the risk of serotonin syndrome.
This includes the antibiotic linezolid and intravenous methylene blue.
You also need to wait at least 14 days after stopping sertraline before starting an MAOI.
Do not take sertraline with pimozide, an antipsychotic medicine.
Do not take sertraline oral solution if you take disulfiram (Antabuse), because the solution contains alcohol.
Sertraline can raise the risk of bleeding.
This risk is particularly high if you also take aspirin.
It is also high if you take NSAIDs such as ibuprofen or naproxen.
The risk increases if you take other antiplatelet medicines.
The risk increases if you take warfarin.
The risk increases if you take other anticoagulants.
Screening for a personal or family history of bipolar disorder, mania, or hypomania is recommended before starting sertraline.
Treating a depressive episode with an antidepressant can trigger a manic episode.
This risk applies to someone with bipolar disorder.
Do not stop taking sertraline suddenly.
Adverse reactions can occur when sertraline is stopped.
Adverse reactions are especially likely when sertraline is stopped abruptly.
The dose should be lowered gradually where possible.
You should not take sertraline if you are allergic to sertraline or any of the ingredients in sertraline.
You should not take sertraline if you take an MAOI or have taken one in the past 14 days.
You should not take sertraline if you take pimozide.
Do not take the oral solution if you take disulfiram.
To make sure sertraline is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have or have ever had:
liver problems
kidney problems
heart problems, or risk factors for a heart rhythm problem called QTc prolongation
bipolar disorder or mania
seizures or convulsions
low sodium levels in your blood
bleeding problems, or you take a medicine that thins the blood
narrow angles in the eye or glaucoma
a stroke or high blood pressure
Pregnancy:
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant, plan to become pregnant, or become pregnant while taking sertraline.
Studies of women exposed to sertraline in the first trimester suggest no overall difference in the risk of major birth defects.
This difference is compared with the background rate.
Some studies have reported increases in specific defects.
Those results are inconclusive.
Taking sertraline late in pregnancy may raise the risk of newborn problems that need longer hospital stays, breathing support, and tube feeding.
It may also raise the risk of persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (PPHN), a serious lung problem.
Use in the month before delivery has been linked with an increase in the risk of postpartum hemorrhage.
Postpartum hemorrhage is heavy bleeding after birth.
The increase in risk is less than two-fold.
Stopping treatment is not automatically the safer choice.
In one study of 201 pregnant women with a history of major depression.
Those who stopped their antidepressant during pregnancy were more likely to relapse.
Those who continued were less likely to relapse.
This is a decision to make with your doctor.
Sertraline oral solution contains 12% alcohol.
It is not recommended during pregnancy.
This is because there is no known safe level of alcohol exposure in pregnancy.
There is a pregnancy registry that collects information on antidepressant use in pregnancy.
You can join by calling 1-866-961-2388.
Breastfeeding:
Low levels of sertraline and its breakdown products pass into breast milk.
A pooled analysis of 53 mother and infant pairs was conducted.
Exclusively breastfed infants had an average of 2% of the sertraline levels measured in their mothers' blood.
The levels ranged from 0% to 15%.
No side effects were seen in those infants.
There is no information on whether sertraline affects milk production.
Talk with your doctor about the benefits of breastfeeding alongside your need for treatment.
Children:
Sertraline is approved for OCD in children aged 6 to 17 years.
It is not known to be safe and effective in children under 6.
It is not known to be safe and effective for any other condition in this age group.
Children and teenagers taking sertraline should be monitored for worsening symptoms and suicidal thoughts.
This monitoring is especially important early in treatment and when the dose changes.
Children's weight and growth should be checked.
Older adults:
Older adults clear sertraline more slowly.
In one study, plasma clearance in people aged 65 and over was about 40% lower than in adults aged 25 to 32.
It took 2 to 3 weeks to reach steady levels rather than about 1 week.
Doctors usually start older adults at the low end of the dose range.
Older adults are at greater risk of low sodium levels in the blood.
This risk is particularly high if older adults take diuretics.
This risk is also particularly high if older adults are dehydrated.
Take sertraline exactly as prescribed, once a day, in either the morning or the evening.
Take sertraline at around the same time each day.
Tablets and capsules can be taken with or without food.
Taking sertraline with food causes only a small increase in the amount absorbed.
Swallow capsules whole.
Do not open, crush, or chew them.
Your doctor may start you on a low dose.
Your doctor may raise the dose gradually.
This is usually done no more often than once a week.
Keep taking sertraline even if you feel well.
Do not stop without talking to your doctor first.
Tell any doctor or lab that you take sertraline before you have tests.
Sertraline can cause a false-positive result on urine screening tests for benzodiazepines.
This can continue for several days after you stop taking it.
A confirmatory test will tell the difference.
Store sertraline at room temperature, 68°F to 77°F (20°C to 25°C).
How to take sertraline oral solution:
Sertraline oral solution must be diluted before you take it.
Do not mix sertraline in advance.
Use the dropper supplied with the bottle.
The dropper has graduation marks for 25 mg and 50 mg only.
Mix your dose with 4 ounces (half a cup) of water, ginger ale, lemon or lime soda, lemonade, or orange juice only.
Do not mix sertraline with any other liquid.
Drink sertraline straight away.
A slight haze may appear after mixing.
This is normal.
The dropper dispenser contains dry natural rubber, a form of latex.
Tell your doctor or pharmacist if you have a latex allergy.
The usual starting dose of sertraline is 25 mg or 50 mg once a day.
This depends on the condition being treated.
The usual treatment range is 50 mg to 200 mg a day.
The maximum dose for most conditions is 200 mg a day.
Condition | Starting dose | Usual range or maximum
Depression (MDD), adults | 50 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
OCD, adults | 50 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
OCD, ages 6 to 12 | 25 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
OCD, ages 13 to 17 | 50 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
Panic disorder, PTSD, social anxiety disorder | 25 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
PMDD, taken every day | 50 mg a day | Up to 150 mg a day
PMDD, taken in the luteal phase only | 50 mg a day | Up to 100 mg a day
If the starting dose is not enough, the dose can be raised.
The dose can be raised in steps of 25 mg to 50 mg a day.
Sertraline stays in the body for about 24 hours.
Doses are usually changed no more often than once a week.
Dosing for PMDD.
Sertraline can be taken every day of the menstrual cycle.
Sertraline can be taken only during the luteal phase.
The luteal phase starts 14 days before the period is due.
The luteal phase continues until the period starts.
This process is repeated with each cycle.
On daily dosing, the dose can go up by 50 mg per cycle.
The maximum dose is 150 mg a day.
On luteal phase dosing, the maximum is 100 mg a day.
The dose is taken as 50 mg for the first 3 days of the dosing cycle.
The dose is 100 mg for the rest of the cycle.
Sertraline 150 mg and 200 mg capsules.
These are not used to start treatment.
They are not used to titrate a dose.
This is because there are no lower capsule strengths.
They can be started in someone who has been taking 100 mg or 125 mg of another sertraline product for at least a week.
The recommended capsule dose is 150 mg or 200 mg once a day.
The maximum dose is 200 mg a day.
Coming off treatment requires switching back to another sertraline product.
This is because the dose cannot be lowered in steps using capsules alone.
Liver problems.
Sertraline is available in tablet and oral solution forms.
Mild liver impairment is defined as a Child-Pugh score of 5 or 6.
In patients with mild liver impairment, the starting dose of sertraline is halved.
In patients with mild liver impairment, the usual dose range of sertraline is halved.
Use of sertraline is not recommended in moderate liver impairment.
Use of sertraline is not recommended in severe liver impairment.
Moderate liver impairment is defined as a Child-Pugh score of 7 to 15.
Severe liver impairment is defined as a Child-Pugh score of 7 to 15.
Sertraline capsules are not recommended in mild, moderate, or severe liver impairment.
The dose cannot be adjusted with the strengths available.
This is the reason for the recommendation.
Kidney problems.
No dose change is needed for mild kidney impairment.
No dose change is needed for moderate kidney impairment.
No dose change is needed for severe kidney impairment.
No dose change is needed for people on dialysis.

Sertraline 50 mg tablets
Sertraline 100 mg tablets
Sertraline oral solution, 20 mg per mL
Sertraline 150 mg capsules
Sertraline 200 mg capsules
Capsule strengths are based on sertraline itself.
A 150 mg capsule contains 168 mg of sertraline hydrochloride.
A 200 mg capsule contains 224 mg.
Capsule appearance and imprints differ between manufacturers.
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember.
If it is almost time for your next dose, skip the one you missed.
Take the next dose at the usual time.
Do not take two doses at once.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
Effects reported after a sertraline tablet overdose include seizures.
These seizures may be delayed.
Effects reported after a sertraline tablet overdose also include changes in mental state.
These changes include coma.
Heart effects can also be delayed.
They include changes in the electrical activity of the heart.
These changes involve QRS and QTc prolongation.
High blood pressure is seen most often.
Low blood pressure is rare.
Low blood pressure can happen when other substances are taken.
Alcohol is one of the substances that can cause low blood pressure.
Serotonin syndrome can occur.
The risk is higher when several drugs that raise serotonin have been taken together.
Do not drink alcohol while you take sertraline.
In healthy volunteers, sertraline did not add to the immediate effects of alcohol on thinking and coordination.
Drinking is still not recommended during treatment.
Do not drive or operate machinery until you know how sertraline affects you.
Sertraline can cause sleepiness.
It can affect your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or react quickly.
Do not start or stop any other medicine without checking first, including over-the-counter products, vitamins, and herbal supplements.
Several medicines interact with sertraline.
Give a full list of everything you take to any provider who treats you.
The most important interactions are:
MAOIs.
Taking sertraline with an MAOI raises the risk of serotonin syndrome.
The combination is contraindicated.
Examples include selegiline, tranylcypromine, isocarboxazid, phenelzine, linezolid, and methylene blue.
Pimozide.
Sertraline raises pimozide levels.
This can lead to QTc prolongation.
This can lead to irregular heartbeats.
The combination is contraindicated.
Other medicines that raise serotonin.
These add to the risk of serotonin syndrome.
Examples include other SSRIs and SNRIs.
Examples include triptans for migraine.
Examples include tricyclic antidepressants.
Examples include opioids.
Examples include lithium.
Examples include tryptophan.
Examples include buspirone.
Examples include amphetamines.
Examples include St. John's wort.
Blood thinners and antiplatelet medicines.
Aspirin, clopidogrel, heparin, and warfarin can add to the bleeding risk.
If you take warfarin, your INR should be monitored carefully.
Medicines broken down by CYP2D6.
Sertraline blocks this liver enzyme.
Levels of these drugs can rise.
Doses may need to be lowered.
Examples include propafenone, flecainide, atomoxetine, desipramine, dextromethorphan, metoprolol, nebivolol, perphenazine, thioridazine, tolterodine, and venlafaxine.
Phenytoin and fosphenytoin.
Sertraline can raise phenytoin levels.
Levels should be checked when sertraline is started or the dose is changed.
Medicines that prolong the QTc interval.
These should generally be avoided.
Examples include certain antipsychotics.
Examples include ziprasidone.
Examples include iloperidone.
Examples include chlorpromazine.
Examples include mesoridazine.
Examples include droperidol.
Examples include certain antibiotics.
Examples include erythromycin.
Examples include gatifloxacin.
Examples include moxifloxacin.
Examples include sparfloxacin.
Examples include Class 1A antiarrhythmics.
Examples include quinidine.
Examples include procainamide.
Examples include Class III antiarrhythmics.
Examples include amiodarone.
Examples include sotalol.
Examples include others.
Examples include pentamidine.
Examples include methadone.
Examples include halofantrine.
Examples include mefloquine.
Examples include dolasetron.
Examples include probucol.
Examples include tacrolimus.
Disulfiram, with the oral solution only, because of its alcohol content.
No dose adjustment is needed when sertraline is taken with cimetidine, diazepam, lithium, atenolol, tolbutamide, or digoxin.
No dose adjustment is needed when sertraline is taken with medicines broken down by CYP3A4.
This list is not complete.
Many other prescription medicines, over-the-counter products, vitamins, and herbal products can interact with sertraline.
Is sertraline the same as Zoloft?
:
Yes.
Sertraline is the active ingredient.
Zoloft is the brand name Sertraline is sold under in the United States.
Sertraline is the generic version of Zoloft.
The two contain the same medicine, sertraline hydrochloride.
You may also see sertraline written as sertraline HCl.
Is sertraline an SSRI, and how does sertraline work?
:
Yes, sertraline is an SSRI, which stands for selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor.
Nerve cells in the brain release serotonin to send signals.
Then, they reabsorb serotonin.
Sertraline blocks that reabsorption.
More serotonin stays available between nerve cells.
Sertraline has very little effect on norepinephrine.
Sertraline has very little effect on dopamine.
Sertraline has no meaningful effect on adrenergic receptors.
Sertraline has no meaningful effect on cholinergic receptors.
Sertraline has no meaningful effect on GABA receptors.
Sertraline has no meaningful effect on histamine receptors.
Sertraline has no meaningful effect on benzodiazepine receptors.
This lack of effect on other receptors is part of why Sertraline is described as selective.
How long does sertraline take to work?
:
Sertraline levels in the blood become steady after about a week of once daily dosing.
The effect on symptoms takes longer.
The trials that established sertraline ran for 6 to 12 weeks.
These trials were for depression.
These trials were for OCD.
These trials were for panic disorder.
These trials were for PTSD.
These trials were for social anxiety disorder.
The trials ran for over three menstrual cycles.
These trials were for PMDD.
This duration gives a sense of the time frame over which the benefit was measured.
It takes time.
Doses are usually raised no more often than once a week.
You should keep taking sertraline even if you do not feel a change yet.
How long does sertraline stay in your system?
:
Sertraline has a half-life of about 26 hours.
This means half of what is in your blood is cleared in roughly a day.
At that rate, most of a dose has left your bloodstream within about 5 to 6 days.
Its main breakdown product, N-desmethylsertraline, lasts longer.
It has a half-life of 62 to 104 hours.
N-desmethylsertraline is much less active than sertraline itself.
Sertraline can also cause false-positive urine screening tests for benzodiazepines for several days after you stop taking it.
Should I take sertraline in the morning or at night?
:
Either is fine.
Sertraline is taken once a day.
It is taken in the morning or the evening.
The label does not favor one over the other.
What matters more is taking Sertraline at around the same time each day.
Insomnia was reported by 20% of people taking sertraline in trials.
Insomnia was reported by 13% of people on placebo in trials.
Sleepiness was reported by 11% of people taking sertraline in trials.
Sleepiness was reported by 6% of people on placebo in trials.
Some people find one timing suits them better than the other.
Talk to your doctor before changing when you take it.
Does sertraline cause weight gain or weight loss?
:
Reduced appetite is a common sertraline side effect.
This side effect was reported by 7% of adults in trials.
The report rate was 2% on placebo.
Increased appetite was reported less often, in fewer than 2% of people.
In children and teenagers taking sertraline for depression, there was a difference in weight change of roughly 1 kg compared with placebo over 10 weeks.
This difference represented slight weight loss on sertraline.
This difference represented slight weight gain on placebo.
Around 7% of children lost more than 7% of their body weight, compared with none on placebo.
In a longer 34 week extension study, weight gain was similar to what would be expected for their age.
Height and weight should be monitored in younger patients.
Tell your doctor about any weight change that concerns you.
Does sertraline make you tired or sleepy?
:
It can.
Sleepiness was reported by 11% of adults taking sertraline in trials.
This was compared with 6% on placebo.
Tiredness was reported by 12%.
This was compared with 8%.
Sertraline can also affect how clearly you think and how quickly you react.
Do not drive or use machinery until you know how Sertraline affects you.
Is sertraline a controlled substance?
:
No.
Sertraline is not a controlled substance.
A study compared sertraline with alprazolam and d-amphetamine.
Sertraline did not produce effects associated with a potential for misuse.
The other two medicines produced effects such as euphoria or drug liking.
Can sertraline cause diarrhea or nausea?
:
Yes, Sertraline are the two most common sertraline side effects.
Nausea was reported by 26% of people taking sertraline in trials, compared with 12% on placebo.
Diarrhea or loose stools were reported by 20% of people taking sertraline in trials, compared with 10% on placebo.
Nausea was also the most common reason people stopped treatment.
Tell your doctor if Sertraline do not settle.
How do you stop taking sertraline safely?
:
Lower the dose gradually rather than stopping all at once, and only under your doctor's guidance.
Symptoms reported after stopping a serotonergic antidepressant include nausea, sweating, low or unstable mood, irritability, agitation, dizziness, unusual sensations such as electric shock feelings, tremor, anxiety, confusion, headache, lethargy, insomnia, hypomania, ringing in the ears, and seizures.
These symptoms are especially present when a serotonergic antidepressant is stopped abruptly.
Can you drink alcohol while taking sertraline?
:
Drinking alcohol is not recommended while you take sertraline.
In healthy volunteers, sertraline did not increase the immediate effects of alcohol on thinking.
In healthy volunteers, sertraline did not increase the immediate effects of alcohol on coordination.
The advice for patients is still to avoid sertraline with alcohol.
Separately, do not take sertraline oral solution with disulfiram.
The solution contains 12% alcohol.
Can you take sertraline while pregnant?
:
That depends on your situation.
Sertraline is a decision to make with your doctor rather than on your own.
Studies of first trimester use suggest no overall difference in the risk of major birth defects.
Use late in pregnancy has been linked with newborn breathing problems.
Use late in pregnancy has been linked with persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn.
At the same time, stopping an antidepressant during pregnancy raises the risk of depression.
Depression comes back.
The oral solution specifically is not recommended in pregnancy because oral solution contains alcohol.
What are sertraline 150 mg and 200 mg capsules for?
:
Sertraline capsules come in 150 mg and 200 mg only.
Sertraline capsules are not used to start treatment.
Sertraline capsules are for people who need a higher dose.
These people have already been taking 100 mg or 125 mg of another sertraline product.
They have been taking this product for at least a week.
The maximum is 200 mg a day, the same as for tablets.
Capsules should be swallowed whole and not opened, crushed, or chewed.
If you need to come off sertraline, the dose has to be lowered gradually.
This must be done using another sertraline product.
There is no capsule strength below 150 mg.
Capsules are approved for depression in adults.
Capsules are approved for OCD from age 6.
Capsules are not approved for panic disorder.
Capsules are not approved for PTSD.
Capsules are not approved for social anxiety disorder.
Capsules are not approved for PMDD.
Does sertraline interact with warfarin?
:
Yes, in two ways.
Sertraline can add to warfarin's effect on bleeding risk.
Both medicines bind tightly to proteins in the blood.
Taking them together can raise the free level of one or both.
If you take warfarin with sertraline, your INR should be monitored carefully.
Q: How long does Zoloft (sertraline) withdrawal last?
Sertraline (Zoloft) withdrawal symptoms typically last up to 1 to 2 weeks.
They may last even longer in some people.
You should avoid abruptly stopping sertraline treatment unless otherwise directed by your healthcare provider.
Gradually reducing the dose over 2 to 4 weeks can help to prevent severe withdrawal symptoms in most people.
This process should be directed by your doctor.
Q: How long does Zoloft (sertraline) take to work?
Zoloft (sertraline) is a common SSRI antidepressant.
It can take about 4 to 6 weeks of regular dosing to reach Zoloft's full therapeutic effect for depression.
For other uses, a full effect may take up to 12 weeks of treatment.
Other uses include obsessive compulsive disorder (OCD).
Other uses include posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD).
Benefits may be seen in the first menstrual cycle after starting treatment.
This occurs when the treatment is prescribed for premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD).
Q: Why does does sertraline make you sleepy?
Sertraline can make some people feel sleepy.
This is due to Sertraline's effects on chemicals in the brain.
This effect is especially common in the first days to weeks of treatment.
Others experience the opposite problem and develop insomnia instead.
Clinical trials suggest that drowsiness, fatigue, and other sleep-related side effects are common.
Drowsiness is usually mild.
Drowsiness often improves as the body adapts to the medication over time.
Q: Does Zoloft (sertraline) cause weight gain?
Yes, Zoloft (sertraline) can cause weight gain.
For most people, the amount is small.
A large 2024 U.S. study was conducted.
Adults who started sertraline gained about half a pound on average after 6 months.
These adults gained about 3 pounds after 2 years.
Many people gain no weight at all.
Some people, especially children, lose weight.
Prozac and Zoloft represent two of the most widely prescribed antidepressant medications.
These medications are in the United States.
Both medications belong to a class of drugs known as selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs).
SSRIs have revolutionized depression treatment since their introduction in the late 1980s and early 1990s.
Q: Is Zoloft (sertraline) a controlled substance?
A: No, Zoloft (sertraline) is not a controlled substance.
Zoloft is in an antidepressant drug class known as selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs).
Zoloft is commonly used to treat depression, anxiety, panic attacks and other mood disorders.
Doses should be stopped slowly to help prevent withdrawal symptoms.
Zoloft can be used for long-term treatment if needed.
Simvastatin belongs to a group of drugs called HMG CoA reductase inhibitors, or "statins."
Simvastatin is used to lower blood levels of "bad" cholesterol (low-density lipoprotein, or LDL).
It is used to increase levels of "good" cholesterol (high-density lipoprotein, or HDL).
It is used to lower triglycerides (a type of fat in the blood).
Simvastatin is used to lower the risk of stroke, heart attack, and other heart complications.
This is done in people with diabetes, coronary heart disease, or other risk factors.
Simvastatin is used in adults and children who are at least 10 years old.
In rare cases, simvastatin can cause a condition.
The condition results in the breakdown of skeletal muscle tissue.
The condition potentially leads to kidney failure.
Call your doctor right away if you have unexplained muscle pain.
Call your doctor right away if you have tenderness.
Call your doctor right away if you have weakness.
Call your doctor right away if you have a fever.
Call your doctor right away if you have unusual tiredness.
Call your doctor right away if you have dark colored urine.

Q: Do statins cause rhabdomyolysis, and how is rhabdomyolysis treated?
A: Rhabdomyolysis is a rare but serious side effect of statins, a class of cholesterol-lowering medications.
Rhabdomyolysis involves the breakdown of muscle tissue.
The breakdown releases substances into the bloodstream.
These substances can cause kidney damage.
Statins are generally safe.
Rhabdomyolysis from statins is uncommon.
Rhabdomyolysis is most likely to occur at high doses.
Rhabdomyolysis is most likely to occur when combined with certain other drugs.
Q: When is the best time to take simvastatin?
A: The best time to take simvastatin is in the evening.
This is because your body makes most of its cholesterol at night when you are not eating.
One study measured people's total cholesterol and low-density lipoproteins.
The study found that concentrations of these two lipids were significantly lower when simvastatin was taken in the evening.
The concentrations were higher when simvastatin was taken in the morning.
Q: Can I drink grapefruit juice with simvastatin?
You should not drink grapefruit juice with simvastatin.

This increases the risk of side effects.
Side effects include liver damage.
Side effects include a condition called rhabdomyolysis.
Rhabdomyolysis is the excessive breakdown of muscle tissue.
Occasionally, rhabdomyolysis can cause kidney damage and death.
Spironolactone is a potassium-sparing diuretic.
Spironolactone is used for heart failure.
Spironolactone is used for high blood pressure.
Spironolactone is used for fluid build-up caused by liver or kidney disease.
Spironolactone is used for a hormone condition called primary hyperaldosteronism.
Spironolactone is sometimes prescribed off-label for women with acne or excessive facial hair growth (hirsutism).
Spironolactone works by blocking a hormone called aldosterone.
Aldosterone causes your body to pass more salt and water in your urine.
Aldosterone keeps potassium in.
Spironolactone is called potassium-sparing.
Spironolactone makes spironolactone different from most other water pills.
Most other water pills flush potassium out.
Spironolactone blocks androgens such as testosterone from binding to the androgen receptor.
This mechanism explains several of Spironolactone's side effects.
These side effects include breast tenderness and breast growth.
Spironolactone is FDA-approved to treat adults with:
Heart failure (NYHA Class III-IV) with a reduced ejection fraction to help live longer, reduce swelling, and reduce the need for hospital stays.
Spironolactone is taken alongside other heart failure medicines rather than on Spironolactone's own.
High blood pressure as an add-on treatment for people whose blood pressure is not controlled well enough by other medicines.
Lowering blood pressure reduces the risk of stroke and heart attack.
Fluid build-up (edema) caused by cirrhosis of the liver has occurred.
Cutting back on fluid and salt has not been enough to treat this edema.
Swelling caused by nephrotic syndrome (a kidney disorder) has occurred.
Treating the underlying disease has not worked well enough to treat this swelling.
Using other water pills has not worked well enough to treat this swelling.
Primary hyperaldosteronism, which is a condition where the adrenal glands make too much aldosterone.
Spironolactone is used short-term to prepare for surgery.
It is used long-term for people who are not suitable for surgery.
Spironolactone tablets come in 25 mg, 50 mg, and 100 mg strengths.
Spironolactone 25 mg is the strength most often used to start treatment.
There is also a banana-flavored oral liquid for people who cannot swallow tablets.
The liquid and the tablets are not interchangeable.
Never switch between the liquid and the tablets without your doctor changing your dose.

These signs include difficulty breathing.

a fever, sore throat, or unusual bruising or bleeding, which can be signs of a problem with your blood cells; or

Long-term spironolactone side effects:
The main long-term concerns with spironolactone are chemical rather than something you would feel.
Over time, spironolactone can push your potassium too high.
Spironolactone can lower your sodium.
Spironolactone can lower your magnesium.
Spironolactone can lower your calcium.
Spironolactone can raise your blood sugar.
Spironolactone can raise your uric acid.
High uric acid occasionally brings on gout.
This is why you need regular blood tests for as long as you take it, not just at the start.
In animal studies, spironolactone given at high doses over long periods caused tumors in rats.
The tumors were mainly in hormone-producing glands and the liver.
No increase in tumors was seen at the lower doses tested.
These doses were still around five times the highest recommended human dose when adjusted for body size.
Whether this happens in people is not known.

Pregnancy:
Spironolactone blocks the effect of male hormones.
Male hormones are needed for a male baby to develop normally.
In animal studies, male offspring exposed in the womb showed feminized development.
The limited information available in humans has not shown a clear increase in birth defects.
The concern remains.
Your doctor will usually avoid spironolactone in pregnancy.
Your doctor will talk through the possible risk to a male baby with you.
Untreated heart failure, cirrhosis, and high blood pressure also carry real risks in pregnancy.
This is a decision to make with your doctor rather than by stopping on your own.

Spironolactone Spironolactone does not pass into breast milk.

Canrenone has been found in breast milk.
The amounts found are very small.
These amounts are not expected to affect a breastfed baby.
No side effects were reported in the one infant studied over a short period.
The long-term picture is not known.
Talk to your doctor so you can weigh this up together.

Older adults:
Spironolactone is cleared by the kidneys.
Kidney function tends to fall with age.
Older adults are therefore more likely to build up a high potassium level.
Kidney function and potassium need monitoring.

The usual starting dose of spironolactone tablets for adults is 25 mg to 100 mg a day.

Spironolactone dosage for heart failure:
Treatment starts at spironolactone 25 mg once a day.
This is provided your potassium level is 5.0 mEq/L or lower and your kidney function (eGFR) is above 50.
If you tolerate that, the dose may go up to 50 mg once a day.
If your potassium rises too high.
The dose may drop to 25 mg every other day.
If your eGFR is between 30 and 50, your doctor may start you at 25 mg every other day.
This is because of the higher risk of high potassium.
Spironolactone dosage for high blood pressure:
The usual starting dose is spironolactone 25 mg to 100 mg a day, taken as one dose or split into two.
The dose can be adjusted every two weeks.
Doses above 100 mg a day do not generally lower blood pressure any further.

Spironolactone is given for at least five days.

This applies when spironolactone is the only water pill being used.
Spironolactone dosage for primary hyperaldosteronism:
The dose is 100 mg to 400 mg a day when preparing for surger y.
The lowest dose that works is used long term.
This is for people who are not suitable for surgery.

The oral liquid has its own separate doses because oral liquid is absorbed differently.

eplerenone, which should not be taken with spironolactone at all;

mitotane, which should be avoided because spironolactone lowers spironolactone's level in the blood; and
other blood pressure medicines and water pills, which add to the blood pressure lowering effect.

Is spironolactone a diuretic?
:
Yes.
Spironolactone is a diuretic, or water pill.
Spironolactone belongs to the group called potassium-sparing diuretics.
Spironolactone works by blocking aldosterone, a hormone that makes your kidneys hold onto salt and water.
Unlike most water pills, spironolactone makes your body keep potassium in.
Most water pills make you lose potassium.
It is a weaker diuretic than medicines such as furosemide.
Furosemide is one reason spironolactone is often given alongside another water pill.
Spironolactone is often given alongside another water pill rather than instead of one.
Is spironolactone used for acne?
:
Spironolactone is not approved by the FDA for treating acne.
Doctors sometimes prescribe Spironolactone off-label for hormonal acne in women.
Off-label means a doctor is prescribing a medicine for something other than its approved uses.
This practice is legal.
This practice is common.
Off-label means the FDA has not reviewed the medicine for that purpose.
The reason spironolactone is used this way relates to how spironolactone works.
Spironolactone blocks aldosterone.
Spironolactone attaches to the androgen receptor.
The androgen receptor is the docking point for male hormones such as testosterone.
Male hormones increase how much oil the skin produces.
Oil production is one factor in acne.
Acne is not an approved use.
There is no FDA-approved dose for it.
Any dose is chosen by the prescriber.
Spironolactone is generally avoided in pregnancy.
Spironolactone may affect how a male baby develops.
This is an important consideration for anyone taking Spironolactone for a skin condition rather than a heart or kidney condition.
If you are considering spironolactone for acne, this is a conversation to have with a doctor or dermatologist.
A doctor or dermatologist can review your full medical history.
Spironolactone still carries the same risk of raising your potassium level.
This risk exists regardless of what spironolactone is prescribed for.
Does spironolactone cause weight gain?
:
Weight gain is not a recognized side effect of spironolactone.
Spironolactone is not listed in the medicine's official prescribing information.
Spironolactone is a diuretic.
Spironolactone usually causes a small drop in weight in the first week or two as your body sheds extra fluid.
You should tell your doctor if you are gaining weight while taking it.
It is not worth assuming the medicine is to blame.
Rapid weight gain with swelling in your ankles or legs or shortness of breath can be a sign of fluid building up.
This may mean the condition being treated needs a closer look.
Slower weight gain is more often down to appetite, activity, or another medicine.
Does spironolactone cause hair loss?
:
Hair thinning, known as alopecia, is listed as a possible side effect of spironolactone.
How often spironolactone happens is not known.
Spironolactone is reported far less often than breast tenderness or breast growth.
The picture is complicated.
Spironolactone is prescribed off label for hair loss in women.
This is based on the idea that blocking male hormones may help with hormone-related thinning.
Spironolactone is not approved by the FDA for treating hair loss.
If your hair is thinning while you take it, speak to your doctor.
There are several possible causes worth ruling out.
How long does it take for spironolactone to work?
:
It depends what you are taking spironolactone for.
Spironolactone reaches its highest level in your blood in about 2 to 3 hours.
The active breakdown product of spironolactone peaks at around 4 hours.
Spironolactone starts having an effect on your kidneys the same day.
The results you notice take longer.
For fluid build-up, spironolactone is given for at least five days.
The dose is increased after this period.
This delay is because the full effect takes that long to show.
For high blood pressure, the dose is adjusted at two-week intervals.
So, give spironolactone a couple of weeks before judging it.
For heart failure, the benefit is a lower risk of being admitted to hospital and living longer.
This benefit is not something you feel week to week.
How long does spironolactone stay in your system?
:
Spironolactone leaves your blood very quickly.
It has a half-life of about 1.4 hours.
This means half of Spironolactone is gone in that time.
Spironolactone's active breakdown products last much longer, with half-lives of roughly 14 to 17 hours.
Because of half-lives, the effect of a dose carries on well after the spironolactone has gone.
It takes a few days for spironolactone to clear fully after you stop.
Clearance is slower if you have cirrhosis.
Is spironolactone a blood thinner?
:
No.
Spironolactone is not a blood thinner.
Spironolactone does not affect blood clotting.
Spironolactone is not used to prevent clots or strokes.
Warfarin, apixaban, or aspirin are used to prevent clots or strokes.
Spironolactone is a diuretic that lowers blood pressure and reduces fluid build-up.
Spironolactone does interact with some blood thinners indirectly.
Heparin can raise potassium.
Low molecular weight heparin can raise potassium.
Spironolactone can raise potassium.
Taking Heparin with spironolactone increases the risk of a high potassium level.
Taking low molecular weight heparin with spironolactone increases the risk of a high potassium level.
Can I stop taking spironolactone suddenly?
:
Spironolactone does not cause withdrawal symptoms.
Spironolactone does not need to be tapered the way a steroid does.
Even so, do not stop Spironolactone without speaking to your doctor.
Stopping Spironolactone can allow fluid to build up again.
Stopping Spironolactone can allow your blood pressure to rise.
Stopping Spironolactone can allow your heart failure to worsen.
If you are taking Spironolactone for heart failure, Spironolactone is one of the medicines shown to help people live longer.
Spironolactone is one of the medicines shown to help people with heart failure live longer.
If side effects are the reason you want to stop.
A dose change may be an option.
A different medicine may be an option.
Can you take spironolactone at night?
:
There is no fixed time of day for spironolactone.
What matters more is that you take Spironolactone at the same time each day.
You should always take it the same way with regard to food.
This is because food changes how much you absorb.
Many people prefer the morning because spironolactone makes you pass more urine.
A late dose can mean getting up in the night.
If a nighttime dose suits you better, check with your doctor or pharmacist first.
Can you drink alcohol while taking spironolactone?
:
There is no direct interaction between alcohol and spironolactone.
Drinking is best kept modest.
Both can lower your blood pressure and make you light-headed.
Together they raise the chance of feeling dizzy or faint.
This is particularly true when you stand up.
Alcohol is also a problem in Alcohol's own right if you are taking spironolactone for liver disease.
Ask your doctor what is reasonable for your situation.
Can you take spironolactone while pregnant?
:
Spironolactone is generally avoided in pregnancy.
Spironolactone blocks the action of male hormones.
In animal studies, male offspring exposed before birth developed abnormally.
Human data are limited.
Human data have not shown a clear increase in birth defects.
The concern is enough.
The manufacturer advises avoiding Spironolactone.
The manufacturer advises discussing the potential risk to a male baby.
Do not stop spironolactone on your own if you find out you are pregnant.
Tell your doctor straight away.
Untreated heart failure carries serious risks in pregnancy.
Untreated cirrhosis carries serious risks in pregnancy.
Untreated high blood pressure carries serious risks in pregnancy.
Your treatment will need to be changed carefully.
Does spironolactone cause constipation?
:
Constipation is not a listed side effect of spironolactone.
The digestive side effects that have been reported are the opposite.
They include diarrhea and stomach cramping.
They also include nausea, vomiting, and indigestion.
If you are constipated while taking spironolactone, another cause is more likely.
Spironolactone is worth mentioning to your pharmacist.
The pharmacist can check the rest of your medicines.
Is 200 mg of spironolactone too much?
:
It depends on the reason you are taking spironolactone.
The approved dose range for fluid build-up goes up to 200 mg a day.
The approved dose range for primary hyperaldosteronism goes up to 400 mg a day.
200 mg is within the licensed range for fluid build-up.
200 mg is within the licensed range for primary hyperaldosteronism.
For high blood pressure, Spironolactone would be above the usual range.
Doses over 100 mg a day do not lower blood pressure any further.
For heart failure, the usual dose is 25 mg to 50 mg a day.
Therefore, 200 mg would be well above what is normally used.
Higher doses carry a greater risk of high potassium.
Higher doses also carry a greater risk of breast growth in men.
If your dose seems high for your condition, ask your prescriber.
Do not adjust the dose yourself.
Why is spironolactone described as a high-risk medicine?
:
The main reason is the risk of a high potassium level.
A high potassium level can cause a dangerous change in your heart rhythm.
A high potassium level often gives no warning symptoms.
The risk rises if your kidneys do not work well.
The risk rises if you take other medicines that raise potassium.
Several very common medicines raise potassium.
Ibuprofen is a very common medicine that raises potassium.
Some blood pressure tablets are very common medicines that raise potassium.
This risk is manageable.
Blood tests are built into treatment.
Your potassium should be checked within a week of starting spironolactone or changing your dose.
It should be checked regularly after that.
Spironolactone also needs care in pregnancy and in people with liver disease.
Q: Winlevi vs Spironolactone: How do they compare?
A: Winlevi (clascoterone) and spironolactone are both prescribed to treat acne.
Winlevi is a topical cream.
Spironolactone is available in an oral tablet and oral suspension.
Tamsulosin is used to treat the symptoms of an enlarged prostate, also called benign prostatic hyperplasia or BPH.
These symptoms include a weak urine stream.
They include trouble starting to urinate.
They include dribbling.
They include a feeling of not emptying the bladder completely.
They include needing to urinate often or urgently.
They include getting up at night to urinate.
Tamsulosin belongs to a group of medicines called alpha blockers.
Tamsulosin works by blocking alpha-1 receptors in the prostate and bladder neck.
Blocking these receptors relaxes the muscle there.
This improves urine flow and eases BPH symptoms.
Tamsulosin is taken once daily, about half an hour after the same meal each day.
Capsules should be swallowed whole and not crushed, chewed, or opened.
The tamsulosin brand name was Flomax.
Flomax has now been discontinued in the U.S.
Generic tamsulosin is available.
Tamsulosin is not approved to treat high blood pressure.
Tamsulosin is not approved for use in women or children.
Do not take tamsulosin if you are allergic to tamsulosin or to any of the ingredients in the capsule.
Do not take tamsulosin with other alpha blockers.
Examples of other alpha blockers include alfuzosin (Uroxatral), doxazosin (Cardura), prazosin (Minipress), silodosin (Rapaflo), and terazosin (Hytrin).
Do not take tamsulosin 0.4 mg with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor such as ketoconazole.
These medicines raise tamsulosin levels in your blood.
Tamsulosin can lower your blood pressure when you stand up.
This may make you feel dizzy or faint.
This is most likely when you first start taking it.
This is most likely when your dose changes.
This is most likely when you start Tamsulosin again after a break.
Get up slowly from sitting or lying down.
Be careful driving or doing anything that needs you to be alert until you know how tamsulosin affects you.
In rare cases, tamsulosin can cause priapism.
These cases probably occur in fewer than 1 in 50,000 patients.
Priapism is a painful erection that lasts a long time.
Priapism is not related to sexual activity.
Get medical help straight away if this happens, because without prompt treatment priapism can lead to permanent erectile dysfunction.
An enlarged prostate and prostate cancer can occur together.
Ask your doctor about prostate cancer screening before you start tamsulosin and at regular intervals while you are taking it.
Tamsulosin can cause a problem called intraoperative floppy iris syndrome (IFIS) during cataract or glaucoma surgery.
Tell your eye surgeon that you take tamsulosin or have taken tamsulosin in the past, even if you stopped months ago.
Do not stop taking tamsulosin before surgery unless your surgeon tells you to.
Starting tamsulosin is not recommended.
This applies if you are already booked in for cataract or glaucoma surgery.
If you stop taking tamsulosin for several days, talk to your doctor before starting again.
You will need to restart at the 0.4 mg dose.
Many other medicines can interact with tamsulosin.
Tell your doctor about everything you take, including prescription medicines, over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
To make sure tamsulosin is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:
an allergy to tamsulosin or any of tamsulosin's ingredients
an allergy to sulfa drugs
kidney problems
liver problems
low blood pressure (hypotension)
Also tell your doctor if you are planning to have cataract or glaucoma surgery.
Ask your doctor about prostate cancer screening before starting tamsulosin and at regular intervals afterwards.
Tamsulosin is not approved for use in women or in children.
Take tamsulosin exactly as prescribed.
Follow the directions on your prescription label and read any medication guides or instruction sheets.
Take once a day, about 30 minutes after the same meal each day.
For example, 30 minutes after dinner.
Swallow the capsule whole.
Do not crush, chew, break, or open it.
If you stop taking tamsulosin for several days, do not start again without talking to your doctor.
Store at room temperature, away from moisture and heat.
Dosing information:
Usual adult dose for benign prostatic hyperplasia:
0.4 mg by mouth once a day, taken about half an hour after the same meal each day.
If symptoms have not improved after 2 to 4 weeks at 0.4 mg.
The dose may be increased to 0.8 mg once a day.
Tamsulosin 0.4 mg should not be taken with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor such as ketoconazole.
If tamsulosin is stopped or interrupted for several days.
Treatment should start again.
The treatment should start at 0.4 mg once a day.
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember.
If you have missed the dose for the whole day, skip the dose.
Take your next dose at the usual time.
Do not take two doses at once.
If you stop taking tamsulosin for several days in a row, talk to your doctor before starting again.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
An overdose can cause a serious drop in blood pressure.
Lying down flat can help until medical help arrives.
Avoid driving or other activities that could be dangerous until you know how tamsulosin affects you.
It can make you feel dizzy or lightheaded.
Avoid getting up too quickly from sitting or lying down, or you may feel dizzy.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction, such as hives, itching, rash, swelling of your face, tongue, or throat, or trouble breathing.
Rarely, tamsulosin has been linked to severe skin reactions with blistering and peeling of the skin.
Stop taking tamsulosin and call your doctor at once if you have:
a lightheaded feeling, like you might pass out
a painful erection that will not go away and is not related to sexual activity
What are the common side effects of tamsulosin?
:
Common tamsulosin side effects may include:
headache
dizziness
runny or stuffy nose, sore throat, cough, sinus congestion
flu-like symptoms or infection
weakness or lack of energy
back pain, chest pain
diarrhea, nausea
drowsiness or trouble sleeping
tooth problems
abnormal ejaculation, including a smaller amount of semen or no semen at all
reduced interest in sex
blurred vision
Some side effects are more likely at the higher 0.8 mg dose than at the usual 0.4 mg starting dose.
In clinical studies, abnormal ejaculation was reported by about 8 in 100 men taking 0.4 mg.
About 18 in 100 men taking 0.8 mg reported abnormal ejaculation.
Blurred vision was reported by about 2 in 100 men taking 0.8 mg.
Blurred vision was uncommon at 0.4 mg.
This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Tamsulosin is broken down in the body mainly by two liver enzymes, CYP3A4 and CYP2D6.
Medicines that block these enzymes can raise the amount of tamsulosin in your blood.
These medicines can increase the risk of side effects.
These medicines block these enzymes.
Do not take tamsulosin with:
strong CYP3A4 inhibitors, such as ketoconazole
other alpha blockers, such as alfuzosin, doxazosin, prazosin, silodosin, or terazosin
Use with caution, and tell your doctor if you take:
moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors, such as erythromycin
strong CYP2D6 inhibitors, such as paroxetine, or moderate CYP2D6 inhibitors, such as terbinafine
cimetidine
warfarin
medicines for erectile dysfunction or pulmonary arterial hypertension known as PDE5 inhibitors, including avanafil (Stendra), sildenafil (Viagra, Revatio), tadalafil (Adcirca, Cialis), and vardenafil (Levitra).
Taking these with tamsulosin can lower blood pressure enough to cause symptoms.
Some of these cautions matter more at the higher 0.8 mg dose than at 0.4 mg.
Your doctor will decide what is right for you.
No dose change is needed if you take tamsulosin with nifedipine.
No dose change is needed if you take tamsulosin with atenolol.
No dose change is needed if you take tamsulosin with enalapril.
No dose change is needed if you take tamsulosin with digoxin.
No dose change is needed if you take tamsulosin with theophylline.
No dose change is needed if you take tamsulosin with furosemide.
This list is not complete.
Many other medicines may interact with tamsulosin.
This includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.

Tirzepatide is a once-weekly injection.
It is available as two brands.
One brand is Zepbound.
Zepbound is for weight loss, weight management, and obstructive sleep apnea.
The other brand is Mounjaro.
Mounjaro is for type 2 diabetes.
Mounjaro is also to lower the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events.
This risk reduction applies to those with type 2 diabetes at high risk.
Zepbound (tirzepatide) is FDA-approved for weight loss and weight management in adults with obesity.
It is also approved for overweight adults with at least one weight-related medical condition.
Zepbound is also approved to treat moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) in adults with obesity.
Mounjaro (tirzepatide) is FDA-approved for type 2 diabetes in adults and children aged 10 years and older.
It is used to help improve blood sugar (glucose) levels.
It is used alongside diet and exercise.
Mounjaro is approved to lower the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events.
These events include cardiovascular death, non-fatal heart attack, or non-fatal stroke.
This approval applies to adults with type 2 diabetes.
It applies to those who are at high risk for these events.
Tirzepatide is a dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor agonist.
Tirzepatide activates two gut hormone receptors.
These receptors help regulate appetite, digestion, and blood sugar.
Tirzepatide works by reducing appetite.
It increases feelings of fullness.
It slows gastric emptying.
It boosts insulin release when blood sugar is high.
It reduces glucagon secretion.
Tirzepatide is self-administered as a subcutaneous (under the skin) injection using a single-dose pen, KwikPen, or vial with syringe.
It should always be used together with diet and exercise.
Tirzepatide should not be used in people with type 1 diabetes.
It is not known if tirzepatide is safe in people who have had pancreatitis.
Patients on Tirzepatide 15 mg weekly lost an average of 20.9% or 48.6 lb of body weight.
This occurred after 72 weeks in the Zepbound Study 1 (NCT04184622).
Placebo patients lost an average of 3.1% or 7.2 lb of body weight.
Study 1 was a 72-week trial.
The trial compared Zepbound weight loss with placebo.
The trial included adults with obesity or overweight with at least one weight-related condition.
The trial excluded patients with type 2 diabetes.
Zepbound Weight Loss Results at 72 Weeks by Dose:
Treatment dose | Average % weight loss | Average weight lost | Average starting weight
Zepbound 5 mg | 15.0% | 34.0 lb | 226.9 lb
Zepbound 10 mg | 19.5% | 45.5 lb | 233.2 lb
Zepbound 15 mg | 20.9% | 48.6 lb | 232.8 lb
Placebo | 3.1% | 7.2 lb | 231.0 lb
In the Mounjaro prescribing information, SURPASS-1 (NCT03954834) was a 40-week trial.
The trial was in adults with type 2 diabetes.
The adults had blood sugar that was not adequately controlled with diet and exercise alone.
Patients received once-weekly Mounjaro 5 mg, 10 mg, or 15 mg, or placebo.
After 40 weeks, 78% to 85% of adults taking Mounjaro achieved an HbA1c below 7%.
This is compared with 23% of adults taking a placebo.
The average HbA1c change was -1.8% with Mounjaro 5 mg.
The average HbA1c change was -1.7% with Mounjaro 10 mg.
The average HbA1c change was -1.7% with Mounjaro 15 mg.
The average HbA1c change was -0.1% with placebo.
SURPASS-CVOT randomized 13,299 adults with type 2 diabetes and established cardiovascular disease.
The adults were randomized to Mounjaro (tirzepatide, up to 15 mg once weekly) or dulaglutide 1.5 mg once weekly.
Dulaglutide is a GLP-1 medicine already approved to reduce cardiovascular risk.
The study had a median follow-up of about 4 years.
Cardiovascular death, non-fatal heart attack, or non-fatal stroke occurred in 12.1% of those taking Mounjaro.
These events occurred in 13.0% of those taking dulaglutide.
The hazard ratio was 0.92 with a 95% CI of 0.83–1.01.
The results met the trial's goal of non-inferiority.
The results did not demonstrate superiority.
Death from any cause occurred in 8.5% of the Mounjaro group.
Death from any cause occurred in 10.1% of the dulaglutide group.
The hazard ratio was 0.84.
The 95% confidence interval was 0.75–0.94.
This outcome was not a primary endpoint.
This outcome was not adjusted for multiple comparisons.
Therefore, Mounjaro results should be read as exploratory.
The comparator was an active medicine rather than placebo.
These results describe how Mounjaro performs against another GLP-1.
These results do not describe how much Mounjaro lowers risk compared with no treatment.
Common Side Effects:
The most common tirzepatide side effects are:
Diarrhea
Vomiting
Constipation
Nausea (mild to moderate)
Decreased appetite
Abdominal pain.
Serious side effects:
Tirzepatide can cause serious side effects.
Call your doctor right away if you have any of the following:
Stop taking tirzepatide and call your healthcare provider immediately if you have severe pain in your stomach area that will not go away.
This applies if the pain occurs with or without vomiting.
This also applies if the pain spreads to your back.
Severe stomach pain is a sign of pancreatitis.
Diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting can cause dehydration.
Dehydration may lead to kidney problems.
Drink plenty of fluids to reduce this risk.
Tell your doctor if you are unable to keep fluids down.
Gallbladder problems include severe upper stomach pain.
They include fever.
They include nausea.
They include vomiting.
They include yellowing of the skin or eyes (jaundice).
They include clay-colored stools.
Serious allergic reactions require seeking emergency medical attention.
Seek emergency medical attention if you experience hives.
Seek emergency medical attention if you experience difficulty breathing.
Seek emergency medical attention if you feel light-headed.
Seek emergency medical attention if you experience swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.
These symptoms include anaphylaxis or angioedema.
Low blood sugar is hypoglycemia.
Symptoms may include headache, hunger, weakness, sweating, confusion, irritability, dizziness, fast heart rate, or feeling jittery.
Vision changes: Tell your doctor about any blurred vision or other vision changes, particularly if you have type 2 diabetes.
Thyroid tumor: Symptoms may include a lump or swelling in your neck, trouble swallowing, hoarseness, or shortness of breath.
Mental health changes: Tell your healthcare provider right away about any new or worsening depression, mood changes, or thoughts of suicide or self-harm.
Aspiration during surgery may increase the risk of food or liquid entering the lungs.
Tirzepatide slows stomach emptying.
This risk may occur during procedures using anesthesia or sedation.
Tell all your healthcare providers you are taking tirzepatide before any planned surgery or procedure.
This is not a complete list of side effects.
Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects.
Report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.
Who should not use tirzepatide:
Type 1 diabetes patients (tirzepatide is not approved for type 1 diabetes)
Personal or family history of medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC)
Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia syndrome type 2 (MEN 2).
Use with Caution:
History of pancreatitis (safety not established in patients with prior pancreatitis)
Severe gastrointestinal disease
Diabetic retinopathy complications.
Tirzepatide carries a Boxed Warning for the risk of possible thyroid tumors.
Possible Thyroid Tumors (Including Cancer):
Tell your healthcare provider if you get a lump or swelling in your neck, hoarseness, trouble swallowing, or shortness of breath.
These may be symptoms of thyroid cancer.
In studies with rats, tirzepatide and medicines that work like tirzepatide have caused thyroid tumors.
These tumors include thyroid cancer.
It is not known if tirzepatide will cause thyroid tumors.
It is not known if tirzepatide will cause medullary thyroid carcinoma.
Medullary thyroid carcinoma is a type of thyroid cancer.
This applies to people.
Do not use tirzepatide if you or any of your family have ever had a type of thyroid cancer called medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC).
Do not use tirzepatide if you have an endocrine system condition called Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia syndrome type 2 (MEN 2).
Tell your doctor and pharmacist if you are allergic to tirzepatide, any other medications, or any of the ingredients in tirzepatide.
There is a list of ingredients in tirzepatide, at the bottom of this page.
Tell your healthcare team if you are planning surgery or other procedure that uses anesthesia or deep sedation.
Tell your doctor if you have or have ever had kidney or pancreas disease, a history of diabetic retinopathy or any stomach problems such as difficulty digesting food.
Tell your doctor if you plan to become pregnant, are pregnant, or are breastfeeding.
If you become pregnant while taking tirzepatide, you should tell your doctor.
There is a pregnancy exposure registry for women who take Zepbound during pregnancy.
Talk to your healthcare provider about how you can take part.
Call 1-844-524-0039.
Visit https://pregnancyregistry.lilly.com/zepbound.
