You should follow the directions on your prescription label carefully.
You should ask your pharmacist or doctor to explain any part you do not understand.
It is important to use this medicine exactly as directed.
Do not take more or less of it or take it more often than prescribed by your doctor.
Tirzepatide is injected under the skin (subcutaneously) using a pre-filled single-dose pen.
It is also injected using a multi-dose KwikPen (for single patient use).
Additionally, it is injected using a single-dose vial with a syringe.
Caregivers or patients should be trained on proper injection techniques.
They should read the instructions for use.
Children should not inject children with the KwikPen.
An adult caregiver should give a child a child's injections.
Tirzepatide is usually given:
once a week.
can be given with or without meals at any time of the day.
may be injected into the thigh, abdomen, or have another person inject Tirzepatide into the back of your upper arm
You should rotate injection sites with each dose.
Do not inject into a muscle (intramuscularly) or vein (intravenously).
Do not mix insulin and tirzepatide together in the same injection.
You may give an injection of tirzepatide and insulin in the same body area, but not right next to each other.
Your doctor will probably start you on a low dose.
This dose may be gradually increased.
However, it will not be increased more than once every 4 weeks.
You may change the day of the week you use tirzepatide as long as there are at least three days between doses.
Do not stop taking Tirzepatide without talking to your doctor.
Tirzepatide KwikPen:
The KwikPen contains 4 fixed doses.
One dose is injected weekly (subcutaneously).
For your weekly dose, you need to turn the dose knob all the way until KwikPen stops.
You also need to turn the dose knob until the 1 icon is in the dose window.
After 4 doses, throw away (discard) the Pen, including the unused medicine.
The Pen will prevent you from dialing a full dose after you have given yourself 4 weekly doses.
Do not inject the leftover medicine.
Do not transfer the leftover medicine from your Pen into a syringe.
Do not share your tirzepatide Pen with other people, even if the pen needle has been changed.
You may give other people a serious infection or get a serious infection from other people.
People who are blind or have vision problems should not use the Pen.
They should not use the Pen without help.
The help must come from a person who has been trained to use the pen.
Usual Zepbound Dose for Weight Loss and OSA (Adult):
Initial dose: 2.5 mg under the skin (subcutaneously) once a week.
After 4 weeks: The dosage should be increased to 5 mg subcutaneously once a week.
Further dose increases may occur after at least 4 weeks on the current dose.
The dosage may be increased in 2.5 mg increments.
Recommended maintenance dose for weight loss: 5 mg, 10 mg, or 15 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly.
Recommended maintenance dose for OSA: 10 mg or 15 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly.
Maximum dose: 15 mg subcutaneously once a week.
Dose of Mounjaro for Type 2 Diabetes (Adults and Children 10 Years and Older):
Initial dose: 2.5 mg subcutaneously once a week.
Additional glycemic control is needed.
Increase the dose in 2.5 mg increments after at least 4 weeks on the current dose.
Maximum dose (adults): 15 mg subcutaneously once a week.
Maximum dose (children 10 years and older): 10 mg subcutaneously once a week.
Comments: The starting dose and escalation schedule are designed to reduce the risk of gastrointestinal side effects.
Dose of Mounjaro to Reduce Cardiovascular Risk (Adults With Type 2 Diabetes at High Risk):
The same dosing and escalation schedule applies.
Start at 2.5 mg subcutaneously once a week.
Increase in 2.5 mg increments after at least 4 weeks on the current dose.
Continue up to a maximum of 15 mg once a week.
In the SURPASS-CVOT trial, participants were titrated to 15 mg once weekly.
Participants were titrated to their maximum tolerated dose.
How to Use Mounjaro:
Give once weekly at any time of day, with or without meals.
Inject subcutaneously into the abdomen or thigh.
Another person may inject into the back of the upper arm.
Rotate injection sites with each dose.
Missed dose: Give as soon as possible within 4 days (96 hours).
If more than 4 days have passed, skip the missed dose.
Give the next dose on the regularly scheduled day.
The day of weekly administration can be changed, if necessary.
The time between the 2 doses must be at least 3 days (72 hours).
If you miss a dose of tirzepatide, take the missed dose as soon as you remember.
Take the missed dose if it is within 4 days after the missed dose.
However, if more than 4 days have passed since your last dose.
Then skip the missed dose.
And continue your regular dosing schedule.
Do not inject two doses within 3 days of each other.
In case of overdose, call the poison control helpline at 1-800-222-1222.
Information is also available online at Poison Help.
If the victim has collapsed, had a seizure, has trouble breathing, or can't be awakened.
Immediately call emergency services at 911.
Tell your doctor and pharmacist what prescription and over-the-counter medications, vitamins, nutritional supplements, and herbal products you are planning to take or are taking.
They may interact with tirzepatide.
Your doctor may need to change the doses of your medications or monitor you carefully for side effects.
Birth control pills may not work as well while receiving Tirzepatide.
Your doctor may suggest you use another form of birth control for 4 weeks after starting tirzepatide.
Your doctor may suggest you use another form of birth control for 4 weeks after each dose change.
Taking tirzepatide with other medicines to treat diabetes, such as sulfonylureas or insulin, may increase your risk of hypoglycemia.
Talk to your doctor and pharmacist about low blood sugar and how to manage low blood sugar.
Tirzepatide delays gastric emptying.
It may alter the absorption of other orally administered medicines.
This list is not complete.

Single-dose pens and vials (Zepbound and Mounjaro)
Store in the refrigerator between 36°F and 46°F (2°C and 8°C).
Do not freeze.
Do not use if the medication has been frozen.
Keep in the original carton until use to protect from light.
Single-dose pens and vials may be kept at room temperature.
The pens and vials may be kept at room temperature if needed.
They may be kept at room temperature up to 86°F (30°C).
They may be kept at room temperature for up to 21 days.
Once at room temperature, do not return to the refrigerator.
Discard after 21 days at room temperature if unused.
KwikPen (Zepbound KwikPen and Mounjaro KwikPen)
Before first use:
Store in the refrigerator between 36°F and 46°F (2°C and 8°C).
Keep until the expiration date printed on the label.
Do not freeze.
Discard the pen if pen has been frozen.
After first use:
Store in the refrigerator or at room temperature up to 86°F (30°C).
Keep away from heat and light.
Discard the pen 30 days after first use, even if medicine remains.
Discard after 4 weekly doses have been given.
General storage advice (all tirzepatide products)
Keep all pens, vials, needles, and syringes out of reach of children.
Dispose of used pens, needles, and syringes in an approved sharps container.

Zepbound and Mounjaro
Eli Lilly and Company.
Marketed by: Lilly USA, LLC, Indianapolis, IN 46285, USA.
Zepbound and Mounjaro contain tirzepatide.
They are made by Eli Lilly.
Zepbound and Mounjaro are both administered as a once-weekly subcutaneous injection.
Zepbound and Mounjaro have different FDA-approved indications and insurance coverage.
Q: Which GLP-1 drug is best for weight loss?
The most effective GLP-1 drug for weight loss currently is tirzepatide.
Tirzepatide is marketed under the name Zepbound for weight loss.
Tirzepatide is marketed under the name Mounjaro for diabetes.
Clinical trials and recent meta-analyses consistently show tirzepatide delivers the most substantial weight reduction among GLP-1 medications.
The average weight loss is up to 22.5% of body weight.
Q: How long does it take for Mounjaro (tirzepatide) to start working?
Mounjaro (generic name: tirzepatide) will start to lower your blood sugar levels right away.
Mounjaro can take 8 to 12 weeks to reach your target A1C goal.
Studies have shown that Mounjaro can take 8 weeks to reach an A1C target of ≤7%.
Studies have shown that Mounjaro can take 12 weeks to reach an A1C ≤6.5%.
These findings are compared to some other diabetes treatments.
Significant weight loss can occur as early as 28-weeks.
Q: Tirzepatide vs semaglutide: How do Tirzepatide vs semaglutide compare?
A: Semaglutide and tirzepatide are prescription medicines used for type 2 diabetes and long-term weight management.
Semaglutide products include Ozempic, Rybelsus, Wegovy, and Wegovy HD.
Tirzepatide products include Mounjaro and Zepbound.
These medicines differ in dosing, approved uses, side effects, and cost.
You should discuss options with your healthcare provider.
Q: How does retatrutide compare to tirzepatide?
Retatrutide is an investigational triple hormone agonist.
Retatrutide targets GLP-1, GIP, and glucagon.
Retatrutide is showing promising early results for obesity.
Retatrutide is showing promising early results for type 2 diabetes.
Tirzepatide is an FDA-approved dual agonist.
Tirzepatide targets GLP-1 and GIP.
Tirzepatide is available for use today.
Q: How much is Zepbound with or without insurance?
Zepbound may cost $25 for a 1-month or 3-month pen prescription.
This low cost applies if you have insurance and are eligible for the Savings Card.
Without insurance, Zepbound costs start at $299 for a 2.5mg vial or 2.5mg KwikPen.
Without insurance, the cost is $399 per month for a 5mg dose.
Without insurance, the cost is $449 per month for the higher doses.
Q: Can you get tirzepatide from a compounding pharmacy?
Tirzepatide cannot be legally compounded in a U.S. pharmacy at this time.
This is because Tirzepatide was removed from the FDA drug shortage list.
The removal occurred as of October 2, 2024.
When drugs are in short supply, the FDA can allow temporary compounding of some medicines.
This allows patients to access their treatments.
Tirzepatide is now widely available as a prescription medicine.
The medicine is made by Eli Lilly.
It is sold under the brand name Mounjaro for type 2 diabetes.
It is sold under the brand name Zepbound for weight loss and sleep apnea.
Q: How much weight can I lose on Zepbound?
The specific amount of weight you might lose from Zepbound is uncertain.
Significant weight loss ranging from 35.5 lb (16.1 kg) to 52.0 lb (23.6 kg) was observed in the 72-week SURMOUNT-1 clinical trial.
The SURMOUNT-1 clinical trial compared different Zepbound (tirzepatide) doses to a placebo (inactive treatment).
Q: Is Zepbound covered by insurance?
Most major insurance plans cover Zepbound.
Your doctor may need to submit prior authorization before approval.
Check your plan for deductibles, copays, and coinsurance.
Eligible Medicare Part D beneficiaries may receive Zepbound KwikPen through the temporary Medicare GLP-1 Bridge program for $50 per monthly supply.
Tramadol is an opioid agonist.
Tramadol may be used to treat moderate to moderately severe chronic pain in adults.
It is used when other pain treatments such as non-opioid pain medicines do not treat your pain well enough.
It is also used when you cannot tolerate other pain treatments.
Extended-release forms of tramadol may be used in adults who require around-the-clock treatment of their pain for an extended period.
An opioid pain medicine such as tramadol can put you at risk for overdose and death.
You are at risk for opioid addiction, abuse, and misuse that can lead to death.
This risk exists even if you take your dose correctly as prescribed.
Brands of tramadol include ConZip, Qdolo, Ryzolt, Ultram, Ultram ER, and Ultracet.
Many have been discontinued.
Generic tramadol is still available.
Taking tramadol in combination with other pain-relieving medicines can provide better pain relief.
The other pain-relieving medicines include acetaminophen, ibuprofen, or aspirin.
Taking just one pain-relieving medicine by itself provides less relief.
See Can you take tramadol in combination with acetaminophen, ibuprofen, or aspirin?
Tramadol first gained FDA approval on March 3, 1995.
Originally, tramadol was classified as a non-controlled substance.
Tramadol was reclassified in 2014 to Schedule IV of the Controlled Substances Act with restrictions on tramadol's prescribing.
Tramadol is an opioid (see Is tramadol an opioid?
).
A person's genetics can affect the pain-relieving properties of tramadol (see How do genetics affect tramadol?
).
Tramadol is used for the management of moderate to moderately severe pain in adults.
This includes postoperative pain.
Postoperative pain is pain after surgery.
Tramadol treats pain severe enough to require opioids.
It may be used as an alternative to other forms of pain relief.
Those other forms did not relieve pain well enough or were not tolerated.
Tramadol extended-release capsules may be used by adults.
The adults require around-the-clock treatment of their moderate to moderately severe chronic pain.
The treatment is for an extended time.
Tramadol should not be used in children younger than 12 years old.
It should not be used in anyone younger than 18 years old with other risk factors, such as breathing problems.
It should not be used in anyone who has recently had surgery to remove their tonsils or adenoids.
Extended-release tramadol (Ultram ER) should not be given to anyone younger than 18 years.
Yes, tramadol is an opioid.
The opioid activity of tramadol is due to tramadol's binding to mu-opioid receptors.
Get emergency help or call 911 right away if you take too many tramadol (overdose).
Serious or life-threatening breathing problems that can lead to death may occur.
These problems may occur when you first start taking tramadol.
These problems may occur when your dose is changed.
These problems may occur if you take too much (overdose).
Ask your healthcare provider about medicines like naloxone or nalmefene that can be used in an emergency to reverse an opioid overdose.
Taking tramadol with other opioid medicines, benzodiazepines, gabapentinoids, alcohol, or other central nervous system depressants can cause severe drowsiness, decreased awareness, breathing problems, coma, and death.
Other central nervous system depressants include street drugs.
Gabapentinoids include gabapentin or pregabalin.
Do not take tramadol with any other medicine that contains tramadol.
Check the label of every pain medicine you take, as some combination products contain tramadol.
You should be given a Medication Guide each time you fill a tramadol prescription.
Read tramadol every time, even if you have taken tramadol before, because the information may have changed.
Your healthcare provider or pharmacist should also go through the risks of tramadol and how to take tramadol safely with you.
They should also explain what to do in an emergency.
Never give anyone else your tramadol.
Anyone else could die from taking tramadol.
Selling or giving away tramadol is against the law.
Store tramadol securely, out of sight and reach of children, and in a location not accessible by others, including visitors to the home.
Common side effects:
The most common side effects of tramadol are:
dizziness or vertigo
nausea
constipation
headache
drowsiness
vomiting
itching.
Dry mouth is also commonly reported with the extended-release forms of tramadol.
A person's genetics can affect their response to tramadol, including their risk of side effects (see How do genetics affect tramadol?
).
Serious tramadol side effects and warnings:
Tramadol may cause serious side effects, some of which may be fatal.
Tramadol can slow or stop your breathing and may be habit-forming.
Misuse of this medicine can cause addiction, overdose, or death.
This risk is especially high in a child.
This risk is especially high in other people using the medicine without a prescription.
Keep this medicine where others cannot get to it.
Do not take more tramadol than prescribed, as an overdose can be fatal.
Selling or giving away tramadol is against the law.
A person caring for you should give naloxone.
A person caring for you should seek emergency medical attention.
You should receive these actions if you have slow breathing with long pauses.
You should receive these actions if you have blue-colored lips.
You should receive these actions if you find it hard to wake up.
Serious breathing problems may be more likely in older adults.
Serious breathing problems may be more likely in people who are debilitated.
Serious breathing problems may be more likely in people who have wasting syndrome.
Serious breathing problems may be more likely in people who have chronic breathing disorders.
Call your doctor at once if you have noisy breathing.
Call your doctor at once if you have sighing.
Call your doctor at once if you have shallow breathing.
Call your doctor at once if you have breathing that stops during sleep.
Call your doctor at once if you have a slow heart rate.
Call your doctor at once if you have a weak pulse.
Call your doctor at once if you have a light-headed feeling.
Call your doctor at once if you have a light-headed feeling like you might pass out.
Call your doctor at once if you have a seizure.
Call your doctor at once if you have convulsions.
Call your doctor at once if you have low cortisol levels.
Low cortisol levels include symptoms such as nausea.
Low cortisol levels include symptoms such as vomiting.
Low cortisol levels include symptoms such as loss of appetite.
Low cortisol levels include symptoms such as dizziness.
Low cortisol levels include symptoms such as worsening tiredness.
Low cortisol levels include symptoms such as weakness.
Call your doctor at once if you have low sodium levels.
Low sodium levels are also known as hyponatremia.
Symptoms include headache, confusion, weakness, unsteadiness, or feeling sick low blood sugar (hypoglycemia).
Symptoms include shakiness, sweating, hunger, confusion, or a fast heartbeat.
Seek medical attention right away if you have symptoms of serotonin syndrome.
Symptoms of serotonin syndrome include agitation, hallucinations, fever, sweating, shivering, fast heart rate, muscle stiffness, twitching, loss of coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea.
Seizures have been reported in patients taking tramadol.
Your risk of seizures is higher if you are taking higher doses than recommended.
Seizure risk is also higher in those with epilepsy or a history of seizures.
Seizure risk is also higher in those taking certain antidepressants, other opioids, MAO inhibitors, or other medicines that lower the seizure threshold.
You should not take tramadol if you have significant breathing problems.
You should not take tramadol if you have acute or severe asthma in a setting where you cannot be monitored.
You should not take tramadol if you have a blockage in your stomach or intestines.
You should not take tramadol if you have taken an MAO inhibitor in the past 14 days.
Examples of MAO inhibitors include isocarboxazid, linezolid, methylene blue injection, phenelzine, and tranylcypromine.
Tell your doctor before starting tramadol if you have a head injury or increased pressure inside your skull.
Tramadol should be used with particular care in these situations.
Prescribers are advised not to prescribe tramadol for people who are suicidal or prone to addiction.
Prescribers are advised to use tramadol with caution in people taking tranquilizers or antidepressants.
Prescribers are advised to use tramadol with caution in people drinking heavily.
If you are struggling with thoughts of harming yourself, or with your use of any medicine, tell your healthcare provider.
The healthcare provider can help you find the right treatment and support.
Fatal side effects can occur if you use tramadol with alcohol.
Fatal side effects can occur if you use tramadol with other drugs that cause drowsiness or slow your breathing.
People taking extended-release forms of tramadol should not consume alcohol-containing beverages at the same time.
Alcohol is also not advised with immediate-release forms of tramadol.
Serious allergic reactions have occurred with tramadol.
Some of these reactions included anaphylaxis.
Some of these reactions caused death.
People who are allergic to other opioids are at higher risk.
Other hypersensitivity reactions include itching.
They include hives.
They include narrowing of the airways.
They include swelling of the face and airways (angioedema).
They include serious skin reactions such as Stevens-Johnson Syndrome.
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to tramadol.
Symptoms of an allergic reaction to tramadol include hives.
Symptoms of an allergic reaction to tramadol include difficulty breathing.
Symptoms of an allergic reaction to tramadol include swelling in your face.
Symptoms of an allergic reaction to tramadol include swelling in your throat.
Get emergency medical help if you have a severe skin reaction.
Symptoms of a severe skin reaction include fever.
Symptoms of a severe skin reaction include a sore throat.
Symptoms of a severe skin reaction include burning in your eyes.
Symptoms of a severe skin reaction include skin pain.
Symptoms of a severe skin reaction include a red or purple skin rash that spreads.
Symptoms of a severe skin reaction include blistering.
Symptoms of a severe skin reaction include peeling.
Tramadol may make pain worse rather than better in some people.
This effect is called opioid-induced hyperalgesia.
Talk to your doctor if the pain you have increases.
Talk to your doctor if you feel more sensitive to pain.
Talk to your doctor if you have new pain after taking tramadol.
Tramadol extended-release capsules and tablets may complicate a healthcare provider’s clinical assessment of an acute abdominal condition.
They are not recommended for people with severe liver or severe kidney impairment.
Taking tramadol during pregnancy may cause life-threatening withdrawal symptoms in the newborn.
These symptoms are known as neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome (NOWS).
Some people are "ultra-rapid metabolizers" of tramadol.
This is because of a variation in an enzyme.
The enzyme is called CYP2D6.
People's bodies convert tramadol into tramadol's active form faster than usual.
This can produce dangerously high opioid levels.
This can occur even at a normal dose.
Life-threatening breathing problems and deaths have occurred in children for this reason.
This has happened including after surgery to remove the tonsils or adenoids.
Tramadol should not be given to a child younger than 12 years old.
It should not be given to anyone younger than 18 years old who has recently had surgery to remove the tonsils or adenoids.
Extended-release tramadol should not be given to anyone younger than 18 years.
Tramadol may cause drowsiness or affect your ability to drive or perform hazardous tasks.
Do not perform these tasks if tramadol causes confusion, mental impairment, or drowsiness.
Tramadol can cause withdrawal symptom s if stopped suddenly after being taken for a long period of time.
Symptoms may include restlessness, drug craving, anxiety, and sweating.
Do not stop taking tramadol suddenly without talking to your healthcare provider.
Stopping abruptly can cause serious withdrawal symptoms.
Stopping abruptly can cause uncontrolled pain.

Important Information Guiding Use in Pediatric Patients:
Do not give tramadol to a child younger than 12 years of age or extended-release tramadol to anyone under 18 years.
Do not give tramadol to a child younger than 18 years of age after surgery to remove the tonsils and/or adenoids.
Avoid giving tramadol to children between 12 and 18 years of age who have risk factors for breathing problems.
These include having recently had surgery.
These include obstructive sleep apnea.
These include obesity.
These include severe lung disease.
These include neuromuscular disease.
These include taking other medicines that can slow breathing.
Pregnancy:
Tramadol may harm an unborn baby.
It is not usually recommended during pregnancy.
Use of tramadol for an extended period of time during pregnancy can cause withdrawal symptoms in your newborn baby that could be life-threatening if not recognized and treated.
Neonatal seizures, neonatal withdrawal syndrome, fetal death, and stillbirth have all been reported.
Tramadol is also not recommended during or immediately before labor.
It should only be used in pregnancy if the benefits outweigh the risks.
If you are already taking tramadol and become pregnant, do not stop taking tramadol without talking to your healthcare provider.
Stopping tramadol suddenly can be harmful to you both.
Breastfeeding:
Breastfeeding is not recommended while taking tramadol.
Tramadol may harm your baby.
Mothers who are ultra-rapid metabolizers of tramadol can have higher than expected levels of tramadol's active form in their breast milk.
This can be dangerous for a breastfed infant.
Carefully observe infants for increased sleepiness (more than usual), breathing difficulties, or limpness.
Seek immediate medical care if you notice these signs.
Do not change your dose.
Take tramadol exactly as prescribed by your healthcare provider.
Use the lowest dose possible for the shortest time needed.
Tell your doctor if you feel an increased urge to take more of tramadol.
For acute (short-term) pain, you may only need to take tramadol for a few days.
You may have some tramadol left over that you did not use.
See disposal information at the bottom of this section for directions on how to safely dispose of tramadol.
Take your prescribed dose as indicated by your healthcare provider.
Take your prescribed dose as indicated by your healthcare provider.
Immediate-release tramadol is usually taken as 50 mg to 100 mg.
This dosage corresponds to one or two 50 mg tablets.
The medication is taken every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain.
This dosing schedule occurs after your dose has been settled.
Do not take more than your prescribed dose.
Do not take more than 400 mg of tramadol in one day.
For immediate-release tramadol, the dosage is eight 50 mg tablets a day.
These tablets are taken in divided doses.
Alternatively, the dosage is 80 mL of the 5 mg/mL oral solution.
Extended-release tramadol has a lower daily limit of 300 mg, taken as one dose once a day.
Always check the strength printed on your own bottle or packet.
Do not take tramadol together with any other medicine that contains tramadol.
If you miss a dose, take your next dose at your usual time.
Swallow the capsule or tablet whole to avoid exposure to a potentially fatal overdose.
Do not crush, chew, break, open, or dissolve.
Never crush or break a tramadol tablet to inhale the powder or mix tramadol tablet into a liquid to inject tramadol into your vein.
This practice has resulted in death.
May be taken with or without food.
If you take the oral solution:
Tramadol oral solution is measured in millilitres (mL), not milligrams (mg).
It contains 5 mg of tramadol in every 1 mL.
Confusing the two units can cause a fatal overdose.
Always measure your dose with a graduated oral syringe or measuring cup marked in mL.
Your pharmacy can supply these tools.
Never measure your dose with a household teaspoon or tablespoon.
If your prescription is written in milligrams, ask your pharmacist to confirm how many millilitres that is.
Do this before you take your first dose.
Notes:
You may have withdrawal symptoms if you stop using tramadol suddenly.
Ask your doctor before stopping tramadol.
Ask your healthcare provider about medicines like naloxone or nalmefene that can be used in an emergency to reverse an opioid overdose.
Do not keep leftover tramadol.
Just one dose can cause death if someone uses leftover tramadol accidentally or improperly.
Ask your pharmacist where to locate a drug take-back disposal program.
If there is no take-back program, mix the leftover medicine with cat litter or coffee grounds in a sealed plastic bag.
Throw the bag in the trash.
Call your healthcare provider if the dose you are taking does not control your pain.
If you have been taking tramadol regularly, do not stop taking tramadol without talking to your healthcare provider.
Adult Tramadol Dosage for Pain:
Immediate-release formulations (tablets and oral solution)
Treatment usually starts at 25 mg a day.
The dosage increases by 25 mg every 3 days.
This process continues until the target of 100 mg a day (25 mg four times a day) is reached.
Doses of 25 mg are measured using the oral solution.
Immediate-release tramadol tablets are only available as 50 mg.
For the immediate-release tablets, a common starting dose is one 50 mg tablet a day.
This dose is used to see how well you tolerate 50 mg tablet.
Alternatively, the dose prescribed by your doctor may be used.
If needed, the total daily dose can be increased by 50 mg every 3 days as tolerated.
The increase continues to reach 200 mg a day.
This target dose is equivalent to one 50 mg tablet four times a day.
After this titration, 50 mg to 100 mg can be taken every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain.
This amount corresponds to one or two 50 mg tablets.
The maximum is 400 mg a day.
This is eight 50 mg tablets in divided doses.
Alternatively, it is 80 mL of the 5 mg/mL oral solution.
Extended-release capsules and tablets
Tramadol ER 100 mg once a day (one 100 mg tablet or capsule) at approximately the same time each day.
Can be increased by 100 mg every 5 days if needed, to a maximum of 300 mg a day, taken as one dose once a day.
Extended-release tramadol is not intended for as-needed pain relief.
It should not be taken with any other tramadol product.
Dosage in kidney or liver impairment
Severe kidney impairment requires specific dosing adjustments for immediate-release tramadol.
For this condition, doses are spaced 12 hours apart.
The maximum daily dose is 200 mg.
This maximum consists of four 50 mg tablets.
These tablets are given as two 100 mg doses.
The doses are administered 12 hours apart.
Severe liver impairment is a condition.
For immediate-release tramadol, the recommended dose is 50 mg (one 50 mg tablet) every 12 hours.
This is 100 mg a day.
Extended-release tramadol is not recommended for people with severe kidney or severe liver impairment.
Dosage in older adults
No separate maximum dose applies on the basis of age.
Tramadol should be started at the low end of the dosing range.
The dose should be increased slowly.
Frequent review is needed for drowsiness and breathing problems.
Side effects that led people to stop treatment were more common over the age of 75.
These side effects were mostly stomach and bowel effects.
Examples included constipation.
Kidney function tends to decline with age.
Declining kidney function affects how tramadol is cleared.
Your doctor may take kidney function into account when choosing your dose.
Tramadol is used for pain.
You are unlikely to miss a dose.
Skip any missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose.
Do not use two doses at one time.
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
An overdose can be fatal.
This is especially true in a child or other person using the medicine without a prescription.
Overdose symptoms may include severe drowsiness, pinpoint pupils, slow breathing, or no breathing.
Your doctor may recommend you get naloxone.
Naloxone is a medicine to reverse an opioid overdose.
You should keep naloxone with you at all times.
A person caring for you can give naloxone if you stop breathing or don't wake up.
Your caregiver must still get emergency medical help.
Your caregiver may need to perform CPR (cardiopulmonary resuscitation) on you while waiting for help to arrive.
Anyone can buy naloxone from a pharmacy or local health department.
Make sure any person caring for you knows where you keep naloxone and how to use it.
Do not drink alcohol or use prescription or over-the-counter medicines that contain alcohol.
Dangerous side effects or death could occur.
Avoid driving or hazardous activities until you know how tramadol will affect you.
Dizziness or drowsiness can cause falls, accidents, or severe injuries.
You may have breathing problems or withdrawal symptoms if you start or stop taking certain other medicines.
Tramadol is broken down by two liver enzymes called CYP3A4 and CYP2D6.
Medicines that block or speed up these enzymes change how much tramadol and how much of tramadol's active form are in your body.
Starting one of these medicines and stopping one both shift the balance.
Tell your doctor whenever you begin or finish a course of an antibiotic, antifungal medication, heart or blood pressure medication, seizure medication, or medicine to treat HIV or hepatitis C.
Many other drugs can be dangerous when used with tramadol.
Tell your doctor if you also use:
medicine for allergies, asthma, blood pressure, motion sickness, irritable bowel, or overactive bladder
other opioid medicines
buprenorphine, butorphanol, nalbuphine, or pentazocine.
These opioids work differently.
These opioids can block tramadol's pain-relieving effect.
These opioids can bring on sudden withdrawal symptoms.
Opioids are generally not taken with tramadol.
a benzodiazepine sedative like Valium, Klonopin, or Xanax
sleep medicine, muscle relaxers, or other drugs that make you drowsy
drugs that affect serotonin, such as antidepressants, stimulants, or medicine for migraines or Parkinson's disease
carbamazepine, which is not recommended with tramadol because carbamazepine can significantly reduce tramadol's pain-relieving effect and add to the risk of seizures
digoxin or warfarin, which need extra monitoring when taken with tramadol.
This list is not complete.
Other drugs may interact with tramadol.
These drugs include prescription and over-the-counter medicines.
These drugs also include vitamins and herbal products.
Not all possible interactions are listed here.

Do not share with others.
Q: Can You Take Tramadol with Acetaminophen, Ibuprofen, or Aspirin?
Tramadol can be safely combined with acetaminophen.
Tramadol can be safely combined with ibuprofen.
Tramadol can be safely combined with aspirin.
Combining tramadol with these medicines provides better pain relief.
This relief is better than using either medicine alone.
However, there are important age restrictions and clinical considerations to be aware of.
Q: How much tramadol should I give my dog?
A: How much tramadol you give your dog depends on the size of your dog.
Smaller dogs do not need as much tramadol as larger dogs need to relieve pain.
If you give too much tramadol your dog is likely to have more severe side effects.
The tramadol dosage for dogs is usually between 0.45 to 1.8 mg per pound of body weight (1mg to 4 mg/kg).
The frequency varies depending on what you are giving tramadol for.
For general pain, tramadol is given every 8 to 12 hours.
For cancer-related pain, tramadol can be administered every 6 hours.
Q: How long does it take for tramadol to start working?
Tramadol generally starts to relieve pain within an hour.
This applies to its fast-acting forms.
These forms are used for short-term pain management.
With slow-release or extended-release (ER) forms, Tramadol may take longer to start because it's gradually released over 12 or 24 hours.
Pain relief lasts longer.
Q: How long does tramadol withdrawal last?
A: Symptoms of tramadol withdrawal can start 8-24 hours after the last dose.
Untreated, withdrawal symptoms usually last for 4-10 days.
Withdrawal is caused by stopping tramadol suddenly.
Q: Is tramadol stronger than codeine?
Both tramadol and codeine are prescription painkillers used to treat moderate pain.
Tramadol and codeine are generally considered comparable in terms of pain relief.
Tramadol is sometimes considered slightly more effective for moderate to moderately severe pain.
This is due to Tramadol's dual mechanism of action.
Both are classified as "weak" opioids.
Q: How long does tramadol stay in your system?
Tramadol is eliminated from your system within roughly 2 days of taking tramadol.
This does not mean that tramadol can no longer be detected by certain drug tests.
Q: Can you take ibuprofen 800 mg with tramadol 50 mg?
A: Yes, you can take these medications together.
Tramadol is safe to take with ibuprofen.
It may be used to provide additional pain relief.
But be aware 800mg ibuprofen is a high dose of ibuprofen.
This high dose may cause gastrointestinal side effects.
These side effects include abdominal pain or reflux.
These effects may occur if taken long term.
Q: Is tramadol an anti-inflammatory or muscle relaxant?
A: No, Tramadol is not an anti-inflammatory drug or muscle relaxer.
Tramadol likely won't reduce any swelling you have when taken alone.
This is because it's not an anti-inflammatory drug.
Q: Does tramadol raise or lower blood pressure?
A: Tramadol has been linked to both high blood pressure and low blood pressure.
But, neither adverse reaction is common when Tramadol is taken as directed.

Q: What are the strongest sleeping pills?
A: There are many different types of sleeping pills available.
Some require a prescription.
Others are available over the counter.
In general, prescription sleeping pills are stronger than those found over the counter.
Some strong sleeping pill names include zolpidem, temazepam, and suvorexant.

Q: Does trazodone cause weight gain?
Trazodone may cause weight gain for some people.
Trazodone is not a common side effect of the medication.
Weight loss was shown to be slightly more common with trazodone.
Q: Does trazodone make you sleepy?
Trazodone can help you sleep.
Sleepiness is a side effect of the medication.
Doctors will sometimes prescribe trazodone for insomnia.
This is particularly for people who have both depression and difficulty sleeping.
However, the benefit of taking trazodone for insomnia is not well established.
The American Academy of Sleep Medicine recommends specific usage guidelines for trazodone and other antidepressants.
These medications should only be used for insomnia when other treatments have failed.
They should also be used for insomnia in patients who have depression.
Q: How long does trazodone stay in your system?
A: Trazodone is mostly cleared from a healthy adult’s body within 1 to 3 days after a single dose.
Trazodone's half-life is 5 to 13 hours.
It takes about 5.5 half-lives for a drug to leave the system.
Trazodone is typically eliminated in about 27 to 72 hours.
Q: Is trazodone a controlled substance or narcotic?
The FDA does not classify trazodone as a controlled substance.
Trazodone is not considered a narcotic.
Q: Can trazodone cause a false positive on a drug test?
Trazodone has been shown to cause false positive results on urine drug tests.
The false positive results are for amphetamines, MDMA, and LSD.
This potential for cross-reactivity is due to one of trazodone's primary metabolites.
The metabolite is meta-chlorophenylpiperazine (m-CPP).
m-CPP can interfere with specific immunoassay screenings.
Q: How long does it take for trazodone to work?
Trazodone may start to relieve depression 1 to 2 weeks after you begin taking it.
Trazodone can take up to 6 weeks for the full benefit of the medication to set in.

Ask your doctor before using opioid medication, a sleeping pill, a muscle relaxer, or medicine for anxiety or seizures.
Tell your doctor about all your current medicines.
Many drugs can affect venlafaxine, especially:
any other antidepressant, or other medicines containing venlafaxine or desvenlafaxine;
amphetamines;
fentanyl;
cimetidine;
tramadol;
St. John's wort, tryptophan (sometimes called L-tryptophan);
diet pills, weight loss medicine (such as phentermine);
a blood thinner (warfarin, Coumadin, Jantoven);
medicine to treat mood disorders, thought disorders, or mental illness - buspirone, lithium, and many others; or
migraine headache medicine - sumatriptan, zolmitriptan, and others.
This list is not complete and many other drugs may interact with venlafaxine.
List includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.
Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.
Q: How long do venlafaxine withdrawal symptoms last?
Venlafaxine withdrawal symptoms typically last up to 1 to 4 weeks.
They may persist even longer.
The timing varies widely.
People who stop suddenly, took higher doses, or used venlafaxine for years may have symptoms for two to three months or longer.
Q: What drugs can cause serotonin syndrome?
Common drug classes that can cause serotonin syndrome include selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs), and opioids like tramadol.
